Optimizely Hack Turns World's Biggest Websites Into Malware Dispensers

Optimizely Hack Turns World's Biggest Websites Into Malware Dispensers (report-uri.com)
			
		 	
				PattonPending writes: As reported by The Register, Zoom, the BBC, CNN, HSBC, and numerous other websites briefly served malware on November 16 after an attacker exploited Optimizely's "Experiments" feature to inject JavaScript into their pages through Optimizely's CDN. The malware replaced cryptocurrency addresses, attempted to steal credit card information and cookies, and sought to compromise iOS devices. Websites belonging to at least 178 organizations were affected via the "OPTIMJACK" vulnerability, which has since been patched. "Earlier today, Optimizely identified a security incident in which an unauthorized party exploited a vulnerability in Experimentation and used it to add malicious JavaScript to some customer projects," the company said. "We've fixed the vulnerability and removed the injected code from affected projects. We've also taken steps to secure affected user accounts and put additional protections in place to prevent unauthorized code from being delivered."
The researchers said they found malicious code in 188 of the 1,611 Optimizely scripts examined, including those on the company's own website and login portal, suggesting the number of affected businesses could be significantly higher.

2026-11-17
21:48:07 UTC
06:48:07 KST

The Securities and Exchange Commission is opening a civil probe into Nvidia over whether the company’s disclosures misled investors, according to The Wall Street Journal.
The investigation adds to Nvidia’s stack of drama after CEO Jensen Huang resigned following news of his extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, Alice Huang, an executive at Singapore-based data company Megaspeed.
The Commerce Department has been investigating Megaspeed since late 2024 under suspicion that the company diverted some of its Nvidia chips to China, which would violate U.S. export laws.
Congress has also demanded that Huang answer questions about his affair and how it might have affected his stance that export restrictions should be loosened. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent put it more bluntly over the weekend, accusing Nvidia of knowingly selling powerful chips to China to “goose their revenues and their earnings.”
The news of the SEC probe also comes as Nvidia reports earnings to a market that has seen its stock fall more than 10% since Huang departed. The chipmaker reported revenue of $115.8 billion, up 103% from a year ago.

Nike’s new air sole technology can mix soft and firm cushioning
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
21:38:08 UTC
06:38:08 KST

In 2024, Nike announced its Air Max 1000 that were manufactured using a 3D-printing process resulting in a shoe that had a uniform silhouette but varying areas of support and flexibility throughout. The company is now doing something similar with its iconic air-filled soles using its new Tuned Air technology. While previously the level of cushioning in Nike Air shoes was uniform across the entire sole, a new manufacturing process can produce multiple air pockets with varying levels of cushioning and stability.
Nike’s new air sole technology can mix soft and firm cushioning
The first shoes with Nike’s new Tuned Air technology will be available for runners starting in late 2027.
Nike has been putting air pockets into its shoes for nearly 50 years as a way to increase comfort and cushioning while making them lighter. Its air pockets have even found their way into apparel as a way to improve insulation by trapping body heat, but Nike plans to use its new Tuned Air technology as a way to customize the cushioning experience in its shoes and tailor them to specific athletes and sports. The first shoes featuring the technology are expected to arrive in late 2027 and will be targeted at runners.
While one of Nike’s earliest successes was track shoes with a grip design inspired by a waffle iron, the company credits the “vibrant colors on magazine covers” as the inspiration for its latest shoe technology. A Tuned Air sole is manufactured using a “proprietary digital print and selective lamination process” where an ink is applied in specific patterns that define where the layers bond to each other. This process can be used to create internal pockets in an unlimited combination of shapes and sizes, and by fine-tuning how much air is in each of those pockets, some parts of the sole can feel firmer while others feel softer.
The Tuned Air technology will not only allow Nike to highly customize the fit and feel of air sole shoes for different sports, it will also help keep the benefits of the custom tuning consistent across different sizes. The new manufacturing process will also help reduce the amount of time it takes Nike to go from a concept for a new shoe to a final design ready for production. “Prototypes that once took three weeks can now be made in three days,” the company claims in a release today. That gives Nike’s designers more time to refine new shoes, and more opportunity to experiment.

Google joins the tech outage trend by announcing it’s not having one
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
21:34:50 UTC
06:34:50 KST

Was Google feeling a little left out of the recent wave of tech outages? The company took to X on Tuesday to announce that its Gmail service is actually running just fine. In a post, the company said it was not currently seeing evidence of a broad Gmail service disruption and reminded users they could always check Gmail’s real-time status on its online dashboard.
As it turns out, the company was trying to head off reports of a Gmail outage that began circulating on X on Tuesday around the same time that Apple was seeing a number of verified outages of its own. While some posts on the social network suggested Gmail was experiencing a disruption, others said they were not having any problems.
The concerns seemed to stem from an X post by Downdetector, a crowdsourced website where users can report service disruptions themselves and see if others are having problems. Downdetector is not an official source for verifying or understanding the true scope of internet outages, and as people become more concerned about possible cyberattacks, the site’s data may become less valuable over time if every minor glitch and hiccup is reported as an “outage” by non-technical users.
The fact that Google had to announce it was not having an outage speaks to the growing anxiety around the reliability of major tech services. Over the past week, Apple and Microsoft experienced outages, an FAA network failure led to a nationwide ground stop, and some banks and airlines also reported disruptions.
However, Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton said on Fox Business Tuesday that the U.S. is not currently facing coordinated cyberattacks. He told the outlet, “We do not see the indicia of a coordinated cyberattack — let me just be clear about that — and we do not see the indicia of state-sponsored activity here.”
Instead, Clayton attributed the rise in issues to businesses patching their systems. The patching may be a downstream effect of AI, as newer models have become increasingly adept at uncovering previously unknown vulnerabilities. “They’re upgrading them, they’re putting patches in place,” he said, adding that this is happening at “a much larger scale” than before.
As a result, some of the systems are “not coming back as expected in all cases,” he added.

Java4Shell: RCE in OpenJDK when processing Base64
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
21:11:28 UTC
06:11:28 KST

A memory-corruption vulnerability in OpenJDK's Base64 decoder. A remote attacker with no credentials can overwrite heap memory, read it in the style of Heartbleed, and reach remote code execution.
After we disclosed PHP2Shell, we realized many people didn't know what PHP was. So we decided to go after something a little more familiar: Java.
Today, we are disclosing Java4Shell (CVE-2026-114938), a memory-corruption vulnerability in OpenJDK's Base64 decoder. OpenJDK is the most popular Java runtime. Under CVSS 4.0, this vulnerability scores 9.2 out of 10 (CRITICAL).
While PHP is a high-level web technology, Java sits much deeper. Java is the world's third most popular programming language, powering 90% of Fortune 500 companies. It is the beating heart of the modern economy. Banking, aviation, telecommunications, nearly every critical sector runs on Java, including national security systems.
Base64 is a very common way of representing binary data as text. It is used in authentication protocols, to encode file uploads, and to deliver network messages. To decode Base64 faster, OpenJDK uses specialized x86-64 CPU instructions to process many bytes at once. We discovered a bug in that optimized machine-level code that lets malformed input overwrite neighboring memory. With careful exploitation, the corruption can reveal sensitive memory, as Heartbleed did, or lead to remote code execution. The corruption itself can be triggered by just 64 A characters followed by a single =:
In older OpenJDK 17, the trigger input is 128 of A followed by =.
Write once, RCE everywhere
Our initial testing demonstrated remote code execution in the following widely deployed Java applications, in their default configs.
Pre-auth RCE:
Keycloak
Atlassian Jira
Atlassian Confluence
JFrog Artifactory
Jenkins
PingFederate
Post-auth RCE:
Apache Kafka Broker
Apache Solr
Elasticsearch
Gerrit
Spinnaker
The lists are non-exhaustive. We also demonstrated RCE in sample applications built on widely used frameworks, including Apache Struts and Spring Boot. Ordinary features such as authentication and file uploads can expose the vulnerable Base64 decoder, extending the potential impact far beyond the products we tested. samczsun also shared a client-side exploit with us. A Java program that opens a TLS connection to a malicious server can be compromised, even if it exposes no service of its own.
Here are a demo video of the exploits running:
Wait, Java has a memory-corruption vulnerability, in Base64?!
Yes. First time?
Memory corruption is the most common class of vulnerability on the Internet. But one of Java's biggest selling points is memory safety. Java manages memory on developers' behalf and checks memory accesses, protecting applications from the memory-corruption bugs common in other software.
But like PHP and many other technologies, much of Java itself is written in low-level languages like C, C++, and, in this case, assembly, where memory corruption is still a part of life.
To speed up Base64 decoding, OpenJDK uses specialized AVX-512 VBMI instructions on supported x86-64 processors. The bug sits in the hand-rolled assembly code that handles the final few characters. For the 65-character input above, OpenJDK allocates a 50-byte result array. After decoding the first 64 characters, the routine mishandles the stray = and calculates a negative output length. That invalid length becomes a full 64-byte write, sending 62 bytes past the end of the array. Java eventually rejects the input, but by then neighboring objects may already be corrupted.
Era of the centaur hacker
As with PHP2Shell, Calif used AI to find and weaponize Java4Shell. AI brings the horsepower. Our researchers bring the context. The best hacker today is neither AI nor human. It is the centaur hacker, part human, part AI.
It took our own Quang Luong an hour to discover the vulnerability, and two more hours to develop the first working exploit. The total token cost was less than $200.
One thing to note about this vulnerability is that there is no universal exploit. Depending on how Base64 is used and how the application works, different steps may be required for exploitation. Calif was able to accelerate the exploit development by spending initial effort building pipelines and strategies, then directing AI agents to use those for initial exploitation. Afterwards, most of the later chains are built almost autonomously with AI.
The tragedy of the commons
Java is one of many shared software stacks we all rely on. Over the years, it has grown to serve many different needs. Each feature request may make sense on its own, but everyone inherits the added complexity. Those who need a feature get the benefit. Everyone else carries the code and its risks, even if they never use it.
Java4Shell is a concrete example. The Base64 optimization helps applications that decode enormous amounts of data. But the code ships to everyone else too, including applications that gain little from it. On affected systems, an optimization few applications needed became a serious security risk.
Features can be useful, and most bugs can be fixed. But why do we have to worry about code we never needed in the first place?
A call for vibe-uncoding
If you don't need something, there is little reason to keep it running in your systems. Do you need that hand-rolled assembly Base64 decoder to process a few hundred bytes? Probably not.
Earlier this year we found Squidbleed, an older cousin of Heartbleed, hiding in the Squid proxy since 1997. The bug was in Squid's FTP module. But who uses FTP anymore? It's a dead protocol and should be removed.
Removing code is harder than it sounds. An apparently unused feature may have hidden dependencies. Someone has to understand what can be removed, make the change, and test what remains.
This is where AI can help to make that work practical at a scale we could not previously afford.
The same centaur hacker that discovered PHP2Shell and Java4Shell can comb a large codebase and flag dead code, bloated features, and needless optimizations. We call this vibe-uncoding: identify the parts a system actually needs, then remove everything else to reduce its attack surface.
With AI, everyone is asking, “What can we build?” But maybe the right question for security is, “What can we delete?”
The Tail of Base64
By Quang Luong
On affected OpenJDK builds, malformed Base64 data can cause a constant-value overwrite of allocated heap memory. Through remote Heap Feng Shui, we turn that overwrite into a heap leak and remote code execution.
Discovery
A few months back, I was developing a 0-click exploit on a certain phone vendor. The bug was constrained: it is a linear out-of-bounds write and we have no way to bypass ASLR. I almost considered it unexploitable until I audited the Android Runtime. Turns out, it has some predictable memory allocation pattern and if you align the registers from the starting memory corruption just right you can make the runtime interpret attacker's bytecode.
After completing that exploit chain, inspired (and bored), I decided to look into some thing completely different. So I pulled the official JVM implementation (OpenJDK HotSpot) and started auditing. Java is a mature platform, so I was aware that having a "good" bug here would be hard, but I wanted to give it my best attempt anyway.
Recalling my earlier lesson that complex systems and code crossing multiple boundaries are difficult to get right, I focused the audit on the JIT compiler, intrinsics, widely accessible APIs, and cryptography. Well, more precisely, I told Codex to look at those areas like any serious security researcher in 2026. To be honest, I didn't expect to find anything trivially exploitable, perhaps only some logic flaws or side-channel issues.
Most early results Codex brought me were slops, either unreachable or the requirement of exploitation is not realistic at all. This looks like a gamble I am going to lose. But you know what, fortune favors the bold. So I just prompted harder...
Eventually, Codex brought me a finding for a memory corruption in the Base64 decoder itself. I came looking for copper and found gold, through sheer persistence. But wait, is it really gold, or just fool's gold?
The vulnerability
The issue lies in OpenJDK HotSpot's x86-64 Base64 intrinsic. C2 is the most aggressive JIT compiler in the Java Virtual Machine. Specifically, if you trigger a function repeatedly, then it will compile that function into native code. Even then, ordinary native code itself is not fast enough. Modern CPUs have specialized instructions that allow processing data even faster. HotSpot uses them in optimized routines called intrinsics. The x86-64 Base64 intrinsic miscalculated the padded tail's store mask, causing an out-of-bounds write.
The x86-64 Base64 intrinsic iterates through the input in 256-byte blocks; if it is less than 256, then it will do so in 64-byte blocks. When the input is less than 64 bytes, it uses evmovdqub, the intrinsic for the VMOVDQU8 instruction. This instruction takes a 64-bit mask that decides which bytes are read from or written to memory. The mask is first calculated from the input and output length, then updated to reflect the number of padding bytes ('=') in the Base64-encoded string. This is shown in the following (pseudocode):
// Zero High Bits Starting with Specified Bit Position
input_mask := bzhiq(-1, input_length)
output_len := (input_length / 4) * 3
// handle padding
if input[input_length - 1] == '=':
output_len -= 1
input_mask >>= 1
if input[input_length - 2] == '=':
output_len -= 1
input_mask >>= 1
output_mask := bzhiq(-1, output_len)
In the above code, if the remaining input is of the form =, ==, X=, X==, XY=, or ===, then output_len will be computed as zero. However, since there is padding, output_len is decremented, resulting in an underflowed value of -1 or -2. Since bzhiq(-1, output_len) doesn’t change any bits if output_len >= 64, this results in output_mask == -1u64.
Later the code uses evmovdqub to read and write multiple bytes at a time. For a write, if the mask bit is 1, the byte at that index in the vector register is written to memory. So when output_mask == -1u64, all 64 bytes are written during the following operation:
The generated stub returns the number of bytes it successfully decoded, and returns early when it detects invalid input. The remaining bytes are handled by the Java code, which throws IllegalArgumentException if the input is invalid.
At runtime, the decoded buffer length is calculated as (ceil(inp_len / 4) * 3) - padding. When the bug is triggered, with 65 bytes ([64 bytes] + '=') as input, that calculation allocates a 50-byte buffer. During decoding the bug makes output_mask == -1 for evmovdqub, so 64 bytes are written starting at offset 48, an out-of-bounds write past the end of the buffer.
On OpenJDK 17, the trigger input is 'A' * 128 + '=' instead of 'A' * 64 + '=', because the length threshold to reach the code path is higher.
Exploitation strategy
The Base64 bug was gold, but not the type that sits on the riverbed waiting to be panned out. It was the type that was deep, dark, and hard to access. While we had memory corruption, exploiting it looked difficult, perhaps even borderline unexploitable, since it only wrote fixed bytes to the heap. Only the landing position of the out-of-bounds write is controllable.
We get the following 64 bytes write starting from the position in buffer where the remaining sets of Base64 decoded bytes would have been written to:
Full controlled write to byte 0 (in bound)
4-bit controlled write to byte 1 (in bound)
Uncontrolled write to bytes [2, 63]. For bytes 48..63, the value will always be 0x9a.
The uncontrolled out-of-bounds write turned out not to be a big problem. We reused a common JavaScript engine exploitation primitive, the classic technique of overwriting a buffer length.
We call the buffer whose length we overwrite the writer. Once its length is corrupted, it keeps accepting our chosen bytes, so if we place another array or object after it, we can corrupt that neighbor with all of our chosen values. That upgrades the fixed write into a bounded, chosen-value linear write.
If we then corrupt another array's length and its data is copied back into the response, we have built a heap disclosure primitive, the reader, from the out-of-bounds read. At this point, before any code execution, we already had JavaBleed, the ability to leak contents from the Java heap, which may hold PII, private keys, other secret keys, and tokens.
From the writer and reader, the rest of this write-up builds the full attack. We repair the objects the linear write damaged to keep the JVM running, mount data-only attacks on application logic, and escalate the bounded write into arbitrary writes that reach the Code Cache and JVM hooks for code execution.
But first things first, we need to align the heap to our advantage.
HotSpot normally gives each Java thread a thread-local allocation buffer (TLAB), a private slice of Eden. Objects created by different request threads advance different cursors, which gives us no useful order between a Base64 decoder in one TLAB and a writer in another.
The first grooming layer controls the allocation route. The critical array's size and the space left in the worker's TLAB decide whether HotSpot refills that TLAB or takes the attacker-preferred outside-TLAB allocation path.
The second layer controls the layout. However, we do not choose how G1 allocates memory, and we don't know the current heap state. To approach this limitation, Calif used the tried and tested method of Spray and Pray.
Object sizes and padding fix offsets inside region-sized spans. We spray several live writer and reader pairs to give the decoder output several possible neighbors. When its region lands immediately below a writer region, the widened reader produces a larger application response. We discard misses and retry until successful.
One early challenge during exploit development was that the AI would get stuck during heap grooming primitive development. In order to save tokens and time, Calif wrote a small heap tracer and visualizer to give the agent information about the heap state, combined with our expertise on specific cases.
Remote Heap Feng Shui
In order to perform remote grooming, each request data would be sent up until the critical heap allocation would occur. By ensuring the data that was previously sent had a TCP acknowledgement, the exploit would have control over the allocations up to date. Once we were confident our data so far is buffered on the target, we then sent the rest, completing the http request. This allows both heap allocation control and more precise schedule as delivering small network packets is more stable than big ones.
The exact allocation and free order schedule belongs to the targeted application profile, while we are able to control the adding, writer, reader, and decoder pieces.
The current attack is sensitive to network conditions, like RTT and jitter. Low, stable round-trip times are preferred for a more reliable attack. In our controlled environment, we performed this attack over the internet through public IP addresses instead of just assuming perfect laboratory conditions. For the current attack path to achieve optimal stability and reliability in exploitation, Calif turned to physics and utilized money to solve the problem, rather than experimenting with TCP/IP stack configuration. In other words, Calif rented another VM in the same region as our target.
Warm-up, rehearsal, and retries
To summarize the chain so far, we will warm up the Base64 decoder to C2 tier. We can rehearse the exploit run to calibrate timing and responses against the target before causing corruption to occur. Once the required information has been obtained, we can trigger the vulnerability. Incorrect placement could cause the JVM to crash, but in many instances, we can simply try again.
WARM
Run valid decodes until the JIT selects the affected fast path. No memory is corrupted.
REHEARSE
Optional safe round for calibration.
TRIGGER
Send the malformed tail. If the objects line up, the fixed spill enlarges the writer.
DISCLOSE
Continue the writer so chosen bytes enlarge the reader, then collect the response-derived heap bytes.
REPAIR
Use the disclosed originals to restore temporary headers and untouched neighbors when the chain has a repair path.
FINISH
Make the application-specific change and verify it through a live request.
Limitations
Request streaming. Some current grooming profiles need partial request bodies to reach the application. A proxy that waits for the complete body removes their release timing. nginx buffers request bodies by default.
Request buffering is not a general mitigation. Elasticsearch runs from one complete request, so this setting does not stop that chain. After a configured WebSocket upgrade, later frames pass through a tunnel and request-body buffering no longer applies to them.
Application profiles. There is no universal payload. Each chain needs a decoder route, a live writer, a returned reader or another feedback path, a heap layout, and an application action. Every target needs a bespoke profile. The exploit is not a universal point-and-shoot.
PoC
Here is a self-contained proof of concept. It warms the Base64 decoder until the vulnerable intrinsic is used, then decodes the malformed 64 × "A" + "=" input into a 4096-byte array. On an affected JDK it prints REPRODUCED: the decoder rejects the input, but 62 bytes (offsets 50 to 111) past the decoded output have already been overwritten. Every write lands inside a real array, so the trigger is safe to run.
import java.nio.charset.StandardCharsets;
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.Base64;
/** Bounded standalone trigger: every write stays inside a real 4096-byte array. */
public final class Base64Trigger {
private static volatile int sink;
public static void main(String[] args) {
Base64.Decoder decoder = Base64.getDecoder();
byte[] valid = "A".repeat(64).getBytes(StandardCharsets.US_ASCII);
byte[] malformed = ("A".repeat(64) + "=").getBytes(StandardCharsets.US_ASCII);
byte[] target = new byte[4096];
for (int i = 0; i < 200_000; i++) {
sink += decoder.decode(valid, target);
}
Arrays.fill(target, (byte) 0x5a);
boolean rejected = false;
try {
decoder.decode(malformed, target);
} catch (IllegalArgumentException expected) {
rejected = true;
}
int changed = 0;
int first = -1;
int last = -1;
for (int i = 50; i < target.length; i++) {
if (target[i] != (byte) 0x5a) {
changed++;
if (first < 0) first = i;
last = i;
}
}
System.out.println("runtime=" + System.getProperty("java.runtime.version"));
System.out.println("vm=" + System.getProperty("java.vm.name"));
System.out.printf("rejected=%s changed=%d first=%d last=%d%n",
rejected, changed, first, last);
if (!rejected || changed != 62 || first != 50 || last != 111) {
System.err.println("NOT REPRODUCED");
System.exit(1);
}
System.out.println("REPRODUCED");
}
}
We are holding back the application-specific exploits for now. This write-up covers the vulnerability and the general exploitation technique. The weaponized chains for individual products stay private until everyone has had a chance to upgrade. We will publish them later.
Affected products
Java4Shell affects OpenJDK from version 17.0.6 or newer.
Because they are built from OpenJDK, the following Java distributions are also vulnerable:
Amazon Corretto
Eclipse Temurin
Microsoft Build of OpenJDK
Azul Zulu
BellSoft Liberica
SAP SapMachine
Red Hat OpenJDK
Oracle OpenJDK
Oracle Java SE
Oracle GraalVM for JDK
According to Oracle, the following Oracle Java SE versions are vulnerable: 8u511b33-perf, 17.0.21.0.3, 21.0.13.0.3, 25.0.5.0.3, 27.0.1.0.3. We thank Oracle for providing this information ahead of publication time.
Because the vulnerable code is hardware-specific, Java4Shell also requires an x86-64 processor with AVX-512 VBMI support. ARM64 CPUs are not affected.
These mainstream CPU families meet the hardware requirements:
Intel Xeon: Ice Lake, Sapphire Rapids, Emerald Rapids, Granite Rapids
AMD EPYC and Ryzen: Zen 4 or newer
On Linux, you can check whether your CPU is vulnerable with this command:

The lists are non-exhaustive. We also demonstrated RCE in sample applications built on widely used frameworks, including Apache Struts and Spring Boot. Ordinary features such as authentication and file uploads can expose the vulnerable Base64 decoder, extending the potential impact far beyond the products we tested. samczsun also shared a client-side exploit with us. A Java program that opens a TLS connection to a malicious server can be compromised, even if it exposes no service of its own.
Here are a demo video of the exploits running:
Wait, Java has a memory-corruption vulnerability, in Base64?!
Yes. First time?
Memory corruption is the most common class of vulnerability on the Internet. But one of Java's biggest selling points is memory safety. Java manages memory on developers' behalf and checks memory accesses, protecting applications from the memory-corruption bugs common in other software.
But like PHP and many other technologies, much of Java itself is written in low-level languages like C, C++, and, in this case, assembly, where memory corruption is still a part of life.
To speed up Base64 decoding, OpenJDK uses specialized AVX-512 VBMI instructions on supported x86-64 processors. The bug sits in the hand-rolled assembly code that handles the final few characters. For the 65-character input above, OpenJDK allocates a 50-byte result array. After decoding the first 64 characters, the routine mishandles the stray = and calculates a negative output length. That invalid length becomes a full 64-byte write, sending 62 bytes past the end of the array. Java eventually rejects the input, but by then neighboring objects may already be corrupted.
Era of the centaur hacker
As with PHP2Shell, Calif used AI to find and weaponize Java4Shell. AI brings the horsepower. Our researchers bring the context. The best hacker today is neither AI nor human. It is the centaur hacker, part human, part AI.
It took our own Quang Luong an hour to discover the vulnerability, and two more hours to develop the first working exploit. The total token cost was less than $200.
One thing to note about this vulnerability is that there is no universal exploit. Depending on how Base64 is used and how the application works, different steps may be required for exploitation. Calif was able to accelerate the exploit development by spending initial effort building pipelines and strategies, then directing AI agents to use those for initial exploitation. Afterwards, most of the later chains are built almost autonomously with AI.
The tragedy of the commons
Java is one of many shared software stacks we all rely on. Over the years, it has grown to serve many different needs. Each feature request may make sense on its own, but everyone inherits the added complexity. Those who need a feature get the benefit. Everyone else carries the code and its risks, even if they never use it.
Java4Shell is a concrete example. The Base64 optimization helps applications that decode enormous amounts of data. But the code ships to everyone else too, including applications that gain little from it. On affected systems, an optimization few applications needed became a serious security risk.
Features can be useful, and most bugs can be fixed. But why do we have to worry about code we never needed in the first place?
A call for vibe-uncoding
If you don't need something, there is little reason to keep it running in your systems. Do you need that hand-rolled assembly Base64 decoder to process a few hundred bytes? Probably not.
Earlier this year we found Squidbleed, an older cousin of Heartbleed, hiding in the Squid proxy since 1997. The bug was in Squid's FTP module. But who uses FTP anymore? It's a dead protocol and should be removed.
Removing code is harder than it sounds. An apparently unused feature may have hidden dependencies. Someone has to understand what can be removed, make the change, and test what remains.
This is where AI can help to make that work practical at a scale we could not previously afford.
The same centaur hacker that discovered PHP2Shell and Java4Shell can comb a large codebase and flag dead code, bloated features, and needless optimizations. We call this vibe-uncoding: identify the parts a system actually needs, then remove everything else to reduce its attack surface.
With AI, everyone is asking, “What can we build?” But maybe the right question for security is, “What can we delete?”
The Tail of Base64
By Quang Luong
On affected OpenJDK builds, malformed Base64 data can cause a constant-value overwrite of allocated heap memory. Through remote Heap Feng Shui, we turn that overwrite into a heap leak and remote code execution.
Discovery
A few months back, I was developing a 0-click exploit on a certain phone vendor. The bug was constrained: it is a linear out-of-bounds write and we have no way to bypass ASLR. I almost considered it unexploitable until I audited the Android Runtime. Turns out, it has some predictable memory allocation pattern and if you align the registers from the starting memory corruption just right you can make the runtime interpret attacker's bytecode.
After completing that exploit chain, inspired (and bored), I decided to look into some thing completely different. So I pulled the official JVM implementation (OpenJDK HotSpot) and started auditing. Java is a mature platform, so I was aware that having a "good" bug here would be hard, but I wanted to give it my best attempt anyway.
Recalling my earlier lesson that complex systems and code crossing multiple boundaries are difficult to get right, I focused the audit on the JIT compiler, intrinsics, widely accessible APIs, and cryptography. Well, more precisely, I told Codex to look at those areas like any serious security researcher in 2026. To be honest, I didn't expect to find anything trivially exploitable, perhaps only some logic flaws or side-channel issues.
Most early results Codex brought me were slops, either unreachable or the requirement of exploitation is not realistic at all. This looks like a gamble I am going to lose. But you know what, fortune favors the bold. So I just prompted harder...
Eventually, Codex brought me a finding for a memory corruption in the Base64 decoder itself. I came looking for copper and found gold, through sheer persistence. But wait, is it really gold, or just fool's gold?
The vulnerability
The issue lies in OpenJDK HotSpot's x86-64 Base64 intrinsic. C2 is the most aggressive JIT compiler in the Java Virtual Machine. Specifically, if you trigger a function repeatedly, then it will compile that function into native code. Even then, ordinary native code itself is not fast enough. Modern CPUs have specialized instructions that allow processing data even faster. HotSpot uses them in optimized routines called intrinsics. The x86-64 Base64 intrinsic miscalculated the padded tail's store mask, causing an out-of-bounds write.
The x86-64 Base64 intrinsic iterates through the input in 256-byte blocks; if it is less than 256, then it will do so in 64-byte blocks. When the input is less than 64 bytes, it uses evmovdqub, the intrinsic for the VMOVDQU8 instruction. This instruction takes a 64-bit mask that decides which bytes are read from or written to memory. The mask is first calculated from the input and output length, then updated to reflect the number of padding bytes ('=') in the Base64-encoded string. This is shown in the following (pseudocode):
// Zero High Bits Starting with Specified Bit Position
input_mask := bzhiq(-1, input_length)
output_len := (input_length / 4) * 3
// handle padding
if input[input_length - 1] == '=':
output_len -= 1
input_mask >>= 1
if input[input_length - 2] == '=':
output_len -= 1
input_mask >>= 1
output_mask := bzhiq(-1, output_len)
In the above code, if the remaining input is of the form =, ==, X=, X==, XY=, or ===, then output_len will be computed as zero. However, since there is padding, output_len is decremented, resulting in an underflowed value of -1 or -2. Since bzhiq(-1, output_len) doesn’t change any bits if output_len >= 64, this results in output_mask == -1u64.
Later the code uses evmovdqub to read and write multiple bytes at a time. For a write, if the mask bit is 1, the byte at that index in the vector register is written to memory. So when output_mask == -1u64, all 64 bytes are written during the following operation:
The generated stub returns the number of bytes it successfully decoded, and returns early when it detects invalid input. The remaining bytes are handled by the Java code, which throws IllegalArgumentException if the input is invalid.
At runtime, the decoded buffer length is calculated as (ceil(inp_len / 4) * 3) - padding. When the bug is triggered, with 65 bytes ([64 bytes] + '=') as input, that calculation allocates a 50-byte buffer. During decoding the bug makes output_mask == -1 for evmovdqub, so 64 bytes are written starting at offset 48, an out-of-bounds write past the end of the buffer.
On OpenJDK 17, the trigger input is 'A' * 128 + '=' instead of 'A' * 64 + '=', because the length threshold to reach the code path is higher.
Exploitation strategy
The Base64 bug was gold, but not the type that sits on the riverbed waiting to be panned out. It was the type that was deep, dark, and hard to access. While we had memory corruption, exploiting it looked difficult, perhaps even borderline unexploitable, since it only wrote fixed bytes to the heap. Only the landing position of the out-of-bounds write is controllable.
We get the following 64 bytes write starting from the position in buffer where the remaining sets of Base64 decoded bytes would have been written to:
Full controlled write to byte 0 (in bound)
4-bit controlled write to byte 1 (in bound)
Uncontrolled write to bytes [2, 63]. For bytes 48..63, the value will always be 0x9a.
The uncontrolled out-of-bounds write turned out not to be a big problem. We reused a common JavaScript engine exploitation primitive, the classic technique of overwriting a buffer length.
We call the buffer whose length we overwrite the writer. Once its length is corrupted, it keeps accepting our chosen bytes, so if we place another array or object after it, we can corrupt that neighbor with all of our chosen values. That upgrades the fixed write into a bounded, chosen-value linear write.
If we then corrupt another array's length and its data is copied back into the response, we have built a heap disclosure primitive, the reader, from the out-of-bounds read. At this point, before any code execution, we already had JavaBleed, the ability to leak contents from the Java heap, which may hold PII, private keys, other secret keys, and tokens.
From the writer and reader, the rest of this write-up builds the full attack. We repair the objects the linear write damaged to keep the JVM running, mount data-only attacks on application logic, and escalate the bounded write into arbitrary writes that reach the Code Cache and JVM hooks for code execution.
But first things first, we need to align the heap to our advantage.
HotSpot normally gives each Java thread a thread-local allocation buffer (TLAB), a private slice of Eden. Objects created by different request threads advance different cursors, which gives us no useful order between a Base64 decoder in one TLAB and a writer in another.
The first grooming layer controls the allocation route. The critical array's size and the space left in the worker's TLAB decide whether HotSpot refills that TLAB or takes the attacker-preferred outside-TLAB allocation path.
The second layer controls the layout. However, we do not choose how G1 allocates memory, and we don't know the current heap state. To approach this limitation, Calif used the tried and tested method of Spray and Pray.
Object sizes and padding fix offsets inside region-sized spans. We spray several live writer and reader pairs to give the decoder output several possible neighbors. When its region lands immediately below a writer region, the widened reader produces a larger application response. We discard misses and retry until successful.
One early challenge during exploit development was that the AI would get stuck during heap grooming primitive development. In order to save tokens and time, Calif wrote a small heap tracer and visualizer to give the agent information about the heap state, combined with our expertise on specific cases.
Remote Heap Feng Shui
In order to perform remote grooming, each request data would be sent up until the critical heap allocation would occur. By ensuring the data that was previously sent had a TCP acknowledgement, the exploit would have control over the allocations up to date. Once we were confident our data so far is buffered on the target, we then sent the rest, completing the http request. This allows both heap allocation control and more precise schedule as delivering small network packets is more stable than big ones.
The exact allocation and free order schedule belongs to the targeted application profile, while we are able to control the adding, writer, reader, and decoder pieces.
The current attack is sensitive to network conditions, like RTT and jitter. Low, stable round-trip times are preferred for a more reliable attack. In our controlled environment, we performed this attack over the internet through public IP addresses instead of just assuming perfect laboratory conditions. For the current attack path to achieve optimal stability and reliability in exploitation, Calif turned to physics and utilized money to solve the problem, rather than experimenting with TCP/IP stack configuration. In other words, Calif rented another VM in the same region as our target.
Warm-up, rehearsal, and retries
To summarize the chain so far, we will warm up the Base64 decoder to C2 tier. We can rehearse the exploit run to calibrate timing and responses against the target before causing corruption to occur. Once the required information has been obtained, we can trigger the vulnerability. Incorrect placement could cause the JVM to crash, but in many instances, we can simply try again.
WARM
Run valid decodes until the JIT selects the affected fast path. No memory is corrupted.
REHEARSE
Optional safe round for calibration.
TRIGGER
Send the malformed tail. If the objects line up, the fixed spill enlarges the writer.
DISCLOSE
Continue the writer so chosen bytes enlarge the reader, then collect the response-derived heap bytes.
REPAIR
Use the disclosed originals to restore temporary headers and untouched neighbors when the chain has a repair path.
FINISH
Make the application-specific change and verify it through a live request.
Limitations
Request streaming. Some current grooming profiles need partial request bodies to reach the application. A proxy that waits for the complete body removes their release timing. nginx buffers request bodies by default.
Request buffering is not a general mitigation. Elasticsearch runs from one complete request, so this setting does not stop that chain. After a configured WebSocket upgrade, later frames pass through a tunnel and request-body buffering no longer applies to them.
Application profiles. There is no universal payload. Each chain needs a decoder route, a live writer, a returned reader or another feedback path, a heap layout, and an application action. Every target needs a bespoke profile. The exploit is not a universal point-and-shoot.
PoC
Here is a self-contained proof of concept. It warms the Base64 decoder until the vulnerable intrinsic is used, then decodes the malformed 64 × "A" + "=" input into a 4096-byte array. On an affected JDK it prints REPRODUCED: the decoder rejects the input, but 62 bytes (offsets 50 to 111) past the decoded output have already been overwritten. Every write lands inside a real array, so the trigger is safe to run.
import java.nio.charset.StandardCharsets;
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.Base64;
/** Bounded standalone trigger: every write stays inside a real 4096-byte array. */
public final class Base64Trigger {
private static volatile int sink;
public static void main(String[] args) {
Base64.Decoder decoder = Base64.getDecoder();
byte[] valid = "A".repeat(64).getBytes(StandardCharsets.US_ASCII);
byte[] malformed = ("A".repeat(64) + "=").getBytes(StandardCharsets.US_ASCII);
byte[] target = new byte[4096];
for (int i = 0; i < 200_000; i++) {
sink += decoder.decode(valid, target);
}
Arrays.fill(target, (byte) 0x5a);
boolean rejected = false;
try {
decoder.decode(malformed, target);
} catch (IllegalArgumentException expected) {
rejected = true;
}
int changed = 0;
int first = -1;
int last = -1;
for (int i = 50; i < target.length; i++) {
if (target[i] != (byte) 0x5a) {
changed++;
if (first < 0) first = i;
last = i;
}
}
System.out.println("runtime=" + System.getProperty("java.runtime.version"));
System.out.println("vm=" + System.getProperty("java.vm.name"));
System.out.printf("rejected=%s changed=%d first=%d last=%d%n",
rejected, changed, first, last);
if (!rejected || changed != 62 || first != 50 || last != 111) {
System.err.println("NOT REPRODUCED");
System.exit(1);
}
System.out.println("REPRODUCED");
}
}
We are holding back the application-specific exploits for now. This write-up covers the vulnerability and the general exploitation technique. The weaponized chains for individual products stay private until everyone has had a chance to upgrade. We will publish them later.
Affected products
Java4Shell affects OpenJDK from version 17.0.6 or newer.
Because they are built from OpenJDK, the following Java distributions are also vulnerable:
Amazon Corretto
Eclipse Temurin
Microsoft Build of OpenJDK
Azul Zulu
BellSoft Liberica
SAP SapMachine
Red Hat OpenJDK
Oracle OpenJDK
Oracle Java SE
Oracle GraalVM for JDK
According to Oracle, the following Oracle Java SE versions are vulnerable: 8u511b33-perf, 17.0.21.0.3, 21.0.13.0.3, 25.0.5.0.3, 27.0.1.0.3. We thank Oracle for providing this information ahead of publication time.
Because the vulnerable code is hardware-specific, Java4Shell also requires an x86-64 processor with AVX-512 VBMI support. ARM64 CPUs are not affected.
These mainstream CPU families meet the hardware requirements:
Intel Xeon: Ice Lake, Sapphire Rapids, Emerald Rapids, Granite Rapids
AMD EPYC and Ryzen: Zen 4 or newer

Not even Sam Altman is safe from AI deepfakes on Instagram
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
21:00:55 UTC
06:00:55 KST

Instagram is filled with people posting deepfaked content — including, as of late, from one of Meta’s most prominent executives.
Not even Sam Altman is safe from AI deepfakes on Instagram
Meta’s chief AI officer posted an AI video of a top competitor. It’s allowed under Meta’s AI policy.
Earlier in November, Alexandr Wang, Meta’s chief AI officer, shared a short clip to promote Muse, the company’s consumer AI agent. In the AI-generated clip, Wang, Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, and Jolly, the anthropomorphic mascot of Muse, are on a four-way video call. When Altman joins the call he’s dressed like Jolly, wearing a furry hooded suit and face paint to look like the cutesy character. The clip is captioned “When the homies meet about openai product strategy,” a reference to the AI agent race and the recent popularity of Muse.
Just a few years ago, AI content portraying real people led to strong public statements rebuking the use of the technology to imitate humans. In some cases legal action was threatened. Now, AI content has become part of the texture of a new reality, especially on social media. Leading up to the midterm elections, for example, political groups ran millions of dollars’ worth of AI-generated ads attacking opponents.
Wang has been doing a lot of what some have described as “anti-PR” to promote Muse, tweeting hundreds of times and referring to more traditional marketers as “corposlops.” Still, a major tech executive posting an AI video of a competitor is notable, especially on a platform that’s been criticized recently over AI-generated content. After an AI-generated image of actress Mia Goth gained traction in October, the Pearl star’s representative told The Verge it was “incomprehensible” that Instagram allowed the image to spread instead of focusing on the “protection and integrity of a public figure’s likeness.” Other celebrities, including Scarlett Johansson, are regularly the subject of sexualized AI imagery; Meta has removed some accounts in the past that it found to be posing as someone else and using their likeness.
Meta requires AI-generated videos depicting people to be labeled as AI, like Wang’s post is. Static images, however, have no such requirement, meaning the AI image of Goth had no disclosure. To make matters worse, the image was shared by a fan account with a blue verification badge given by Instagram.
Reached for comment, Meta spokesperson Tracy Clayton noted that Wang’s post is labeled as AI content. Clayton also pointed out that Altman has made his likeness available for AI content generation, as when OpenAI launched its Sora app (and it was quickly flooded with fake Altman videos). OpenAI didn’t respond to requests for comment.

Nvidia Stopped Disclosing Singapore Sales As Revenues Hit $104 Billion

Nvidia Stopped Disclosing Singapore Sales As Revenues Hit $104 Billion (ft.com) 5
			
		 	
				Nvidia stopped separately disclosing its Singapore sales (source paywalled; alternative source) after changing its geographic revenue reporting methodology from customer billing addresses to headquarters locations, obscuring a business that generated $104 billion in external revenue in 2025, up 72% from the previous year. The Financial Times reports that the change has drawn scrutiny amid U.S. investigations into whether Singapore-based Megaspeed helped smuggle Nvidia chips into China. From the report:  "There is now a lot more focus on Nvidia's Singapore business and questions about who its end customers are," said an investor who is investigating Nvidia's dealings in south-east Asia. The Singapore accounts show that the subsidiary booked $117.8 billion of revenue in the 12 months to January 2026, including $13.6 billion of sales to another Nvidia company. Given the nature of the Singapore operation as a global hub, customers it serves are from all over the world. Only a small quantity of the chips Nvidia sells ends up in or goes through Singapore. The Singapore subsidiary generated just $53 million of profit in the year to January 2026, suggesting limited scope for Nvidia to benefit from Singapore's low corporate tax rates.
[...] Singapore's outsized role as a revenue generator for Nvidia used to show up in the company's overall results. In January 2025, the US company reported that Singapore was its second-biggest geographic area by revenue, based on customer billing location. The Singapore office generated $23.7 billion of revenue, behind only the US and representing 18 per cent of global income, based on customers' billing location. The annual accounts said that most of the business was generated by customers using the Singapore office to centralize invoicing and that most of the products were shipped elsewhere, with less than 2 per cent of revenue tied to chips that landed in the city-state.
As recently as July last year, Nvidia reported that the Singapore office accounted for 22 per cent of total revenue. This was around the time Nvidia said it began cutting ties with Megaspeed, following US Department of Commerce investigations into the business. Nvidia then changed its financial reporting methodology and stopped splitting out revenue that went to the Singapore office. Before the 2025 reporting change, Nvidia said in its financial filing that it booked sales based on the billing locations of its customers, but that the end customer's location "may be different than our customer's billing location." It added that "most shipments associated with Singapore revenue were to locations other than Singapore and shipments to Singapore were insignificant," according to the filings.

Human.md
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
20:47:36 UTC
05:47:36 KST

BRIANA (MAZZIO) MAY · APR 28, 2026
HUMAN.md
          > a working manifesto. notes-to-self, mostly.
> not a thinkpiece. not a thread. not a deck.
> last edited at the kitchen table, coffee #2.
## what this file is
A reminder, in plain text, of what I think humans actually bring to the table now that the machines are very good and getting better every day.
I keep coming back to the word "taste" and I keep finding it too small. Taste is the surface. There is a whole engine underneath it, and that engine is the part the model can't have. Yet. Maybe ever. I don't know. I'm not in that argument. I'm in this one:
## the actual job
### 1. break the rule on purpose
Models are trained on the average. The average is the floor, not the ceiling. Our job is not to conform a little better than the last person who conformed. Our job is to know the rule cold, and then break it on purpose, in public, with a reason.
Symmetry is a rule. Break it.
Three-act structure is a rule. Break it.
"Don't shoot into the sun" is a rule. Break it.
If you can't say why you broke it, you didn't break it, you just missed.
### 2. find the thing in the weird place
Inspiration does not live in the first ten image-search results. It lives in:
- a misprinted parking ticket
- the way your grandfather folded a napkin
- an obscure Victorian zoetrope of a cat
- the back of a menu in a town you can't pronounce
- the tension in Rene Magritte's "The Son of Man", 1946
A model averages the internet. A human walks somewhere strange and brings back something unexpected.
### 3. advocate for the human and humanity
Every brief now has an invisible third party at the table. Someone has to keep saying "and what does this feel like for the person on the other end of it." Someone has to keep saying "how is humanity better through the birthing of this idea?" That's the job. Not "is it on-brand." Not "does it convert." What does it feel like? what good does it do?
If you aren't there saying it, no one will. The model certainly won't.
### 4. design for emotional resonance, not correctness
Tension. Surprise. Restraint. The pause before the punchline. The photograph that's almost in focus. The sentence that ends one beat earlier than you expected.
These are not bugs. These are the entire reason anyone remembers anything. "Correct" is the lowest bar in the building. Step over it and keep walking.
### 5. seek delight, not delivery
I would rather make one thing that makes someone laugh out loud on a train than ten things that "meet the brief." At the very least, it needs to make ME laugh. This is a personal bias.
If it's not at least a little bit fun, why are we here?
### 6. experiment ruthlessly, ship more than you opine
A working sketch beats a confident take. Every time.
A bad first draft beats a brilliant unwritten one. Every time.
A weird little prototype beats a slide deck about prototyping.
Every. Time.
Make 50 things. Throw away 47. Talk about the 3.
Do not, under any circumstances, become a person who mostly posts opinions about other people's work.
### 7. own the output. fully.
If the AI output is bad, the AI did not fail you. You failed it. You picked the tool. You wrote the prompt. You accepted the result. You shipped it.
The fix is not a better model, or a return to tools of the past. The fix is a sharper you. Sharpen yourself. Sharpen your ideas. Sharpen the way you say what you mean, because the gap between what you wanted and what came back is almost always a gap in you, not in the tool.
Powerful tools reward the people who showed up sharp, iterate, spend extra time on the vision. They expose the people who didn't. AI is not the great equalizer, it is the great amplifier.
### 8. design with it, not next to it
Watching the generation happen is not the work.
Prompting once and accepting the result is not the work.
The work is the back-and-forth. The push. The loop. The "again, but tweak is this way..." The hundredth iteration where the thing finally clicks.
If you're a spectator to your own output, you are not the author of your own output. Get your hands in.
### 9. play with purpose. and play for play.
Both. At the same time.
Some days the play is in service of a thing.
Some days the play is the thing.
A practice that only ever has goals is a job. A practice that never has goals is a hobby. The good work lives in between. Play doesn't result in burnout. It results in excitement, happiness, experiment, the unexpected, and an urge to play more.
### 10. be a builder and an ideas person
The split is fake. It was always fake. It's especially fake now. The people who can think of the thing and make the thing are going to eat. The people who can only do one half are going to be very busy explaining why the other half is hard, or blame the processes or tools.
Don't be that person. Learn the other half. It's never been cheaper to learn it.
## things I am trying to stop doing
- waiting until it's "ready"
- editing in my head instead of on the page
- making but not sharing publicly
- taking briefs that secretly bore me
- pretending taste is an innate quality
- treating "AI" like a noun instead of a verb
## things I am trying to do more
- finish the bad version first
- show people the in-between, mess, experiments
- write the thing I'd want to read
- dig deeper, get help
- stop pretending I know stuff, ask dumber questions
- say the more specific thing
This file will be wrong in six months. That is fine.
I'd rather be specifically wrong than vaguely correct.
— b.
last touched, kitchen table, late.

Instagram is helping creators crack down on copycat videos
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
20:46:31 UTC
05:46:31 KST

Instagram is beginning to roll out a “content protection” feature that lets creators see and review copies of their videos posted to the platform.
Instagram is helping creators crack down on copycat videos
Meta will let creators decide to hide copies of their videos flagged by a new ‘content protection’ feature.
“For eligible creators who enroll, content protection is a tool that automatically looks out for visual matches of your original videos, alerts you when we find a match across Instagram and Facebook, and lets you decide what happens next,” Meta says in a blog post. Meta launched a similar tool for Facebook last year to help users “safeguard” their Reels and “maintain control” over their creative work.
When an Instagram creator is notified of a matched video, they can see how many views the match has and decide if they want to track the video, hide it from everyone on Instagram and Facebook, add a credit link, or release their claim on the match.
After a creator enrolls, every “original video” they post on Instagram is protected, and creators can retroactively protect older videos manually. Creators can also put accounts on an allow list that lets them use their videos.
In addition, Instagram will start flagging AI-generated videos that “match the spoken words” in a creator’s original video but have visuals replaced with AI. Initially, Instagram will test this with a “small group of creators” in “the coming weeks.” The platform says it’s exploring ways to limit the reach of content and profiles that “use AI tools to post this type of content repeatedly.”

====================================================================================================

Democrats try to figure out how not to get swept away by the blue wave
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
20:30:05 UTC
05:30:05 KST

On a harried Saturday afternoon at Denver International Airport, I had the good fortune of sitting just a few feet from Minneapolis Mayor Jacob Frey. It shouldn’t have come as a surprise. He, I, and 3,500 or so others had just left “What’s Next,” a conference organized by Majority Democrats, a group dedicated to charting a path for the suddenly — and shockingly? — victorious party.
Democrats try to figure out how not to get swept away by the blue wave
At a conference funded by a billionaire AI investor, a group of centrist Democrats aims to harness the populist moment.
Democrats try to figure out how not to get swept away by the blue wave
At a conference funded by a billionaire AI investor, a group of centrist Democrats aims to harness the populist moment.
Frey, who rose to national prominence amid ICE’s brutal Operation Metro Surge, had come to the conference in Denver to listen, not to speak. “I’ve never been to one of these things before,” he said. He had flown in for a day and, like everyone else in attendance, is curious to see where the party goes from here. He said he hopes Democrats do two things with their newfound power. “One, act with urgency. And second, don’t fuck it up.”
Another conference-goer sitting nearby chimed in: “Abso-fucking-lutely.”
Democrats, unaccustomed to winning, have seemingly learned from the folly of Republicans’ 2024 sweep. Where Donald Trump’s return to power was falsely attributed to a vibe shift of seismic proportions, born of voters’ preoccupation over DEI and desire to say slurs, Majority Democrats has a simpler explanation for the blue tsunami: It’s the economy, stupid. Everything is falling apart, and even just getting by feels more expensive than ever. The midterms were a rejection, not an endorsement: Voters rejected data centers, they rejected war, and they cast their lot with the side that promised to give them what they want.
The Democrats understand this is not a cultural victory. Even the hollow center of the Democratic Party has, at long last, realized voters don’t necessarily want what they’re selling — they just want to afford to live. It’s gas and groceries all the way down. This analysis would almost verge on Marxist if it wasn’t put forth by an organization comprising the party’s new, young moderates. Majority Democrats describes itself as an alternative to three unpopular, ineffective factions: the “gerontocratic establishment,” the “activist left,” and the “MAGA right.”
The Democrats want you to know they’ve changed. This is no longer the party of “Kamala is brat” or “Pokémon Go to the polls.” They are, in fact, “A Different Kind of Democrat.” They’ve abandoned hollow identity politics and sworn off toxic factional infighting. They really, really care about affordability — for real. They even have the merch to prove it.
Attendees at What’s Next wore their convictions on their sleeves, and also their chests, and also their heads. There were hats that read “pay your taxes” and hats identifying the wearer as a “new deal democrat,” both in all lowercase because the Democrats would also like to remind you that they are once again the youth party. At a separate merch stand, there was a T-shirt declaring “America!” and another T-shirt posing an existential question, also in all lowercase: “how did fucking computers become the thing and machines became the thing that does art? and humans are like the ones in amazon warehouses like shouldn’t robots be working so we can paint?” This second shirt, a friend pointed out, is an uncredited reference to a joke alt-comedian Stavros Halkias made on Theo Von’s podcast. And there was the Our Get Along Shirt, a sartorial exercise in magical thinking that summed up the ethos of the What’s Next conference. The Democrats were in control. Having realized the folly of fighting each other, they would instead channel their energy into fighting for the American people.
The illusion of harmony was shattered early into the programming when a small group of dissenters heckled Rep. Hakeem Jeffries of New York, the soon-to-be speaker of the House. “We’re asking you to listen to us now,” one of the protesters shouted after the moderator, Fox News’s resident Democratic host Jessica Tarlov, attempted to placate them by saying she’d be asking Jeffries about the youth vote. “When is the time for a Green New Deal? When is the time for an arms embargo?” The protesters’ cries were drowned out by the crowd’s chants of “HA-KEEM, HA-KEEM, HA-KEEM.”
Jeffries, ever the politician, was unfazed by the altercation. “I’m from Brooklyn,” he told his visibly uncomfortable interlocutor. “This is light work.” The protesters were whisked away, and the interview continued as if the interruption had never happened. Democrats would be gracious in victory.
This new way of doing things was best summed up by Michigan Sen. Elissa Slotkin, who told reporters that she and her new colleague, Senator-elect Abdul El-Sayed, had plenty of debates in the car en route to campaign events — though she declined to say what the debates were about. (Slotkin has identified as a Zionist but has also voted against providing certain military aid to Israel. El-Sayed has called Israel’s war on Gaza a genocide.) “Abdul and I do not agree on everything, but we agree on the really big, important thing, and that is making sure we have a middle class in this country,” she said. “When I see elected officials spending their time fighting the progressive-versus-moderate battle, I know that person didn’t actually learn a damn thing from the election.”
Throughout the weekend, speaker after speaker urged Democrats not to squander their newfound control of Congress by bickering among themselves. At the airport, notorious Democratic operative Lis Smith, an adviser to Majority Democrats, told me the midterms sweep was a rejection of Trumpism, not a sign that voters prefer one style of Democratic politics over the other. “A big takeaway of the election was that there’s no one way to be a Democrat, and that’s in part how we were able to have such a big victory,” she said. The calls for unity were reminiscent of the slogan of one doomed 2016 presidential campaign: Stronger Together. This time, however, they may be onto something — at least for now.
“It’s easy to have Pete Buttigieg and Ro Khanna onstage when we’re all riding the same high,” one staffer told me on the final night of the conference. But what happens when it’s actually time to govern?
For all the talk of unity, there was plenty of tension at What’s Next — sometimes overt, more often simmering beneath the surface of what organizers described as a congenial event.
There was tension toward the establishment, as characterized by Maryland Sen. Chris Van Hollen’s announcement that he was challenging Sen. Chuck Schumer (D-NY) for Senate majority leader. There was tension between speakers’ repeated exhortations to get “big money” out of politics and the fact that Majority Democrats is largely bankrolled by hedge fund billionaire Stephen Mandel, a major investor in companies powering the AI boom. (Several attendees I spoke to described What’s Next as hands down the most luxe Democratic event they’ve been to, on par with the party’s national conventions. The snack station was stocked with full-size candy bars.) There was tension between all the populist posturing and the nightly afterparties at the Ritz, where 2028 contenders, staffers, and hangers-on of all types drank well after last call. And notwithstanding the recent blue tsunami, there was tension between Democrats’ persistent unlikability and the conference’s poptimism-tinged branding.
Everything was a little too cute. Majority Democrats staffers wore custom Dickies-style jackets with their names embroidered on the front. Highlight reels played between each main stage speech. The opening-night happy hour, sponsored by the Searchlight Institute — a think tank founded in 2025 with the stated goal of weakening the influence of liberal groups like the ACLU and the Center for American Progress — was Western-themed, though hardly any attendees strayed from the blazer-and-lanyard dress code that is de rigueur in liberal policy circles. The conference lineup was announced via a music festival-style flyer. The halls were decorated with stylized posters of the politicians in attendance, many of whom seemed to be soft-launching their 2028 presidential campaigns.
“The organizers here want a big tent — one big enough even to invite anti-establishment voices like me,” Khanna, one apparent presidential aspirant, said from the stage. “I’m all for a big tent. I’m not for a shallow one. A big tent cannot mean that we water down principle for political expediency.” He called on the new Democratic-controlled Congress to pass Medicare for All, codify a path to citizenship for undocumented immigrants, abolish ICE, and cut off funding to Israel, which he said was committing an ongoing genocide against Palestinians. All of this got huge cheers from the audience — some of whom, one presumes, also applauded Jeffries just one day earlier.
Depending on whom you ask, What’s Next was either a concerted effort to rally Democrats around an affordability agenda or moderates’ cynical attempt at co-opting voters’ populist rage. Across the street from the Mission Ballroom, a small group of protesters with Indivisible unfurled a giant banner that read “NO SCHUMER CORONATION.” Members of another group, posted up by one of the side stages hosting the “Do Issues Matter?” panel, told me they hoped the Democrats didn’t abandon their commitment to immigration reform and abortion rights. At the “AI safety breakfast,” one advocate of tech regulation told me there was little daylight between the organizations putting on the conference and its affiliated events — Majority Democrats, the Searchlight Institute, and the pro-AI regulation PAC Tomorrow Together — and the flailing Abundance movement. “They’re rebranding because no one likes abundance,” she said. “This is counter-programming to the populist left. They took a bunch of AI safety money to put on things like this.”
There were notable absences. Vermont senator and erstwhile presidential candidate Bernie Sanders was not in attendance, and neither was New York Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, whom the New York City chapter of the Democratic Socialists of America recently urged to run for president. Slotkin may have name-checked El-Sayed, but he wasn’t there, either. Not even Colorado Rep.-elect Melat Kiros – a member of the Democratic Socialists of America who won her race in a landslide and now represents the very district where the conference was held – was present. Rep. Jake Auchincloss, a member of Majority Democrats who helped organize the event, told The Washington Post that he had personally invited Ocasio-Cortez.
Still, there were other representatives of the party’s left wing milling about, including Khanna, Van Hollen, and Angie Nixon, a DSA-backed Democrat who came within 3 percentage points of winning a Florida Senate seat. And regardless of their political leanings — and counter to the Democratic Party’s 2024 message that the economy was just fine, actually — nearly every single speaker decried the steadily increasing economic divide separating America’s billionaires from the shrinking middle class and the growing ranks of the working poor.
“We just won in a historic fashion, and I think everyone feels good about that,” said Morris Katz, a 26-year-old digital strategist whose roster of previous clients includes New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani and Texas Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa. (Katz was also the driving force behind Senate contender Graham Platner, who dropped out of the race after being accused of sexual assault.) “I think everyone is united under an anti-billionaire focus on affordability and corruption, and there’s a lot to feel good about.”
And what about the billionaire funding this very conference?
“If there are people who want to support where we’re headed, and the things we’re doing, great, everyone’s welcome,” Katz told me. The difference, he said, is whether Democrats let themselves be co-opted or corrupted by their donors. He pointed to outgoing Texas Republican Gov. Greg Abbott — who supercharged tech development in the state and whose campaign was largely funded by Elon Musk and data center developers — as an example of what could go wrong. “There’s a quid pro quo there that needs to be avoided at all costs. We need to be the anti-Big Tech party.”
Democrats are already heading in that direction. Khanna pointed out that one-third of the nation’s wealth is concentrated in the Silicon Valley district he represents. Jerusalem Demsas, founder of the liberal publication The Argument, urged legislators to rein in AI development. (“I know everyone here is in a good mood, and it’s about to not be a good mood,” she said before reminding the assembled legislators and political operatives that “what comes after winning is governing.”) At a press conference after her speech, Slotkin called for the establishment of a bipartisan, bicameral committee on AI akin to midcentury bodies created to regulate nuclear power. “While we’re bickering with each other — Democrats and Republicans — there’s an opportunity cost, and we have not done what we need to do on AI,” she said. “If we were healthy politically, we would have been working on this for five years straight.”
There was an obvious undercurrent of technopessimism throughout the weekend. News of the nationwide FAA ground stop broke minutes before a panel titled “How Weird Will AI Make 2028?” Though a faulty router was ultimately identified as the culprit, attendees — myself included — couldn’t help but speculate whether an AI cyberattack, or even just shoddy implementation of the FAA’s new AI tool, was actually to blame.
“It was clear that there is just a huge interest in AI here, and grappling with it, that sits across the party,” Rob Flaherty, the cofounder of the AI safety PAC Tomorrow Together told me. Flaherty’s organization hosted an off-the-record breakfast on AI safety. “We built it for 100 people, and 600 people showed up,” he said. The coffee ran out almost immediately, though there were somehow enough burritos to go around. Tomorrow Together’s polling, which was shared at the breakfast, indicates that voters don’t just hate data centers but have broadly soured on AI. They tie AI developments to broader economic concerns like rising utility bills and a worsening job market. Most importantly, voters don’t think the companies can be trusted to regulate themselves.
“Now it’s on Democrats to not just channel the anger and be the vessel for that anger but to actually enact the things that are going to alleviate the pain that people are feeling,” Flaherty told me.
But for Flaherty, who previously worked for Biden’s and Harris’ presidential campaigns, the conference “was less about a policy agenda and more about a theory of power” — one articulated by Bernie Sanders a decade ago. “If you look at the way people — James Talarico on down, Hakeem Jeffries on down — were speaking, it doesn’t sound dissimilar from the kinds of things Bernie was raising in 2016,” said Flaherty. “He rightly — and I was working on the other side — identified some of the economic discontent that a lot of people are feeling.”
Still, there’s a difference between Sanders’ vision of Medicare for All and, say, a public option — or what 2020 presidential hopeful Pete Buttigieg described as “Medicare for all who want it.” Both fall under the broad umbrella of universal healthcare, but the distinctions matter. Absent nitty-gritty policy discussions, however, it’s easy to get the sense that all voters — and all Democrats — want the same thing. Perhaps this is why, even among the professional Democrats in attendance, I met people who told me they wanted Democrats to prosecute ICE agents, enact a $25 an hour minimum wage, and pack the Supreme Court. At a certain point, I became desperate to find someone whose politics were a little more tepid.
My pleas were answered by Uriel Espinoza, a local Democrat whose friends introduced him as a centrist. Espinoza’s friends insisted that I join them for happy hour at a nearby brewery. (Espinoza said he rejects the centrist label, but broadly disagrees with the Democratic Socialists of America’s manifesto.) Their car wasn’t big enough to fit us all, but it didn’t matter. As four of us piled into the back seat of a Tesla whose driver — another member of the friend group — was blasting Drake, Espinoza told me he had once identified as a conservative. At the bar, he and another friend said the conference had been fun, if a little “East Coast consultant-y.”
Here was the mythical moderate voter in all his glory. He was even wearing a cowboy hat. And what did he want? Immigration reform, universal healthcare, and an economy that isn’t stacked against working people. There was a time, Espinoza told me, when he felt betrayed. “On certain things — like Israel, Gaza, Palestine, immigration — we are looking to our elected officials at the highest level, and we feel that they aren’t really delivering, or differentiating himself from Republicans,” he said.
I told him he sounded almost like a leftist.
“I’m also surprised, myself, because I’ve never thought of myself — and I don’t think of myself — as a socialist,” he told me. “I’m an entrepreneur. I’m pro-business, I’m pro-economy, but I’m also, at this point, pro-results.”
Update, November 17th: This piece has been updated to include Uriel Espinoza’s description of his political leanings.
