The Big Idea: Emily Gray
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
20:20:35 UTC
05:20:35 KST

The Big Idea: Emily Gray
Posted on November 17, 2026 Posted by Athena Scalzi Leave a Comment
Romance, spaceships, and an argument for basic human decency and safety? That’s what today’s Big Idea brings us, as author Emily Gray recounts the turbulent early 2020’s as an inspiration for her newest novel, By the Light of the Stars. Read on to see how the safety and kindness people deserve on Earth should also be afforded to those who are in space.
EMILY GRAY:
Every book needs a hero—but not every hero needs to be brave.
I began writing By the Light of the Stars at a moment in my life when I wasn’t feeling particularly brave. In my mid-twenties, when I was both freshly postpartum and living through a global pandemic, every little task was laced with indecision. The most meaningful human interaction I had on a daily basis was with the employees at my local Dunkin Doughnuts drive-thru. (Which is also the reason one of my son’s first words was “hashbrowns.”)
I was lonely. I was scared. Every single choice I made seemed to hold a hidden moral weight. Everything became a debate—physical health vs. mental health. Community support vs. community safety. It was in this environment of constant ethical embattlement that I found myself wanting to write something simple and fun as a relief from the never ending deluge of horror.
I had never thought about writing science fiction before. I’d also never written an adult romance before, but I had just come out of a writing mentorship program where I had worked with a professional romance author, and I was itching to try out my new skills. The premise came first—a romance novel set in space. A man, a woman, and a spaceship with one bed. What could go wrong?
What ended up on the page was much less straightforward. As the character of Lyrii Thana began to develop over the course of the first few drafts, I found that she reflected the same level of ambient anxiety that we were all living with. At the beginning of the book she is torn between fear for her physical safety and the duty she owes to her family. As the story progresses the conflict begins to shift as she realizes that the systems she was willing to risk her life for may not hold the same promise of safety for everyone.
And that idea, finally, became the Big Idea behind By the Light of the Stars. No matter who you are—a princess, a rogue space pilot, or a refugee stranded on a tiny moon—we all deserve peace. There is no amount of shame or fear you can feel that makes you unworthy of love. There is no place you could possibly come from that makes you undeserving of safety.
We live in a world that loves to make us fight for our space in the box of “worthy” people who deserve basic human decency. Think about the metaphor “crabs in a bucket.” When one crab tries to climb out of the bucket, the other crabs will pull it back down. We are always looking for reasons that someone else doesn’t deserve the things that we want, even if those things should be afforded to every single human being on this planet without hesitation or qualification.
The Big Idea behind By the Light is one that I had been mulling over since 2020 and the dark years that followed, when we all engaged in spirited debates about whether or not Walmart employees were important enough to protect from exposure to deadly diseases at work, or if spending a counterfeit twenty dollar bill was an adequate reason to be executed on the street by the police.
No matter who you are, no matter where you come from, you deserve to be loved as you are. You deserve peace, and safety, and the right to determine your own future. That is the world I want to live in, and if you want to live in it too, we’re going to have to fight for it.
By the Light of the Stars: Amazon|Barnes & Noble|Bookshop|Powell’s|Scuppernog Books (signed copies)

Israel vs. Wikipedia

New report ·
Israel Is Attacking Wikipedia
Read our new report: twenty years of documented attacks on Wikipedia, and what this means for the information you get from search and AI.
Press: media@techforpalestine.org
Key findings
- 01 Twenty years of attempts to skew Wikipedia 17 efforts since 2001, nine still active We document 17 pro-Israel efforts to manipulate Wikipedia since 2001. Nine are still active, from CAMERA’s secret editor recruitment to a “Wikipedia war room” and editing courses run by Zionist groups. Read more: Section 3
- 02 The evidence from the Anti-Defamation League (ADL) doesn’t hold up 0 ADL claims that survived scrutiny We checked every claim in the ADL’s “Editing for Hate” report. No claim survived scrutiny. It doesn’t show a single false fact, or any antisemitic content added to Wikipedia. Read more: Section 6
- 03 A conspiracy built from circular logic 22–2 votes to keep vs. delete “Racial profiling in Israel”, an article the ADL used as evidence of collusion The ADL picked 30 editors and called them “bad-faith”. It then listed articles these editors voted to keep, as if that proved they were working together. But the ADL left out the vote counts. We added them up. On “Racial profiling in Israel”, 22 editors voted to keep and 2 voted to delete. On “Allegations of Israeli apartheid”, it was 37 to 14. Nearly everyone who voted agreed. Read more: Section 6.3
- 04 $96 million behind the political attacks $96M from pro-Israel PACs to the 23 signatories The ADL report was used in four US government actions against Wikipedia, including a letter from 23 members of Congress. Those 23 signatories have received more than $96M from pro-Israel PACs. Read more: Section 4
- 05 The ADL admits it In its October 2026 follow-up report, the ADL describes the “sustained, multi-front external pressure on the platform” from Congress, federal inquiries and its own reporting. Read more: Section 6.5
- 06 Volunteer editors under surveillance 45 full-time ADL analysts “tracking” editors Four groups are targeting Wikipedia editors: the Heritage Foundation, the ADL, NPOV and Builders for Tomorrow. Some editors who were doxxed have received death threats. The ADL’s CEO says he has 45 full-time analysts “tracking” editors, whom he calls “weirdos with email addresses.” Read more: Section 4.6
- 07 The real target is AI $729M Israel’s 2026 propaganda budget, up from $150M Wikipedia feeds Google Search and every major AI chatbot. Israel’s 2026 propaganda budget is $729M, up from $150M in 2025. That includes a $46.5M contract to make AI chatbots more pro-Israel. Read more: Section 2.2
- 08 The “Tech for Palestine campaign” that wasn’t ~260 edits by our volunteer workgroup over 8 months The ADL blamed a coordinated editing campaign on us. Our volunteer workgroup made about 260 edits over 8 months, none of them in the core Palestine–Israel articles. That’s one fewer edit than the ADL’s own 2021 editing campaign. Read more: Section 6.6
How we did this
This report started in March 2025, when the ADL published “Editing for Hate”. The report claims Wikipedia editors colluded to add antisemitism. US politicians started using it to attack Wikipedia. We wanted to know if the report itself was part of an influence operation.
We worked on three tracks:
- Israel’s efforts to influence Wikipedia. We collected 17 publicly known campaigns from public reports, at least 9 of them still active. This is the first time they are documented in one place. (Sections 3 and 4)
- The ADL report. We went through every claim and traced each one back to the Wikipedia edits and sources it cites. None held up. (Section 6)
- The propaganda tools behind the attack. (Sections 2 and 5)
The ADL analysed Arabic Wikipedia through English translations. We went back to the original Arabic. (Section 6.4)
Links to Wikipedia are permalinks to the exact edit. Content from pro-Israel organisations and paywalled sources is linked through archive.org, so it can’t be removed or changed. (Appendix B)
Where we used AI for research, we name the tool and the prompts and publish the full conversation. AI-reconstructed links used in the report were checked by hand. (Appendices C, D and E)
Every claim in this report is footnoted, more than 500 in total.
This is a living document. We update it when corrections or new information come in. Found an error? Email contact@techforpalestine.org.

Is Everything Broken?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
20:05:18 UTC
05:05:18 KST

Briefly. It seems that every day there’s another service down or impacted. The first thing to know is that this is the week before Thanksgiving, and as a person who has run online services that need to perform on Black Friday, I can confirm that the two or three weeks before Black Friday is when a lot of complex work lands in order to support the traffic surge. I would assume that all industries that need to handle holiday traffic (digital or otherwise) are doing the same proactive work.
AI has definitely impacted this situation in two ways:
First, a lot of humans have been laid off because of the belief that AI is going to increase productivity. In these layoffs, I am certain that humans who “just know how it works and never bothered to write it down” left the building.
Second, our collective AI paranoia is at an all-time high, and the news outlets know this.

====================================================================================================

Web Browser Engineering (2021)
Unknown

Web browsers are ubiquitous, but how do they work? This book
explains, building a basic but complete web browser, from networking to
JavaScript, in a couple thousand lines of Python.
Follow this book’s blog, Mastodon, or Twitter for updates. There’s
a discussion
forum for the book on Github, or you can email us directly.
Part 1: Loading Pages
- Downloading Web Pages
URLs and HTTP requests
- Drawing to the Screen
Creating windows and drawing to a canvas
- Formatting Text
Word wrapping and line spacing
Part 2: Viewing Documents
- Constructing an HTML Tree
Parsing and fixing HTML
- Laying Out Pages
Inline and block layout
- Applying Author Styles
Parsing and applying CSS
- Handling Buttons and Links
Hyperlinks and browser chrome
Part 3: Running Applications
- Sending Information to Servers
Form submission and web servers
- Running Interactive Scripts
Changing the DOM and reacting to events
- Keeping Data Private
Cookies and logins, XSS and CSRF
Part 4: Modern Browsers
- Adding Visual Effects
Blending, clipping, and compositing
- Scheduling Tasks and Threads
The event loop and the rendering pipeline
- Animating and Compositing
Smooth animations using the GPU
- Making Content Accessible
Keyboard input, zooming, and the accessibility tree
- Supporting Embedded Content
Images, iframes, and scripting
- Reusing Previous Computation
Invalidation, editing, and correctness

Highly Classified NSA Network Goes Down
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
20:00:00 UTC
05:00:00 KST

Highly Classified NSA Network Goes Down (cnn.com) 31
			
		 	
				"CNN and other outlets are reporting that a classified network at the NSA is down," writes longtime Slashdot reader frdmfghtr. "This follows a week of major outages at Microsoft, the FAA, and JPMorgan Chase." From a report:  The network, called NSANet, allows employees to communicate classified information internally. At this point there is no evidence of a cyberattack, sources said. As the agency works to understand the scope of the problem, it has enacted staggered arrivals for personnel, the sources said.
"We see no evidence that this was a cyberattack," White House spokesperson Lauren Bis said Tuesday on Newsmax. "There is no crisis, or anything like that, for Americans to be worried about." [...] The US shares intelligence with a group of countries in what is known as the Five Eyes intelligence-sharing alliance, which includes the US, UK, New Zealand, Australia and Canada. That intelligence sharing system has not been impacted as a result of the NSANet outage, according to a source from one of the countries and a former US intelligence official.  The NSA released a statement addressing the network issues: "The National Security Agency experienced temporary degradation during scheduled routine maintenance. While some internal systems were affected, mission-critical capabilities remain fully operational and our ability to defend national security continues. NSA's infrastructure is designed with multiple layers of redundancy to maintain operations during planned maintenance or unexpected events."

Mapped: The Most Popular Grocery Chain in Every State
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
20:00:00 UTC
05:00:00 KST

Mapped: The Most Popular Grocery Chain in Every State
Grocery shopping — we all have to do it. But grocery stores vary widely based on region and culture, and certain chains have beloved followings for a variety of reasons, be it their excellent snacks, fresh produce, or prices that are easy on the wallet. Naturally, we were curious what makes people choose to shop at one grocery store over another and how that differs across the country. So we set out to find the best grocery store in every state.
To be clear, we pulled and analyzed data from a variety of sources, but we were by no means scientific. We started with Google, referencing the most-searched grocery store in every state. We then turned to Yelp data (which they hand-pulled for us!), which identified the clear regional favorites by weighing consumer interest metrics, such as page views, photos, reviews, and ratings. Finally, we polled our Instagram followers as well as our staff for the stores that needed special consideration. Here’s what we found.

Waymo suspends freeway rides nationwide after California DMV order
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
19:49:41 UTC
04:49:41 KST

Waymo robotaxis are no longer allowed to operate on California freeways after the state’s Department of Motor Vehicles restricted the Alphabet-owned company’s operations following several incidents that raised safety concerns.
California’s DMV, which regulates the testing and deployment of autonomous vehicles in the state, issued the operational restrictions on Waymo on November 11. However, the restrictions weren’t made public until Tuesday. San Francisco Business Times was the first to report the order.
The DMV confirmed the new restrictions to TechCrunch, stating that the department took action after three Waymo autonomous vehicles entered an active construction closure on U.S. Route 101 near Burlingame, and another separate incident involving California Highway Patrol vehicles on Highway 280 in San Francisco.
Waymo has complied and is preventing its robotaxis from accessing freeways in any of the markets it operates in, including those outside of California. Waymo currently has commercial operations in 15 U.S. cities.
The DMV said Waymo’s freeway operations pose an imminent hazard to public safety.
“The Department determined that Waymo’s driverless freeway operations pose an imminent hazard to public safety due to recurring incursions by its vehicles into active construction zones where construction workers, vehicles and equipment are present, despite a prior restriction and corrective actions implemented following similar incidents that occurred on May 18, 2026,” according to an emailed statement sent to TechCrunch.
The DMV said the restrictions will remain in place until Waymo can demonstrate that it has taken “effective corrective actions” to address the deficiencies.
Waymo spokesperson Dana Gallagher told TechCrunch the company has temporarily restricted freeway operations across its other operating markets to evaluate its performance.
“Safety is at the foundation of everything we do,” Gallagher said in a statement. “Following recent freeway construction zone encounters on U.S. 101 in California that did not involve any injuries or collisions, the California DMV has implemented an operating restriction on freeway operations in the state. In alignment with our safety-first principles, we have also temporarily restricted freeway operations across our other operating markets to evaluate our performance. Our ride-hailing service continues to operate safely on surface streets, serving over half a million trips each week. We are working with the California DMV and other state officials diligently and responsibly to resume freeway operations.”
Waymo began offering freeway rides in late 2025, a commercial milestone that has been central to its expansion in major metro areas. Freeway access allows Waymo to connect riders to airports and other destinations more efficiently, slashing travel times by avoiding slower surface streets.
That has been particularly important in the San Francisco Bay Area because Waymo’s service area stretches from the city south to Silicon Valley. Freeway access has dramatically shortened trips across the Peninsula that previously took 45 minutes or more than an hour.
In May, Waymo abruptly stopped operating on highways. The company didn’t cite a specific reason at the time, but several social media posts showed Waymo robotaxis driving through traffic cones and struggling to navigate construction zones.
Waymo robotaxis returned to freeways in July after the company said it updated the self-driving software system to improve performance around freeway construction zones.

Effects of a Nuclear Detonation in Low Earth Orbit
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
19:46:57 UTC
04:46:57 KST

Physics > Physics and Society
  [Submitted on 6 Nov 2026]
Title:Effects of a Nuclear Detonation in Low Earth Orbit
View PDF HTML (experimental)
            Abstract:More than 13,000 satellites operate in low Earth orbit (LEO), and concern has grown that nuclear weapons could be used in space to target them. Beyond the initial X-ray burst, electrons released by radioactive fission products can become trapped in Earth's magnetic field, repeatedly exposing satellites as an artificial radiation belt. Here we couple prompt radiation, electron trapping and belt evolution to satellite failures, collisions and atmospheric drag. For a 1-Mt detonation at 500km over the South Atlantic, repeated belt exposure disables approximately 98% of modeled satellites within one week in our central case. Tripling the assumed radiation tolerance delays widespread failure by only days: approximately 97% are still disabled within two weeks. The loss of maneuverability sharply increases collision risk, with approximately 20 collisions expected within two years. Drag removes many disabled spacecraft from lower altitudes, but replacement satellites could remain exposed to radiation and debris for years. These findings show how a single detonation could devastate orbital infrastructure and support efforts to prevent the deployment and use of nuclear weapons in space.

Framework’s better couch keyboard is now available for preorder
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
19:38:42 UTC
04:38:42 KST

Framework announced its Wireless Touchpad Keyboard earlier this year with the goal of delivering a better couch keyboarding experience than the Logitech K400. According to The Verge’s Sean Hollister who went hands-on with Framework’s new all-in-one keyboard and touchpad last April, the company has succeeded in that goal, but it turns out you’ll be paying a bit more for Framework’s than Logitech’s.
Framework’s better couch keyboard is now available for preorder
Logitech’s K400 may have met its match.
Logitech’s K400 may have met its match.
While the K400 couch keyboard is $35, the Framework Wireless Touchpad Keyboard is $99 with a $50 fully refundable deposit required to preorder one starting today. That makes it nearly three times more expensive than Logitech’s, but Framework’s upgrades and improvements could make it worth the premium.
Instead of starting from scratch, Framework uses the same keyboard module found in its Laptop 12 paired with a precision 120Hz touchpad and controller that’s also used in all of the company’s computers. A Bluetooth connection is the easiest way to pair the keyboard to PCs running Windows, Linux, or macOS, or mobile devices, game consoles, and streaming boxes. You can quickly switch between four wirelessly connected devices with a button press including one that uses an included USB receiver. If you want more responsiveness, you can also tether it to a device with a USB-C cable.
On a full charge of its replaceable 1,200mAh battery the keyboard will run for well over a year with sporadic use. If you’re instead using it for hours every day on a desk, Framework estimates the battery life will be closer to over a month. For customizability and remapping the keyboard’s open source firmware is based on a fork of ZMK.

California just banned Waymo from all state freeways
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
19:38:05 UTC
04:38:05 KST

California has pulled the plug on Waymo’s driverless freeway rides across the entire state, citing potential safety hazards.
California just banned Waymo from all state freeways
The state DMV labeled Waymo’s highway operations an “imminent hazard to public safety” after its robotaxis drove into an active construction zone.
In a statement, the California Department of Motor Vehicles said that the ban went into effect on November 11th after two recent high-profile Bay Area incidents, in which Waymo robotaxis drove into an active construction closure on US-101 near Burlingame, and a separate run-in involving California Highway Patrol vehicles on Highway 280 in San Francisco.
In its statement, the DMV explicitly labeled Waymo’s freeway operations as an “imminent hazard to public safety,” noting that this isn’t an isolated glitch. Waymo has experienced “recurring incursions into active construction zones, the DMV said, arguing the company was putting road workers at risk.
Waymo has struggled with freeway operations over the years. The company has been testing its vehicles on California’s freeways since at least August 2024, before eventually opening up to public passengers in November 2025. Waymo voluntarily paused freeway operations in May 2026 over concerns about construction sites — but then resumed rides in late July.
Operating on freeways is crucial to Waymo’s robotaxi business; without freeways, the company will be forced to take longer routes on local roads that will force customers to pay more for their fares. On average, Waymo rides are already more expensive than human-driven Uber or Lyft trips.
In a statement, Waymo spokesperson Chris Pappas said the company voluntarily paused freeway driving across all its markets to review performance, though its surface-street ride-hailing service remains unaffected. Waymo is currently collaborating with state regulators to safely resume highway operations.
“Safety is at the foundation of everything we do,” Pappas said.
In order to get back on the freeway, Waymo will need to demonstrate it has taken sufficient action to improve its operation around construction zones, the DMV said. The company will need to submit evidence to the agency for review, at which point regulators will determine whether it can safely resume operations.
Update, November 17th: Updated to include a statement from Waymo spokesperson.

Model ML raises $250M to automate investment banking grunt work
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
19:32:11 UTC
04:32:11 KST

Model ML, a London-based startup that uses AI to automate work traditionally performed by junior bankers and consultants, including building financial models, preparing pitch decks, and writing due diligence reports, has raised $250 million in Series B funding at a $1.5 billion valuation. The new round, which comes a year after its $75 million Series A round was announced, was co-led by General Atlantic and earlier backer QED Investors.
Brothers Chaz and Arnie Englander co-founded the three-year-old startup after previously co-founding two other companies, including Fat Llama, a peer-to-peer rental marketplace acquired by Swedish rival Hygglo for $41.5 million in 2022.
Model ML develops AI agents that integrate with clients’ systems, data, and tools, including Excel and PowerPoint. The startup says its agents understand the full context of deals that bankers and consultants are working on and can automatically update financial models and presentations when new developments affect their analysis.
Other startups automating the routine, repetitive tasks for investment bankers and management consultants include Hebbia and Rogo, which raised $160 million at a $2 billion valuation in April.
Anthropic and OpenAI have also built tools for financial professionals, but Model ML says it runs on open source models and that doing so allows it to operate at about 2% of the cost of frontier models while delivering faster results.

Esoteric Rituals Meet K-Pop in a Stop-Motion Music Video for Balming Tiger’s ‘Janjan’
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
19:26:31 UTC
04:26:31 KST

Esoteric Rituals Meet K-Pop in a Stop-Motion Music Video for Balming Tiger’s ‘Janjan’
Deep in a phantasmagorical cave, a gathering of uncanny figures and critters emerges from dark waters in the music video for “Janjan” by alternative K-pop group Balming Tiger. Both playful and a little eerie, the animated work celebrates collectivity and freedom of expression.
Directed and animated by Takahashi Riku, a.k.a. DOKUZEN, the surreal music video is composed predominantly of stop-motion scenes set to a thumping rhythm to which fantastical paper figures dance as if performing esoteric rituals. It also features an interlude of drawn animations—a kind of world within a world.

350 years ago, this man discovered that light has a finite speed
Norway
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
20:25:27 CET
04:25:27 KST

350 years ago, this man discovered that light has a finite speed
Ole Rømer was the first to demonstrate that light takes time to travel.
In September 1676, Ole Rømer made a prediction to the members of the Académie des Sciences in Paris.
He said that on November 9, Io, one of Jupiter's moons, would emerge from the planet's shadow ten minutes later than expected.
Rømer was right.
In doing so, he proved that light travels at a finite speed. This knowledge became crucial to how we understand the universe today.
Until then, people had believed that light travelled instantaneously from one place to another.
Regularly disappears behind Jupiter
Ole Rømer was a Danish astronomer and physicist. In 1671, he travelled to Paris, where he spent ten years as a member of the scientific academy and an astronomer at the Paris Observatory, according to the Great Norwegian Encyclopedia.
In the years leading up to the discovery, Rømer and other astronomers had been closely observing Io, Jupiter's third-largest moon.
"Io regularly disappears behind Jupiter, and this regularity made it possible to use the moon as a clock," Jacob Wüsthoff Linder tells Science Norway.
He is a professor at NTNU's Department of Physics.
Such a precise ‘clock’ could be useful for navigation, among other things. Ole Eggers Bjælde, a curator at the Science Museums at Aarhus University, writes this in an article on the popular-science media outlet Videnskab.dk.
Rømer studied and recorded the exact times when Io disappeared into Jupiter’s shadow and when it emerged on the other side.
Discovered a pattern
Bjælde explains that because Io orbits so close to Jupiter, only one of these events could be observed on any given night.
In the days and months before Jupiter and Earth reached their closest point to each other, astronomers could observe the moon disappearing into Jupiter’s shadow.
In the period after Jupiter and Earth were closest to each other, they could see Io emerging from the shadow.
Rømer compiled detailed measurements of these events and eventually noticed a pattern.
"Rømer noticed that the eclipses occurred slightly later than expected when Earth was moving away from Jupiter, and slightly earlier when Earth was moving towards Jupiter," says Linder. "He realised that this was because the light had to travel a longer or shorter distance."
"Impressive"
In 1676, he therefore predicted that on November 9, Io would emerge from Jupiter's shadow ten minutes later than expected.
He was correct. If light travelled instantaneously, Io should have appeared ten minutes earlier.
Rømer demonstrated that light does not travel infinitely fast, says Linder. The finding was published shortly afterwards, on December 7, in Journal des sçavans.
Rømer was not the first to suggest that light has a finite speed, but he is regarded as the first to demonstrate it with evidence.
Other philosophers and scientists had previously theorised that light has a finite speed, including the Greek philosopher Empedocles, the Muslim scientists Ibn al-Haytham and Abu Rayhan al-Biruni, and the English philosopher and natural scientist Roger Bacon, according to Wikipedia.
"How accurate was Rømer's estimate of the speed of light compared with what we know today?"
"Not very accurate by modern standards, but impressive for 1676," says Linder.
Rømer concluded that light took approximately 22 minutes to cross the diameter of Earth's orbit, says Linder. That corresponds to roughly 225,000 kilometres per second, whereas the correct value is around 300,000 kilometres per second.
"So his estimate was about 25 per cent too low. What really mattered, however, was not the exact figure, but that he demonstrated that the speed of light was finite and could be measured," he says.
Gained support
Was Rømer's finding quickly accepted by other scientists?
"It was probably a gradual process, and there were critical voices at first. The head of the observatory where Rømer worked, for example, never fully accepted his explanation. Nevertheless, his findings received support fairly quickly from prominent scientific figures such as Huygens, and later Newton," says Linder.
Christiaan Huygens was a Dutch mathematician and astronomer. He is known for developing the wave theory of light, among other things.
"The decisive blow came with James Bradley's discovery of stellar aberration about 50 years later, which provided a completely independent and strong confirmation that light has a finite speed," says Linder.
Stellar aberration is the phenomenon in which stars appear to shift slightly in the sky throughout the year because Earth moves around the Sun while light travels at a finite speed, the researcher explains.
Looking into the past
"Rømer was the first to demonstrate that light actually takes time to travel. It was also the first time anyone had produced a reasonably realistic estimate of the speed of light," says Linder.
This became very important for astronomy.
"Because when we look out into space, we are actually seeing things as they were when the light began its journey towards us. The farther away something is, the farther back in time we are looking," he says.
This has been important for understanding how the universe has evolved.
———
Translated by Alette Bjordal Gjellesvik
Read the Norwegian version of this article on forskning.no

The latest news from Science Norway, sent twice a week and completely free.

"How accurate was Rømer's estimate of the speed of light compared with what we know today?"
"Not very accurate by modern standards, but impressive for 1676," says Linder.
Rømer concluded that light took approximately 22 minutes to cross the diameter of Earth's orbit, says Linder. That corresponds to roughly 225,000 kilometres per second, whereas the correct value is around 300,000 kilometres per second.
"So his estimate was about 25 per cent too low. What really mattered, however, was not the exact figure, but that he demonstrated that the speed of light was finite and could be measured," he says.
Gained support
Was Rømer's finding quickly accepted by other scientists?
"It was probably a gradual process, and there were critical voices at first. The head of the observatory where Rømer worked, for example, never fully accepted his explanation. Nevertheless, his findings received support fairly quickly from prominent scientific figures such as Huygens, and later Newton," says Linder.
Christiaan Huygens was a Dutch mathematician and astronomer. He is known for developing the wave theory of light, among other things.
"The decisive blow came with James Bradley's discovery of stellar aberration about 50 years later, which provided a completely independent and strong confirmation that light has a finite speed," says Linder.
Stellar aberration is the phenomenon in which stars appear to shift slightly in the sky throughout the year because Earth moves around the Sun while light travels at a finite speed, the researcher explains.
Looking into the past
"Rømer was the first to demonstrate that light actually takes time to travel. It was also the first time anyone had produced a reasonably realistic estimate of the speed of light," says Linder.
This became very important for astronomy.
"Because when we look out into space, we are actually seeing things as they were when the light began its journey towards us. The farther away something is, the farther back in time we are looking," he says.
This has been important for understanding how the universe has evolved.
———

2026-11-17

04:19:10 KST

If you’re looking to upgrade your headphones before the big wave of Black Friday deals arrives, you can buy JBL’s Tour One M3 for a new low price of $149 ($300 off) at Amazon, Best Buy, and directly from JBL. The deal is so good that my colleague John Higgins, The Verge’s audio and TV reviewer, popped in on his day off to tell us just how much of a steal they are at this price.
JBL’s headphones that are great for in-flight entertainment are 67 percent off
They include a transmitter that let you listen to in-flight entertainment without plugging in your headphones.
JBL’s Tour One M3 stand out largely because they come with the Smart Tx wireless audio transmitter. Frequent fliers will likely appreciate the accessory, which plugs into a plane’s in-flight entertainment system via a 3.5mm cable, allowing you to listen to audio wirelessly. It also works with other devices that lack Bluetooth support but have a headphone jack. The small touchscreen-equipped transmitter also lets you easily adjust the headphones’ noise cancellation, EQ settings, and volume. It can be used to answer calls, too.
Fancy tricks aside, the headphones get the essentials right. According to John, they offer excellent passive noise isolation, and their active noise cancellation is good enough to block out low-frequency sounds, like airplane engines. He also praises their overall sound quality, though he notes that JBL’s default tuning slightly affects midrange clarity, something you can easily fix by adjusting the EQ. They also support high-resolution lossless audio over the included USB-C or 3.5mm cable, plus spatial audio with head tracking. JBL says they can last up to 70 hours on a single charge with noise cancellation off — enough to get you through multiple long-haul flights — while the USB-C-chargeable transmitter offers up to 18 hours of battery life.

Google expands access to Gemini Tasks, which can help with calls, purchases
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
19:02:20 UTC
04:02:20 KST

Google is expanding the rollout of Tasks, a toggle in Gemini that converts the chatbot into a more active helper.
Unlike Gemini’s Chat, which merely allows users to talk to Google’s AI assistant for informational purposes, Tasks empowers the app to complete basic secretarial functions, deputizing it as a kind of personal assistant.
How, specifically, can you use it? Google says that the app can call businesses and book appointments on users’ behalf; it can also complete purchases for them via Google Pay, and even sign into websites securely if prompted to do so.
In a blog post, Josh Woodword, VP of Gemini, claims to have recently used the feature to call local car dealerships to schedule a service appointment for his family’s minivan. He says he also used it to analyze his electrical bill.
Tasks was initially launched to select testers back in May as Gemini Spark, a 24/7 agentic helper, and was expanded to U.S. subscribers of its Google AI Ultra tier. Gemini Spark eventually became “Task Mode.” Google is now making the feature available for free to hundreds of millions of people who are using the company’s new Gemini 4 Hydrogen model.
Getting the function up and running is easy. Users merely flip a toggle within the app from Chat to Tasks, which enables the feature. Users can also send texts to Gemini or call the app to give it commands.

Sandbox Router sends every job to the fastest available sandbox, and Afterburner adds up to 25% more CPU and up to 50% more RAM to a running step at no extra cost. Up to 2.32x faster than GitHub-hosted runners.

Volume discounts available
Everything you need to evaluate StarSling for your CI.
StarSling is AI-native CI for GitHub Actions: drop-in Ubuntu runners plus agents that open optimization PRs for your workflows. The runners replace ubuntu-latest and ubuntu-24.04 and run up to 33% cheaper than equivalent GitHub-hosted runners. Together with agent PRs, they made GitHub Actions up to 10x faster in public benchmarks, and the agents continuously analyze your workflows to keep the pipeline improving.
Agent-native CI is CI that improves itself. Beyond running your workflows on faster hardware, AI agents continuously analyze your jobs, run logs, and machine telemetry, then open pull requests that optimize your pipeline: caching, parallelization, dependency installs, test sharding, and workflow structure. Traditional CI is static; agent-native CI gets faster the longer you use it. Read more about AI-native CI.
Two things move the needle: faster hardware and removing redundant work. Swap ubuntu-latest for a runner with stronger CPUs, then let AI agents find dead steps, cache fixes, and parallelization opportunities your team hasn't had time to fix (in StarSling's public benchmarks, runners plus agent PRs made CI up to 10x faster). For a deeper guide, read fast GitHub Actions. You can also work through the GitHub Actions CI best practices yourself, without StarSling, or follow Levels of CI for every way to make GitHub Actions cheaper and faster.
Usually the runner. GitHub-hosted ubuntu-latest runs on older shared hardware, so even simple builds spend time waiting on CPU. Switching to faster CPUs (StarSling Runners use 5th Gen AMD EPYC) typically cuts wall-clock time significantly before any workflow optimization.
Yes. Change runs-on: ubuntu-latest to runs-on: starsling-ubuntu-26.04 (or ubuntu-24.04 to starsling-ubuntu-24.04) in your GitHub Actions workflow files. No other config changes required. See StarSling vs GitHub Actions and GitHub Actions alternatives.
StarSling is not a full replacement for GitHub Actions. It is a GitHub Actions runner alternative for supported Ubuntu/Linux jobs: you keep GitHub Actions workflows and change the runner label. If you are comparing full CI/CD platforms, read GitHub Actions alternatives.
$0.008 per minute for 4 vCPU Linux runners with 16 GB RAM, 33% cheaper than GitHub Actions' $0.012/min. New paid subscriptions are billed by the second, with no one-minute minimum. Queue time is never billed. New accounts get 2,000 free minutes in their first month. See StarSling pricing.
Yes. StarSling Disks are persistent directories mounted into StarSling Runners, so dependency stores and build output, such as pnpm's store or Rust's target/, are already in place when the next job starts. In a benchmark on Mastra's 4.5 GiB pnpm store, dependencies were ready in about 25 seconds, 4.5x faster than GitHub's actions/cache. Pull requests from the same repository read the default branch's published disk. Storage is $0.50 per GB-month.
StarSling Runners are x64 Linux, from 2 to 64 vCPU. Need ARM? Email founders@starsling.dev.
Building Docker images in CI? Email founders@starsling.dev to talk through caching layers on StarSling Runners.
Yes. StarSling GPU Runners are drop-in GitHub Actions runners backed by a dedicated NVIDIA GPU: RTX PRO 6000 at a normalized $0.06422 per minute, RTX 5090 at $0.03022, and RTX 4090 at $0.02522, with H100 coming soon. Set runs-on: starsling-ubuntu-26.04-gpu and pick a card in the label. The GPU is dedicated to that job for as long as it runs, so nothing reclaims it partway through your suite. Explore StarSling GPU Runners.
RTX PRO 6000 is the default, and it suits large-model and memory-heavy CI workloads. RTX 5090 suits fast inference tests and model evals. RTX 4090 covers everyday GPU tests at the lowest per-minute cost. You choose per job in the runs-on: label, so different jobs in the same workflow can use different cards. Explore StarSling GPU Runners.
Yes. StarSling Review Runners are code review agents you configure and run in your own CI. One workflow file, and every pull request is analyzed as a GitHub Actions job on a StarSling runner using your model key, review skills and scripts from your codebase, and team-defined reviewers. Skills and instructions load from the trusted base branch, so a pull request cannot rewrite its own reviewer. The output is a review comment on the pull request, before a human opens it. Explore StarSling Review Runners.
A review is a GitHub Actions job on a StarSling runner in the same account as the rest of your CI. It runs on starsling-review, a 4 vCPU runner with 16 GB of memory. Review Runner time has a normalized rate of $0.004 per minute. New paid subscriptions are billed in whole seconds from job start to finish. Partial seconds round up, with no one-minute minimum. Existing paid subscriptions remain billed in whole minutes with a one-minute minimum until migrated. Queue time is never billed. Concurrency is unlimited. Model tokens are billed by your own provider, on the account you already have. Explore StarSling Review Runners.
After installing the StarSling GitHub App, AI agents continuously analyze your workflows, jobs, run logs, and machine telemetry, then open pull requests that optimize your setup: cache fixes, faster install strategies, Docker health checks, test sharding, parallelization, workflow structure, and job ordering. You review and merge. Optimization PRs are on paid plans, off by default for new accounts.
Coding agents (Claude Code, Cursor, Codex) push PR volume up several-fold, which compounds CI cost and queue times fast. The right fit is a runner with unlimited concurrency, continuous AI optimization (so the pipeline gets faster as PR volume grows), and per-minute pricing with no seat fees. StarSling Runners are built for this pattern.
Failed jobs are billed for the time they run: whole seconds on new paid subscriptions, with partial seconds rounded up; whole minutes on existing paid subscriptions until migrated. Queue time is never billed. New accounts get 2,000 free minutes in their first month.
Each job runs in its own single-use, hardware-isolated microVM that's destroyed after the run. That's the same isolation model GitHub-hosted runners use, so there's nothing for a later job or a fork PR to persist on or reach. Secrets pass directly from GitHub to your job and are never stored by StarSling. More details in our docs.
Sign up with GitHub and install the StarSling GitHub App, switch runners with one line, and run 2x faster on day one. StarSling agents keep finding the next speedup.

School Phone Bans Are Gradually Improving Test Scores
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
19:00:00 UTC
04:00:00 KST

School Phone Bans Are Gradually Improving Test Scores 9
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from The New York Times: School cellphone bans are beginning to modestly improve students' academic performance, according to the first large national analysis that compared students' test scores before and after the bans. Schools with strict bans -- requiring students to lock their phones in pouches all school day -- saw math and reading scores improve as if students had an additional week or two of school a year, the study found. It was published Tuesday by the Center for Education Policy Research at Harvard.
U.S. students' test scores began falling over a decade ago. The drop coincided with the rise of smartphones, raising questions about their role in the decline. The evidence so far on the academic effects of phone bans has been mixed. But the new study indicates they are starting to have an influence.
The study looked at 3.3 million students between 2021 and 2026. They were in third through 10th grade, attending 4,600 U.S. schools. Using scores on MAP tests, which track achievement from fall to spring, it compared the performance of students at schools that put in strict phone bans with those at similar schools that did not have such bans. The annual gains reversed about half the annual loss that an average student experienced in the years leading up to the pandemic. (Students lost even more ground during pandemic closures.) "The earlier studies on cellphone bans have been dismissed as implying small impacts, and in-school cellphone use has been ruled out as playing a big role in the decline," said Thomas Kane, a professor at the Harvard Graduate School of Education and an author of the study. "But we think that's mistaken."

Google is rebranding its Spark AI agent as “Tasks” and making it free to use. The feature, which is powered by Google’s new Gemini 4 Hydrogen AI model, can do things like create meal plans, manage your calendar, and dig through your emails to find recurring expenses you might not need.
Google’s Spark AI agent becomes ‘Tasks’ — and now it’s free
Tasks in Gemini allows the AI agent to call businesses on your behalf, purchase items with Google Pay, and more.
Users in the US will get access to a few other features, including the ability for the AI agent to call businesses to compare product pricing and check inventory, purchase items with Google Pay, and sign into accounts that support Google Password Manager. US users can also assign Gemini tasks by emailing gemini@google.com or texting it.
Google first introduced its Spark AI agent in May, but it was only available to Google AI Pro and Ultra subscribers. The launch of tasks in Gemini brings Spark’s capabilities to all users in the US, Canada, Japan, Korea, Australia, and India. You can find tasks by opening Gemini and changing the toggle on the homepage from “Chat” to “Tasks.”
Tasks in Gemini is just one of the many agentic AI features that have emerged over the past year, including Claude Duet, OpenAI’s Dots, and Meta’s Muse. Google says Gemini will perform tasks in a “hardware-isolated virtual machine that’s dedicated to your account and never shared with other users.” You can also provide instructions for Gemini while it completes tasks. When performing more sensitive tasks, like purchasing an item, it will stop working and ask for your permission before following through.

Solid 2.0
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
18:56:17 UTC
03:56:17 KST

Solid 2.0 - The Long Hold
Solid 2.0 reaches stable after five years: async in the reactive graph, server functions, diagnostics built for agents, and a smaller API.
5 years is a long time to work on anything. It took 5 years from my first commit of Solid to 1.0, and it's been another 5 since then.
A lot changes in 5 years. Solid 1.0 made a bet that fine-grained reactivity, signals, was the right foundation for building user interfaces. Back then it was an unusual position. Today signals are everywhere. In Angular, in Svelte's runes, in Vue Vapor, and in a proposal before TC39 to put them in the language itself. It even pushed the conversation into React's compiler.
More recently that conversation has shifted to AI, arguably an even larger change in how we build. But Solid has always been about building the right things; the performant things. So for us the mission hasn't really changed all that much. The things that we identified as standout about our approach still stand out now. But instead of living in the shadow of more popular solutions Solid 2.0 carves out a path that others can follow. Deliberately this time.
Today that solution reaches stable.
Async Graph
If fine-grained reactivity's greatest strength is knowing the shape of our application, that knowledge shouldn't end with the process, or the machine the code is running on. Real applications are distributed. Real applications are born from data that lives on a server. The coordination of these pieces should be intentional and by design.
This starts with bringing async into the graph. A computation can return a Promise or an async iterator, and everything downstream just understands:
This allows authoring by affordance while automating the handling of race conditions. The introduction of async doesn't cause an app refactor. Speculative state is automatically rolled back without manually written processes ensuring the server remains the authority on truth.
SolidStart introduce the world to server functions in early 2022, and now they have found their way home to Solid's core, better than ever. Not only do they support highly capable RPC calls over Request/Response but persistent connections over Server Sent Events. And consuming it remains as simple as the code below.
This is only the beginning of what can be done with Server Functions and we will be announcing some new features (like reactive Server Components) in coming weeks that leverage this capability.
Built to Be Written by Agents
"But, we aren't writing the code anymore!" We hear you. A system built on constraints by construction ends up with less code to maintain and less edge cases. What used to be considered a DX shortcoming of reactive frameworks are now their greatest strength. And Solid 2.0 goes all in.
Every framework now says it "works great with AI", and usually that means models have seen a lot of it. That's a statement about training data, not about the framework. The question that matters is what happens when the agent gets it wrong.
In most frameworks a reactive mistake is silent. The app runs, it's just wrong, and the framework can't tell you, because it can't see it either. Solid can. A fine-grained graph knows exactly what was read, where, and what will update. So Solid 2.0's development builds report mistakes as they happen, with a stable code, the location in your source, and the fix:
These handle everything from strict errors to helpful heuristics. Thanks to async in the graph we can detect waterfalls, poorly constructed effect loops, poorly scaling graph shapes like wide graph fanouts and fan-ins. Today along with linting rules we can provide the best in class direction for agents at compile and at runtime.
This area is still evolving as we learn more about how agents build applications with Solid. The hard part, defining the primitives and semantics, is done. We've integrated with new dev tooling, and Chrome's Performance Tracks. And this capability goes beyond development as this observability powers things like our updated Sentry plugin so you can get the same information on your production builds.
We've also been looking at alternative syntax. Solid 2.0 adds experimental support for ReactiveScript and TSRX, both aiming to use compilation to further improve agent authoring.
One Framework for All
I've always been a big believer that React's super power is its composability. That it allowed the ecosystem to build on its humble pieces. However, we've learned a lot since 2013. At that point composable state, async patterns, and server rendering lacked the primitives to build upon and the ecosystem came up with their own solutions. This was fine for a while but tied React's hands and fractured their ecosystem. Something only a framework as popular as React could survive.
Newer frameworks have taken more on themselves and tend to only have one metaframework, or one router. We respect React's original positioning but have updated it for 2026. It isn't about restricting the solution but giving developers the primitives to build upon in the way React originally did. Server functions are example of this. Supporting any router, and cache solution for Single-Flight Mutation is another.
We retired our metaframework SolidStart, since every primitive needed to build a Solid Metaframework is now provided by the core or by framework agnostic Vite plugins. We believe that building an opinionated layer like a metaframework shouldn't have you needing to re-implement everything again. We've taken care of the hard parts so you can focus on what you care about. And these days a lot of that work is going to be taken on by your agent. This means less to maintain but no loss in capability.
And this makes it easier for the ecosystem. Solid 2.0 only requires one Clerk or Sentry plugin since we provide the primitives for Request/Response (built on web standards) and the hooks for observability that work across any library. We've already seen success with TanStack Query and Router being as capable as anything we've built first party, bringing in Tanner's impeccable attention to detail for the APIs people love to use.
Less is More
Somehow even though Solid 2.0 brings more capability to Solid, we've collapsed the surface area at almost every point. There are less primitives needed to be learnt, less ceremony around handling async. createResource, batch, startTransition, on, produce, createComputed, createMutable are all gone, because we no longer need them.
The equivalent code written for Solid 2.0 is shorter and clearer on intent. It informs our ability observe the runtime, provide better compiler errors, and removes the need for as many escape hatches (like createEffect) that can get us in trouble. This is something we believe important in an age of AI. Safe by construction rather than by exhaustion. Reduce complexity by deriving(reducing) state rather than putting it in a reducer.
When the framework carries the guarantees, all loading state, race conditions, and cancellation logic disappears and what you are left with is the UI.
The Road Here
The first discussions of what a Solid 2.0 would look like started at the moment 1.0 was released. We saught feedback from the community on new reactivity ideas. The real effort started in late 2024. We announced the road to 2.0 in February 2025. Over a year of experiments followed, rewriting the reactive core from scratch until the async model held up to everything we threw at it. The beta landed in March, and the Release Candidate in August, with the API frozen.
Then 23 release candidates in 14 weeks. In that time alone, 971 pull requests were merged and 753 issues closed. We set one bar for stable. A full week with no regression in ordinary app code, and no crash or hang anywhere. It took a while to get there. By the end, the bug reports coming in were from people(agents) deliberately hunting edge cases in the deepest corners of the scheduler, and finding them.
So many people have touched the project over the past 5 years, from working on the initial reactivity prototypes, to testing the latest RC builds on their production apps. I've tried to thank as many of you as I can and attached hundreds of handles in the RC announcement. There are so many. There are also the many great organizations that have sponsored our development over the years: Sentry, Netlify, Google Chrome, JetBrains, Anomaly, Cursor, Code Rabbit, Anthropic, Tembo and so many more.
But mostly I want to thank everyone who has believed in the project. We've accomplished great things and there is much more to come. That isn't possible without all of you. Personally, the past 2 and half years have been some of the most difficult of my life, but knowing what we are building that impacts so many people leaves me with a sense of fulfillment. Solid has always been bigger than its core team, and 2.0 is the clearest proof of that yet.
Solid 2.0 is here. Let's build.
The Long Hold refers to a diagnostic warning in Solid 2.0. It fires when a hold has been shown(isPending) but sufficient time has passed since the last input. Holds were at the heart of our async work for 2.0, and we acknowledge it took a while for this all to land.

We announced the road to 2.0 in February 2025. Over a year of experiments followed, rewriting the reactive core from scratch until the async model held up to everything we threw at it. The beta landed in March, and the Release Candidate in August, with the API frozen.
Then 23 release candidates in 14 weeks. In that time alone, 971 pull requests were merged and 753 issues closed. We set one bar for stable. A full week with no regression in ordinary app code, and no crash or hang anywhere. It took a while to get there. By the end, the bug reports coming in were from people(agents) deliberately hunting edge cases in the deepest corners of the scheduler, and finding them.
So many people have touched the project over the past 5 years, from working on the initial reactivity prototypes, to testing the latest RC builds on their production apps. I've tried to thank as many of you as I can and attached hundreds of handles in the RC announcement. There are so many. There are also the many great organizations that have sponsored our development over the years: Sentry, Netlify, Google Chrome, JetBrains, Anomaly, Cursor, Code Rabbit, Anthropic, Tembo and so many more.
But mostly I want to thank everyone who has believed in the project. We've accomplished great things and there is much more to come. That isn't possible without all of you. Personally, the past 2 and half years have been some of the most difficult of my life, but knowing what we are building that impacts so many people leaves me with a sense of fulfillment. Solid has always been bigger than its core team, and 2.0 is the clearest proof of that yet.
Solid 2.0 is here. Let's build.
The Long Hold refers to a diagnostic warning in Solid 2.0. It fires when a hold has been shown(isPending) but sufficient time has passed since the last input. Holds were at the heart of our async work for 2.0, and we acknowledge it took a while for this all to land.

Apple services, including iCloud, App Store, and Apple Music, hit by outage
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
18:55:40 UTC
03:55:40 KST

After a recent slew of tech outages, service disruptions, and flight chaos this week, now it’s Apple customers who are facing technical issues.
The iPhone maker’s System Status page indicates that there are issues with iCloud’s Account & Sign In services affecting “all users,” which could make the service slow or unavailable. The App Store, AppleCare on Device, and Apple Music are also experiencing issues, the page indicates.
Like all major tech companies, Apple faces outages and other technical issues affecting its cloud services at times. However, today’s issues are likely more noticeable to end users because problems with iCloud can lead to account sign-in problems. This could cause your iPhone to ask for your Apple ID and password, and you could see other attempts to sign in to services with your Apple account information fail.
On Reddit, some folks also noted that they were unable to activate a new iPhone, which is likely related to these ongoing issues.
Given the recent increase in AI-driven cyberattacks, users across social media have already begun to speculate about the cause. In particular, users are expressing concerns about the Apple outage, as it arrives only days after technical failures at Microsoft led to its cloud systems that power flagship services like Outlook and Teams failing due to cascading software issues.
Similarly, a brief outage of Chase’s banking app this week garnered attention, as did an ongoing partial outage (at the time of publication) of the National Security Agency’s internal classified network, both of which were blamed on software glitches.
So far, none of the outages in the past week have been attributed to cyberattacks, but rather to technical problems.
Apple responded to TechCrunch’s request for comment (which it initially gave to Bloomberg), noting that the outages were caused by an internal update and not a cyberattack. “Many of our systems are back up and running after an internal update caused some outages this morning. We’re working to get the remaining systems online as soon as possible,” the statement read.
After the initial outages were reported, Apple’s status page was updated to include other outages impacting Apple School Manager, iCloud Mail, iCloud Web Apps (iCloud.com), Schoolwork, and the Volume Purchase Program.
This article was updated after publication with the status of the outages.

Google to keep Gemini chats for two months, up from 72 hours, even with history off
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
18:48:23 UTC
03:48:23 KST

Google is extending how long it keeps temporary Gemini chats and conversations made with chat history turned off to around two months, saying the change aims to improve safety and security as AI systems become more capable.
The change, which will roll out globally in the Gemini app over the coming weeks, marks a significant increase from the previous 72-hour retention period mentioned in Google’s Gemini Apps Privacy Hub, according to an archived version from November 12.
OpenAI, by comparison, says it may keep temporary chats in ChatGPT for up to 30 days, whereas Anthropic retains incognito chats in Claude for 30 days by default. However, companies may keep some data longer for legal or safety reasons, or to investigate policy violations.
Google did not answer questions about what prompted the change and whether it would affect how conversations are accessed or reviewed, instead pointing to a blog post that says chats are kept longer to respond to users, process feedback, and protect its services and users. Its updated Gemini Apps Privacy Hub also cites potential system failures as a reason for retaining chats and says temporary conversations are not used to train AI models.
The longer retention period comes as Google introduces separate settings for Gemini’s chat history and AI training. Until now, both were tied to a single “Keep Activity” setting. Users will now be able to keep their conversations in their chat history without using them to train Google’s generative AI models.
Google says both new settings will be turned off by default for users who previously disabled Keep Activity. For those who had Keep Activity turned on, their chat history will remain enabled. They can, though, decide separately whether Google can use their conversations to train AI models.

Chorizo Cornbread Stuffing
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
18:35:32 UTC
03:35:32 KST

Chorizo Cornbread Stuffing
A savory cornbread base stuffing with Mexican chorizo, softened lightly browned onions, tender vegetables, and warm spices, baked until golden and lightly crisp on top.
Serves6 to 8
Prep20 minutes
Cook50 minutes to 55 minutes
Why You’ll Love Chorizo Cornbread Stuffing
- A fun twist on a holiday classic. This stuffing brings bold Mexican-inspired flavors to the holiday table while keeping everything you love about traditional stuffing.
- The texture is irresistible. The stuffing bakes up soft and tender in the center with lightly golden crisp edges.
- It’s easy to make ahead. The cornbread can be toasted in advance, making assembly a breeze when you’re preparing a big holiday meal.
Chorizo was always a staple in our Mexican American household. Whether it was tucked into a warm tortilla for breakfast or cooked alongside potatoes, it was one of those ingredients that always brought so much flavor to the table. So when it came to putting my own spin on a classic Thanksgiving stuffing, adding Mexican chorizo just made sense.
This chorizo cornbread stuffing brings together slightly sweet, tender cornbread with smoky chorizo, softened vegetables, and warm spices. The chorizo adds the best smoky, salty, and spicy bite, while the cornbread soaks up all those delicious flavors and bakes until golden and lightly crisp on top (my favorite part!). It’s a fun twist on a classic that deserves a spot on your holiday table, but it also makes a wonderful weeknight side dish whenever you’re craving something smoky and slightly sweet. Truly, it’s the best of all the worlds combined into one baking dish.
Straight from Our Recipe Tester
“This recipe has me convinced that chorizo was destined for cornbread stuffing. The heavily spiced, super-savory sausage is the perfect complement to sweet cornbread. I used a tray of cornbread from the grocery store bakery and it was such an easy shortcut I’ll definitely be repeating.” —Rachel, October 2026
Why You Should Trust Me
As a Mexican American recipe developer with over a decade of experience in professional kitchens and food media, I love finding ways to bring the flavors I grew up with into classic American dishes. This chorizo cornbread stuffing combines my love for traditional holiday comfort food with the bold, savory flavors of Mexican chorizo.
Key Ingredients in Chorizo Cornbread Stuffing
- Cornbread: Creates a slightly sweet, tender base that absorbs the savory flavors while developing lightly crisp edges as it bakes.
- Mexican chorizo: Adds a rich, smoky, slightly spicy flavor while the rendered fat seasons the vegetables and cornbread.
- Jalapeño: Adds a gentle kick of heat that balances the sweetness of the cornbread.
- Eggs: Bind the stuffing together, helping it hold its shape.
How to Make Chorizo Cornbread Stuffing
- Toast the cornbread. Bake the diced cornbread until lightly browned and dried.
- Cook the chorizo. Brown the chorizo in a large skillet, breaking into small pieces.
- Cook the vegetables. Add the onion, carrot, celery, jalapeño, and salt. Cook until softened, then stir in the garlic and spices.
- Combine the stuffing. Stir the toasted cornbread, followed by the whisked broth, eggs, and melted butter.
- Bake the stuffing. Transfer to a greased baking dish, top with cheese if using, and bake until golden-brown and set. Garnish with cilantro and freshly ground black pepper.
Helpful Swaps
- Store-bought or homemade cornbread both work well in this recipe. Just make sure it’s thoroughly dried out before assembling the stuffing.
- Swap pepper Jack for Monterey Jack if you want a milder, creamier finish, if using cheese.
Chorizo Cornbread Stuffing Recipe
A savory cornbread base stuffing with Mexican chorizo, softened lightly browned onions, tender vegetables, and warm spices, baked until golden and lightly crisp on top.
Prep time 20 minutes
Cook time 50 minutes to 55 minutes
Serves 6 to 8
Nutritional Info
Ingredients
- Cooking spray
- 6 cups diced store-bought or homemade cornbread (18 ounces)
- 1 pound Mexican chorizo, casings removed
- 1 medium yellow onion, small dice (1 1/4 cups)
- 1 medium carrot, peeled and small dice (1/2 cups)
- 1 medium stalk celery, small dice (1/2 cup)
- 1 medium jalapeño pepper, stemmed, seeded, and small dice (1/4 cup)
- 2 cloves garlic, minced
- 1/2 teaspoon kosher salt
- 1/2 teaspoon smoked paprika
- 1/4 teaspoon freshly ground black pepper, plus more for garnish
- 1 1/2 cups low-sodium chicken or vegetable broth
- 2 large eggs
- 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted
- 4 ounces pepper Jack or Monterey Jack cheese, shredded (1 cup, optional)
- Coarsely chopped fresh cilantro, for garnish (optional)
Instructions
- Heat the oven to 350ºF. Coat a 9x13-inch baking dish with cooking spray.
- Arrange 6 cups diced cornbread in an even layer on a rimmed baking sheet. Bake, stirring halfway through, until lightly brown, toasted, and dried out, 15 to 20 minutes total. Let cool slightly.
- Heat a large 12-inch skillet over medium heat. Add 1 pound Mexican chorizo and cook, breaking it up into small pieces and stirring occasionally, until browned and cooked through, 6 to 8 minutes.
- Stir in 1 small-diced medium yellow onion, 1 peeled and small-diced medium carrot, 1 small-diced medium celery stalk, 1 small-diced jalapeño pepper, and 1/2 teaspoon kosher salt. Cook until the vegetables are softened and the onion begins to brown, 8 to 10 minutes. Stir in 2 minced garlic cloves and cook until fragrant, about 40 seconds. Turn off the heat.
- Stir in 1/2 teaspoon smoked paprika and 1/4 teaspoon black pepper. Stir in the cornbread; it's OK if it begins to break or crumble up.
- Whisk 1 1/2 cups low-sodium chicken broth, 2 large eggs, and 2 tablespoons melted unsalted butter together in a medium bowl. Pour evenly over the cornbread mixture. Stir gently until combined and the broth mixture is absorbed.
- Transfer to the baking dish and arrange into an even layer. Sprinkle with 4 ounces shredded pepper Jack cheese if using.
- Bake until heated through and lightly browned on top, 20 to 25 minutes. Garnish with more black pepper and chopped fresh cilantro if desired.
Recipe Notes
Make ahead: The cornbread can be toasted up to 1 day ahead, cooled, and stored in an airtight container at room temperature.
Storage: Refrigerate leftover stuffing in an airtight container for up to 4 days.

Purism Launches Librem 5 v2, a RISC-V Phone to Empower People
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
18:34:10 UTC
03:34:10 KST

Powered by open-source software and designed to put users in control, the Librem 5 v2 offers an alternative to Big Tech’s smartphone ecosystems.
San Francisco, Calif. (Nov 17th, 2026) – Purism, a Social Purpose Corporation dedicated to privacy, security, and digital freedom, today announced its second smartphone crowdfunding campaign for the Librem 5 v2, the next generation of its flagship smartphone.
Powered by an eight-core RISC-V processor and running PureOS, the Librem 5 v2 offers an alternative to Android and iOS—grounded in free software, open standards, and user ownership.
The campaign is now live at https://puri.sm/librem-5-v2-crowdfunding-campaign inviting privacy advocates, open-source enthusiasts, and consumers seeking digital independence to help bring the Librem 5 v2 to market.
“The Librem 5 v2 is the answer we all have been waiting for,” said Todd Weaver, Purism Founder & CEO. “A smartphone that works for you, not for the companies collecting your data. No Android, no iOS, no mandatory Big Tech account. A smart phone designed to put you in control of your digital life.”
The move to RISC-V is central to Purism’s long-term effort to build a more open and auditable mobile platform. The company says the architecture offers a path toward greater independence from proprietary processor designs. Bringing the platform to a fully functioning phone will require substantial engineering, including work on graphics, peripherals, power management, and app compatibility.
“RISC-V is the future, and Librem 5 v2 is the perfect product to bring it to a mainstream audience,” said CTO Jonathon Hall. “Purism’s Librem 5 v2, built on the open RISC-V architecture and PureOS, represents a rare opportunity to give users the privacy, transparency, and control they deserve by rethinking the mobile platform from the processor up.”
The Librem 5 v2 crowdfunding campaign is underway. The phone-only reward is listed at $1,599; campaign options also include payment plans and a phone-and-cellular-service bundle. Purism anticipates delivery approximately 14 to 15 months after the campaign ends. Because development is ongoing, the company notes that campaign rewards and timelines are not guaranteed.
“Technology should serve people—not turn them into products,” said Weaver. “The Librem 5 v2 is our next step toward a mobile future built around ownership, transparency, and digital freedom.”
Key features include:
Purism first entered the smartphone market through crowdfunding for the original Librem 5, demonstrating demand for an alternative to Android and iOS.
The Librem 5 v2 builds on that foundation, bringing together Purism’s experience in independent hardware development, free software, and privacy-focused product design.
Through its new crowdfunding campaign, Purism is inviting customers and supporters to help bring the next generation of the Librem 5 to market.
“My vision at the outset was to create technologies that respect the people using them,” Weaver said. “It is why we formed as a Social Purpose Corporation, to put individual digital rights above all else.” Weaver shares.
Purism’s commitment goes beyond product development.
The company has authored a Digital Bill of Rights outlining fundamental principles for digital freedom, including the right to protect personal data, verify software, change providers, and avoid unwanted tracking.
These principles are central to Purism’s identity as a Social Purpose Corporation, where its mission to advance users’ digital rights is embedded in its articles of incorporation.
Take control of your digital future. Back Librem 5 v2 today.
Visit https://puri.sm/librem-5-v2-crowdfunding-campaign to support the campaign.
Purism is a social purpose company that develops devices and services with a focus on freedom, privacy, and security.
Media contact:
Purism Public Relations
pr@puri.sm

Meta is testing a new standalone messaging app called Direct
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
18:33:02 UTC
03:33:02 KST

Meta announced Direct, a new messaging app from Instagram that it’s currently testing in Turkey and Thailand on iOS and Android. Direct is essentially Instagram’s direct messaging without Instagram’s other features. Meta says your Instagram chats will sync across both apps if you login with the same account. You can also sign up with an email or phone number, but Meta says if you use Direct without an Instagram account, “only your contacts can start a chat with you or add you to a group.”
Meta is testing a new standalone messaging app called Direct
Direct lets you access Instagram chats in a dedicated app, but it isn’t widely available yet.
This is far from Meta’s first messaging app — it also has Messenger, which requires a Facebook account to use, and WhatsApp, which Meta (then Facebook) acquired in 2014. Meta also recycled the “Direct” name, which it previously used for a Snapchat-like app it shut down several years ago.
The new Direct app has a UI similar to Instagram’s messaging tab, but with extra features for customizing chats and sending different types of media, including:
• Draw: Draw and sketch directly inside your chat threads.
• Notes: Catch quick updates from friends right at the top of your inbox.
• Stickers: Create custom stickers to express yourself and share with friends and family.
• Voice Effects: Add fun audio effects like crowd laughter or underwater sounds to your replies.
• Themes: Personalize your chat backgrounds with custom visual themes.
Meta says users have to be at least 13 years old to use Direct, and teens who log in with their Instagram account will have the same Teen Account messaging protections and restrictions on Direct.
