Phrack 73 Released
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
18:31:25 UTC
03:31:25 KST

Title : Introduction
|=-----------------------------------------------------------------------=|
|=---------------------=[     Introduction     ]=------------------------=|
|=-----------------------------------------------------------------------=|
|=---------------------=[     Phrack Staff     ]=------------------------=|
|=---------------------=[   [email protected]   ]=------------------------=|
|=-----------------------------------------------------------------------=|
|=---------------------=[  September 30, 2026  ]=------------------------=|
|=-----------------------------------------------------------------------=|
--[ Past ]
Last year marked the 40th anniversary of Phrack so we went big. We published
our anniversary launch release in print at Defcon 33, HOPE, WHY25 and some
additional variations at several conferences around the world including
AlligatorCon, B-Sides Canberra, Out of the Box, Datenspuren, DistrictCon,
OffensiveCon, Recon, and Toorcamp. We included a CTF with exploit challenges
and had puzzles and easter eggs included in the zine. We threw parties and
had talks at Defcon and WHY25 to celebrate this milestone.
More importantly, we got new research into people's hands. The point of the
relaunch was to get people reading, researching, writing, and contributing
again. We wanted the next person picking up Phrack to find something that
made them sit down at a computer and start pulling a system apart.
We owe a huge thanks to the authors, editors, artists, publishing partners,
donors, and staff who made it happen. Writing the paper, working through
revisions, putting the print editions together, paying the bills, and
getting boxes of zines to the right places all take time and effort. Most of
that work is invisible when you are holding the finished issue. We
appreciate everyone who did it.
And to the readers: thank you for giving a shit. Seeing someone get excited
about an article, connect it to something they are working on, or realize
that they could contribute something themselves makes the effort worthwhile.
That exchange is how this thing keeps going. The relaunch worked, and this
issue is the follow-through.
--[ Present ]
This is the third release curated by the new staff, and we are not slowing
down. This issue is larger than last year's, with 23 main papers and eight
additional articles in Line Noise. The subjects range from exploitation and
reverse engineering to machine learning, hardware, and the history and
culture that grew around this work.
We have container escapes through the vDSO, use-after-free exploitation, QNX
userland shellcode, Linux kernel research, and a Chromium renderer sandbox
escape through Skia. Elsewhere, the issue takes on Windows smart card
architecture, static taint analysis in Binary Ninja, and autonomous
underwater vehicles.  There is also work examining backdoors in machine
learning and what it means to trust an AI system. One paper takes us from
PhrackCTF to two WinFSP zero-days, which is a pretty good argument for
continuing to put challenges in a zine.
We also have four more articles in Phrack World History, including the story
of the infamous SSH1 CRC32 vulnerability featured in The Matrix Reloaded as
well as stories about blue boxing free phone calls, a confession of a hacker
who tried to backdoor Linux kernel in 2003, and an early discovery of
botnets for hire. Our Prophile features Halvar Flake, whose contributions to
reverse engineering and security research are legendary.
--[ Future ]
In 2026, we find ourselves in a time of rapid technological change. It is
hard to say what vulnerability research or red teaming will look like in
another five years. AI has moved well beyond completing a line of code into
agentic systems capable of analyzing source code and reverse engineering
binaries to find and exploit vulnerabilities.
We can be sure that the future will require us to continue to explore new
ideas, create new tools, and push the boundaries of what is possible in the
face of new challenges and opportunities created by the advancement of AI
and automation systems. As the effort required to put ideas into practice
falls, creativity and human intuition forged through experience is valued
even higher.
We hope that this release of Phrack will inspire new ideas, discussions, and
collaborations that will keep the human side of hacking a critical element
in shaping the future of technology and society. We will continue to do our
part to support the community and provide a platform for sharing knowledge
and ideas. We look forward to seeing what the future holds and we are
excited to be a part of it.
Read the papers, try the ideas, and challenge the conclusions. When you find
something worth sharing, write it up and send it in. We have another issue
to put together, and some of you should be in it!
--[ Greetz ]
To those who have supported us the past three years helping to restore
vitality to Phrack, we thank you.
Conferences who funded prints: Defcon, OffensiveCon, Recon, Unprompted[AU],
Unprompted[US], BSides Canberra, Alligatorcon, TenguCon
Sponsors & Major Donors - WatchTowr, Elltam, Verichains, BShield,
TrustedSec, Binarly, Trenchant, Praetorian (WWSUL), EFF, Blaze, EMX,
Mullvad, ToorCon, Hacker Initiative, Endrazine, Dug Song, Napster, halvar,
Horizon, Huku, Julio Auto, Gadi, and the rest of the people who stepped up with
smaller donations.
Artists - Ian Dash, pinguino, tainted, pepper, mar, xer0, abstrakt, ToscaKaiser,
h7, zir, Mick, p0rtL, netspooky, ackmage, digitalis, Tedsig42, and the many
others who submitted their work for consideration.
Reviewers - barbie, bsdaemon, C, chompie, danielcuthbert, devinc, grugq, joern,
jordan9001, K3, richinseattle, roddux, saber, skyper, SSHad0w, TMZ, zh4ck
All the authors past and present who are now bound to the history of phrack.
The unnamed ones who have helped us with logistics and carrying literal tons

Phrack 73 Released
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
18:31:25 UTC
03:31:25 KST

Title : Introduction
|=-----------------------------------------------------------------------=|
|=---------------------=[     Introduction     ]=------------------------=|
|=-----------------------------------------------------------------------=|
|=---------------------=[     Phrack Staff     ]=------------------------=|
|=---------------------=[   [email protected]   ]=------------------------=|
|=-----------------------------------------------------------------------=|
|=---------------------=[  September 30, 2026  ]=------------------------=|
|=-----------------------------------------------------------------------=|
--[ Past ]
Last year marked the 40th anniversary of Phrack so we went big. We published
our anniversary launch release in print at Defcon 33, HOPE, WHY25 and some
additional variations at several conferences around the world including
AlligatorCon, B-Sides Canberra, Out of the Box, Datenspuren, DistrictCon,
OffensiveCon, Recon, and Toorcamp. We included a CTF with exploit challenges
and had puzzles and easter eggs included in the zine. We threw parties and
had talks at Defcon and WHY25 to celebrate this milestone.
More importantly, we got new research into people's hands. The point of the
relaunch was to get people reading, researching, writing, and contributing
again. We wanted the next person picking up Phrack to find something that
made them sit down at a computer and start pulling a system apart.
We owe a huge thanks to the authors, editors, artists, publishing partners,
donors, and staff who made it happen. Writing the paper, working through
revisions, putting the print editions together, paying the bills, and
getting boxes of zines to the right places all take time and effort. Most of
that work is invisible when you are holding the finished issue. We
appreciate everyone who did it.
And to the readers: thank you for giving a shit. Seeing someone get excited
about an article, connect it to something they are working on, or realize
that they could contribute something themselves makes the effort worthwhile.
That exchange is how this thing keeps going. The relaunch worked, and this
issue is the follow-through.
--[ Present ]
This is the third release curated by the new staff, and we are not slowing
down. This issue is larger than last year's, with 23 main papers and eight
additional articles in Line Noise. The subjects range from exploitation and
reverse engineering to machine learning, hardware, and the history and
culture that grew around this work.
We have container escapes through the vDSO, use-after-free exploitation, QNX
userland shellcode, Linux kernel research, and a Chromium renderer sandbox
escape through Skia. Elsewhere, the issue takes on Windows smart card
architecture, static taint analysis in Binary Ninja, and autonomous
underwater vehicles.  There is also work examining backdoors in machine
learning and what it means to trust an AI system. One paper takes us from
PhrackCTF to two WinFSP zero-days, which is a pretty good argument for
continuing to put challenges in a zine.
We also have four more articles in Phrack World History, including the story
of the infamous SSH1 CRC32 vulnerability featured in The Matrix Reloaded as
well as stories about blue boxing free phone calls, a confession of a hacker
who tried to backdoor Linux kernel in 2003, and an early discovery of
botnets for hire. Our Prophile features Halvar Flake, whose contributions to
reverse engineering and security research are legendary.
--[ Future ]
In 2026, we find ourselves in a time of rapid technological change. It is
hard to say what vulnerability research or red teaming will look like in
another five years. AI has moved well beyond completing a line of code into
agentic systems capable of analyzing source code and reverse engineering
binaries to find and exploit vulnerabilities.
We can be sure that the future will require us to continue to explore new
ideas, create new tools, and push the boundaries of what is possible in the
face of new challenges and opportunities created by the advancement of AI
and automation systems. As the effort required to put ideas into practice
falls, creativity and human intuition forged through experience is valued
even higher.
We hope that this release of Phrack will inspire new ideas, discussions, and
collaborations that will keep the human side of hacking a critical element
in shaping the future of technology and society. We will continue to do our
part to support the community and provide a platform for sharing knowledge
and ideas. We look forward to seeing what the future holds and we are
excited to be a part of it.
Read the papers, try the ideas, and challenge the conclusions. When you find
something worth sharing, write it up and send it in. We have another issue
to put together, and some of you should be in it!
--[ Greetz ]
To those who have supported us the past three years helping to restore
vitality to Phrack, we thank you.
Conferences who funded prints: Defcon, OffensiveCon, Recon, Unprompted[AU],
Unprompted[US], BSides Canberra, Alligatorcon, TenguCon
Sponsors & Major Donors - WatchTowr, Elltam, Verichains, BShield,

Mullvad, ToorCon, Hacker Initiative, Endrazine, Dug Song, Napster, halvar,
Horizon, Huku, Julio Auto, Gadi, and the rest of the people who stepped up with
smaller donations.

Amflow’s new urban e-bike series is deliciously overpowered for flat city streets
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
18:30:03 UTC
03:30:03 KST

DJI spinoff Amflow has spent the last couple of years shaking up the electric mountain bike (eMTB) market with ridiculously high-torque, lightweight models. Today, it’s bringing that same overengineered energy to pavement with the UE Series, its first urban commuter line.
Amflow’s new urban e-bike series is deliciously overpowered for flat city streets
After shaking up the mountain bike world, DJI spinoff Amflow is hitting city streets with the UE Carbon and UE Carbon Pro — bringing 125Nm of torque, 82 miles of range, and Find My tracking — starting at $3,500.
The series consists of two models: the UE Carbon and UE Carbon Pro. Both pack 125Nm of torque, 1,100W of peak power, and an 8x max assist ratio capable of climbing 30 percent grades — all while operating quietly under 45dBA (quieter than a household refrigerator). A swappable 600Wh battery provides up to 132km (about 82 miles) of range and fast-charges from zero to 100 percent in 2 hours and 23 minutes.
While most city e-bikes deliver their power modestly, Amflow is strapping a mountain-bike-grade motor onto a 20.9 kg (46 pounds) upright step-through frame — and pricing it slightly below its flagship trail bikes. The UE Carbon starts at €3,099 (about $3,500 USD), while the Pro will sell for €3,499 ($3,900 USD).
But this isn’t just another commuter bike. With its proprietary Avinox drivetrain, Amflow has been ahead of the curve in blending consumer gadget UX — features like wireless phone charging, Apple Watch proximity unlock, Find My integration, and camera controls — with serious hardware specs. That’s one of the reasons why The Verge’s own Thomas Ricker has been so bullish on the brand for the past few years.
With 125Nm of max torque and 1,100W of peak power, the Avinox M2 in the UE Series is slightly more dialed back from Amflow’s 150Nm flagship eMTBs. That said, 125Nm is still massive overkill for flat city streets — but it won’t hurt to have it when encountering any grades over 30 percent. A new Auto Boost mode automatically dumps extra power when starting at a traffic light or hitting a steep hill. And traction control will help prevent any spin-outs on wet asphalt.
The 600Wh removable battery is good for up 82 miles in Eco mode — and likely less than that while relying on higher levels of assist. It’s worth noting that the 1,100W peak power and 8x assist ratios will likely be software-capped depending on where you live and what regulations allow.
As a brand within the larger DJI ecosystem, Amflow loves to integrate a lot of tech into its bikes. A 1.4 inch touchscreen is integrated into the handlebars alongside a 15W wireless phone charger — plus a 20W USB-C charger, if you prefer. The cockpit also features plenty of Apple tie-ins, like the aforementioned Find My tracker and Apple Watch proximity unlocking. The UE can also pair with a heart rate monitor, allowing riders to set a target heart rate zone that will dictate how much pedal assist the bike provides. And, of course, there’s direct recording controls for DJI Osmo cameras, in case you like to film your rides.
Additional security is provided by a built-in 10mm hardened steel wheel lock keyed to match the removable battery. And if the bike is paired with “Lost Mode” in the Amflow app, the user can remotely brick the drive system if the bike ever gets stolen.
There’s plenty of commuter-friendly touches, like a 65mm suspension fork, suspension dropper seatpost, and heavy-duty racks rated for 20kg (44 pounds) on the front and 27kg (60 pounds) on the rear.
The bikes are on sale globally starting today via Amflow’s website and retail partners. The company says a smaller XS frame size is coming in 2027 for shorter riders.
Images by Amflow

MAI Code 2 and Code 2 Flash
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
18:25:13 UTC
03:25:13 KST

MAI Cyber 2 Flash: Leading security. Lower cost
MAI Code 2 and MAI Code 2 Flash: two new coding models built for the everyday enterprise coding tasks, delivering leading performance at a fraction of the cost of competing models.
MAI Code 2 is at the frontier of price-performance for common enterprise coding tasks and excels in taking code to production, deep debugging, multi-file implementation, repo wide reasoning, and UI. It does all of this while also delivering changes code maintainers would merge, at a fraction of frontier cost.
Meanwhile, MAI Code 2 Flash is designed for high-volume work such as code explanations, targeted edits, routine fixes, and rapid iterations, while bringing quality at a much lower cost.
To deliver the best price-performance, we’ve built these models to prioritize the coding work our customers do every day. They’re explicitly not yet tuned for harder problems, like fundamental science research and very long horizon agentic work.
Averaged over four widely used benchmarks that target the everyday enterprise coding tasks we see in GitHub Copilot (DeepSWE 1.1, Terminal Bench 2.1, SWE-Atlas and FrontierCode v1.1), MAI Code 2 gives you 98% of the performance of GPT 6.1 Sol at 40% of the cost, and 96% of Claude Opus 5.5’s performance at just 5% of the cost.1
MAI Code 2 is also tuned for producing great dashboards and visualizations, and excels at visual coding. In blind frontend app evaluations, reviewers preferred its results over GPT-6 Sol 64% of the time.2
In addition, MAI Code 2 Flash offers comparable performance to GPT-6 Luna and Claude Haiku 5.5 at 65% and 20% of the cost, across three coding benchmarks measuring day-to-day, high-volume routine coding tasks.3
MAI Code 2 is also becoming the workhorse behind Microsoft products. From Code 2 Flash we further optimized a single model, MAI Work Flash, to serve multiple Microsoft products across M365 and Fabric IQ. 
Excel: Our model is on par for accuracy and token efficiency with GPT 6 Luna across key internal Excel customer-scenario benchmarks.
PowerPoint: On key production benchmark scenarios that measure frequent PowerPoint tasks, our model performs on par with GPT 6 Luna in both quality and token efficiency.
Word: Our model performs similar to GPT 6.1 sol (while being significantly smaller) on 5 key internal benchmarks using ~42% fewer input tokens per call than GPT6.1 Sol in production.
Teams: Our model beats GPT 6 Luna at tracking decisions, filling knowledge gaps, and adapting custom instructions using up to 28% fewer tokens on instruction following and tool calling.
FabricIQ: In offline evaluation runs our MAI model outperforms GPT-5.6-sol, GPT-6-astra, and Opus-5.5 at DAX query generation, the core capability behind “Chat with your Data” for Power BI semantic models.
Get started
MAI Code 2 and Code 2 Flash are available in GitHub Copilot, and Code 2 Flash is in limited-access preview in Microsoft Foundry. Soon, we’ll be releasing it on Open Router, Vercel, Hermes, Open Code, and Kilo.
Learn more about customizing MAI Code 2 on our Custom Models page.
About these results
Results reflect evaluations on specific coding tasks and configurations. Performance and cost comparisons vary by workload, model configuration, reasoning effort, and pricing assumptions.

====================================================================================================

Not Everything Needs an LLM
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
18:16:18 UTC
03:16:18 KST

LLMs love to yap. Ask a simple question and you get a few hundred words of "it depends." It's annoying as hell.
This is why we love decision models like Jev for simpler tasks. You ask a structured question and get a typed answer with a probability, which looks something like this:
- Noul. Are hedgehogs mammals? → Yes, 0.99
- Choice. Which animal family do hedgehogs belong to? → Mammal, 0.99
- Score. What is the current conservation status of hedgehogs? → Least Concern, 0.77
Whereas LLMs return free-form and overly confident prose, decision models make model uncertainty explicit. In a calibrated model, answers that score 0.8 are right about 80% of the time. That means a score of 0.5 is a coin flip.
We've been experimenting with them at PostHog and several use cases have made it to production. This is everything we've learned about using decision models so far: when to use them, how to use them, and mistakes to avoid.
1. Use them to gate expensive LLM calls
Decision models are up to 444.6x cheaper and 193.6x faster than frontier models,1 so you can use them to check whether you even need to make an expensive LLM call at all.
We do this in a pipeline that scrubs personal info in Replay Vision, our product that watches and summarizes user session recordings for you. Here's how it works:
- Each session summary goes through 2 yes/no questions from a decision model. Using narrow questions like this cuts false flags to about a tenth of what a single question produced. 
  - 1. “Does this name a real person?”
  - 2. “Does this show contact details or an ID?”
- If either scores 0.4 or above, Gemini rewrites the summary without the personal info and it gets checked again.
- Otherwise, it's clean and passes through the gate. In production, 98.7% skip the LLM call entirely.
Without the decision model, we'd have to keep fussing with prompts and regexes that miss things, or sending every summary to Gemini to catch the 1% that need it.
But remember they make mistakes
A probability score of 0.85 still means ~150 wrong answers per 1,000 checks, so plan ahead for false positives and false negatives.
In the PII example, a false positive only costs us an extra LLM call, whereas a false negative stores personal data as clean. That's why we set the threshold low at 0.4.
When false positives are the costlier mistake, use higher thresholds. For example, we recently updated the PostHog in Slack bot, so it can answer questions without being tagged. To avoid being annoying, it checks every message in a channel to see (1) whether it's asking for information, and (2) whether PostHog could answer it. Only messages that pass both at 0.85+ go through.
Takeaway: Use decision models to filter jobs before they hit an expensive LLM call, and set the threshold based on which mistake costs you more.
2. Use them as model routers
Our PostHog in Slack bot also uses a decision model as a model router.
When the bot gets tagged, it first checks whether the message asked for a specific model. If not, one choice question picks from the list:
“Pick the cheapest option that will most likely finish the task correctly on the first try. A failed run costs more than a smarter option.”
Each option in the list carries a short note on what that model is good for. GPT Luna is described as “a light and very cheap model. Good for short answers, lookups, summaries and very small edits.” The decision model then scores the request against each option’s text.
But don't assume they're interchangeable
Different tasks, models, and providers all have different thresholds.
We were experimenting with using them to auto-merge similar logs in Error Tracking and found that thresholds changed between v1 and v2 of the same model. We had to make its thresholds configurable per PostHog project and keep the judge in shadow mode while recalibrating based on hand-labeled data.
This is one of the reasons why Fernando on our growth team pushed for a model-agnostic interface early. We now have an internal AI gateway for swapping and testing models, including self-hosted ones.
Takeaway: Use a choice question to route free-form LLM requests to the cheapest model that can handle it. Keep in mind that thresholds don't carry over, so set them per task and per model.
3. Use them for face-value checks
If a question requires more context or chains of reasoning, it's better to use an LLM. However, you can still use a decision model if you have a way to flatten, or pre-process the input, so it's checkable at face-value.
In Replay Vision, when a user searches for a theme like "onboarding friction," there's a chance for the query to return a few unexpected results because of the way embedding distance works.
A decision model can’t "Rank the top 10 sessions that contain summaries that match the user's search query." It’s too complicated.
Instead, we use the embeddings first to derive an initial list of session recordings to pass to the decision model. Then, the only task left for the decision model is to look at how closely each result in the list matches the user's original search term:
"Does this summary describe a session that matches what the search looks for?"
Results are then reranked by that probability. On 20 test queries, this improved ranking quality by about 25%.
But avoid multi-step reasoning
We've tried using a decision model for multi-step reasoning before. Before embeddings were ready in Replay Vision, we tried ranking a "Highlights" feed of sessions worth a human's time. The questions were:
- Is this session worth watching?
- If so, why is it worth watching? (With choices like “error on screen,” “slow or stuck,” “user confused”, “user gave up", etc.)
Processing these two questions in order required an hourly job that sent large requests – up to 32 sessions and 12-38k tokens each – which flooded the model server and blocked other products' calls for hours.
So yes, technically, you can chain decision models into multi-step judgments, but it's not operationally worth it. Leave that to LLMs, or do the heavy lifting before the decision model sees the data like in the embedding search example above.
Takeaway: Decision models judge what's in front of them in a single pass. Leave multi-step reasoning to LLMs.
4. Use them in place of overqualified LLMs
Decision models can take any job where an LLM is overkill, either because it's too slow or too expensive.
One example is our smarter chart picker for SQL queries. PostHog used to pick a visualization using deterministic rules like:
- A date column, a number column and multiple rows → Line chart
- Two or more text columns plus a number → Heatmap
- Any number column → Bar chart
Now, a decision model looks at both the SQL itself if your org opts in, and the first three rows of the results to suggest a better chart.
The example below is based on a simple query about pageviews by browser. The original rules picked a basic aggregate line chart; with Jev, it chose a bar chart split by browser:
But keep LLMs for open-ended jobs
Anything more open-ended should still go to an LLM.
Still, out of curiosity, we tried Jev on ranking our internal PR review agent's findings with five noul questions that roughly asked if the change is worth a developer's attention. The results were inconclusive with an AUC of 0.52.
If Jev were an LLM, we’d solve this by sending more context, but Jev can’t handle large requests. Its long-context mode is also slower and less accurate.2 Typical decision models just aren’t built for this kind of reasoning.
Hybrid decision models like Jeeves can help bridge this gap, though. Developed by our in-house AI research team, Jeeves is a decision model that reasons before deciding when it’s unsure.
It's now our default for any decision call at PostHog that doesn't name a model, and there are similar variations by other developers, like Kev.
Takeaway: Use decision models as evaluators for inputs that are too simple for a language model, yet too fuzzy for a regex. Anything more complex is still too much to hand over to a decision model. For now, at least.
Words by Jina Yoon, who expects this article to be obsolete in two months.

- We made experiment queries 4x faster using precomputation – Juraj Majerik

- Technical Customer Success Manager - EMEA
- Technical Customer Success Manager - Americas
- AI Research Engineer
- Animator & Motion Graphics Designer
- Product Manager, Observability (ex-founder or ex-product engineer)
- Site Reliability Engineer (US Pacific Time)
According to TypeSafe AI.
Jev takes 64k tokens per request and 32k per question by default. And even though TypeSafe added an opt-in header that raises both to 262k, they still warn that the long-context mode is slower and less accurate. Source: TypeSafe Docs

====================================================================================================

As decision model use skyrockets, is customization is the next frontier?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
18:10:00 UTC
03:10:00 KST

In October, more than one in four requests on Vercel’s AI model router went to decision models, not LLMs — an enormous share of work for a kind of computerized intelligence that was invented only a month earlier.
Decision models are LLMs that don’t produce output tokens; instead, they’re tuned to use their general intelligence to quickly and cheaply decide between preset options, while registering its level of confidence in the choice. They’re useful in production environments, where fast decisions can be more important than the reasoning provided by a slower and more expensive frontier model.
TypeSafe became a $7.5 billion company after releasing its Jev family of models, which continues to dominate usage (at least where we can see metrics from services like OpenRouter, which handled more than 1.5 billion queries to TypeSafe’s models last week).
However, it’s easy to make a decision model, and dozens were released in the wake of Jev. One Amazon engineer made one in a weekend, and soon every hyperscaler had its own option. On Tuesday, Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella said that his company is rolling out its decision model, Microsoft-Decision-1.1, for wide use on Azure immediately.
The action is happening on both ends of the ecosystem: Sqwish, a U.K.-based AI startup behind a decision model called kettei fat, announced a $3 million pre-seed round today. Co-founders Ushnish Sengupta and Federica Freddi, who met as Cambridge grad students, had been developing small LLMs when Jev arrived on the scene, and they realized their architectures could be repurposed as a powerful decision model.
Data appears to be the key to a successful model in this category — TypeSafe CEO Diogo Almeida told TechCrunch that the hardest part of building Jev was finely calibrating its judgement using statistically-adjusted synthetic data. Sqwish, too, has been working with synthetic data, but its unique offering is built-in post-training tools that use a customer’s own data to improve the model’s judgement.
“What makes us not a commodity, I think, is the fact that we are offering our customers the same power…they can keep uploading their production data, and just the same way that we trained our models, their models can also keep getting better at the tasks that they care about,” CEO Ushnish Sengupta told TechCrunch.
Attempting to rank decision models is difficult; Sqwish’s “kettei fat” model has performed well on some benchmarks, but most builders of the technology say that the real proof is in the pudding: It’s generally simple for developers to sub in one model for another and see how it does in a specific context.
“The benchmark is more of an invitation to try the model out, and then the continual tuning is the reason why they should stick to us,” Sengupta said.
The Microsoft-Decision-1.1 model is also the result of the company carefully balancing across cost, latency, intelligence and calibration. Achint Srivastava, the engineering VP who led the project, agreed that too much focus on benchmarks is a mistake.
“What we see is that ‘benchmaxed’ models fall flat under real enterprise usage very quickly,” Srivastava wrote in an email. “Our testing underscores just how challenging it is to deliver accuracy, reliability, speed and cost efficiency in a single model. Microsoft-Decision-1.1 and Jev are among the few that perform strongly across all four dimensions, even when evaluated on ‘held out’ or unseen benchmarks reflective of real use cases.”
For startups like Sqwish, competition with hyperscalers and frontier labs is an opportunity. Freddi predicts that future systems will see so-called System One intelligence, like decision models, working in concert with System Two intelligence, like frontier LLMs.
“Even in that future, System One is maybe a $10 billion, $50 billion [opportunity], and OpenAI is doing this IPO with you know $1 trillion, $2 trillion market value in mind,” she told TechCrunch. “This is all we care about; whereas for them, this is just a side project.”

====================================================================================================

Pedestal AI raises $4.7 million to agentify the supply chain
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
18:04:16 UTC
03:04:16 KST

Delivery supply chains are the lifeblood of contemporary businesses — and a new startup is betting that purpose-built AI agents can make them run a lot more smoothly.
On Tuesday, Pedestal AI emerged from stealth with $4.7 million in seed funding. The company’s goal is to use agentic AI to make supply chains faster and more reliable — a plan that’s already brought them together with Fortune 500 companies and some of the largest shipping firms in the world.
The seed round was led by Eclipse, with participation from RRE, Converge VC, Timescale Ventures, and VitalStage. Pedestal says the funding will be used to expand its product and engineering time, grow the marketing organization, and support additional deployments.
In practice, much of Pedestal’s work is about removing friction from the shipping process — with agentic technology used as a means to that end. In one case, the company reduced missed deliveries for a major furniture company by 20% by deploying agents to take on some of the work that drivers were supposed to do. The agentic system they built would call customers 30 minutes before a delivery and collect any special instructions; if the call didn’t go through, a text would be sent from a separate system. Along the way, the deployment collected a wealth of data about friction points in the process, which could be used to optimize the system even further.
CEO Raj Aggarwal says contemporary AI systems are particularly useful for dealing with that data. “The challenge is that the data is messy and it’s constantly changing,” Aggarwal told TechCrunch. “It’s disparate because it sits in so many legacy systems. That’s what makes it hard.”
Supply chain AI has become a particularly hot space in recent months as alumni from autonomous driving teams look to launch their own startups. The supply chain startup Atomic, launched by a team of Tesla alumni, came out of stealth in 2025 with an emphasis on streamlining inventory. Another venture called Loop was launched by Uber alums to focus on predictive analytics.
As Aggarwal sees it, deliveries are particularly well-suited to training reinforcement learning systems. Because of their high volumes and easily measured outcomes, it’s easy to fine-tune contemporary AI systems toward what’s working and away from what isn’t.
Perhaps most importantly, it’s the kind of problem that can have an immediate effect on a client’s bottom line, making it easy to justify the expense.
“If you can get it right,” he told TechCrunch, “you can have really big impact.”

Jensen Huang Says He'd Be 'Honored' to Return As Nvidia CEO (npr.org) 49
			
		 	
				In a post on X today, former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang says he would be "honored" to return to the company if its board gives the approval. "I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at NVIDIA, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life," he says. "I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board's independent investigation."
Huang resigned from the company last week after acknowledging multiple affairs, including one with a women whom U.S. intelligence officials suspect of being a Chinese spy. NPR reports:  His comments come as some in Silicon Valley have tried to argue that Huang was pushed out of the company too quickly, and circulated a petition to "stand with Jensen."
"We don't want rushes to judgment. We don't want political pressure from the left or the right to force a board into action before we've even taken the time to turn over the cards and learn what was done," said Brad Gerstner, the founder and CEO of Altimeter Capital, an investor in Nvidia, at Semafor's Silicon Valley and the World conference on Monday.  The report notes that Huang and Nvidia's general counsel Tim Teter will answer questions behind closed doors before three congressional committees on December 1 and 2.

YouTube found a new way to inflate view counts
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:50:22 UTC
02:50:22 KST

YouTube’s view counts will now include the number of people that it estimates are watching a video together on TV. In an update on Monday, the platform says it’s bringing “co-views” to public view counts across long-form videos, Shorts, and live content to provide a more “complete picture” of how people are using its platform.
YouTube found a new way to inflate view counts
YouTube view counts will now include estimates of how many people are watching a video together on TV.
YouTube, which has become even more popular on TVs than phones, also says the change will help it align with “TV industry standards,” which similarly record views by estimating the number of people watching shows. The company says it doesn’t use devices, like cameras or microphones, to estimate how many people are watching a video on TV. Instead, it estimates co-views using a “privacy safe statistical model” that takes into account “demographic patterns and viewing times.”
This marks yet another update to YouTube’s view count system. In August, the platform began counting a view as soon as a video starts playing. The company said at the time that creators will notice their view counts increasing faster as a result of this change, and it seems like adding co-views could boost these numbers as well.
YouTube notes that it will only apply co-views to new views on TV going forward, and that they won’t appear retroactively. Co-views also won’t affect a creator’s ad revenue, YouTube Partner Program eligibility, or revenue.

iCloud, App Store, and other Apple services hit by ongoing outage

2026-11-17

02:49:01 KST

Apple services are having a rough Tuesday. iCloud, the App Store, and other services are experiencing an apparent outage, with users reporting connection failures and trouble signing in.
Downdetector is showing reports across multiple Apple services, even as Apple’s System Status page continues to list them as available.
Affected services include the App Store, iCloud, Apple Pay, Apple Support, iPhone Activation, and more.
Apple confirms a number of services experiencing issues on its general and developer status pages.
Apple retail stores and corporate offices are also reportedly experiencing outages.
Users are reporting unexpected iCloud sign-outs on iPhone and Mac, followed by verification failures or unknown errors when trying to sign back in.
Others say the App Store won’t load or download apps, while Apple Music and device activation are also running into problems. Developers have separately reported trouble accessing App Store Connect.
Top comment by DEMinSoCal
I noticed it when I was trying to setup an old iPad and it gave an error that it couldn't connect to activation servers, then a minute later, iCloud Drive on my PC threw an error that it couldn't sync.
Of course, the worthless Apple System Status was all lit up green.
The disruption follows last week’s widespread Microsoft Outlook outage.
Yesterday, banking services also experienced problems after a technical issue at JPMorgan Chase disrupted online access and delayed some transactions involving other banks.
Are Apple services working for you? Let us know what you’re seeing in the comments.
Update: Apple now says an internal update caused the outage, ruling out a cyber attack, and many services are coming back online.

GPT‑6.2 Astra and Sol

====================================================================================================

Who Builds the Machines That Build the Machines?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:45:39 UTC
02:45:39 KST

On October 28, 2026, the White House issued a proclamation under Section 232 of the Trade Expansion Act of 1962. This followed a Commerce Department investigation that concluded imports of robotics and industrial machinery “threaten to impair the national security.” No new tariffs have been set yet. Section 232 opens a 180-day window till April 26, 2027 to negotiate with trading partners.
This ties back to one theme that keeps coming back to me as I write on Core Matter. The bottleneck for scaling physical AI sits below the finished machine, in the components inside it, the materials inside those components (eg, rare earth magnets), and the machines that build them (this piece’s topic), as we covered in Part 4 of the actuator series. It’s like a Russian doll, layer after layer.
With the fresh Section 232 news, I went down the rabbit hole of the US machine tool landscape. Here’s what I found.
- The US imports about half of the machine tools it uses, $5.8bn of $11.4bn in 2025. Assuming demand (which is not the trend, see point 3) stays flat, US output would need to grow 63% to 83% ($4.4bn to $5.8bn a year) to replace imports with domestic machines.
- The US builds everyday machines and imports the precise ones. About 55% of import value is the high-end versions of machines US plants already make, such as 5-axis machining centers. Precision machines, small-module gear grinders, precision grinders and the heaviest forging presses have no confirmed US volume builder. Imports rose 29.5% for grinders and 76.7% for gear machines in Jan to Sep 2026. These machines make the ball screws, bearings, gears and forgings that aircraft, turbines and robots depend on.
- Orders are at a record by value since 1998, while a domestic ramp is limited by people and components. US machine tool orders grew 45.0% to $5.76bn in Jan to Sep 2026, after 3 straight years of decline. Aerospace, power generation, munitions and shipbuilding are pulling it up. A domestic ramp is limited by people, castings and imported components (the components that make the machines, the next doll in). We dive into the key cost drivers and where industrial AI could change the cost structure.
This piece also launches our first Core Matter model, a US machine tool demand model built up by end market. It puts US machine tool consumption at $12.7bn to $15.4bn a year on average over the 2026 to 2030 build-out, up from $11.4bn in 2025. Paid subscribers can download the full model, with every input and source, at the end of this piece.
In this piece
- US Machine Tool Imports Are Half of an $11.4bn Market
- Which Machine Tools the US Makes vs. Imports
- Aerospace, Gas Turbines, Munitions and Ships Drove Machine Demand in 2026
- Who Builds Machine Tools in the US, and How Fast Can Supply Ramp?
- Fixed Costs and People Keep New Machine Tool Builders Scarce
- Machine Tool Startups Building the Machines That Build the Machines
- Section 232, the 1986 Machine Tool Precedent and What the 180 Days Decide
1. US Machine Tool Imports Are Half of an $11.4bn Market
Assuming domestic demand stays flat, which is not the trendline as we’ll discuss in section 3, closing the import gap means $4.4bn to $5.8bn of new US machine output a year, or output up 63% to 83%. The low end replaces net imports, and the high end replaces every imported machine.
In 2025 the US produced $7.0bn of machine tools, consumed $11.4bn, imported $5.8bn and exported $1.4bn, per Gardner’s World Machine Tool Survey.
Forming and stamping ($1.48bn) and machining centers ($1.43bn) are the top 2025 imports. Imports of precision grinders or gear grinders rose rapidly at 29.5% and 76.7% in January to September 2026, against about 9.6% for all machine tool imports. We could not confirm US volume production.
2. Which Machine Tools the US Makes vs. Imports
The US imports 5-axis machines, horizontals with pallet systems, large gantries and boring mills, precision and gear grinders, and presses. It builds in volume entry and mid-range 3-axis mills and lathes.
“Japanese and DACH (Germany, Austria and Switzerland) machines generally fulfill buyer needs in the higher segment while US and Taiwanese (ROC) generally fulfill them in the lower.”
One distributor analyst put it succinctly in a filing to the Section 232 docket.
What do higher vs. lower segments mean? Higher means more axes, tighter precision, bigger work envelopes and more automation, at a higher price. The average imported machining center cost about $196k in 2024, roughly 2x our estimate of about $96k per machine at Haas, the largest machine tool builder in North America.
See the table below of what these machines do, and who the main buyers are.
While Okuma (Charlotte), Makino (Ohio), United Grinding (Ohio), Kapp Niles (Colorado) and DN Solutions (New Jersey) have large US sites, the sources we found describe them as sales, service or headquarters sites.
The largest two import classes feed autos, aerospace and defense. Forming and stamping machines make stampings, shell bodies and aerospace forgings. Machining centers make everything from brackets and housings to aerospace structures and turbine parts. My guess is that a cutoff at the high end would hit aerospace, defense and power first.
The US built its own heavy forging presses in the 1950s. The newest ones came from a German builder. Heavy forging presses squeeze titanium, aluminum and nickel into one-piece parts such as landing gear, airframe bulkheads and turbine discs. Because so few exist, these presses can cap how many aircraft and engine parts get made here.
Robot reducers need ultra-precision gear machines, which are imported and in low volumes. Robot joints use reducers, small gearboxes whose teeth are cut and ground on gear machines. Leaderdrive, a Chinese reducer maker, told the Shanghai stock exchange that a plant for 1 million harmonic reducers a year needs 30 ultra-precision gear machines, at about $833k each.
Assuming 23 reducers per humanoid (on the low end, based on Unitree G1 teardown), 1 million humanoids a year (Tesla’s stated line capacity) would need about 690 of those machines, or about $115mn a year bought over the 5 years to 2030. That is 2.3x and 1.2x all US gear-machine imports, $50.7mn in 2025 and about $100mn a year at the 2026 pace.
I ranked the hardest machines to ramp domestically based on, a) which are whether we found a US builder making them in volume, b) how long new capacity takes, and c) how much it costs. Details are in the Core Matter Industrial Machinery Model.
Machines to build in the US, ranked from hardest to ramp:
- Small-module gear grinders. They finish the gears in robot reducers and aerospace gearboxes. We could not confirm volume production of these at any US builder.
- Precision cylindrical and thread grinders. These grinders make the ball screws and bearings that other machines need. United Grinding, the Swiss grinding group, serves the US from an import and service site in Ohio. We found 2 US builders, Weldon (Pennsylvania) and Drake (Ohio), and could not confirm volume production at either.
- Heavy forging presses. The 2 largest US presses we found built or rebuilt since 2000 came from a German builder. Each project took years and $100mn or more.
- Large gantries and boring mills. Ingersoll and Fives build them here, so these are the likeliest candidates to ramp. The constraint is size and lead time.
- High-end 5-axis machines and automated horizontals. Mazak and DMG MORI already build some in the US, so expansion of existing plants is the shorter path here.
3. Aerospace, Gas Turbines, Munitions and Ships Drove Machine Demand in 2026
US machine tool orders have picked up significantly in 2025 and 2026. Order value rose 22.5% to $5.74bn in 2025, and another 45.0% to $5.76bn in January to September 2026. That was after 3 straight years of decline, including a 3.8% fall in 2024. AMT calls the first half of 2026 its strongest half since the series began in 1998.
Who buys these machines in the US?
- Aerospace orders rose 45.1% in 2025, and in the first half of 2026 aerospace ordered more machinery than in any half on record. AMT ties 2026 demand to the space economy, commercial aircraft backlogs and munitions, so commercial demand is part of it alongside defense.
- Power generation orders in the first half of 2026 ran 14% above automotive.
- Contract job shops, AMT’s largest customer group, grew orders 19.1% in 2025.
- Data centers drive gas turbines machinery demands. Klingelnberg says that in the US “the rising energy needs of these data centers are providing additional momentum, for example through investments in gas turbines.”
The US buys $11.4bn of machine tools a year today. Four build-outs (munitions, ships, gas turbines, commercial aerospace) add $1.0bn to $2.9bn a year of extra buying for 5 years, on top of 1% to 3% normal growth. That puts US buying at $12.7bn to $15.4bn a year in 2026 to 2030.
Munitions is the largest piece. The one new US plant we found, Haas Henderson, adds about $0.5bn of machines sold at home. So imports rise from $5.8bn to about $6.9bn to $9.5bn a year, and the import share goes from 51% to 54% to 62%.
4. Who Builds Machine Tools in the US, and How Fast Can Supply Ramp?
A domestic ramp-up faces four limiting factors.
- Workforce. This is the manufacturing story repeating all over again. Gleason’s executive put it first, saying “First you need a large enough, trained workforce. This needs to be the top priority to expand the industrial base.” Haas is hiring more than 1,000 people for Henderson at starting pay of about $23 an hour.
- Castings. Castings are the heavy iron bed and columns of a machine. Haas bought “more than 100 million pounds of castings each year” back in 2006 and worried that shrinking US foundry numbers “could limit their company’s growth.” At 10,000 to 13,000 machines a year, that is roughly 7,700 to 10,000 pounds (3.5 to 4.5 tonnes, about the weight of 2 to 3 cars) of castings per machine.
- Imported components. The parts inside the machines are imported too, including controls (FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain, Mitsubishi), spindles, ball screws and linear guides. Tariffs raise their cost, and control deliveries have seen delays. 
  - In April 2025 Haas asked for “tariff exemptions for raw materials and components vital to the U.S. machine tool industry.” DMG MORI’s 2025 results blame late deliveries partly on “delays in switching to new European-made CNCs.”
- Time. “A shift in global production takes a lot of time,” per GMTA, a US importer of German gear machines. One example is Haas’s plant in Henderson, Nevada. The city closed the land sale in November 2019, with completion planned for 2022. Haas asked the city to extend its deadline, citing the pandemic, supply chains and construction costs. They eventually held the groundbreaking in October 2024. The plant is scheduled to open in Q1 2027, 5 years behind schedule.
Imports are slow to fill a jump in demand too, since foreign builders hold about 7 months of sales in backlog, with DMG MORI at 7.0 months on September 30, 2026 and Klingelnberg at about 7 at the end of March 2026. Klingelnberg’s backlog has halved since 2023.
5. Fixed Costs and People Keep New Machine Tool Builders Scarce
Would this spur a new generation of startups to build the machines that build the machines? As I read the filings of listed builders, the economics are brutal.
What I took away from these financials is that for European and Japanese builders, people cost about as much as materials. Personnel is 30% to 41% of revenue or operating output and materials 38% to 41%. At Chinese builders, total employee pay is 15% to 19% of revenue (Haitian and Kede, 2025) and materials 50% to 62%. Personnel, depreciation and overhead are fixed costs, so margins swing with the cycle. 2025 was a weak year. In 2023 Hermle earned an operating margin of about 21.8% and DMG MORI 10.0%. Labor productivity in US metalworking machinery “has grown only 0.9% per year over the last 30 years.”
What are the key materials? Neway, a Chinese CNC machine builder (688697.SS)’s IPO prospectus published their bill of materials. Of Rmb840.0mn ($122mn) of purchases in 2020, the split is below.
- Functional components (hydraulics, tool magazines, rotary tables) are 25.6%
- Castings are 22.3%
- Electrical parts (mainly the control) are 20.9%. The control alone is about 17.7% of purchases.
- Transmission parts (spindles, guides, screws, bearings, gears) are 15.4%
- Accessories, machined parts, sheet metal and other are 15.8%
Control and distribution are two levers that can help expand margins.
- The control. A CNC control is the machine’s computer, software, servo drives and motors. Most builders we checked buy it from FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain or Mitsubishi, and FANUC earns a 21.4% operating margin (company-wide) against 3% to 10% at most builders we checked.
- Cutting the middlemen. US distribution typically has 2 levels, “a wholesaler and a dealer,” and “each level marks their cost up as much as 20%.”
6. Machine Tool Startups Building the Machines That Build the Machines
Could venture capital close the machinery gap with the next wave of startups? I went searching and found no venture-backed US company building production machining centers, grinders or gear machines. (If you are building them, I’d love to hear from you!)
What we can learn from history. Haas, the largest machine builder in North America, was founded in 1983, during the import surge behind the 1983 machine tool Section 232 petition. It started with indexers and built its first vertical machining center in 1987. Today it builds about 10,000 to 15,000 machines a year on about $1.2bn of revenue, and is private.
The small US builders popped up in the 2000s. They include Tormach (Wisconsin, 2001), Pocket NC, now Penta Machine (Montana, 2011), and Langmuir Systems, a plasma-table maker bought by ShopSabre in 2023. They design and sell desktop and small-shop machines.
US venture money has gone to companies that use machine tools or build equipment for their own factories. Examples include Hadrian, Machina Labs, Divergent, VulcanForms, Freeform and Seurat. Hadrian’s new Alabama factory is the first of three supported by a $900mn Navy contract, with a goal to “automate 80% of the work.”
This year’s capital went to factory software and sensing. Arda, led by OpenAI’s former research chief Bob McGrew, came out of stealth in October 2026 with $232mn of funding, its Series A at a $1.5bn valuation, building AI that turns product designs into assembly lines. Matter, whose cofounder and CEO Adi Prasad came from Tesla, offers final assembly, test and packaging for robots in the US. In machining software, CloudNC raised $20mn (UK, September 2026, with Lockheed’s venture arm), Limitless Labs $20mn (Israel) and IPercept $16.5mn (Sweden, entering the US).
Funded machine builders abroad tell a different story. Ethereal Machines (India) raised a $28.5mn Series B in 2026. It builds its own multi-axis CNC machines, sells machining as a service from them, and is building its own controller and a 300,000 sq ft plant. Shanghai Xinghe (China) builds gear grinders and raised several hundred million yuan in July 2026.
Government money has gone to training and adjacent supply. America’s Cutting Edge, a national CNC machining training initiative backed by Oak Ridge National Laboratory (ORNL), received $42mn from the Defense Department from 2020 to 2022 for machine tool training and R&D. The Pentagon’s industrial base program has awarded over $3.3bn since FY2024. The releases we read do not name any machine tool builder.
With Section 232, I believe two opportunities arise domestically in the US.
- Foreign builders establish US bases to build machines. They are more experienced in navigating thin margins, but that would likely still require government incentives to boost investments.
- Startups in the same spirit as Ethereal Machines launch in the US. They would need to think carefully about how to cut labor cost, solve the materials supply chain bottleneck, and build the control in-house from day one.
7. Section 232, the 1986 Machine Tool Precedent and What the 180 Days Decide
This Section 232 has until April 26, 2027 for its first negotiation update. Section 232 last came for machine tools in 1983, 43 years ago. Will history repeat or rhyme?
The US lost its lead in the 1970s and 1980s. Japanese machine tool exports to the US rose over 1,200% between 1964 and 1967, and by 1986 imports were 75% of the numerically controlled (NC) machines sold in the US, the category that includes today’s CNC machines. Across all machine tools, imports went from about 10% of US consumption in the mid-1970s to 25% by 1981 and about 50% by 1986.
- March 1983. The builders’ association petitions for import quotas under Section 232.
- May 20, 1986. Reagan chooses negotiation. “Voluntary restraint agreements will be sought with Taiwan, West Germany, Japan, and Switzerland.”
- Dec 16, 1986. Japan and Taiwan agree to 5 years of restraint from January 1987. The White House projects up to 16,500 more domestic machine sales over the 5 years and cites industry estimates of about $115mn of new plant investment. The full outcome is hard to score. One NBER study found US output rose from 41,992 machines in 1987 to 48,668 in 1988.
- By Oct 1991. Reports said “at least 10 Japanese machine-toolmakers have established plants in the U.S.”
- Dec 1991. Bush keeps the restraints on computer-controlled machines for a 2-year phase-out from January 1992. Japan’s extension ran to Dec 31, 1993, so 5 years became 7.
- Effects. The US International Trade Commission estimated that in 1987, the first year, the restraints raised prices 4.4% to 4.9% and protected 658 to 671 jobs. AMT says the domestic share held near 50% for the next decade, so the restraints stopped the fall without reversing it. Imports are about 51% of consumption today.
Here is our view.
- A tariff alone raises prices, but buyers keep importing the precise machines. Back in 1987, during the last Section 232, the domestic share held near 50% for the following decade. Fast forward to today, imports are 51% of consumption. One can argue that without the 1987 restraints, the domestic share would be even lower today. This time, imports fell where US plants offer an alternative and kept rising where buyers have few US options. So either the buyers are not price sensitive, or the cost are passed down to the customers. In January to September 2026, with duties in place, machining-center imports fell 3.7% while orders rose 45.0%, and grinder imports rose 29.5% and gear machine imports +76.7%.
- For reindustrialization startups, the openings are in the control and in automation that cuts labor cost. The control is where the profit sits. Builders buy it from suppliers such as FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain and Mitsubishi, and FANUC earns a 21.4% operating margin (company-wide) against 3% to 10% at most builders we checked. Automation goes after the other big cost. On our numbers, personnel is 30% to 41% of revenue or operating output at European and Japanese builders, about as much as materials. Service margins come from machines already in the field, so they follow once the core machine business is running. This in my opinion is an opportunity for agentic robotics, manufacturing AI founders to shine.

Who Builds the Machines That Build the Machines?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:45:39 UTC
02:45:39 KST

On October 28, 2026, the White House issued a proclamation under Section 232 of the Trade Expansion Act of 1962. This followed a Commerce Department investigation that concluded imports of robotics and industrial machinery “threaten to impair the national security.” No new tariffs have been set yet. Section 232 opens a 180-day window till April 26, 2027 to negotiate with trading partners.
This ties back to one theme that keeps coming back to me as I write on Core Matter. The bottleneck for scaling physical AI sits below the finished machine, in the components inside it, the materials inside those components (eg, rare earth magnets), and the machines that build them (this piece’s topic), as we covered in Part 4 of the actuator series. It’s like a Russian doll, layer after layer.
With the fresh Section 232 news, I went down the rabbit hole of the US machine tool landscape. Here’s what I found.
- The US imports about half of the machine tools it uses, $5.8bn of $11.4bn in 2025. Assuming demand (which is not the trend, see point 3) stays flat, US output would need to grow 63% to 83% ($4.4bn to $5.8bn a year) to replace imports with domestic machines.
- The US builds everyday machines and imports the precise ones. About 55% of import value is the high-end versions of machines US plants already make, such as 5-axis machining centers. Precision machines, small-module gear grinders, precision grinders and the heaviest forging presses have no confirmed US volume builder. Imports rose 29.5% for grinders and 76.7% for gear machines in Jan to Sep 2026. These machines make the ball screws, bearings, gears and forgings that aircraft, turbines and robots depend on.
- Orders are at a record by value since 1998, while a domestic ramp is limited by people and components. US machine tool orders grew 45.0% to $5.76bn in Jan to Sep 2026, after 3 straight years of decline. Aerospace, power generation, munitions and shipbuilding are pulling it up. A domestic ramp is limited by people, castings and imported components (the components that make the machines, the next doll in). We dive into the key cost drivers and where industrial AI could change the cost structure.
This piece also launches our first Core Matter model, a US machine tool demand model built up by end market. It puts US machine tool consumption at $12.7bn to $15.4bn a year on average over the 2026 to 2030 build-out, up from $11.4bn in 2025. Paid subscribers can download the full model, with every input and source, at the end of this piece.
In this piece
- US Machine Tool Imports Are Half of an $11.4bn Market
- Which Machine Tools the US Makes vs. Imports
- Aerospace, Gas Turbines, Munitions and Ships Drove Machine Demand in 2026
- Who Builds Machine Tools in the US, and How Fast Can Supply Ramp?
- Fixed Costs and People Keep New Machine Tool Builders Scarce
- Machine Tool Startups Building the Machines That Build the Machines
- Section 232, the 1986 Machine Tool Precedent and What the 180 Days Decide
1. US Machine Tool Imports Are Half of an $11.4bn Market
Assuming domestic demand stays flat, which is not the trendline as we’ll discuss in section 3, closing the import gap means $4.4bn to $5.8bn of new US machine output a year, or output up 63% to 83%. The low end replaces net imports, and the high end replaces every imported machine.
In 2025 the US produced $7.0bn of machine tools, consumed $11.4bn, imported $5.8bn and exported $1.4bn, per Gardner’s World Machine Tool Survey.
Forming and stamping ($1.48bn) and machining centers ($1.43bn) are the top 2025 imports. Imports of precision grinders or gear grinders rose rapidly at 29.5% and 76.7% in January to September 2026, against about 9.6% for all machine tool imports. We could not confirm US volume production.
2. Which Machine Tools the US Makes vs. Imports
The US imports 5-axis machines, horizontals with pallet systems, large gantries and boring mills, precision and gear grinders, and presses. It builds in volume entry and mid-range 3-axis mills and lathes.
“Japanese and DACH (Germany, Austria and Switzerland) machines generally fulfill buyer needs in the higher segment while US and Taiwanese (ROC) generally fulfill them in the lower.”
One distributor analyst put it succinctly in a filing to the Section 232 docket.
What do higher vs. lower segments mean? Higher means more axes, tighter precision, bigger work envelopes and more automation, at a higher price. The average imported machining center cost about $196k in 2024, roughly 2x our estimate of about $96k per machine at Haas, the largest machine tool builder in North America.
See the table below of what these machines do, and who the main buyers are.
While Okuma (Charlotte), Makino (Ohio), United Grinding (Ohio), Kapp Niles (Colorado) and DN Solutions (New Jersey) have large US sites, the sources we found describe them as sales, service or headquarters sites.
The largest two import classes feed autos, aerospace and defense. Forming and stamping machines make stampings, shell bodies and aerospace forgings. Machining centers make everything from brackets and housings to aerospace structures and turbine parts. My guess is that a cutoff at the high end would hit aerospace, defense and power first.
The US built its own heavy forging presses in the 1950s. The newest ones came from a German builder. Heavy forging presses squeeze titanium, aluminum and nickel into one-piece parts such as landing gear, airframe bulkheads and turbine discs. Because so few exist, these presses can cap how many aircraft and engine parts get made here.
Robot reducers need ultra-precision gear machines, which are imported and in low volumes. Robot joints use reducers, small gearboxes whose teeth are cut and ground on gear machines. Leaderdrive, a Chinese reducer maker, told the Shanghai stock exchange that a plant for 1 million harmonic reducers a year needs 30 ultra-precision gear machines, at about $833k each.
Assuming 23 reducers per humanoid (on the low end, based on Unitree G1 teardown), 1 million humanoids a year (Tesla’s stated line capacity) would need about 690 of those machines, or about $115mn a year bought over the 5 years to 2030. That is 2.3x and 1.2x all US gear-machine imports, $50.7mn in 2025 and about $100mn a year at the 2026 pace.
I ranked the hardest machines to ramp domestically based on, a) which are whether we found a US builder making them in volume, b) how long new capacity takes, and c) how much it costs. Details are in the Core Matter Industrial Machinery Model.
Machines to build in the US, ranked from hardest to ramp:
- Small-module gear grinders. They finish the gears in robot reducers and aerospace gearboxes. We could not confirm volume production of these at any US builder.
- Precision cylindrical and thread grinders. These grinders make the ball screws and bearings that other machines need. United Grinding, the Swiss grinding group, serves the US from an import and service site in Ohio. We found 2 US builders, Weldon (Pennsylvania) and Drake (Ohio), and could not confirm volume production at either.
- Heavy forging presses. The 2 largest US presses we found built or rebuilt since 2000 came from a German builder. Each project took years and $100mn or more.
- Large gantries and boring mills. Ingersoll and Fives build them here, so these are the likeliest candidates to ramp. The constraint is size and lead time.
- High-end 5-axis machines and automated horizontals. Mazak and DMG MORI already build some in the US, so expansion of existing plants is the shorter path here.
3. Aerospace, Gas Turbines, Munitions and Ships Drove Machine Demand in 2026
US machine tool orders have picked up significantly in 2025 and 2026. Order value rose 22.5% to $5.74bn in 2025, and another 45.0% to $5.76bn in January to September 2026. That was after 3 straight years of decline, including a 3.8% fall in 2024. AMT calls the first half of 2026 its strongest half since the series began in 1998.
Who buys these machines in the US?
- Aerospace orders rose 45.1% in 2025, and in the first half of 2026 aerospace ordered more machinery than in any half on record. AMT ties 2026 demand to the space economy, commercial aircraft backlogs and munitions, so commercial demand is part of it alongside defense.
- Power generation orders in the first half of 2026 ran 14% above automotive.
- Contract job shops, AMT’s largest customer group, grew orders 19.1% in 2025.
- Data centers drive gas turbines machinery demands. Klingelnberg says that in the US “the rising energy needs of these data centers are providing additional momentum, for example through investments in gas turbines.”
The US buys $11.4bn of machine tools a year today. Four build-outs (munitions, ships, gas turbines, commercial aerospace) add $1.0bn to $2.9bn a year of extra buying for 5 years, on top of 1% to 3% normal growth. That puts US buying at $12.7bn to $15.4bn a year in 2026 to 2030.
Munitions is the largest piece. The one new US plant we found, Haas Henderson, adds about $0.5bn of machines sold at home. So imports rise from $5.8bn to about $6.9bn to $9.5bn a year, and the import share goes from 51% to 54% to 62%.
4. Who Builds Machine Tools in the US, and How Fast Can Supply Ramp?
A domestic ramp-up faces four limiting factors.
- Workforce. This is the manufacturing story repeating all over again. Gleason’s executive put it first, saying “First you need a large enough, trained workforce. This needs to be the top priority to expand the industrial base.” Haas is hiring more than 1,000 people for Henderson at starting pay of about $23 an hour.
- Castings. Castings are the heavy iron bed and columns of a machine. Haas bought “more than 100 million pounds of castings each year” back in 2006 and worried that shrinking US foundry numbers “could limit their company’s growth.” At 10,000 to 13,000 machines a year, that is roughly 7,700 to 10,000 pounds (3.5 to 4.5 tonnes, about the weight of 2 to 3 cars) of castings per machine.
- Imported components. The parts inside the machines are imported too, including controls (FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain, Mitsubishi), spindles, ball screws and linear guides. Tariffs raise their cost, and control deliveries have seen delays. 
  - In April 2025 Haas asked for “tariff exemptions for raw materials and components vital to the U.S. machine tool industry.” DMG MORI’s 2025 results blame late deliveries partly on “delays in switching to new European-made CNCs.”
- Time. “A shift in global production takes a lot of time,” per GMTA, a US importer of German gear machines. One example is Haas’s plant in Henderson, Nevada. The city closed the land sale in November 2019, with completion planned for 2022. Haas asked the city to extend its deadline, citing the pandemic, supply chains and construction costs. They eventually held the groundbreaking in October 2024. The plant is scheduled to open in Q1 2027, 5 years behind schedule.
Imports are slow to fill a jump in demand too, since foreign builders hold about 7 months of sales in backlog, with DMG MORI at 7.0 months on September 30, 2026 and Klingelnberg at about 7 at the end of March 2026. Klingelnberg’s backlog has halved since 2023.
5. Fixed Costs and People Keep New Machine Tool Builders Scarce
Would this spur a new generation of startups to build the machines that build the machines? As I read the filings of listed builders, the economics are brutal.
What I took away from these financials is that for European and Japanese builders, people cost about as much as materials. Personnel is 30% to 41% of revenue or operating output and materials 38% to 41%. At Chinese builders, total employee pay is 15% to 19% of revenue (Haitian and Kede, 2025) and materials 50% to 62%. Personnel, depreciation and overhead are fixed costs, so margins swing with the cycle. 2025 was a weak year. In 2023 Hermle earned an operating margin of about 21.8% and DMG MORI 10.0%. Labor productivity in US metalworking machinery “has grown only 0.9% per year over the last 30 years.”
What are the key materials? Neway, a Chinese CNC machine builder (688697.SS)’s IPO prospectus published their bill of materials. Of Rmb840.0mn ($122mn) of purchases in 2020, the split is below.
- Functional components (hydraulics, tool magazines, rotary tables) are 25.6%
- Castings are 22.3%
- Electrical parts (mainly the control) are 20.9%. The control alone is about 17.7% of purchases.
- Transmission parts (spindles, guides, screws, bearings, gears) are 15.4%
- Accessories, machined parts, sheet metal and other are 15.8%
Control and distribution are two levers that can help expand margins.
- The control. A CNC control is the machine’s computer, software, servo drives and motors. Most builders we checked buy it from FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain or Mitsubishi, and FANUC earns a 21.4% operating margin (company-wide) against 3% to 10% at most builders we checked.
- Cutting the middlemen. US distribution typically has 2 levels, “a wholesaler and a dealer,” and “each level marks their cost up as much as 20%.”
6. Machine Tool Startups Building the Machines That Build the Machines
Could venture capital close the machinery gap with the next wave of startups? I went searching and found no venture-backed US company building production machining centers, grinders or gear machines. (If you are building them, I’d love to hear from you!)
What we can learn from history. Haas, the largest machine builder in North America, was founded in 1983, during the import surge behind the 1983 machine tool Section 232 petition. It started with indexers and built its first vertical machining center in 1987. Today it builds about 10,000 to 15,000 machines a year on about $1.2bn of revenue, and is private.
The small US builders popped up in the 2000s. They include Tormach (Wisconsin, 2001), Pocket NC, now Penta Machine (Montana, 2011), and Langmuir Systems, a plasma-table maker bought by ShopSabre in 2023. They design and sell desktop and small-shop machines.
US venture money has gone to companies that use machine tools or build equipment for their own factories. Examples include Hadrian, Machina Labs, Divergent, VulcanForms, Freeform and Seurat. Hadrian’s new Alabama factory is the first of three supported by a $900mn Navy contract, with a goal to “automate 80% of the work.”
This year’s capital went to factory software and sensing. Arda, led by OpenAI’s former research chief Bob McGrew, came out of stealth in October 2026 with $232mn of funding, its Series A at a $1.5bn valuation, building AI that turns product designs into assembly lines. Matter, whose cofounder and CEO Adi Prasad came from Tesla, offers final assembly, test and packaging for robots in the US. In machining software, CloudNC raised $20mn (UK, September 2026, with Lockheed’s venture arm), Limitless Labs $20mn (Israel) and IPercept $16.5mn (Sweden, entering the US).
Funded machine builders abroad tell a different story. Ethereal Machines (India) raised a $28.5mn Series B in 2026. It builds its own multi-axis CNC machines, sells machining as a service from them, and is building its own controller and a 300,000 sq ft plant. Shanghai Xinghe (China) builds gear grinders and raised several hundred million yuan in July 2026.
Government money has gone to training and adjacent supply. America’s Cutting Edge, a national CNC machining training initiative backed by Oak Ridge National Laboratory (ORNL), received $42mn from the Defense Department from 2020 to 2022 for machine tool training and R&D. The Pentagon’s industrial base program has awarded over $3.3bn since FY2024. The releases we read do not name any machine tool builder.
With Section 232, I believe two opportunities arise domestically in the US.
- Foreign builders establish US bases to build machines. They are more experienced in navigating thin margins, but that would likely still require government incentives to boost investments.
- Startups in the same spirit as Ethereal Machines launch in the US. They would need to think carefully about how to cut labor cost, solve the materials supply chain bottleneck, and build the control in-house from day one.
7. Section 232, the 1986 Machine Tool Precedent and What the 180 Days Decide
This Section 232 has until April 26, 2027 for its first negotiation update. Section 232 last came for machine tools in 1983, 43 years ago. Will history repeat or rhyme?
The US lost its lead in the 1970s and 1980s. Japanese machine tool exports to the US rose over 1,200% between 1964 and 1967, and by 1986 imports were 75% of the numerically controlled (NC) machines sold in the US, the category that includes today’s CNC machines. Across all machine tools, imports went from about 10% of US consumption in the mid-1970s to 25% by 1981 and about 50% by 1986.
- March 1983. The builders’ association petitions for import quotas under Section 232.
- May 20, 1986. Reagan chooses negotiation. “Voluntary restraint agreements will be sought with Taiwan, West Germany, Japan, and Switzerland.”
- Dec 16, 1986. Japan and Taiwan agree to 5 years of restraint from January 1987. The White House projects up to 16,500 more domestic machine sales over the 5 years and cites industry estimates of about $115mn of new plant investment. The full outcome is hard to score. One NBER study found US output rose from 41,992 machines in 1987 to 48,668 in 1988.
- By Oct 1991. Reports said “at least 10 Japanese machine-toolmakers have established plants in the U.S.”
- Dec 1991. Bush keeps the restraints on computer-controlled machines for a 2-year phase-out from January 1992. Japan’s extension ran to Dec 31, 1993, so 5 years became 7.
- Effects. The US International Trade Commission estimated that in 1987, the first year, the restraints raised prices 4.4% to 4.9% and protected 658 to 671 jobs. AMT says the domestic share held near 50% for the next decade, so the restraints stopped the fall without reversing it. Imports are about 51% of consumption today.
Here is our view.
- A tariff alone raises prices, but buyers keep importing the precise machines. Back in 1987, during the last Section 232, the domestic share held near 50% for the following decade. Fast forward to today, imports are 51% of consumption. One can argue that without the 1987 restraints, the domestic share would be even lower today. This time, imports fell where US plants offer an alternative and kept rising where buyers have few US options. So either the buyers are not price sensitive, or the cost are passed down to the customers. In January to September 2026, with duties in place, machining-center imports fell 3.7% while orders rose 45.0%, and grinder imports rose 29.5% and gear machine imports +76.7%.
- For reindustrialization startups, the openings are in the control and in automation that cuts labor cost. The control is where the profit sits. Builders buy it from suppliers such as FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain and Mitsubishi, and FANUC earns a 21.4% operating margin (company-wide) against 3% to 10% at most builders we checked. Automation goes after the other big cost. On our numbers, personnel is 30% to 41% of revenue or operating output at European and Japanese builders, about as much as materials. Service margins come from machines already in the field, so they follow once the core machine business is running. This in my opinion is an opportunity for agentic robotics, manufacturing AI founders to shine.
