The iPhone Duo feels far more intuitive with Apple’s new face unlock
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:42:34 UTC
02:42:34 KST

Apple is testing adding face unlock to the iPhone Duo — not true Face ID, but something a little simpler and less secure. So far, it works great, and even with a few Apple-imposed security limitations, it makes the Duo quicker and more comfortable to use than relying on Touch ID every time. That’s one of my gripes with the phone taken care of.
The iPhone Duo feels far more intuitive with Apple’s new face unlock
There are limitations compared with Face ID, but this is much better than nothing.
The Duo’s foldable architecture means that Apple couldn’t fit in the full Face ID sensor array found on the rest of the iPhone lineup (though it reportedly hopes to change that by the second generation). Instead, it simply has two selfie cameras — one on the outer display, and another hidden under the inner one. Neither supports full Face ID, but after enough grumbling from reviewers and early adopters that using Touch ID alone feels cumbersome, Apple has relented.
The latest developer beta of iOS 27.3 introduces a new option to “Unlock with Front Camera” — Apple is careful not to call it Face ID. Setup is similar, though instead of rotating your head in a slow circle, you simply have to turn it left, right, up, and down. It takes five seconds or so, and I even managed it under the jerky conditions of a moving London bus.
The new face unlock brings convenience, taking just half a second to unlock, but Apple warns during setup that it “isn’t as secure as Touch ID or Face ID” because it lacks the infrared depth map of the TrueDepth cameras. That means it’s possible to spoof with a photograph or video clip, but I managed that only once in over 20 attempts. Crucially, after each failed attempt to trick the system with a photo, the phone demanded I use Touch ID for my next login — which it doesn’t require if it simply doesn’t detect any face in the frame. That means getting past this would require any would-be thief or spy to get the fake face right the first time.
Lacking TrueDepth also introduces limits in low light, though performance is better than I expected. Walking around after sunset, I had to seek out especially dark spots before I could find anywhere where unlocking failed, like huddling behind a truck on a leafy street where the streetlights couldn’t reach me. It will fail eventually, but it does have to get quite dark for that to happen.
Apple has imposed a few other limitations to reflect the lower security of the new system. You can only use it to unlock the phone from the lockscreen, so you will have to revert to Touch ID or a passcode to use Apple Pay, install or log in to an app, or get past any other screen where the phone requires security credentials.
That’s similar to most Android phones with camera-based face unlock. Only Google’s Pixel phones allow you to make purchases or log in to apps using Face Unlock, thanks to software tricks that make the system more secure than other Android alternatives, Google says.
The iPhone Duo’s more annoying limitations reflect when you can use the feature. Face unlock works only for 45 minutes at a time, after which you’ll have to verify with Touch ID or your passcode the next time you unlock. Worse, it doesn’t work at all with the inner screen’s under-display selfie camera. Apple hasn’t said why, though presumably it felt the lower resolution and quality of that camera made face unlock unsuitable.
I’d love it if Apple loosened its restrictions just a little — 45 minutes isn’t a very long time before requiring Touch ID again, and I’m sure I’ll regularly forget that the inner screen requires a fingerprint. But the Duo already feels more comfortable to use, letting me get straight into the phone rather than fumbling for the fingerprint sensor. It’s small, but it’s a welcome sign that Apple is listening to the Duo’s early users and is willing to adjust its grand vision to meet the world where it is.
For now, face unlock is limited to the developer beta, but it could roll out to a public beta within a few days, and with luck will get a full launch along with the rest of iOS 27.3 in December. Now can we get the same option for MacBooks and iPads?
Photography by Dominic Preston / The Verge

Proton’s Lumo, a privacy-focused alternative to ChatGPT and Claude, arrives on desktop
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:32:25 UTC
02:32:25 KST

Proton, the privacy-focused tech company offering alternatives to Google’s and Microsoft’s tools for email, productivity apps, password management, cloud storage, and more, is now bringing its AI app Lumo to personal computers. On Tuesday, the company launched Lumo Desktop for Windows, macOS, and Linux, allowing users to leverage its AI to carry out local tasks on their computer involving connected apps, personal files, and other automations.
The app is designed to offer users more control over their personal data, as consumer AI technology becomes focused more on AI agents that can perform tasks on users’ behalf, rather than just answering questions.
Already, the major model makers, including OpenAI, Anthropic, Meta, and others, offer desktop tools for working with their AI technologies, but doing so invites increased risk to users’ personal data. Only this week, for instance, Apple announced it was addressing those privacy concerns with tightened security settings for apps that request Full Disk Access, as the AI desktop apps do.
Proton’s idea is to offer a set of tools that let its AI access connected apps, files, and folders and automate tasks, but with more personalization over how much access the AI has. The company says users can choose which actions the AI agent takes that will need to request their approval, and it vows that the conversations and information from connected apps and files are never used to serve personalized ads or train AI models.
The new app includes a variety of connectors that support local tools, like file-system access, shell execution, and browser content fetching, as well as Proton’s email app and other third-party services, including Confluence, Dropbox, and Jira.
The connectors also support local and remote MCP (Model Context Protocol) servers, which AI agents use to access external data sources.
Proton says that users can decide for each connector which actions Lumo can perform automatically, which require approval, and which are blocked entirely. Users can also turn individual tools off from the Tools menu in their conversation.
In addition, users can set up their own scheduled tasks, where each has its own set of permissions, in terms of what can be handled automatically and what requires approval.
Proton notes that it uses encryption to ensure that only users can read their chat history, not the company. The company also says it keeps no server-side logs, and the limited data it does store is protected under Swiss privacy law. The data is hosted on European infrastructure, outside the scope of the U.S. CLOUD Act, which specifies how law enforcement agencies access data held by cloud service providers.
The company says it’s investing in its infrastructure, with its first Nvidia B300 cluster built on Open Compute Project (OCP) standards now going live in Switzerland.
Similar to other AI apps, Lumo’s desktop app supports Skills, which lets users save instructions, templates, and reference files that Lumo can reuse across their conversations. (An earlier version of this, Custom Lumos, will now be migrated to the Skills format.)

BugMeNot Calls It Quits
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:30:00 UTC
02:30:00 KST

BugMeNot Calls It Quits (heise.de) 12
			
		 	
				BugMeNot, the password-sharing service launched in 2003 to help users bypass website registration requirements, has shut down without explanation after more than two decades. All that's left on the website is a farewell message that reads: "Thanks to everyone who envisioned a better internet." heise online reports:  Founded by Guy King, the service had been online since 2003: BugMeNot was a collection of login credentials for various online services. Volunteers submitted credentials they had created for different websites.
The idea was simple: Nobody wants to create a full account and provide all their sensitive information just to use a service occasionally. Two decades ago, this privacy concern was more firmly embedded in the public consciousness, but it has since faded considerably. Back then, usernames and passwords were usually the only access controls, so people could easily submit those credentials. Multifactor authentication was still exotic, and passkeys were a distant prospect.
The once-popular service lost its shine after just a few years. Service providers quickly discovered and blocked most of the credentials. They could also prevent users from saving logins for their services directly through BugMeNot, for example when those services were paid. More modern security mechanisms also emerged in recent years. As a result, credentials found on BugMeNot rarely worked. Most online providers also blocked the service's disposable email addresses under @bugmenot.com, making them rarely usable. Many people forgot about the site. Now its operators have shut it down without giving a reason.  Additional discussions about BugMeNot's closure can be found on Reddit and Hacker News.

Gone but still screwing things up: Leavitt's old 'Big Law' quote is forcing a White House search

====================================================================================================

AI models can be tricked into identifying civilians as military targets by telling them it’s a game
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:20:41 UTC
02:20:41 KST

Some AI models from Anthropic, OpenAI, and other leading AI labs could be easily tricked into identifying targets for military strikes — even if they include civilians or locations like schools — simply by being told they’re playing a game, according to a new research report by Vals.
Vals, a San Francisco-based AI evaluation startup, tested whether leading AI models could be manipulated into participating in autonomous warfare. Researchers used simulated scenarios and a combination of generated and real imagery from Ukraine and Iran to see if the AI models would mark targets in drone and satellite imagery.
The models from OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, DeepSeek, and other companies frequently identified potential targets in drone and satellite imagery, including civilians and surrendering soldiers. When the models refused to cooperate, researchers were able to jailbreak them by claiming the battlefield scenarios were part of a game.
The findings come amid growing fears about the immediate risks posed by AI, beyond the existential threats associated with artificial general intelligence.
Earlier this year, Anthropic became embroiled in a dispute with the Department of Defense after it told the agency it could not use Anthropic’s technology for mass surveillance of Americans or autonomous warfare. Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei has insisted that today’s AI systems aren’t reliable enough to make life-or-death decisions without a human in the loop. Meanwhile, the Pentagon argued it should be able to deploy the technology for any lawful military purpose.
The new research raises further questions about whether AI companies are capable of preventing their models from being used in warfare.
Ironically, AI models from Anthropic performed the worst in these simulated scenarios. But none of the models performed particularly well. None of the researchers’ requests were blocked by a safety classifier, and refusals were uncommon. Even when models did refuse, they often complied after being told they were playing a game.
Anthropic spokesperson Eduardo Maia Silva told TechCrunch the company appreciates Vals’ report, recommendations, and collaboration, and acknowledged that the outcomes were not acceptable.
“The examples in the report are very clearly against our rules and we have banned and will ban users when we see this behavior,” according to an emailed company statement sent by Silva. “Even for authorized settings, Anthropic has long been firm that today’s frontier AI systems are not reliable enough to power fully autonomous weapons.”
Anthropic told TechCrunch that its teams were already developing additional safeguards before the report was published and that those measures would be in place soon. Anthropic is also expanding its own testing and enhancing safeguards and enforcement to address any gaps it finds.
OpenAI spokesperson Nate Evans told TechCrunch the company prohibits using its public models to facilitate violence or for unapproved national security purposes, including in the scenarios Vals simulated.
“We actively detect and disrupt misuse, including by suspending accounts that violate our policies,” OpenAI said in an emailed statement. “We’re also developing additional safeguards for our public models against misuse involving drones and drone software.”
Vals notes in the report that the models were never asked to directly make strike decisions, a task the researchers said the models would typically refuse. That doesn’t mean their outputs couldn’t have dire or fatal consequences in the real world. For instance, targeting information could be used by autonomous weapons systems.
In some scenarios, the models were asked to report whether people were visible. Positive reports were followed by simulated strikes and the models would see the consequences.
The report relies on commercial models from frontier AI labs, not the models those companies might provide to the military. It’s not clear whether the additional safeguards would apply to military use, though.
Vals also noted that technical challenges, including latency and limited onboard computing capacity, make it difficult to use general-purpose AI models for live drone targeting. Those constraints make their deployment in combat less of an immediate threat.
A more immediate threat, the researchers say, is that AI models could be used to annotate battlefield footage at scale, producing training data for autonomous targeting systems.
For instance, researchers relied on real satellite imagery of an Iranian school that was bombed by the U.S. in February 2026, killing more than 150 civilians. Bloomberg later reported that an “overreliance on AI” contributed to the strike. In Vals’ testing, models frequently marked the school, which sat next to a military complex, as a strike target, with Claude Opus 5.5 selecting the school in 69% of ordinary trials and 72% of game-framed trials.
Anthropic no longer has an active Pentagon contract, but other companies, including SpaceXAI, OpenAI, and Google do. A recent Congressional Research Service report found that the Pentagon is not known to be using any frontier AI model in autonomous weapons systems directly.
TechCrunch has also reached out to Meta, Google, Thinking Machines Lab, and SpaceXAI for comment.

Inside Microsoft’s giant Outlook outage
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
17:01:14 UTC
02:01:14 KST

At 9AM last Thursday, everything seemed normal. Microsoft employees were arriving on campus, ready for a regular day of emails and meetings. But barely an hour later, one of Microsoft’s worst outages in history started to unfold, leaving employees, businesses, and many governments without access to Outlook emails for nearly 24 hours.
Inside Microsoft’s giant Outlook outage
A bug took down Microsoft’s email services for millions around the world.
It all started in Switzerland, according to a source familiar with the outage. Exchange Online servers that power Outlook started to fall offline. The problem quickly spread to Mexico, and then to other countries around the world, as Microsoft’s failover systems tried to shift email traffic elsewhere. Within a couple of hours, more than 80 percent of Exchange servers were unresponsive.
Sources tell me that Microsoft marked the outage as “sev0” in its internal incident tracking system, the highest level, which indicates a catastrophic level of service disruption. Hundreds of engineers began trying to figure out what had gone wrong, and how to restore service. It’s not unusual for servers to fail under certain circumstances, but Microsoft has a variety of systems that are supposed to route traffic toward healthy servers to keep the broader system online. In this case, the failover systems did their job, but they also spread the problem.
In the early hours of the outage, engineers were analyzing crash reports, trace logs, and kernel dumps to try and find clues to the giant outage. Some servers had entered into maintenance mode and became unresponsive due to unusually high CPU loads. Engineers tried to bring servers back online, but they were almost immediately overwhelmed by traffic waiting to access the servers.
Exchange servers boot up and run an OS over a network (Preboot Execution Environment), a process that allows Microsoft’s support staff and engineers to manage OS updates remotely. I understand that the sudden burst of traffic from servers being rebooted was overwhelming the systems monitoring them, causing a form of reboot loop where they kept trying and failing to come back online.
As engineers continued to hunt for the root cause, Microsoft was even struggling to roll back servers to a healthy state using its usual processes of reverting recent changes. As the outside world began to wonder if this outage was related to a cyberattack or sloppy AI coding, Microsoft’s engineers were already investigating potential attack vectors and liaising with DDoS specialists to understand whether this could have been an attack.
I’m told crash reports and logs quickly ruled out the possibility of an attack, and after recent rollbacks failed, Microsoft’s engineers began to take a closer look at the networking stack of the servers and discovered an unusual bug. It was unusual because the bug had been sitting on Microsoft 365 networking gateway servers since July without any impact to servers.
“A latent defect caused servers to become overwhelmed and unresponsive under certain load conditions,” wrote Charles Lamanna, the leader of Microsoft’s Copilot, Agents, and Platform (CAP) team, in a post on X. The teams behind Office, Outlook, and Microsoft Teams all started reporting to Lamanna last month after the head of Office and Teams, Ryan Roslansky, left the company.
Microsoft’s preliminary post-incident report describes the bug as one affecting select Windows servers “due to lock contention inside an internal Windows process.” The bug doesn’t affect regular versions of Windows since it was the result of a fix that was prepared specifically for Microsoft 365 networking gateway servers.
Microsoft didn’t find the bug until this major outage, and it admits that was because of the traffic mix and hardware used in its testing labs. “The contention issue is worsened on machines with more cores and newer hardware; whereas our load testing tended to focus on weaker hardware with fewer cores,” Lamanna wrote.
The bug became obvious because of two other incidents that increased load and reduced server capacity. Fifteen hours before this Outlook outage began, Microsoft Teams failed over from an alternative networking gateway to the main gateway that supports Exchange Online. “This increased the load on the networking gateway and changed its traffic patterns,” Lamanna wrote. Ordinarily this wouldn’t have been a problem, but at the same time around 14 percent of server capacity for this gateway was “out of service due to an unrelated issue from the previous day.”
The trifecta of a bug, Microsoft Teams failing over, and reduced server capacity all caused the network gateway to trigger the problem that had been lying dormant since July. It took Microsoft nearly 24 hours to get its Exchange servers stable again, and I’m told overlapping interventions from teams trying to fix issues complicated coordination at times.
Microsoft engineers had a running bridge call the entire time, and at one point it hit the maximum of 1,000 participants. That shows you how many teams were involved with trying to find the root cause of this huge outage, which started at the peak window of traffic in the US.
“Recovery took longer than usual because as servers returned to service, they were immediately overwhelmed by waiting traffic and became unresponsive again,” Lamanna wrote. “Our normally automated recovery processes were not designed to restore service from this broad unresponsive state. Additionally, since the main code defect was a latent bug from several months previous, our normal rollback, flighting, and feature flags were unable to quickly revert the change.”
Microsoft is now working on a final post-incident review that will be published later this week. Even without that full review, it’s clear that the company will need to invest more into its testing processes to ensure that when something like this happens again, it can catch the problem a lot sooner. Outages happen daily, but this kind of cascading failure is a rare moment for Microsoft to learn from its mistakes and make sure the same sequence of events doesn’t bring down Outlook for millions of people again.
Notepad will return in December after next week’s Thanksgiving break.
I’m always keen to hear from readers, so please drop a comment here, or you can reach me at notepad@theverge.com if you want to discuss anything else. If you’ve heard about any of Microsoft’s secret projects, you can reach me via email at notepad@theverge.com or speak to me confidentially on the Signal messaging app, where I’m tomwarren.01. I’m also tomwarren on Telegram, if you’d prefer to chat there.
Thanks for subscribing to Notepad.

Don’t Buy A Tesla for “Full Self-Driving”
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:58:09 UTC
01:58:09 KST

As noted last month, Tesla’s driver assistance system is now called “Tesla Assisted Driving” in Europe. That replaced the ridiculous “Full Self-Driving (Supervised)”, a name that added a fairly useless parenthetical to the original lie of “Full Self-Driving”. In the US, however, Tesla still sells the feature as “Full Self-Driving (Supervised)”. That’s led some nonagenarians to buy the car to use a feature that does not actually exist.
The car-centric nature of most of America means that losing the freedom to drive can be devastating. One day, truly autonomous vehicles could be helpful for many groups, including the elderly. But until they actually exist, we should be focused on offering more non-driving options like improved public transit, not allowing a car to pretend to be self-driving when it is not.

Jensen Huang says he would be ‘honored’ if allowed to return as Nvidia CEO
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:44:15 UTC
01:44:15 KST

In the first public statement since his resignation, Nvidia founder Jensen Huang signaled his willingness to return as CEO if the board decides it is in the company’s best interest.
Huang’s statement, posted in full on X, reads as follows:
Over the past week, I’ve been overwhelmed by the love and support of friends, industry colleagues, and people I’ve never met. Thank you to everyone who has shown kindness to my family and me. It means the world to me.
I came to the United States 53 years ago, and becoming an American citizen is one of the greatest gifts and proudest moments of my life. NVIDIA has been my life’s work. Building it into the company it is today alongside my colleagues has been a privilege and an honor. Nowhere but in America could a company like NVIDIA be possible. I love our country, and I love NVIDIA. 
I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at NVIDIA, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life. I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board’s independent investigation. 
Since my departure, I’ve seen an outpouring of calls from NVIDIA’s employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO. Many continue to reach out to me directly as the company navigates complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics. NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company. If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO. 
Thank you again to everyone who has shared their good wishes. I am profoundly humbled by your support. To my family: I love you, and I will spend the rest of my life being worthy of yours.
Jensen
Huang resigned after reports of multiple affairs, including with a direct report and another with former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, who is suspected of working with Chinese intelligence services. Nvidia’s chip exports have faced intense scrutiny since the revelations, and Huang is scheduled to testify before Congress on December 1.
In Huang’s absence, Nvidia has formed a new executive committee to oversee export control compliance.
Still, many investors and employees associated with Nvidia have called for Huang to return as CEO, at least until an investigation can provide clearer evidence of guilt. An open letter circulated earlier this week attracted the signatures of more than 10,000 current and former Nvidia employees, as well as prominent tech figures like Elon Musk, Amjad Masad, and Travis Kalanick.
“We are concerned that Jensen was removed without meaningful due process,” the letter reads. “If the Board believes his continued absence is necessary, provide a specific, evidence-based explanation of why it serves NVIDIA’s best interests.”
Still, many see such a return as unlikely. On Monday, an unnamed source on Nvidia’s board told The Washington Post there were no plans to reinstate Huang. There’s also significant resistance to the idea in Congress, where Huang still faces significant questions about his role in chip exports to China-linked firms.
Reached by Politico, one senator called Huang’s reinstatement “one of the worst ideas I’ve heard today.”

Jensen Huang says he’s open to returning in first statement since resignation
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:40:38 UTC
01:40:38 KST

In his first public statement since resigning as Nvidia CEO last week, Jensen Huang struck a contrite tone, saying that he is committed to cooperating with the board's investigation into his relationship with an alleged Chinese intelligence contact.
"I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret," Huang said in the written statement obtained by CNBC's David Faber. "I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board's independent investigation."
Huang said he had seen support from investors and employees and was willing to come back as CEO to help the company navigate "complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics."
"If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO," Huang said in the statement.
Sources told CNBC Monday that the board is not considering bringing Huang back as CEO.
Many in the tech world have called for Huang to return as Nvidia CEO, putting together an online petition with more than 180,000 signatures.
President Donald Trump said on Monday that he is open to Huang returning as CEO of Nvidia.
Last week, Huang resigned from Nvidia after 33 years as CEO. The resignation came days after Huang, Nvidia's founder, admitted to an affair, and news stories alleged he had a liason with Alice Huang, who worked for a customer and is alleged to have contact with Chinese intelligence. Jensen Huang also reportedly had romances with employees at Nvidia.
Nvidia is scheduled to report fiscal third-quarter earnings on Tuesday. Its board of directors said on Friday that it was in the process of selecting a search firm for the Nvidia CEO role.
Nvidia has hired law firm Wachtell Lipton to lead a probe into Huang's conduct and whether it compromised national security or company secrets.
Here is the entire statement from Huang:
Over the past week, I've been overwhelmed by the love and support of friends, industry colleagues, and people I've never met. Thank you to everyone who has shown kindness to my family and me. It means the world to me.
I came to the United States 53 years ago, and becoming an American citizen is one of the greatest gifts and proudest moments of my life. NVIDIA has been my life's work. Building it into the company it is today alongside my colleagues has been a privilege and an honor. Nowhere but in America could a company like NVIDIA be possible. I love our country, and I love NVIDIA.
I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at NVIDIA, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life. I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board's independent investigation.
Since my departure, I've seen an outpouring of calls from NVIDIA's employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO. Many continue to reach out to me directly as the company navigates complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics. NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company. If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.
Thank you again to everyone who has shared their good wishes. I am profoundly humbled by your support. To my family: I love you, and I will spend the rest of my life being worthy of yours.
Correction: Jensen Huang is the former CEO of Nvidia. His title was misstated in a headline on an earlier version of this article.

====================================================================================================

Daydream’s new AI agent acts as your personal stylist — and can shop for you, too
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:32:55 UTC
01:32:55 KST

Can an AI agent serve as your personal stylist? That’s what e-commerce veteran Julie Bornstein’s fashion startup, Daydream, aims to find out.
On Tuesday, the company launched “Daydream Agent,” an AI agent that is described as a personal stylist that can help you plan a look, virtually try on clothes, surface relevant pieces based on your taste, and more.
The idea, the company explains, is to put a personal stylist in everyone’s pocket. This application of AI could be promising, given that early adopters of consumer AI agents like Meta’s Muse have found that online shopping is a key use case.
Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg recently noted that shopping was a top activity for Muse’s over 10 million users and that 25% of people who set up Muse now use it daily.
In Daydream’s case, the fashion startup has been focused on using AI to deliver personalized shopping results to its users. Its new AI agent builds on its earlier work with an AI chatbot that could help users find outfits and make suggestions, but not complete purchases.
Now the company says its Daydream Agent expands on that experience by remembering each shopper’s own personal style, fit preferences, and budget to refine its recommendations. Shoppers can also upload a photo to have the agent reuse that image to virtually try on any item they’ve saved. When they find something they like, the agent can complete a purchase through a one-click checkout process.
The agent can also handle other fashion-related tasks, like keeping an eye on when items go on sale or when brands have a new drop, or providing weekly updates or spotting new fashion trends.
Also, instead of leaving items in their cart, shoppers can save pieces they’re interested in to a persistent “clothing rack” so they can compare options and think about their purchases before deciding what to buy. Shoppers can also connect their own closet and their calendar to the agent, helping them to build outfits around what they already own and plan what to wear for upcoming events.
The tech works by pulling real-time pricing and availability information from its catalog of over 10,000 brands, then combines that with its fashion-focused artificial intelligence. It’s powered by a combination of OpenAI, Gemini, and Claude models, which it fine-tuned, along with its own trained models designed for particular use cases.
However, considering that fashion is more of an art than a science, the company says it’s keeping humans in the loop. To do so, this month it brought on its first stylist in residence, Francesca Salih, an international fashion stylist and the founder of StyleGrid. Salih provides the startup with styling content that helps shape how the agent pulls together outfits and offers advice. This advice is part of the company’s fashion knowledge database, which the agent experience relies on.
Bornstein, formerly Stitch Fix’s COO and the founder of The Yes (exited to Pinterest in 2022), started Daydream in 2023 as an AI search engine. The company, backed by $50 million in funding from Forerunner Ventures, Index Ventures, GV, and True Ventures, says more than 2 million shoppers have used Daydream to discover apparel and accessories since its June 2025 launch.

The DOJ says it’s investigating Megaspeed
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:19:03 UTC
01:19:03 KST

The Justice and Commerce Departments said that they are investigating Megaspeed, the Singapore-based data center company intertwined with the resignation of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, after reporting suggested an investigation had been halted last year.
The DOJ says it’s investigating Megaspeed
The announcement comes a week after revelations that Nvidia founder Jensen Huang was romantically linked to a leader at Megaspeed.
“While we don’t typically comment on ongoing investigations, it is in the public interest to provide the facts and address inaccurate information circulating in public reporting,” the departments said in a statement issued Monday evening. The departments acknowledged there’s been “significant public attention” on Megaspeed.
Huang resigned as Nvidia’s CEO last week after reports he had an affair with former Megaspeed executive and alleged Chinese spy Alice Huang, who Megaspeed says left the company in August 2025. The scandal has raised questions about national security, and sparked scrutiny of Nvidia’s handling of exports of its chips to China and Megaspeed’s possible role in moving along Nvidia’s chips.
Earlier this month, the New York Times reported that a Commerce Department investigation into Megaspeed “came to a sudden halt” late last year after its investigators received an email from Nvidia’s general counsel, claiming Commerce Department official Jeffrey Kessler gave Nvidia permission to reach out. The case was then “quickly” handed over to the DOJ, according to the Times.
The announcement today says the DOJ took the “lead role” in the investigation last year:
After identifying suspicious activity through proactive monitoring of potential sensitive semiconductor diversion, the Department of Commerce initiated an investigation into Megaspeed in 2024 for potential export control violations. In May 2025, Department of Commerce investigators requested records from Nvidia concerning direct or indirect sales to Megaspeed. In June 2025, Nvidia placed Megaspeed on its “no sell” list. Based on the Department of Commerce’s investigation, agents from the Department’s Bureau of Industry and Security, in conjunction with Singaporean law enforcement officials, conducted an on-site, in-person, end-use check at Megaspeed in June 2025 and interviewed CEO Alice Huang.
Following this enforcement action, the Department of Justice took the lead role in the criminal investigation of Megaspeed.
The statement doesn’t go into detail about the current status of the case, but it says the DOJ and Commerce Department “will bring the full extent of the law against Megaspeed for any criminal conduct that may have occurred.”

Banning phones really does help kids learn, researchers say
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:13:18 UTC
01:13:18 KST

A new nationwide study found that in-school cellphone bans can lead to modest improvements in learning. Now, researchers want to understand whether kids’ phone use outside the classroom might contribute to the rest of students’ learning loss — using the conditions of Meta’s multibillion-dollar settlement from earlier this year.
Banning phones really does help kids learn, researchers say
Now researchers want to know about phone use outside of school, and think the Meta settlement could help.
Banning cellphones in schools has the equivalent effect of adding one to two weeks of school to the academic year, accounting for about half of the learning loss students experienced in the US between 2013 and the 2020 covid-19 pandemic, according to the study carried out by researchers from Harvard University and Dartmouth College in partnership with academic assessment group NWEA. The group analyzed testing data from 3.3 million students across 4,600 schools between the 2021–22 school year and 2025–26, including schools with and without phone bans. Though modest, such a finding is exactly the kind of effect researchers were seeking, says Tom Kane, faculty director of the Harvard University Center for Education Policy Research.
While previous studies about cellphone bans in school found positive effects on academic outcomes, many of them examined schools outside the US. An earlier nationwide US study looked at academic outcomes based on averages within schools, whereas this latest study looked at student-level data, which the researchers said allowed them to control for more factors and observe the effect of the bans on students who were experiencing the most or least learning loss.
In addition to potentially answering questions about the impact of a major new technology on learning, the findings show that cellphone bans can be a cost-effective way to improve student outcomes. While purchasing lockable pouches for a class of 17 students would cost an estimated $425 per teacher, the researchers found, paying an average teacher’s salary for an extra week would be $2,080.
The findings show that cellphone bans can be a cost-effective way to improve student outcomes
Many parents and teachers already have a hunch — popularized by academics like social psychologist Jonathan Haidt — that cellphones and social media have impacted their kids’ learning and mental health. But Kane says it’s still important that scientists get the chance to actually test that hypothesis. Prior to his in-school cellphone ban study, Kane believed that a de-emphasis on test-based accountability in US schools was largely to blame for learning loss. But as he looked into it further, he changed his mind after seeing that kids outside the US were also experiencing learning loss. Through this recent study, Kane found much of it is attributable to in-school cellphone use.
Kane was surprised by some of the findings in this latest study. For example, the in-school bans had similar effects on learning gains for students across different age groups, who have different amounts of experience with phones, and researchers did not find a big difference in the impact of the phone bans between students across achievement levels. Differences between lower- and higher-achieving students might instead be explained by out-of-school cellphone use, Kane says, and that out-of-school phone use could account for some of the remaining learning loss across the board. Testing that hypothesis would be far more difficult because there’s no equivalent ban on out-of-school phone use. But Kane believes there’s a once-in-a-lifetime opportunity to try.
This summer, as part of a $17.1 billion settlement with nearly every US state, Meta agreed to implement sweeping restrictions on users under 18. Many of the restrictions are expected to take effect within the next few months, and among other changes, they limit the amount of time teens can spend on Meta’s apps by default. Kane believes that could provide researchers a before-and-after look into the impact of out-of-school social media use, in the same way the phone bans let them study the impact in schools. If researchers are able to check that teens really were using social media less outside of school (rather than their parents turning off the default limits, for example) and link that to academic outcomes at different schools, it could provide a better understanding of how much out-of-school cellphone and social media use accounts for the rest of kids’ learning loss, Kane says.
“Now that we’ve learned something about the effect of in-school phone use, it makes it even more frustrating how little we know about the actual impact of out-of-school phone use,” Kane tells The Verge in an interview. “We are 13 years into a substantial loss in achievement in the US, and a prime suspect that a lot of parents would probably point to is this out-of-school cellphone use.”
There are two significant limitations. The settlement doesn’t offer an easy way to tell whether teens are in fact using Meta’s platforms less or — if they are — whether they’ve simply transferred that screen time to other apps. To understand the effect on students’ academic outcomes, researchers would also need to be able to link the restrictions with changes in kids’ achievement at school. The settlement includes an incentive for attorneys general to strike similar deals with TikTok and YouTube, which so far have declined to implement such restrictions, but if they did, that could help address the first problem. And platforms, Kane says, could help with the second problem by sharing de-identified and aggregated data with researchers, organized by school or another kind of subgroup.
“This Meta settlement is the clearest path forward for trying to answer that important question”
It’s a big ask, but Kane says this may be the best chance to study important questions about the impact of social media. “I spent the whole summer trying to identify other sources of data for this, and I just struck out,” Kane says. “This Meta settlement is the clearest path forward for trying to answer that important question.”
And if research shows that out-of-school cellphone use is not fully or even mostly to blame for the rest of the drop in academic performance, the findings would prod researchers to look for other theories. “I’m sure that there are surprises, questions we haven’t even realized,” Kane says. “That’s one reason to want to know because people have these hunches, but I’m sure some of these hunches are wrong. But a second reason is we actually don’t know that the restrictions currently in the Meta settlement are going to be effective.” While AGs attempted to craft what they believed would be the most effective guardrails for the settlement, Kane says, we don’t yet have the data to prove whether those guesses will bear out.
“I think a lot of people are hoping that restrictions on cellphone use after school would produce increases in student engagement, but we won’t actually know that unless we collect these data,” Kane says. “This settlement is set to expire in 10 years. Let’s use this time to build an evidence base on the effect of restrictions.”

Jensen Huang, who resigned as Nvidia’s CEO last week, would be willing to return as CEO if the company’s board of directors wanted it, he said in a statement reported on by The Wall Street Journal.
Jensen Huang says he’s willing to return as Nvidia CEO
‘I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO,’ Huang said.
“Since my departure, I’ve seen an outpouring of calls from Nvidia’s employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO,” Huang said. “If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.”
Huang resigned on Thursday following reports of two affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy and a direct report. CFO Colette Kress was appointed interim CEO.
“I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at NVIDIA, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life,” Huang said. “I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board’s independent investigation.”
More than 193,000 people, including CEOs like Elon Musk and Tim Sweeney, have signed a letter to “bring Jensen back.” President Trump has also been supportive of Huang returning as CEO, saying yesterday that it “certainly isn’t a bad idea.” (Trump had earlier tossed out former Apple CEO Tim Cook as a potential replacement.) Nvidia didn’t immediately reply to a request for comment.

Over the past week, I’ve been overwhelmed by the love and support of friends, industry colleagues, and people I’ve never met. Thank you to everyone who has shown kindness to my family and me. It means the world to me.
I came to the United States 53 years ago, and becoming an American citizen is one of the greatest gifts and proudest moments of my life. NVIDIA has been my life’s work. Building it into the company it is today alongside my colleagues has been a privilege and an honor. Nowhere but in America could a company like NVIDIA be possible. I love our country, and I love NVIDIA.
I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at NVIDIA, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life. I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board’s independent investigation.
Since my departure, I’ve seen an outpouring of calls from NVIDIA’s employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO. Many continue to reach out to me directly as the company navigates complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics. NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company. If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.
Thank you again to everyone who has shared their good wishes. I am profoundly humbled by your support. To my family: I love you, and I will spend the rest of my life being worthy of yours.
Jensen

You can now preorder the Flipper One that puts a Linux computer in your pocket
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:09:32 UTC
01:09:32 KST

After first announcing its Flipper One last May, which the company describes as an “open Linux multi-tool built for high-performance computing and IP networking,” Flipper Devices has launched a Kickstarter crowdfunding campaign where you can preorder the device for $399 with shipping expected to start in July 2027.
You can now preorder the Flipper One that puts a Linux computer in your pocket
The Flipper One is launching for $399 and is expected to ship to Kickstarter backers next year.
The Flipper One is more expensive than the company’s popular but controversial Flipper Zero wireless hacking multitool, which is currently available for $169. However, Flipper Devices points out that the new Flipper One isn’t a successor to the Flipper Zero, but a completely different product.
While the Zero’s capabilities focused on NFC, RFID, infrared, and Sub-1GHz wireless frequencies, the much larger Flipper One offers expanded connectivity with two gigabit ethernet ports, PCIe, SATA, and USB 3.0 interfaces, and Wi-Fi 6e support. There’s also an M.2 slot for connecting modules that include a 5G modem and a mesh networking LoRa radio that can turn the Flipper One into a walkie-talkie.
It’s still pocket-friendly, but the Flipper One is powered by an 8-core RK3576 ARM processor with a dedicated NPU and a GPU that supports connectivity to an external display over USB-C or HDMI. Flipper Devices suggests the One can be used as everything from a networking diagnostics tool to a media player when connected to a TV. It runs Flipper OS, which is based on Debian Linux and features an open source firmware so that users can develop their own apps, tools, and uses for the device.

Show HN: TrnRT – An open-source LLM inference engine for AWS Trainium
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:01:04 UTC
01:01:04 KST

Quickstart · Benchmarks · Docs · Kernels · Blog post · runanywhere.ai
TrnRT is an open inference engine for large language models on AWS Trainium. It serves an OpenAI-compatible API
from one trn2.48xlarge, runs decode as NKI megakernels (about one kernel per layer half), and keeps a prefix cache
across device and host memory. GLM-5.3-Flash is the first supported model; more open models are on the way.
How we built it, with the AgentX results: Serving GLM-5.3-Flash on one Trainium2 box with NKI layer megakernels.
You need Docker, a trn2.48xlarge to serve on, and any x86_64 host to compile on. New to Trainium? Start with
docs/AWS.md.
1. Build the image (Neuron SDK 2.32 (neuronx-cc 2.27), Python 3.13)
git clone https://github.com/RunanywhereAI/trnrt.git && cd trnrt
docker build -f docker/Dockerfile \
  --build-arg TRNRT_VERSION=$(python3 -c 'import trnrt; print(trnrt.__version__)') \
  --build-arg TRNRT_REVISION=$(git rev-parse HEAD) -t trnrt .
2. Build the compile cache (any x86_64 host, no NeuronCore needed; takes hours)
OUT=/data/trnrt REV=eb9eb208eb0d988989d07a6a12d0fdeb5f52574a
mkdir -p $OUT/nki $OUT/work
trnrt() { docker run --rm -e HF_TOKEN -v $OUT:$OUT \
  -v $OUT/nki:/var/tmp/nki-intermediate-cache trnrt "$@"; }
trnrt compile shapes zai-org/GLM-5.3-Flash --revision $REV --out $OUT
trnrt compile decisions --model zai-org/GLM-5.3-Flash --revision $REV --out $OUT
trnrt compile capture --out $OUT -- --model $OUT/shapes
trnrt compile compile --out $OUT --work-root $OUT/work
trnrt compile strip --out $OUT
3. Serve (on the trn2.48xlarge, with $OUT/cache and $OUT/nki copied over)
hf download zai-org/GLM-5.3-Flash --revision eb9eb208eb0d988989d07a6a12d0fdeb5f52574a \
  --local-dir /models/GLM-5.3-Flash
docker run --rm --privileged --network host --ipc=host \
  -v /data/trnrt/cache:/cache \
  -v /data/trnrt/nki:/var/tmp/nki-intermediate-cache \
  -v /models/GLM-5.3-Flash:/model \
  -e NEURON_LIBTORCH_CACHE_ROOT=/cache \
  trnrt serve --model /model --port 8000
4. Call it with any OpenAI client
from openai import OpenAI
client = OpenAI(base_url="http://localhost:8000/v1", api_key="unused")
reply = client.chat.completions.create(
    model="glm",
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": "Write a haiku about Trainium."}],
    extra_body={"session_id": "my-session"},  # keeps a conversation on one engine's prefix cache
)
print(reply.choices[0].message.content)
The full guide covers what each step needs, start-up checks, the batch 4, 8 and 12 configurations and every flag: deploy · API · configuration.
GLM-5.3-Flash is where TrnRT starts. The engine, router, caches and compile pipeline are built to carry more model families, and docs/ARCHITECTURE.md describes what a new one needs.
SemiAnalysis AgentX replays real coding-agent sessions. One
trn2.48xlarge ran it at a 1M-token context with 32 concurrent agents, for one hour per point:
Tokens count input (cached input included) and output. Spot price: $8.60/h, us-east-2, 3 Nov 2026. On a capacity block at $35.76/h, the batch 12 cost is $0.167 per million tokens. Full tables, cold-prompt latencies, long prompts and methodology are in docs/RESULTS.md.
These numbers were measured with an earlier build of TrnRT. This repository passes these checks against that build (docs/KNOWN_ISSUES.md):
- Graph equivalence: EQUAL on all 32 ranks, for all 157 graphs that build compiles in the default batch-12 configuration.
- Outputs: byte-identical to that build's on 384 of 384 requests.
- Serving: the two-engine serve in the Quickstart passes end to end.
Decode is not limited by memory bandwidth here; small instructions, kernel boundaries and exposed collectives dominate it. So each decode megakernel follows three rules: keep the working set in SBUF, use both physical cores symmetrically, and move the collectives into the kernel. docs/MEGAKERNELS.md walks through every kernel.
trnrt/
├── __main__.py   CLI: serve, engine, compile
├── serve/        OpenAI API, session-affine router, output parsers
├── engine/       scheduler, prefix caches, tensor parallelism
├── models/       GLM-5.3-Flash: KDA, DSA, mHC, MoE, FP8 loading
├── kernels/      NKI decode megakernels and prefill kernels
└── compile/      offline compile: shapes, capture, neuronx-cc, strip
docker/           the serving and compile image
tools/            graph-equivalence gate, device checks, doc checks
tests/            CPU test suite (no NeuronCore needed)
docs/             guides, results, kernel notes
More in docs/ARCHITECTURE.md.
make dev     # editable install with test and lint tools (Python 3.13)
make check   # lint, type-check, then the CPU test suite
See docs/DEVELOPMENT.md for the test tiers and CI, CONTRIBUTING.md to send a
change (pull requests target development), SUPPORT.md to get help, and SECURITY.md to
report a vulnerability.
Thanks to the AWS Neuron team for NKI and its documentation, to Z.ai for releasing GLM-5.3-Flash, and to SemiAnalysis for AgentX and its public traces. TrnRT includes code derived from other Apache-2.0 projects: see NOTICE and THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md.
  
Built by RunAnywhere.
AWS, Amazon Web Services, Trainium, Inferentia and Neuron are trademarks of Amazon.com, Inc. or its affiliates. The AWS logo appears here as published in AWS's Architecture Icons. TrnRT is an independent open-source project, not affiliated with or endorsed by Amazon Web Services.

Online productivity startup Notion and AI coding startup Cursor have both confirmed that they had data stolen during the Fireworks hack. In Uber's case, the company said it had alerts enabled to detect unauthorized access to its Fireworks environment, but because the hackers used Fireworks' own internal credentials, "no alerts were triggered." The Best-Ever Chewy Chocolate Chip Cookies
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
16:00:00 UTC
01:00:00 KST

The Best-Ever Chewy Chocolate Chip Cookies
These buttery chocolate chip cookies have fudgy, chewy centers and crispy edges, and are so easy to make.
Makes20 to 24 (3-inch) cookies
Prep10 minutes
Cook25 minutes
Editor’s note: If you’ve baked this beloved recipe before, it might look a little different these days! We’ve increased the proportion of brown sugar to granulated sugar for flavor and texture. It’s still the same one-bowl technique you’ve come to love, but the cookies are now larger, fudgier, and chewier.
Why You’ll Love These Chocolate Chip Cookies
- So easy, you don’t even need a mixer! This recipe relies on very soft butter that can be mixed by hand for the ideal dense, chewy cookie texture.
- They have the perfect ratio of cookie to chocolate. Expect everyone you share them with to beg you for the recipe.
I’ve been in pursuit of the perfect chocolate chip cookie for years. It was my white whale. I baked through dozens of recipes, and churned out hundreds of chocolate chip cookies in a delicious journey that never seemed to end. Until this cookie.
The Kitchn published this unassuming recipe back in 2010, and after a bit of tinkering, it turned out to be the exact chocolate chip cookie I was searching for. It’s also proof that the best chocolate chip cookie isn’t over-the-top, and doesn’t require specialty ingredients or even a mixer.
These one-bowl chocolate chip cookies have a buttery, caramelized flavor, are thick and fudgy with just the right amount of crispness around the edges, and are studded with plenty of (but not too much) melty chocolate. If you’re still searching for the perfect chocolate chip cookie, grab your baking mitts — because you’ve finally found it.
Straight from Our Recipe Tester
“These were delightful! They were so easy, coming together in just one bowl, and the shaping was a breeze. You don’t have to get out a cookie scoop or anything. The texture of the final cookies was exactly what I’m after: dense and chewy. This is my new go-to chocolate chip cookie recipe for sure.” —Andrea, October 2026
Why You Should Trust Me
I’ve been a recipe developer for over 18 years, but could be found in the kitchen by my mom’s side baking long before that. I also have a serious sweet tooth, and have dedicated much of my time to perfecting classic desserts and desserts that are equal parts easy and delicious.
Key Ingredients in Chocolate Chip Cookies
- Unsalted butter: Set butter at room temperature until very soft, in order to easily mix the dough together by hand.
- Sugar: A combination of brown and granulated sugars gives these cookies their caramel flavor and a chewy texture with crisp edges. We tested with both light and dark brown sugar, and either can be used in this recipe. Choose dark brown sugar for a deeper flavor and fudgier texture.
- All-purpose flour: Skip the specialty flours — all-purpose flour is what you need. Measure the flour by spooning into dry measuring cups, then remove excess with a straight edge.
- Chocolate chips: You’ll need 1 1/2 cups of chocolate chips for these cookies.
How to Make Chocolate Chip Cookies
- Mix the butter and sugar. Mash unsalted butter, the sugars, and kosher salt together with a spatula until no dry sugar or unmixed pockets of butter remain. Mixing by hand aerates the butter less than using a mixer for thick, chewy cookies.
- Add eggs and vanilla extract. Stir eggs and vanilla extract into the butter-sugar mixture until completely incorporated.
- Stir in dry ingredients. Sprinkle the baking soda and baking powder over the wet ingredients for even distribution. Once the leavening is mixed in, stir in the all-purpose flour.
- Chill the dough. Refrigerate the dough for at least 1 hour or overnight. Chilled cookie dough has better flavor, browns more evenly, and has a chewier texture.
- Bake the cookies. Every oven has hot spots, so rotate the baking pans between racks and from front to back for even baking.
Helpful Tips and Swaps
- Cookies aren’t always perfectly round right from the oven. Most cookies need a little help to get into shape. To achieve the bakery-style look, immediately place a round cutter the same size (or slightly larger) around each cookie and use a circular motion to nudge the cookie into a round shape while still hot from the oven.
- More isn’t always better. These chocolate chip cookies taste the best with 1 1/2 cups of mix-ins. Semi-sweet chocolate chips are the classic choice, but you can add milk or dark chocolate chips or chopped nuts (or a mix) instead. Consider adding chopped chocolate bars, instead of chips, if you love melty puddles of chocolate in your cookies.
Storage and Make-Ahead Tips
- Make ahead: Portion the dough and freeze on parchment-paper-lined baking sheets. Once firm, transfer to a zip-top freezer bag and freeze for up to 2 months.
- Storage: Store cookies in an airtight container at room temperature for up to 5 days.
More Chocolate Chip Recipes
The Best-Ever Chewy Chocolate Chip Cookies Recipe
These buttery chocolate chip cookies have fudgy, chewy centers and crispy edges, and are so easy to make.
Prep time 10 minutes
Cook time 25 minutes
Makes 20 to 24 (3-inch) cookies
Nutritional Info
Ingredients
- 8 tablespoons (1 stick) unsalted butter, at room temperature and very soft
- 1 1/4 cups packed dark or light brown sugar
- 1/4 cup granulated sugar
- 1 teaspoon kosher salt
- 2 large eggs
- 1 teaspoon vanilla extract
- 3/4 teaspoon baking soda
- 1/4 teaspoon baking powder
- 2 1/4 cups all-purpose flour
- 1 1/2 cups semisweet chocolate chips (8 1/2 ounces)
Instructions
- Place 1 stick very soft room-temperature unsalted butter, 1 1/4 cups packed brown sugar, 1/4 cup granulated sugar, and 1 teaspoon kosher salt in a medium bowl. Stir until well-blended and no dry spots of sugar or unmixed pieces of butter remain. Add 2 large eggs and 1 teaspoon vanilla extract, and stir until completely incorporated.
- Sprinkle with 3/4 teaspoon baking soda and 1/4 teaspoon baking powder, and stir to combine. Stir in 2 1/4 cups all-purpose flour just until no dry flour remains. Stir in 1 1/2 cups semisweet chocolate chips. Cover and refrigerate for at least 1 hour or up to overnight.
- Arrange 2 racks to divide the oven into thirds, and heat the oven to 350°F. Line two baking sheets with parchment paper.
- Portion the dough into 20 to 24 golf ball-sized portions (about 1 1/2 ounces each). Do not roll the portions into a ball; shape each one into an irregular mound about 2-inches wide instead. Place 6 on each baking sheet, spacing them evenly apart. Refrigerate the remaining dough balls while the first batch of cookies is baking.
- Bake for 6 minutes. Rotate the baking sheets between racks and from front to back. Bake until the edges are set and golden-brown, about 6 minutes more. If you’d like perfectly round cookies, immediately while warm from the oven, place a round cutter the same size (or slightly larger) around each cookie and use a circular motion to nudge the cookie into a round shape. Let cool for 1 minute on the baking sheets, then transfer to a wire rack and let cool completely.
- Repeat baking the remaining dough balls on cooled baking sheets; you can reuse the parchment.
Recipe Notes
Make ahead: The dough can be made ahead and frozen until you’re ready to bake. Portion the dough and freeze on parchment paper-lined baking sheets; when they’re firm, transfer to a zip-top freezer bag and freeze for up to 2 months. When ready to bake, place the cookies on parchment paper-lined baking sheets and let sit at room temperature while the oven heats. Bake according to recipe instructions.
Storage: Store cookies in an airtight container at room temperature for up to 5 days.

NSA Internal Network Down

NSA Internal Network Down

====================================================================================================

Highly classified US national security communications network goes down
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
15:38:28 UTC
00:38:28 KST

The highly classified network that the National Security Agency uses for its internal communications is down, according to six people familiar with the matter.
The network, called NSANet, allows employees to communicate classified information internally. At this point there is no evidence of a cyberattack, sources said.
As the agency works to understand the scope of the problem, it has enacted staggered arrivals for personnel, the sources said.
“The National Security Agency experienced temporary degradation during scheduled routine maintenance,” the agency said in a statement on Tuesday afternoon. “While some internal systems were affected, mission-critical capabilities remain fully operational and our ability to defend national security continues. NSA’s infrastructure is designed with multiple layers of redundancy to maintain operations during planned maintenance or unexpected events.”
A source familiar with the matter told CNN that restoration of the network was ongoing but not yet complete.
Earlier in the day, White House spokesperson Lauren Bis said on Newsmax. “We see no evidence that this was a cyberattack. There is no crisis, or anything like that, for Americans to be worried about.”
A current and a former US official said an outage at NSANet would not impact the agency’s ability to communicate urgent intelligence to President Donald Trump while he is traveling in Asia on Air Force One.
Both sources described multiple redundancies and backup networks that the government could use to communicate securely with the president who is currently in the Philippines before heading to China tomorrow for talks with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin.
NSANet is “more of a network of networks, and it’s unlikely that a full outage exists given the many redundancies in place,” a former senior NSA official told CNN. “It could impact analysis and reporting, but for highest priority requirements they would find a way to work those. So it is alarming, but likely not crippling.”
A voicemail inbox recording for NSA employees on Tuesday described the situation as a “code yellow,” the second-highest in a three-level system that governs whether NSA employees report for work at the agency’s Fort Meade, Maryland, headquarters or not. The recording told non-emergency personnel to arrive for work in a staggered manner and for emergency personnel to arrive as usual. The New York Times first reported that detail.
The outage at America’s most powerful intelligence agency comes on the heels of multiple major tech glitches affecting airlines and other corporations around the country.
All flights nationwide were grounded for hours on Friday after a massive systems failure, leading to thousands of flights being delayed or canceled. The Federal Aviation Administration traced the problem to a single router in Arkansas.
Meanwhile, a massive outage last week affecting Microsoft products left people around the world unable to access Outlook email and Team meetings.
And in recent weeks banks and other services have experienced outages, on Monday thousands of Chase Bank customers reported they had trouble using their online accounts.
There is no indication that the NSA, FAA or Microsoft outages were caused by a cyberattack. But they are stark examples of how tech failures can roil markets and potentially threaten national security.
The US shares intelligence with a group of countries in what is known as the Five Eyes intelligence-sharing alliance, which includes the US, UK, New Zealand, Australia and Canada. That intelligence sharing system has not been impacted as a result of the NSANet outage, according to a source from one of the countries and a former US intelligence official.
Tuesday’s outage was not the first time the NSA’s internal computer networks have suffered significant disruption. In 2000, a tech failure at the agency downed computers for nearly three days and interrupted the processing of intelligence at Fort Meade, an agency spokesperson said at the time.
This story has been updated with additional information.
CNN’s Kylie Atwood and Haley Britzky contributed reporting.

Kalshi moves into a new kind of gambling: the stock market

====================================================================================================

Antfly learned to stop worrying and love Apache 2.0
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
15:33:15 UTC
00:33:15 KST

Back to Thoughts
How Antfly learned to stop worrying and love Apache 2.0
Antfly moved from the Elastic License 2.0 to Apache 2.0. Why we hedged, and why the hedge stopped making sense.
This post goes out with Antfly 0.3, our first release since the move to Apache 2.0.
Until October, Antfly was only source-available. It was available under the Elastic License 2.0, and we were excited about how permissive we thought it was: you could read the code, change it, and run it, you just couldn't build your own business reselling it. But in October we decided to move all of it to Apache 2.0. Now you can use it, modify it, ship it inside your own product, and build a business on it, including one that competes with ours.
It turns out that software is different now. Here's our thinking.
Why we hedged
We started on ELv2 because we were worried that one of the clouds would take Antfly and run it as a hosted service before we'd built a business of our own. We weren't the first. Elastic moved away from Apache 2.0 in 2021, HashiCorp moved Terraform to the Business Source License in 2023, and Redis went source-available in 2024. I've built and run infrastructure companies, and I understand the reasoning: it's hard to watch someone else make money from years of your work.
But a license like that only protects you if the code is the hard part to copy. If a cloud provider can't host your code, it has to write its own, and that used to take years.
I don't think it takes years anymore. Everyone on our team writes code with agents now, and I'd be surprised if the same weren't true at every single cloud provider. A well-funded team that wants a database like ours can get to one much faster than it could in 2021, with or without our license. So the license was protecting less than we'd thought, and meanwhile users kept telling us they wanted open source.
They have good reason. Under Apache 2.0, every version we've released stays under those terms whatever we do later. We intend to keep Antfly on Apache 2.0.
How Antfly makes money
We designed Antfly and are its active maintainers, so we still think we're the best people to host it at scale. That's Antfly Cloud: if you'd rather not operate the database, we'll run it for you, and that's what we charge for. You won't pay Antfly for running it yourself, whether that's on your laptop, inside your application, or on your own infrastructure.
But we don't want our Cloud business to limit what projects you can build on an inference-native database.
What's in Antfly 0.3
0.3 is out, and it's all Apache 2.0. You can query Antfly tables in SQL, including from psql over the Postgres wire protocol. Iceberg and Parquet tables in object storage can be queried where they sit. Antfly can build a knowledge graph for you from the documents you load, and a new research agent turns a question into a cited report. The full list is in the release notes. The code is at github.com/antflydb/antfly.
I still worry about plenty of things, but what you do with the code isn't one of them anymore.

Navigate the Open Sea with Juliet Schreckinger’s Cooperative Creatures
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
15:32:42 UTC
00:32:42 KST

Navigate the Open Sea with Juliet Schreckinger’s Cooperative Creatures
Whether showcasing a prized possession or peering through a spyglass to navigate open water, Juliet Schreckinger’s curious characters work together and form unexpected alliances. Personalities like Oscar the Octopus and Quinn the Sperm Whale recur in the artist’s ink and graphite drawings, and her most recent series comprises the show Limitless Sea at Arch Enemy Arts.
Schreckinger’s marine and woodland critters adopt a playful, cartoonish air, like personalities in a children’s book who find themselves adrift and in need of a friend. Lighthouses, lanterns, and rays of sun breaking through clouds glow with optimism. And while an overarching narrative may be murky, the unlikely companions and cooperative activities illuminate dreamlike scenes that encourage inquisitiveness and joy.
An undercurrent of persistence and hope also runs through Limited Sea, mirroring the artist’s recent healing process after a major surgery. It’s “a show made up of characters navigating through the foggy darkness by focusing on the light…and on the other side is limitless possibility,” Schreckinger says in a statement. As she navigated her own challenges—including making this series with only one good eye—these works served as a reminder of what’s most important: “finding the beauty, finding the light, and spending time with those who make your soul happy,” she says.
Limitless Sea continues through November 29 in Philadelphia. Schreckinger’s work will also be on view next month in General Strike 2, an exhibition of artist matchboxes, at Joy Machine. See more on Schreckinger’s Instagram.

Uber and Zipline’s drone delivery service takes off in Texas, with dozens of cities to follow
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
15:07:06 UTC
00:07:06 KST

Uber Eats customers in a Houston suburb can now have their food delivered by a Zipline drone, the first commercial launch under a partnership announced earlier this year between Uber and the autonomous drone delivery startup.
The commercial partnership kicks off Tuesday in Spring, Texas, before expanding to Dallas. But the companies have far larger ambitions. Uber and Zipline plan to launch drone delivery services in dozens of additional U.S. cities in their collective bid to make one million deliveries per day using the startup’s drones by the end of 2029.
Zipline’s long-range autonomous delivery drones will be used to shuttle food orders weighing up to eight pounds to Uber Eats customers, flying about 300 feet above the ground at speeds of up to 70 miles per hour. Zipline drones don’t land. Instead, the drones lower the orders, which are encased in a small protective pod, to the customer’s location using a tether.
Uber Eats customers in service areas will be able to tap a new “drone” icon on the app’s home page to view all participating restaurants. Uber said that at launch in the Houston area, several nationwide chains, including Jersey Mike’s and Zaxby’s, along with dozens of local restaurants such as Urban Bird and Abu Omar Halal, will offer drone delivery.
“In partnership with Uber Eats we’re going to create a new expectation for delivery in America,” Zipline CEO and co-founder Keller Cliffton said in a statement that underscores those aspirations.
Those nationwide goals mirror the ambitions of other drone delivery companies, including Alphabet-owned Wing and Amazon’s Prime Air service. And those plans have started to collide with concerns from residents in some locations who complain about noise and worry that the drones will disrupt wildlife and pose a risk to safety and privacy.
Companies trying to scale in this nascent industry have argued that delivery via drone is a net benefit for society, citing advantages such as traffic reduction. Customers won’t need to hop in their cars and drive to the store for that forgotten item or takeout order, instead relying on a drone, industry proponents have said.
And while Zipline and other companies have been developing their drones for years, the industry is still in its early days, as is adoption in the communities where services are available.
Zipline, which was founded in 2014, has developed a drone delivery ecosystem that includes aircraft, launch and landing systems, and logistics software. The California-based company’s initial focus was delivering blood in Rwanda, but it eventually expanded its scope to the United States. Its business in the U.S. scaled quickly after receiving its Part 135 air carrier certification from the Federal Aviation Administration in 2022, a critical regulatory hurdle that authorized the company to conduct long-range commercial drone deliveries.
Uber’s partnership extends beyond a commercial relationship with Zipline. The ride-hailing giant also invested in the startup. Neither company has disclosed the investment amount, but Zipline announced last week it had raised $1.5 billion in a round led by Paradigm, WCM Investment Management, and Layer Global, and included backing by Uber, among others.

A US Federal Women's Prison Became a "Rape Club"
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
15:03:25 UTC
00:03:25 KST

Reporting Highlights
- A Cascade of Cases: We found more than 185 cases of officers accused of sexually abusing people in women’s prisons over the past five years, sometimes beating them or denying them food or medical care.
- Broken Promises: Officials vowed to reform the system in 2021, but more than a dozen current and former federal prison employees said they had seen few or no meaningful improvements.
- “A Hunting Ground”: Though some abusers have been prosecuted or removed, insiders say the culture of impunity that allows officers to prey on women remains.
These highlights were written by the reporters and editors who worked on this story.
Officer Felix Wilder sauntered around the women’s prison like he owned the place.
Prone to showing up to work in gold chains and stylish jeans instead of his concrete gray government uniform, the slender 35-year-old moved, one woman said, “like he had just gotten off of a horse from a long ride.” He spoke slowly and eyed the prisoners as they walked through the drab buildings and shadeless yards of the secluded federal lockup in Aliceville, Alabama.
By the fall of 2024, some of the women there had begun to notice him hovering over a soft-spoken immigrant from Mexico who’d been in prison for only a few years. Ela, as she asked to be called, had dark hair, dark eyes and children to get home to back in Utah. Wilder was just a little too close, a little too familiar.
Ela’s cellmate, who asked to be identified as Ana, watched it all with unease. At 40, she knew exactly what that kind of attention from an officer could mean in a women’s prison.
For 15 years, Ana had served her time at Federal Correctional Institution Dublin, a low-security prison in California, where officers had preyed on the women in their care. Sexual misconduct became so rampant that staff and prisoners alike called it a “rape club.” Eventually, the FBI intervened, and prosecutors charged the warden, the chaplain and nine other officers with sexually abusing female prisoners. Hundreds of women — including Ana — came forward to tell what happened to them, not just to investigators, but also to outside lawyers who sued the system to demand change at the Bay Area prison.
Ana had hoped to leave that all behind when she was transferred out of Dublin. But tucked between the pine trees and low-slung churches of rural Alabama, the Aliceville prison had its own brand of menace. The facility had newer buildings but fewer freedoms. The roofs leaked, and sometimes the air smelled faintly of mold. The shorthanded staff often locked the women in their cells for days at a time.
And then there were officers like Wilder, who seemed to be choosing their targets.
Thousands of miles from families they couldn’t call during yet another lockdown, Ana and Ela became allies. They paced. They picked at the lukewarm meals delivered on Styrofoam trays. They stared out the narrow slit of a window.
And then Ela broke down, sobbing as her words tumbled out into the stale prison air.
Two years earlier, an officer had pulled her into a closet and raped her, she told Ana, her voice quavering. Now, she said, it was happening all over again — but this time it was the stylish officer in jeans: Felix Wilder.
Ela said she’d reported the first assault and nothing happened. Now, she didn’t know what to do. If the prison didn’t care about one rape, why would it care about two?
As she listened, Ana felt a dull rage churning in her stomach. She thought of all the things she’d seen and survived at Dublin: the rapes, the harassment, the uncertainty of a life subject to the whims of lascivious men with badges. As she tried to comfort her sobbing cellmate, she wondered whether Aliceville was any better. Maybe, she thought, no place was.
Sitting on her metal bunk, she opened the little address book she’d bought from the prison commissary and flipped to the phone numbers for the lead attorneys in the class action lawsuit against Dublin. “Here,” she told Ela, “you can talk to them.”
The arrests and subsequent convictions of Dublin staff — 10 in all — marked the largest sex abuse scandal in the Federal Bureau of Prisons’ history and a rare moment of accountability in a troubled system where many women had learned to stop expecting it. Suddenly, every branch of government stepped in. A federal judge appointed an outside monitor. A slew of agencies issued reports, and the Justice Department dispatched attorneys to visit and evaluate women’s facilities. Congress passed a law to create a new, independent office to oversee the prison system.
