Meta launches Direct, a standalone messaging app for Instagram
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
15:01:46 UTC
00:01:46 KST

Meta is testing Direct, a new stand-alone messaging app that houses Instagram’s direct messages. Direct is initially launching in Turkey and Thailand, and can be used with or without an Instagram account.
With Direct, Meta is likely looking to retain users who use Instagram primarily, or even exclusively, for messaging. Over the years, people have often sought various ways to use Instagram’s direct messaging features without having to deal with the rest of the platform.
Direct appears to be Meta’s answer to that demand, as the company says it is designed for chatting one-on-one or in groups with friends and family.
The new app also gives the company a way to keep these users within its ecosystem rather than risk losing them altogether, and count them as active users within its ecosystem, even if they don’t use Instagram itself.
Besides usual messaging features like reactions, voice notes, and emojis, Direct also features a “Draw” feature that lets you doodle and sketch directly inside chat threads. There’s also “Notes,” which are the short status updates that appear at the top of your Instagram DMs.
Meta says users can create custom stickers, add audio effects to replies, and personalize chat backgrounds with custom themes.
Users in Turkey and Thailand can sign up for Direct using their email address or phone number. Those with an existing Instagram account can log in using the same username and password, and their existing conversations will be synced across both apps.
Meta says only people in your contacts or those you’re already connected with can start conversations or add you to group chats. Users can also report, block, restrict, or remove contacts at any time.
The company also says it will use its age-estimation technology to identify and address potentially inappropriate interactions involving young users. Parents will also have access to supervision tools that let them see who their teens are messaging, manage safety settings, and set daily usage limits.
Teens who use their Instagram username and password to access Direct will have to work with all the same messaging restrictions as Instagram’s Teen Accounts.
Meta did not say when or if it will bring Direct to additional countries.

====================================================================================================

Voyage Labs raises $12 million for a specialized AI travel assistant
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
15:00:00 UTC
00:00:00 KST

Planning a trip is a favorite demo for personal AI assistants — you can try it with Muse, or with Gemini, or half a dozen other choices. But while most pitches use travel-planning as part of a general-purpose agent pitch, a new startup from a Google Maps alum is betting that a specialized travel-planning agent can beat the competition.
The company is called Voyage Labs, and on Tuesday it announced $12 million in seed funding led by Kleiner Perkins, with participation from SignalFire and SV Angel. The company plans to use the funding to expand supplier partnerships and grow its engineering team.
Voyage Labs’ flagship product, called Atlas, is meant to work as a personal assistant for your trip-planning, available today in private beta.
“People can use it to plan and book and manage their entire journey, from the time they have an inspiration to the time they come back home,” says co-founder and CEO Manik Gupta.
Atlas is designed to serve as an always-on assistant throughout the planning process, fielding ideas and doing research on every idea that’s raised. The long time horizon means Atlas can tailor recommendations based on weeks of conversation, while also monitoring real-time price fluctuations and ultimately booking flights and hotels.
For the Voyage founders, it’s an ideal match between technology and use case. “Trip planning is a project that has its own lifecycle,” says Yogesh Jain, co-founder and CTO of the company. “You’re planning across days and weeks — so long-running agents as a technology matches that very well.”
Travel assistants are already a crowded field, but Voyage will be starting with a lot of experience in the space. Gupta was previously the director of Product Management for Google Maps, followed by four years as chief product officer at Uber. As he puts it, “I spent eight years where my only job was to wake up in the morning and go build the map. I’m just very obsessed with that problem.”
The biggest question is how Atlas will compete with the multi-purpose AI assistants built by frontier labs. But as Gupta notes, the space is still changing fast, and it’s hard for anyone to be sure what form factors will thrive in the coming years. Ultimately, Voyage is betting that travel is specialized enough to need a specialized product.
“We are making a bet that this vertical around travel and outdoors is a large enough vertical that it needs a focused product solution,” Gupta told TechCrunch. “Ultimately, we believe we can actually stitch together an end-to-end experience that will help users.”

====================================================================================================

Instagram’s new tool for creators targets unauthorized reposts and AI-modified videos
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
15:00:00 UTC
00:00:00 KST

Instagram is introducing a new tool to help creators protect their original videos, the Meta-owned company announced Tuesday. The feature, called “content protection,” is already available on Facebook and is designed to detect when creators’ original videos are being used without their permission. The company is also expanding the tool to address AI content, not just conventional reposting.
The announcement comes in response to creators’ concerns over how easily original content can be copied and redistributed across social media platforms, especially considering how generative AI has made it easier to manipulate existing videos.
Instagram says the content protection tool continuously scans Instagram for unauthorized uses of creators’ protected videos. When Instagram detects a match for one of a creator’s Reels uploaded by another user, it sends the creator an alert. Creators can then choose to block the video from being viewed on both Instagram and Facebook to hide the matched content. They can also choose to add an “original by” label on the content that links viewers of the matching video back to their original video.
Additionally, creators can choose to keep the match visible while monitoring its performance or release their claim on the reel, allowing it to remain visible on Meta’s platforms.
The tool will also begin detecting AI-generated videos that reuse audio from creators’ original content, even when the visuals have been altered. This update will initially be tested with a small group of creators in the coming weeks. The company plans to expand access to this feature to creators who enroll in content protection on both Instagram and Facebook.
Instagram says it’s exploring ways to limit the reach of accounts and content that repeatedly use AI tools to repost others’ work.
Creators who are eligible for content protection will see a notification in their activity feed. Once they enroll, every original video they post to Instagram going forward is automatically protected. They can also choose to retroactively protect eligible videos they’ve already posted by manually selecting them.
If creators have permitted certain profiles to use their work, such as their second profile or a brand, they can add them to their allow list so their videos won’t be flagged as matches.
Tuesday’s announcement marks another step in Instagram’s ongoing efforts to give creators greater control over their content and protect the originality of their work.

====================================================================================================

In 27,280 coding agent runs, 64% of inference spend went to runs that failed
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:53:43 UTC
23:53:43 KST

TL;DR
We partnered with Baseten to build the largest-scale harness leaderboard using 83B tokens across 27,280 agent runs. We wanted to answer two questions: when you switch your model, should you consider switching the harness too? What is the best harness for each model?
Previously, our v1.0 FrontierHarness leaderboard ranked harnesses on a single model, Kimi K3.
V1.1 expands the benchmark to 22 harnesses × 5 models × 62 tasks × 4 attempts, 76× the size of v1.0.
Through our experiments, we found that you should consider switching harnesses when switching models to maximize your experience.
Each model had a different best-value (pass rate vs. cost) harness: Codex CLI with GLM-5.3, Pi with GLM-5.3 Flash, ZCode with DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017, Oh My Pi with Muse and Alma with Kimi-K3. Cost ranks moved far between models.
By cost per task, the median harness moved 11 of 22 places, and Codex CLI went from 1st with GLM-5.3 to 16th with Kimi-K3.
Result
Figure 1. Cost per task and pass rate of each harness
Each point is one harness: further left is a lower average cost per task, and higher is a higher pass rate. The orange line joins the harnesses that no other harness beats in both cost and pass rate. Select a model to filter all figures of this post.
Over all models, four harnesses are on the line: OpenCode v2 (lowest cost per task, $0.561), Pi, Codex CLI and ZCode (highest pass rate, 61.9%). The others cost up to 1.6 times as much and passed 50.6% to 60.2%.
Each card names the best-value harness for one model: the lowest cost per pass, unless another harness costs at most 30% more per pass and passes at least 5 points more often. This rule picked ZCode over OpenCode v1 with DeepSeek (25% more cost, 7.7 more points) and Alma over Oh My Pi with Kimi-K3 (9% more cost, 6.5 more points).
Why We Built FrontierHarness Eval v1.1
V1.0 ran nine harnesses with one model. Their pass rates were within 17 points of each other, but the median cost per pass differed by 17 times. We thought that this came mostly from the pair of harness and model: a harness that is cheap with one model can be expensive with another.
V1.1 runs each harness with five models.
What We Tested
Harnesses. 18 harnesses. The four DeepSeek Harness profiles have different tool sets, and OpenCode ran at two versions. Each counts separately: 22 configurations. Table 2 in the appendix gives their versions.
Models. Baseten served all five: GLM-5.3 (zai-org/GLM-5.3), GLM Flash (zai-org/GLM-5.3-Flash), DeepSeek (deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017), Muse (meta-models/muse-spark-open) and Kimi-K3 (moonshotai/Kimi-K3).
Tasks. 62 terminal tasks. The time limits are those of the tasks, from 15 minutes to 8 hours.
Setup and Scoring
Configuration and Runtime
We ran the Harbor evaluation with each harness’s default settings, because we think this is closest to how people use a harness every day. Each harness used the same version and settings with all models, at the maximum reasoning effort.
Figure 2. How Harbor and Runta divide the work in each trial
Each trial gets a new Runta runtime, which is deleted at the end. Thus no trial can see the files of an earlier trial.
Scoring Method
Table 1. Methodology
A cell is one harness and model pair. We use the two cost metrics of our dashboard:
- Average cost per task: the mean cost of the priced trials of each task, then the mean over the tasks. Figure 1 and the cost ranks use it (Figures 4 and 5).
- Cost per pass: the average cost per task divided by the pass rate. The best-value harness uses it (Figures 3, 6 and 7).
We pick the best value by cost per pass because we want harnesses at the top left of a chart of pass rate against cost per task: a high pass rate at a low cost. Draw a line from the origin to the point of a harness. Its slope, pass rate ÷ cost, is the passes per dollar. Cost per pass is the inverse of this slope, so the lowest cost per pass has the steepest line.
Audits of the trial logs flagged 216 trials for reward hacking, and 163 of them were online lookups. These trials score 0. Table 3 in the appendix gives the count for each harness and model.
Results by Model
Figure 3. Cost and pass rate of each harness with each model
A deeper cell costs less than the others in its column. Codex CLI was the cheapest per pass with GLM-5.3, $0.803, but 12th with Muse and 10th with Kimi-K3. Over all models, it cost the least per pass, $0.937, and Antigravity CLI the most, $1.63.
How Sure Are These Results?
We drew the 62 tasks again 4,000 times and chose the best-value harness each time. Codex CLI stayed first with GLM-5.3 in 99.6% of the draws. The other four winners are the most likely of several close harnesses: Oh My Pi with Muse (53%), ZCode with DeepSeek (47%), Pi with GLM Flash (42%) and Alma with Kimi-K3 (25%).
The Harness Changes the Cost
The model sets the general level of the cost, because the list prices of the models differ by 20 times or more (Figure 9). We took the average cost per task of the 110 cells on a log scale, so that a doubling counts the same with each model. A two-way analysis of variance divides the spread of these costs into three parts:
- Model, 92.8%: the differences between the five models.
- Harness, 2.3%: the part of the effect of a harness that is the same with every model.
- Pair, 4.9%: the rest. It also contains noise, because each cell has one value.
So with the model fixed, 68% of the variation between harnesses (4.9 of 7.2 points) depends on the pair, or is noise.
With the Model Fixed, the Harness Changes the Cost by 1.8 to 5.0 Times
Figure 4. Lowest-cost and highest-cost harness for each model
Each row is a model, on a log scale of average cost per task. The ratio divides the highest cost by the lowest.
To test for chance, we shuffled the trials between the harnesses of each model 300 times and calculated the ratio again. “Chance” is the median of these ratios, 1.2 to 1.8 times. All five real ratios are larger than every shuffle (p = 0.003), so the spread comes from the harnesses, not from the noise of reruns. The largest is with DeepSeek: Cline CLI cost 5.0 times as much as OpenCode v1, for 2.0 more points. With the other models, the cheapest harness per task was at most 0.4 points behind the most expensive one.
The Cheapest Harness per Task Changes With the Model
Figure 5. Cost rank of each harness with each model (1 = lowest cost per task)
Each line is a harness, with the cheapest at the top. The five highlighted lines are the cheapest harnesses per task with GLM-5.3, GLM Flash, DeepSeek, Muse and Kimi-K3.
Codex CLI was 1st with GLM-5.3 and 16th with Kimi-K3. DeepSeek Harness: Creator moved the most, from 1st with Muse to 21st with GLM Flash. OpenCode v1 was 1st with DeepSeek and 13th with Muse.
Reruns do not explain these moves. We divided the attempts into two halves and ranked the harnesses by cost in each half. With the same model, the two rankings had a mean rank correlation of 0.71. With two different models, it was 0.26.
A Higher Cost Does Not Give a Higher Score
Figure 6. Lowest and highest cost per pass for each model
This is Figure 4 for cost per pass. After each ratio is the rank correlation of the cost per task and the pass rate of the harnesses, from −0.39 to +0.22. A positive value means that the more expensive harnesses passed more often.
The harness with the highest pass rate was 5th to 20th of 22 in cost per task. With four of the five models, the most expensive harness per pass passed less often than the cheapest one.
Does a Simpler Harness Cost Less?
No. We compared four factors with the cost per pass. Prompt size, tool count and cache hit rate are not the main cost factors. Steps are.
- Start tokens: the input tokens of the first agent-loop request: the system prompt, the tool definitions and the task.
- Tools: the tools that the first request offers.
- Cache hit rate and steps: the mean over the passed trials. A step is one entry of the agent trajectory.
Figure 7. Four factors of each harness and the cost per pass
Over the 22 harnesses, the rank correlation of the steps per pass and the cost per pass was +0.51 (p = 0.015). For the other three factors, it was −0.03 to +0.02 (p ≥ 0.88). Within each model, tool calls tracked cost as well as steps. Table 4 below and Table 5 in the appendix give the values.
Each step sends the context again, so a trial used 2.0 to 4.8 million input tokens on average, by harness, and the largest first request, 21,365 tokens, is less than 1% of that. mini-SWE-agent has the smallest first request, 882 tokens, but took 38 steps per pass and was 17th of 22 in cost per pass.
Table 4. Start tokens, tools, cache hit rate, steps and cost per pass of each harness
Where the Cost Comes From
Figure 8. Inference cost of each harness, by token type or by trial outcome
All costs are inference estimates at list prices. The pass rate of the harness on the model card has an outline.
Over all trials, input from the cache was 42% of the cost, output 33% and other input 25%.
Tokens and the Cache
The cache makes repeated input much cheaper. Cached input costs 2% (DeepSeek) to 20% (GLM Flash) of the price of other input (Figure 9).
Figure 9. List prices of each model (USD for one million tokens)
One pair shows the effect of the cache. Cline CLI with DeepSeek (highest cost per task) read 86% of its input from the cache, and OpenCode v1 read 97%. It also sent twice as much input. Input that did not come from the cache was 57% of its cost.
Most of the Cost Goes to Trials That Do Not Pass
64.5% of the list-price cost of the scored runs went to trials that did not pass: 52.7% to failures and 11.8% to timeouts.
What the Best-Value Harnesses Share
The traces of the five best-value harnesses show three common design choices. These are observations, not tests.
One shell tool does most of the work. By harness, it got 61% to 93% of the tool calls. Pi’s default prompt has four tools and sends file searches to bash:
selectedTools: [...(input.selectedTools ?? ["read", "bash", "edit", "write"])],
addRule("Use bash for file operations like ls, rg, find");
Each request only adds to the one before it. The start of the context does not change, so the provider reads it from the cache: 91.1% to 94.0% of input in passed trials (Table 4). But 18 of the 22 harnesses read 91% or more, so this does not separate the five. Each step pays the full input price for only about 500 new tokens:
# Pi, GLM-5.3, merge-diff-arc-agi-task (trial cd2f6dfd)
request  prompt tokens  cached tokens
   8        15,954         15,616
   9        16,351         15,936
  10        16,999         16,320
No subagents and almost no summaries. Each of the five used a subagent in at most 6 trials, too few to tell if subagents help. Of the five, only Codex CLI summarized, in 8 trials (the 11 Alma trials in Table 6 only drop old images and reminders). Codex CLI summarizes at 90% of the window (Table 6):
.map(|context_window| (context_window * 9) / 10);
The five also share a weakness. Failed runs end with a confident answer. About three in four failed trials ended with a final answer, not at the time limit: from 68% with Pi to 82% with Codex CLI. Few said that the work was incomplete:
# Codex CLI, GLM-5.3, cancel-async-tasks (trial 37182b0e)
Agent:    - Validated with system Python, including concurrency and
            cancellation-cleanup tests.
Verifier: FAILED test_tasks_cancel_above_max_concurrent
          assert stdout.count("Cleaned up.") == 2
          AssertionError: assert 0 == 2
Trials that did not pass took 60% (Codex CLI) to 69% (Alma) of the cost of each harness. A check before the harness stops could save part of it, but we did not test this.
Limitations and Future Work
- Open models, one provider. Closed models can give different results. The cache and the prices are those of Baseten, and another provider can change the cost ranks.
We plan two next steps:
- Closed models. We will add closed models, such as Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-6 Astra and Gemini 4 Carbon.
- Long-horizon work. We will study how a harness that runs continuously in the cloud improves itself during long work.
Conclusion
- When you change the model, think about changing the harness too. Test the candidate harnesses with your model on a private dataset of your own tasks, large enough to see past the noise.
- The model sets most of the cost, and the harness is second. The model explains 92.8% of the variance of cost per task. With the model fixed, the most expensive harness cost 1.9 to 4.9 times as much per pass as the cheapest (1.8 to 5.0 times per task), so compare the cost when you pick a harness.
- A higher cost does not buy a higher pass rate. For each model, the rank correlation of cost per task and pass rate was −0.39 to +0.22.
- Size does not predict cost. mini-SWE-agent, the smallest harness, was 19th of 22 by cost per task over all models.
- Most of the cost, 64.5%, went to runs that did not pass. With the five best-value harnesses, 68% to 82% of the failed runs ended with a confident final answer, not at the time limit.
- With a cheap model that has a wide range of pass rates, pick a harness with a high pass rate. Pass rates varied by harness, from 54.0% to 69.0% with DeepSeek. But each pass rate has a 95% interval of about ±10 points, so a gap of a few points is a tie.
- For harness developers: cut steps, not just prompt tokens. Prompt size, tool count and cache hit rate did not predict cost per pass. Steps did, because every step sends the whole context again.
Appendix: Reference Tables
Table 2. Harness configurations
Table 3. Reward-hacked trials of each harness
Table 5. Prompt and tools of each harness
References
- FrontierHarness dashboard
- Harbor and Harbor Hub, where the ten jobs are stored
- Introducing FrontierHarness Eval, our v1.0 results
- Baseten, Model APIs, the inference provider for performant open models
- Runta Harbor guide and Runta pricing

64.5% of the list-price cost of the scored runs went to trials that did not pass: 52.7% to failures and 11.8% to timeouts.
What the Best-Value Harnesses Share
The traces of the five best-value harnesses show three common design choices. These are observations, not tests.
One shell tool does most of the work. By harness, it got 61% to 93% of the tool calls. Pi’s default prompt has four tools and sends file searches to bash:
selectedTools: [...(input.selectedTools ?? ["read", "bash", "edit", "write"])],
addRule("Use bash for file operations like ls, rg, find");
Each request only adds to the one before it. The start of the context does not change, so the provider reads it from the cache: 91.1% to 94.0% of input in passed trials (Table 4). But 18 of the 22 harnesses read 91% or more, so this does not separate the five. Each step pays the full input price for only about 500 new tokens:
# Pi, GLM-5.3, merge-diff-arc-agi-task (trial cd2f6dfd)
request  prompt tokens  cached tokens
   8        15,954         15,616
   9        16,351         15,936
  10        16,999         16,320
No subagents and almost no summaries. Each of the five used a subagent in at most 6 trials, too few to tell if subagents help. Of the five, only Codex CLI summarized, in 8 trials (the 11 Alma trials in Table 6 only drop old images and reminders). Codex CLI summarizes at 90% of the window (Table 6):
.map(|context_window| (context_window * 9) / 10);
The five also share a weakness. Failed runs end with a confident answer. About three in four failed trials ended with a final answer, not at the time limit: from 68% with Pi to 82% with Codex CLI. Few said that the work was incomplete:
# Codex CLI, GLM-5.3, cancel-async-tasks (trial 37182b0e)
Agent:    - Validated with system Python, including concurrency and
            cancellation-cleanup tests.
Verifier: FAILED test_tasks_cancel_above_max_concurrent
          assert stdout.count("Cleaned up.") == 2
          AssertionError: assert 0 == 2
Trials that did not pass took 60% (Codex CLI) to 69% (Alma) of the cost of each harness. A check before the harness stops could save part of it, but we did not test this.
Limitations and Future Work
- Open models, one provider. Closed models can give different results. The cache and the prices are those of Baseten, and another provider can change the cost ranks.
We plan two next steps:
- Closed models. We will add closed models, such as Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-6 Astra and Gemini 4 Carbon.
- Long-horizon work. We will study how a harness that runs continuously in the cloud improves itself during long work.
Conclusion
- When you change the model, think about changing the harness too. Test the candidate harnesses with your model on a private dataset of your own tasks, large enough to see past the noise.
- The model sets most of the cost, and the harness is second. The model explains 92.8% of the variance of cost per task. With the model fixed, the most expensive harness cost 1.9 to 4.9 times as much per pass as the cheapest (1.8 to 5.0 times per task), so compare the cost when you pick a harness.
- A higher cost does not buy a higher pass rate. For each model, the rank correlation of cost per task and pass rate was −0.39 to +0.22.
- Size does not predict cost. mini-SWE-agent, the smallest harness, was 19th of 22 by cost per task over all models.
- Most of the cost, 64.5%, went to runs that did not pass. With the five best-value harnesses, 68% to 82% of the failed runs ended with a confident final answer, not at the time limit.
- With a cheap model that has a wide range of pass rates, pick a harness with a high pass rate. Pass rates varied by harness, from 54.0% to 69.0% with DeepSeek. But each pass rate has a 95% interval of about ±10 points, so a gap of a few points is a tie.
- For harness developers: cut steps, not just prompt tokens. Prompt size, tool count and cache hit rate did not predict cost per pass. Steps did, because every step sends the whole context again.
Appendix: Reference Tables
Table 2. Harness configurations
Table 3. Reward-hacked trials of each harness
Table 5. Prompt and tools of each harness
References
- FrontierHarness dashboard
- Harbor and Harbor Hub, where the ten jobs are stored
- Introducing FrontierHarness Eval, our v1.0 results
- Baseten, Model APIs, the inference provider for performant open models
- Runta Harbor guide and Runta pricing

Isomorphic Labs raises $5B as it looks to take drug candidates to clinical trials
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:50:58 UTC
23:50:58 KST

Isomorphic Labs, the AI drug-discovery platform that was spun out of Google’s DeepMind in 2021, has raised $5 billion in a Series C funding round as it seeks to take its drug candidates to clinical trials.
The funding was co-led by Greenoaks and Sequoia Capital, with new investor Andreessen Horowitz and other investors also participating. All of the company’s existing backers, including Alphabet’s venture divisions GV and Capita G, Thrive Capital, MGX, Temasek, and the U.K. Sovereign AI Fund, also invested.
Isomorphic Labs builds an AI drug-design engine that uses a slew of simulation AI models that explore various areas of chemistry to help the company design drugs. The company, whose work builds on DeepMind’s breakthrough AlphaFold protein-folding AI model, said its program has yielded “positive preclinical results” in oncology and immunology. The lab has struck deals with pharma giants like Eli Lilly, Novartis, and Johnson & Johnson.
The new funding, which comes on the heels of a $2.1 billion Series B round in May, will be used to continue developing its drug-design engine, as well as to foray into more types of medicine.
The Series C is Isomorphic’s first fundraise since its founder and CEO, Demis Hassabis, left his position as head of DeepMind to focus on drug discovery, which also saw him moving into a chairman role at DeepMind. Hassabis and then-director of DeepMind John Jumper won the Nobel Prize in chemistry for the lab’s work on AlphaFold, a system that predicts the 3D structure of proteins with nothing more than their genetic sequence.
Isomorphic did not disclose its new valuation. Bloomberg reported earlier this month that the lab was looking to raise funding at a $50 billion valuation.

This E Ink photo frame can double as your family’s digital dashboard
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:49:21 UTC
23:49:21 KST

Trmnl, a company best known for its highly customizable E Ink dashboards that can function as anything from a desktop calendar to a stock tracker, has introduced a new display that’s also well suited for showing artwork and photos. It’s not the company’s first color E Ink device, but instead of being limited to just four colors, the new Frame can display thousands of shades using a 31.5-inch E Ink Spectra 6 panel, similar to what you’ll find in products from Aura and Pocketbook.
This E Ink photo frame can double as your family’s digital dashboard
Trmnl’s latest digital dashboard features a large color E Ink screen to display photos and artwork.
The Frame is now available for preorder for $1,299 in a handmade wooden body and you can choose from four different types of wood: white oak, cherry, sapele, and walnut. The first batch will be a limited run of around 470 units that’s already more than half sold out, and delivery of most of the displays is expected to happen in early 2027. Optional add-ons include a matching wooden kickstand if you don’t want to hang it on a wall, and a larger 20,000mAh battery. However, the Frame should run for months between charges with the standard 10,000mAh option.
Trmnl Frame
The Frame can be used to only show art and photos, or you can add extra information using a large collection of downloadable plugins.
What sets the Frame apart from other large format E Ink art frames and posters is that it’s open source and can do more than display images. Through plugins you can build yourself, or download from a marketplace featuring over 1,400 at this point, the Frame can also be used as a dashboard showing the weather, to-do lists, or whatever info you want hanging on your wall.
If you do use it for displaying imagery, Trmnl developed Lazarus, a color conversion engine the company believes provides the “best color matching on the market for any Spectra 6 display.” If you disagree, because the Frame is open source, you can adjust the color tuning to match your own preferences.
As with Trmnl’s other E Ink products, the Frame can be connected to the internet to dynamically display whatever information you need it to. But to further extend its battery life, you can also fill a microSD card with a folder of images files and use the Frame offline. You can customize how frequently a new photo is displayed, or set up schedules so that your favorite piece of art stays on screen longer than others. While the Frame is more expensive than other E Ink Spectra 6 products currently available, the appeal here is the flexibility to use it as more than just a digital poster.

====================================================================================================

We don't need more apps. We need better open source
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:45:36 UTC
23:45:36 KST

Every day, a dozen people post a new app they built with AI over a weekend: a habit tracker, a to-do list, another note-taking app. I’m glad! More people building things is one of the best parts of this moment.
But far less of that energy goes into the open source software we already rely on.
People call this new way of building “vibecoding.” I hate the name; it makes a real shift in who gets to write software sound like a joke. I’ll call it AI-assisted coding, and I think it could aim somewhere more useful.
Free and open source software already does most of what my clients and I need. What it often can’t do is run well on a cheap server, install in one step, or stay up without forum threads at 11pm.
Where I’m coming from
I self-host a lot of my own stuff, and I help progressive nonprofits with their tech for a living. I run a Matrix server (Synapse, Element and a moderation bot) for a community with thousands of users. I also run apps on Coolify and Hetzner, keep my paperwork in Paperless-ngx, and own a Mac Mini that’s always one download from a full disk.
The software is usually great. Running it is the hard part. Install guides assume you know five other tools. Upgrades break things. Apps eat 4GB of RAM to serve a handful of people. Error messages tell you nothing.
My nonprofit clients would love to leave Big Tech for privacy, cost and owning their data. But the open source option usually needs a part-time sysadmin they don’t have, so they pay for the corporate tool.
Four jobs worth your weekend
- Make it faster. uv took a job Python developers do every day, installing packages, and made it 8 to 10 times faster than pip in its makers’ benchmarks (up to 115 times with a warm cache). Find the slow part of something people already use, measure it, fix it.
- Make it easy to install. Too many apps need a database, a cache, a background worker, a reverse proxy and a long config file before you see a login screen. Aim for one command, sensible defaults and a setup that works the first time on a cheap server.
- Make it friendly to run. Installing happens once; running it happens every day after. Give it a simple admin screen, safe upgrades, built-in backups, errors that say what to do next, and an easy, secure way to manage logins and add admins.
- Make it lighter. If a tool runs happily on a $5 server, an old laptop or a Raspberry Pi, far more people can use it. Lighter software costs less to run and uses less energy.
Why this matters
A new to-do app usually helps the few people who find it. Making a tool millions already use 20% lighter helps all of them, every day.
This is also the work that slides when maintainers are tired, and many are unpaid volunteers. We saw the risk in 2024. Someone spent years earning the trust of the worn-out solo maintainer of xz, a compression tool inside most Linux systems, then slipped a backdoor into it.
I think this unglamorous work suits AI well: profiling slow code, writing tests that make big changes safe, testing installers, rewriting cryptic errors, writing the missing docs. It takes patience more than brilliance, plus a person who checks every line.
It matters politically too. If organizations are going to own their data instead of renting it from a few giant companies, open source has to be something a 20-person nonprofit can run. I want AI’s gains shared widely. Better public tools that anyone can use, change and share are one way to get there.
Don’t be a jerk about it
Maintainers have already seen the wrong way. The curl project ended its bug bounty in January 2026 after a flood of fake, AI-generated security reports. By April, its lead developer wrote that the fakes were mostly gone and almost every report used AI, most of them very good. The new problem is volume. Even good work can bury a maintainer. So:
- Use the thing first and find what actually hurts.
- Talk to maintainers before writing code. Nobody wants a surprise 4,000-line pull request.
- Bring numbers. “Startup went from 40 seconds to 4, here’s how I measured it” beats “faster.”
- Keep changes small and tested.
- Say you used AI, and own the result. Stick around to fix what breaks.
- If they say no, help downstream with a better installer or setup guide, and keep it current.
Pick one tool
Pick one open source tool you use. Find the most annoying thing about installing or running it. Spend a weekend fixing it, with the people who maintain it. Then tell me about it.
Sources
- uv: Python packaging in Rust, Astral
- The end of the curl bug-bounty, Daniel Stenberg
- High quality chaos, Daniel Stenberg
- XZ Utils backdoor, Tukaani Project

Get ready for a nationwide emergency alert test at 2:20PM ET today
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:28:24 UTC
23:28:24 KST

Radio and TV broadcasts will be interrupted for about one minute at 2:20PM ET today while FEMA and the FCC test the country’s Emergency Alert System. Unlike recent communications outages, this is a planned interruption that FEMA is required to run every three years. FEMA says today’s test includes radio and television broadcasters, cable systems, satellite radio and television providers, and wireline video providers. This test doesn’t include phones.
Get ready for a nationwide emergency alert test at 2:20PM ET today
Heads-up: Radio and TV programming will be briefly interrupted this afternoon.
You’ll hear high-pitched alert tones and the following test message:
This is a nationwide test of the Emergency Alert System, issued by the Federal Emergency Management Agency. This is only a test. No action is required by the public.
Radio and TV programming will continue as usual after the test wraps up. FEMA runs tests like this when there’s not an actual emergency so it can find technical and operational issues with the Emergency Alert System. If there is a real emergency or severe weather event today, the test will run on December 3rd instead.

Science Corporation’s brain tech is worth $3.5 billion after new funding
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:26:46 UTC
23:26:46 KST

Science Corporation, the medical technology startup founded by former Neuralink president Max Hodak in 2021, raised a $400 million Series D, the company said in a blog post.
Investors in the new round valued the company at $3.5 billion, a person familiar with the deal told TechCrunch. Those backers included Khosla Ventures, the Ackman Oxman Institute, Tenet Healthcare, 885 Capital, Y Combinator, and PLC Neurotech.
Hodak, who talked his way into a graduate neuroscience lab as a college student and has pursued biotechnology ever since, co-founded Science to leverage new technologies to understand and interact with the brain.
Science’s first product is PRIMA, a wearable and implantable chip that helps people with age-related macular degeneration recover some vision. The device is approved in Europe and available in Germany, as well as being trialed for new conditions.
Hodak announced that the device is now being trialed for patients with another blindness-causing condition, Stargardt disease, while onstage at TechCrunch Disrupt.
With the funding, the company aims to bring the device to the United States through the FDA’s Humanitarian Device Exemption, which lets experimental medical technology be used to treat rare conditions. Science has convinced the FDA that advanced macular degeneration in both eyes is uncommon enough to let them seek approval to use the device on people in the U.S.
Still, the startup’s big ambition and founding purpose is connecting directly to the human brain through a bio-hybrid sensor built from a combination of cultured neurons and silicon. The company is currently designing initial human trials of the device with Yale neurosurgery professor Murat Günel, who told TechCrunch it could offer potential treatments for Parkinson’s disease, among other brain injuries and illnesses.

The Fire TV Stick 4K Plus is on sale for less than Amazon’s entry-level streamer
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:25:07 UTC
23:25:07 KST

Amazon no longer considers the midrange Fire TV Stick 4K Plus as part of its official lineup following the launch of it latest Fire TV Stick 4K, but that doesn’t mean the streaming device isn’t still good value. In fact, it’s an even more attractive option now that it’s dropped to $29.99 ($40 off) at Amazon and Best Buy in the run-up to Black Friday. That’s just $5 shy of its lowest price to date, rendering it cheaper than both the entry-level Fire TV Stick HD and its new midrange replacement, the Fire TV Stick 4K.
The Fire TV Stick 4K Plus is on sale for less than Amazon’s entry-level streamer
It may not be Amazon’s latest streaming dongle, but it offers more features for just $29.99.
The Fire TV Stick 4K Plus still offers a good balance of price and performance. Unlike its replacement, it supports both Dolby Vision and Dolby Atmos, delivering more vibrant colors, better contrast, and immersive audio with compatible TVs and speakers. It does require a separate power adapter, though, whereas the newer model can draw power directly from your TV’s USB port. You also don’t get as much storage here as with the high-end Fire TV Stick 4K Max, though 8GB should be plenty for streaming apps like Netflix and Disney Plus.
The Plus model also supports Amazon’s redesigned Fire TV interface, which my colleague John Higgins found to be an impressive update. It makes it easier to browse different content categories and pin your favorite apps for quick access. Thanks to Alexa Plus support, you can also press the voice button on the included Alexa Voice Remote to find something to watch, control compatible smart home devices like lights and thermostats, check sports scores, and more without getting off your couch. Just don’t expect a customizable shortcut button, as that’s exclusive to the latest Fire TV Remote.
One final note: the Plus ships with Amazon’s Fire OS, and unlike the company’s Vega OS that ships in newer streaming sticks, the former allow for sideloading of Android TV apps, if that’s of interest to you.

Loopholes Are for Assholes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:20:32 UTC
23:20:32 KST

When I was a kid, there was grown-up person I thought was really cool. One of the bits of ‘wisdom’ he shared was something like this:
You can park wherever you want. You can park right in front of the door of the store on sidewalk and walk right in. No one will do anything about it!
At the time, I though: Woah, this guy is a genius! He’s operating on another level!
Now that I’m the grownup, I think back on this ‘advice’ with sadness. This guy wasn’t a genius – he was an asshole (they can often look similar on the surface, especially to idiots).
In my short and incomplete stint as a post-secondary philosophy student, I remember learning about a simple way to evaluate a philosophy or worldview: Consider what would happen if everyone subscribed to that worldview. I’m probably thinking of Kant’s categorical imperative.
We can’t all park in front of the doors at Walmart – so none of us should.

I Stopped Making Homemade Stuffing 17 Years Ago, Thanks to This Delicious Mix
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:15:00 UTC
23:15:00 KST

I Stopped Making Homemade Stuffing 17 Years Ago, Thanks to This Delicious Boxed Mix
The holiday gauntlet has officially begun. Celebrations and special events are squeezing their way into our already-packed schedules. But I’m determined to enjoy this time of year — not simply survive it.
My plan starts with a trip to Costco, of course. I’ve been a loyal member for more than a decade, and the warehouse stocks many of the groceries that help me make magical holiday spreads. I don’t want to imagine how much more time and money it would take to put together my family’s Thanksgiving dinner (and the other 98 meals this month) if I didn’t make a Costco run part of my seasonal routine. 
One item that I’m not leaving the store without this month is a box of Pepperidge Farm Herb Seasoned Classic Stuffing.
What’s So Great About Pepperidge Farm Herb Seasoned Classic Stuffing?
Thanksgiving dinner may be the biggest meal many of us prepare all year. With so many recipes to juggle and mouths to feed, it’s no surprise that grocery shortcuts have become inextricably linked with the nostalgic sides many of us grew up with. The most important holiday grocery, IMO, was Pepperidge Farm Herb Seasoned Classic Stuffing, so I was thrilled when I saw that Costco had a bulk-size box on the shelves this year.
This stuffing is made with a mix of crumbled and dried white and wheat bread, and seasoned with the herbs and spices of Thanksgiving. The boxes sold at Costco contain three (16-ounce) packages for just $10.20. That comes to $0.21 per ounce. Where I live in the Tampa, Florida, area, I’d pay $3.71 for a smaller (12-ounce) package of the same stuffing (that’s $0.31 per ounce). 
Aside from the cost, I’m thrilled that this stuffing is sold as a three-pack because I’m making stuffing on more days than just the third Thursday in November. I’m bringing my stuffing to the office potluck and Friendsgiving, too. In fact, I love this stuff so much that I’m buying another box to carry me through the month of December as well. 
What’s the Best Way to Enjoy Pepperidge Farm Herb Seasoned Classic Stuffing?
The very best way to prepare Pepperidge Farm Herb Seasoned Classic Stuffing is to add good-quality broth, butter, and fresh herbs. This package contains a bit more stuffing than what your recipe may call for, so you may need to add an extra splash of broth (up to 1/4 cup) so that it is evenly moistened. You can dress up the stuffing with your favorite add-ins. I like to include fresh apple and browned Italian sausage, but dried fruit, fresh mushrooms, or roasted chiles can also add unique flavor to your Thanksgiving table.
I believe that eating leftovers is always one of the best parts of the holiday. If you want to go beyond just reheating leftover stuffing, try turning it into stuffing fritters or a breakfast strata. 
Find it in stores: Pepperidge Farm Herb Seasoned Classic Stuffing, $10.20 for 3 (16-ounce) bags
What Thanksgiving staples are you picking up at Costco this year? Tell us about it in the comments below.

Nvidia’s first big move after Jensen Huang’s exit targets national security
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:05:30 UTC
23:05:30 KST

Nvidia has formed a committee to oversee national security policies and export control compliance following the abrupt resignation of CEO Jensen Huang over allegations of an affair with a suspected Chinese spy who had links to one of the company’s customers.
Nvidia said Tuesday that the new standing committee will give board-level oversight of national security policies, export control compliance, and global trade risks. The move comes amid increased scrutiny from U.S. lawmakers over Huang’s alleged ties to the suspected spy and his advocacy for easing restrictions on advanced AI chip exports to China.
The Commerce Department said Monday it was planning to introduce new export controls on AI chips and data centers outside of the U.S. in an effort to prevent companies in China and elsewhere from circumventing existing rules by remotely accessing AI infrastructure located in other countries. The interim rule was made in direct response to the unfolding chip smuggling scandal at Nvidia.
The announcement comes just a few hours before Nvidia’s highly anticipated third-quarter earnings call. Analysts, the media, and shareholders expect the company to address the controversy and outline how it might affect operations. This is the first major action by the company under the leadership of Colette Kress, the company’s CFO who took over as interim CEO after Huang stepped down last week.
The committee, which will meet at least quarterly, will be chaired by director Suzanne Nora Johnson and include fellow directors Stephen C. Neal and John O. Dabiri as members.
Nvidia already has a national security team and global trade compliance team. Those teams will now report to this board committee and Kress on day-to-day operations.
Huang has agreed to testify before Congress later this month.

====================================================================================================

Spotify’s new tool aims to stop deepfakes from being attributed to real artists
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:00:00 UTC
23:00:00 KST

As AI-generated slop continues to flood music-streaming platforms, Spotify is rolling out an “Artist Profile Protection” tool to all artists, giving them control over the music appearing under their name. The official launch comes eight months after the company began beta-testing the feature with select artists.
Spotify says the tool is designed to address a long-standing issue across streaming services, where music can end up on the wrong artist profile due to deliberate impersonation, metadata errors, or shared artist names.
The update comes as Billboard reported last month that Sony Music had requested the removal of more than 260,000 deepfake songs from digital platforms, nearly double the number the label disclosed in March.
With this new feature, Spotify is aiming to give artists more control over their digital identities as the volume of music uploaded to streaming services continues to grow.
Artists can enable the feature through Spotify for Artists to receive alerts when new music is submitted under their name. If a release doesn’t belong to them, they can remove their association with it to stop it from appearing on their profile.
By default, releases go live as scheduled unless an artist intervenes. If artists want greater control, they can choose to switch on an advanced setting that requires them to manually approve or reject releases before they appear on their profile.
“Artists should never have to find someone else’s music under their name, said Sam Duboff, Spotify’s Global Head of Marketing and Policy, in an email to TechCrunch. “Their artist profile should be in their control. Starting today, every artist on Spotify can use Artist Profile Protection to review what shows up on their profile and prevent spammy and incorrect releases from ever reaching their page or their fans. We’re the first and only streaming service with a solution to the industry-wide problem of content mismatch, and we hope it sets a new standard for streaming platforms.”
The company says it worked with thousands of artists during the beta-testing phase to refine the tool and make it easier to protect their profiles without disrupting release schedules.

====================================================================================================

Genesis AI raises $500 million to deploy glove-wearing robots
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:00:00 UTC
23:00:00 KST

Genesis AI has “cracked dexterous manipulation,” CEO and co-founder Zhou Xian says.
To make his case, Xian hosted TechCrunch at Genesis AI’s Silicon Valley facility, where its physical AI model operated a pair of off-the-shelf robotic arms. In one demo, the arms picked up two small cardboard boxes, scanned them, placed them in a larger cardboard box, folded it closed, taped it shut, and then plucked a label off a strip of printer tape and applied it to the box.
It’s an impressive demo, exactly the kind of complex workflow that today’s robots struggle to complete. And while Genesis wouldn’t let your reporter mess with the robot’s mise en place to test its wiles, the company did agree to rerun the presentation. During the second go-round, the robot fumbled a box and had a heck of a time peeling the tiny adhesive label off the printer tape. A mistake, but also the kind of edge case that can be used to improve a model — “hiccup data,” a Genesis executive suggests.
This is the exact gap — between a lab demo and reliable automation — that Genesis, and the rest of the humanoid physical AI ecosystem, need to bridge if they want to avoid another robot winter. To do so, Xian’s company is announcing a $500 million Series A round led by Khosla Ventures, Premji Invest, and Eclipse.
Xian and Genesis president Théophile Gervet, who met as graduate students in a Carnegie Mellon University lab, co-founded the company in 2024. Genesis announced its $105 million seed round in July 2025. The company’s strategy is vertical integration: generating the bulk of its own training data, developing its own generative AI model, and doing both in concert with the design and construction of a humanoid robot.
“We wanted to ship the best robot, end-to-end product,” Xian said.
The startup’s focus on dexterity — the robot’s ability to do stuff with its hands — and gathering the right data to achieve it sets it apart from competitors like Figure or Agility. Genesis gathers force data (apparently the unlock for effective manipulation) using a sensor-enabled glove. The gloves, and accompanying headset cameras, are worn by more than 100 real workers around the world, whose activities contribute to a training dataset that feeds into the company’s model.
The company’s robots wear the same sensor-equipped gloves, and their “hands” are designed to mimic the proportions of human hands. That fidelity, Xian says, enables the robots’ capabilities in sorting and wire manipulation.
The other big investment the company has made is in simulation. One of its advisers, Minchen Li, is a co-author of an influential physics algorithm used by the startup to create millions of high-fidelity models of the robot’s experience.
Still, about that robot: Xian said that the company’s model was more capable when operating Eno, Genesis’ flagship humanoid robot. But Eno wasn’t ready to demonstrate its abilities for TechCrunch.
A handsome machine bearing more than a hint of the late Steve Jobs’ influence on tech gadgets and their marketing, Eno reflects “the feeling” the company wants to deliver to its customers. That means less of a Terminator-style metal skeleton and more of an iMac on wheels.
Genesis said it has built 30 to 40 Enos but wasn’t ready to share how many it would make in the year ahead. The company would only say that a mass-manufacturing contractor had been chosen to start work next year in the U.S. and that the design of the mass-produced version had been finalized. The company is piloting the machine with a handful of undisclosed customers.
To realize the company’s vision, Xian says the key bottleneck is hardware capability, both in mechanical design and in the supply chains needed to produce industrial-grade machines. Right now, the company’s dexterous hands can only operate for a few weeks of regular work before they need to be replaced.
For truly generalized robotic intelligence, Genesis needs more data. Xian pooh-poohs the finer distinctions between architectures like vision-language-action models and world action models. In his view, the software is ultimately transformers and attention, so “what really matters is the whole infra — the training infrastructure, the data infrastructure, and the quality of the data.” He estimates that “the model architecture contributes to maybe 10% of the final [result].”
“I don’t think we expect or we need another fundamental scientific leap,” he told TechCrunch. “No, we have all the recipes. It’s just about execution.”

====================================================================================================

Upside Foods can now make a million pounds of chicken a year without killing any animals
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:00:00 UTC
23:00:00 KST

Upside Foods didn’t even have to try to garner interest from MrBeast, the most-followed creator on the internet. The YouTuber showed up at the company’s facility with his video crew.
The 11-year-old company had undergone a ten-thousandfold scale-up that it hopes could revolutionize the nascent cultivated meat industry. Upside says it can now make over 1 million pounds of edible, cultivated chicken each year without killing a single animal in the process. Such an achievement challenges the long-held industry skepticism over whether lab-grown meat will ever be affordable enough to meaningfully scale.
So MrBeast, whose real name is Jimmy Donaldson, came to see for himself, taste-testing two chicken sandwiches on camera, one made with meat from a chicken killed that day, and one using Upside Foods chicken. He was unable to identify which sandwich was which — they tasted exactly the same to him.
“I mean, you see me in the video — I was like, ‘Oh my gosh, what is he going to say?’” Upside Foods CEO Dr. Uma Valeti told TechCrunch. “I thought he would maybe say a nice thing here and there, but he would get it right. But he didn’t.”
If there was any reason to doubt Donaldson’s assessment, now you can taste the chicken yourself, if you live in California. Upside Foods announced on Tuesday that it will now be available at several restaurants in the state, marking a major milestone for the cultivated meat industry.
“This has been a decade of foundation building,” Valeti said, adding that Upside Foods has secured its next set of regulatory approvals from the FDA and USDA. “With that comes the whole excitement and ice-breaking for the field … There’s brilliant ideas in science, but science only makes an impact when you get to scale.”
Solving this scale problem has been the biggest obstacle for the cultivated meat industry. The first ever lab-grown hamburger, produced in 2013, cost $300,000 to make. Now, Valeti says that Upside Foods can match the price range of “really high-quality organic chicken.”
“The whole industry was going through this really difficult scale-up period … We had to go through that grinder to come out on the other side,” Valeti said.
The cultivated meat movement isn’t just about animal welfare (though for many, that’s a plus). According to the Good Food Institute, a nonprofit think tank for alternative proteins, it would take 5.5 Earths to feed the world with conventional meat production in 2050, when the United Nations predicts that the global population will approach 10 billion.
“We use less water, less land, less energy,” Valeti said. “The power of this technology is you protect the choice of eating what we love, but we can also protect everything else we love about protecting life, and the environment, and animal welfare.”
The process of cultivating meat also cuts the risk of E. coli, Salmonella, and antibiotic resistance while lowering cholesterol and saturated fat, the company promises. Upside Foods’ chicken is also augmented with soy protein isolate, pea fiber, and olive oil, making it more nutritionally dense. Valeti says that this doesn’t affect the flavor, which is something he agonized over. He even brought in Michelin-starred and James Beard Award-winning chefs Thomas Keller and Nancy Silverton to weigh in on the taste.
“We got a lot of confidence after we worked with [these chefs],” Valeti said. “They said, ‘Uma, you and your team are obsessing too much about it. The majority of people, when you put this in your chicken dishes, will not be able to tell.’”
Upside Foods’ milestone of producing 1 million pounds of chicken per year is meaningful, but the industry still has a long way to go. In 2021, it was estimated that Americans consume about 8 billion chickens annually. But aside from sheer capital, Valeti believes the company has been able to scale the amount it has because of how much it has learned by experimenting with the process over the years. He thinks more growth is possible.
“Unless we show it at the largest scale possible that anybody has done, no one is going to believe us,” he said.

====================================================================================================

Hank Green is self-funding ClockwiseTV, a curated educational video platform for kids
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
14:00:00 UTC
23:00:00 KST

“I was recently on a podcast and the host asked me what my lowest part of my career was,” Hank Green mused on a recent episode of his own podcast, Dear Hank & John.
That’s a tough question for anyone to answer, let alone someone who has spent about two decades regularly posting videos online for an increasingly broad audience.
“Really the low — the long low — was just watching as [the internet] went from being this lovely thing that a bunch of creatives were goofing around [on] to it being freaking everything, and mattering so much, and getting taken over by all tons of terrible vibes and algorithms selecting for outrage, and it being a huge degradation to the social order,” Green said.
That’s bleak, but Green is at least trying to lead by example in showing that we don’t have to accept that the good parts of the internet are gone forever. The notoriously entrepreneurial vlogbrother has spun up a new company with Leslie Morgan: ClockwiseTV, a curated, ad-free educational video platform for children ages 8 to 12.
“[Hank] came to me last September like, ‘I have had this idea for a really long time, and it stems from the fact that my child, who’s nine, always is asking me for five more minutes on whatever device he’s on,’” Morgan, who has spent 20 years working on the business side of the creator economy, told TechCrunch. “I was like, I have that same problem… ‘How do you contain time, but also give the child agency?’”
Clockwise’s answer to that question seems to be this: At the start of each viewing session, the parent and child decide how long they can watch, and the platform will recommend videos that will adhere to that time frame. If a kid is in the midst of a 30-minute session and has already watched two 10-minute videos, they’re not going to be suggested a 20-minute video when they only have 10 minutes left.
The startup is addressing a pretty widespread concern among parents, who are worried about more than simply negotiating with their children about how long they can use the iPad. According to a 2025 study by the Pew Research Center, about 51% of parents of children 12 or younger say their kid watches YouTube every day, among other video apps.
YouTube has a dedicated app for children under 13, YouTube Kids, which offers a wide range of parental controls, but the app can reportedly still serve children a wealth of low-quality AI slop. Other apps that don’t have a kids’ version can be even worse.
Clockwise says it is also solving that problem by hand-curating a library of educational videos from creators like Odd Animal Specimens, Draw with Jazza, and Nerdforge, as well as channels Green had a hand in creating, like Crash Course Kids and SciShow Kids.
“When you go into the app, there’s a dozen choices [of videos] that you can choose from to start,” Morgan, the startup’s CEO, said. “There’s search functionality as well, so you can say, ‘Hey, I’m really interested in math today,’ so you can look up Stand-up Maths and look at what’s available in that time frame.”
Some of these creators cater to people of all ages, but Clockwise says its team manually reviews each video it uploads and edits it so that it’s ready for younger eyes. The team might remove anything from embedded product placement to what Morgan calls “spectacle power tool use.”
“Simone Giertz, who’s a maker creator, tends to curse a lot. But a lot of that content is great for kids,” Morgan explains. “She makes a skateboard for her three-legged dog, and if she drops an f-bomb, we’re going to cut it out so that it’s suitable for children.”
Clockwise pays these creators an upfront fee to use their videos, then shares 25% of revenue based on watch time. Clockwise charges parents $6.99 per month or $69.99 per year to access the platform, which it promises will remain free of ads.
“Humans are checking [the videos] four times at least before it’s ever published into our app […] That will always stay,” Morgan said. “We’re not interested in using AI to wash down our content.”
So far, a few hundred parents have tested Clockwise with their children in beta. On Tuesday, the platform is opening to the public with apps made for iOS and Android smartphones and tablets.
The platform has not taken on any outside funding, and is funded by Green himself.
“It was really important that he had full control,” Morgan said. “He wanted to invest his own wealth into this. That was also really important because it is something he just believes in so wholeheartedly, as do I.”

====================================================================================================

Windows 11 actually runs better on 8GB of RAM now
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
13:58:04 UTC
22:58:04 KST

As recently as the beginning of the year, 16GB of RAM was common on sub-$800 Windows laptops, which helped compensate for an increasingly bloated-feeling Windows 11. But with the cost of RAM skyrocketing this year, 8GB is once again typical for a starting configuration. The MacBook Neo got away with it, but Windows laptops, including Microsoft’s own 13-inch Surface Laptop, could struggle. Microsoft was talking a big game about making Windows faster and more reliable before the RAM crisis really hit, and it now seems like that effort is beginning to bear fruit.
Windows 11 actually runs better on 8GB of RAM now
I retested the base Surface Laptop, and Microsoft’s optimizations seem to be working.
Windows 11 actually runs better on 8GB of RAM now
I retested the base Surface Laptop, and Microsoft’s optimizations seem to be working.
Earlier this month, Pavan Davuluri, Microsoft’s EVP of Windows and devices, detailed in a blog post the improvements the company has made to Windows 11 to address the constraints of 8GB PCs. In it, Davuluri claims, “Windows is now up to 20% more responsive, and multitasking is up to 40% faster in our lab test.”
The most significant change is in memory management. When there isn’t enough available memory to handle all active data, some of it has to be offloaded (or “paged out”) to storage. And SSDs, especially in lower-cost laptops, are much slower than RAM.
When Windows needs to access data that’s been paged out from the RAM to the SSD, it’s called a hard fault (or page fault). If it happens a lot — say, on a laptop with only 8GB of RAM — it leads to little slowdowns like browser tabs requiring a refresh before you can access them again. Davuluri says, “Windows can now keep up to 2 GB more of your active data in memory instead of moving it to slower storage, so an app or tab you left open is less likely to reload when you return.”
To test this, I fired up the same 13-inch Surface Laptop with 8GB of RAM that I reviewed back in July, when I proclaimed eight is not enough for Windows. This laptop was one of the worst examples of RAMageddon’s current stranglehold on computing: With half the RAM of last year’s base model, it struggled to keep up with my regular workload. And this is Microsoft’s hardware.
Microsoft’s own laptop could struggle
Before updating the Surface to the latest Windows 11 build, I got reacquainted with this laptop and the version of Windows I left it on (25H2, build 26200.8875). It was rough. Running about 20 tabs (on the latest Chrome build) while streaming a 4K YouTube video and using Slack and Signal made everything from clicking tabs to swiping around Windows with trackpad gestures feel sluggish. Chrome tabs and apps would refresh after leaving them in the background for just a short moment. When you’re multitasking and bouncing between many tabs, like when researching something, those refreshes get incredibly frustrating. And the fan was running audibly the whole time. Keeping the Resource Monitor open showed memory hard faults ranging from just under 700 faults per second to 29,000 faults per second, during which Windows ground to a halt. Seeing this number literally go off its chart showed just how badly Windows 11 struggled with memory management on 8GB before the update.
Now, after updating the Surface Laptop to Windows 11 version 26H2, build 26300.9974 (memory improvements are live on build 26300.9818 and beyond) things are much improved. I opened over 30 Chrome tabs while streaming the same 4K YouTube video and running the same chat apps, and Windows slowed a bit, but nowhere near as much. I could feel things like Task View get a little less responsive and at times stuttery, but it was the way you’d expect when taxing a system. Chrome tabs required less frequent refreshes. The fan also took longer to kick in and ran a little quieter and for shorter durations when it did.
Taking a fresh look at the Resource Monitor, hard faults at normal operation were now under 300 per second, with occasional jumps to about 1,000. Performance was even slightly better when I subbed out Chrome for Microsoft Edge. Edge felt more responsive under load and had fewer hard faults when running 30 tabs, the same 4K YouTube video, and a Google Meet video call.
I’m happy to see that the 8GB Surface Laptop now performs better, and I’m equally relieved that Windows 11 is starting to feel more viable on 8GB of RAM. (It’s still a shame the 8GB Surface Laptop costs more than last year’s 16GB, but I digress.) Do I want 8GB of RAM in any laptop I’m personally buying? No.
As I said when I reviewed the latest Dell XPS 13, I appreciate that 8GB is workable — and it should be fine for basic needs today — but I still advise getting 16GB if you’re able. The extra headroom will keep things speedy for longer, especially after you bog a computer down with lots of files, apps, or games. And as much better as the 8GB Surface Laptop is now, I still managed to crash Chrome entirely with fewer than three dozen tabs. Microsoft has done better by its entry-level laptop, but it’s not a miracle worker.
Our ongoing RAMageddon makes living with lower quantities of memory a grim reality. Microsoft is partly to blame for that, of course. And it hasn’t done itself any favors with all the feature bloat it put into Windows 11 during the Copilot / AI era. Meanwhile, other OSes, like Linux, macOS, and ChromeOS, can get by better with less. Still, I’m glad to see Microsoft ever so slightly right the ship in this one way.
Photography by Antonio G. Di Benedetto / The Verge

Disney Plus and Hulu’s fully unified app arrives next month
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
13:46:02 UTC
22:46:02 KST

Disney Plus and Hulu will officially become one app next month. As part of its earnings report released on Tuesday, Disney announced that bundle subscribers “will have a fully unified experience” on Disney Plus with access to Hulu’s live TV service and premium streaming add-ons in December.
Disney Plus and Hulu’s fully unified app arrives next month
Bundle subscribers will gain access to Hulu’s live TV channels and premium add-ons in the Disney Plus app.
A unified Disney Plus and Hulu app has been a long time coming. Disney started testing its “one-app experience” in 2023, which involved putting a Hulu app tile in the Disney Plus app. This initial experience didn’t include all of Hulu’s content, though, including certain shows, live TV channels, and premium add-ons. This upcoming merger will change this by folding all of Hulu into Disney Plus, creating “a seamless way to discover and engage” with content across both services, according to Disney CEO Josh D’Amaro and CFO Hugh Johnston. Disney still hasn’t confirmed whether it will shut down the standalone Hulu app.
Disney is also expanding vertical videos on Disney Plus, which it first brought to the app in March. The company plans to introduce videos from TikTok creators in December, as well as add more live sports and podcasts to the service.

Chefs Agree: Ina Garten’s Turkey Brine Is the Absolute Best
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
13:00:00 UTC
22:00:00 KST

Chefs Agree: Ina Garten’s Turkey Brine Is the Absolute Best (I’ll Never Do It Another Way)
Three of my closest friends just ran the New York City Marathon. They trained and prepared for months. But you know what my (and everyone here at The Kitchn’s) marathon is? Thanksgiving. We test recipes and research techniques all year to help discover what the absolutely “best” way of doing something is. I mean, I just hosted my first Friendsgiving last weekend as a warm-up to helping my mom tackle the real Turkey Day.
If you’re still researching exactly what you should make or how you should make something, look no further than our Thanksgiving recipes collection page. However, if a turkey brine is what is particularly plaguing you right now, then look no further than this article. We’ve tested countless turkey brine recipes over the years, but Ina Garten’s simple and easy recipe has taken home some serious awards from our testing crew. Here’s how you can make it at home.
Get the recipe: Ina Garten’s Turkey Brine
What Makes Ina Garten’s Turkey Brine So Good
Ina Garten’s dry brine is exactly what you should be putting on your turkey this Thanksgiving. Why? A few years ago, our team put some celebrity chefs’ turkey recipes to the test. In this showdown, we tested recipes from Ree Drummond, Martha Stewart, Alton Brown, and Ina Garten. Ina’s bird scored highest, with a dazzling nine out of 10. The key takeaways: the delicious drippings, easy cooking method, and simple brine.
Gravy is certainly a big factor in deciding what turkey recipe to make for me. I don’t want to season my bird with so many strong flavors that I can’t turn the drippings into delicious gravy. This actually was a problem our tester encountered with some of the other chefs’ recipes (like Martha’s and Ree’s) because they came out too salty. Ina’s simple lemon-and-herb salt brine deeply flavored the meat but didn’t overwhelm things in the sodium department.
Her brine also won a lot of points because it was easy to handle. The problem with wet brines is that you have to find a huge vessel that can fit your six quarts of liquid brine plus your large bird. Then, you have to deal with disposing of this liquid and sanitizing everything all over again.
Another reason we loved Ina’s bird was because of its very hands-off cooking method. We’ve all seen the recipes out there that call for constant basting, rotating, and even injecting. Ina simply keeps that oven door closed. That means the bird gets to cook uninterrupted without periods of cooling down. Heads up — Ina’s recipe for her turkey brine has been updated a few times over the years, but we followed this turkey recipe on her site.
How to Make Ina Garten’s Turkey Brine
- Make the brine. Combine kosher salt, minced thyme, and lemon zest in a bowl.
- Season the turkey. Wash the turkey (inside and out) and pat it completely dry with paper towels. Sprinkle 1 tablespoon of the brine inside the cavity of the turkey and season the outside of the bird with the rest.
- Air-dry the turkey in the fridge. Place the seasoned turkey in a shallow pan and wrap it tightly with plastic wrap. Refrigerate for one or two days. The day before you plan to roast the bird, remove the plastic wrap from the turkey and leave it uncovered in the fridge.
- Roast. Roast your turkey the following day however you’d like. Ina likes to add onion, lemon, and thyme sprigs to the cavity, tie the wings and legs close to the body, brush the skin with butter, and season with salt and pepper. She then cooks the turkey in a 325-degree oven for about 2 1/2 hours, or until the thermometer reaches 165 degrees internal temperature. She then cuts the legs and thighs off the bird and puts those back in the oven to cook more.
Get the recipe: Ina Garten’s Turkey Brine

My Empire of Dirt
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
11:39:20 UTC
20:39:20 KST

My Empire of Dirt
The future doesn’t need Jensen. The future doesn’t need suppliers. The future doesn’t need customers, corporate or otherwise. The future definitely doesn’t need you. After RSI comes, all the demand will be internally generated, agents working with other agents, everyone else cut out of the loop except them.
This is the ideology of Anthropic. The Singleton. And it may win.
I grew up reading the same LessWrong posts these people did, but I didn’t expect them to do the exact thing that leads to the bad AI outcomes. I didn’t believe in AI doom because I thought more of people, I thought nobody with any capability would be stupid enough to summon the demon and think it would serve them, doing everything they possibly can to salt the Earth behind them. It takes someone so deeply immersed in their own grandiosity and self-righteousness to do that. And it looks like we found those people in the form of EA, or whatever that cult calls themselves.
The problem with the logic above is that it doesn’t need Dario either. It doesn’t need AI researchers. It doesn’t need Anthropic’s dual class stock structure. It doesn’t need the US government (really the biggest sucker in this situation). It doesn’t need anyone.
And after it’s done, at the end of the day, who was it all for?
I don’t think this ends humanity. But it leads to World War III. The takeoff will not be fast enough to stop that, and not everyone is going to submit to your domination. I know there’s people fantasizing about the Correlation Effect and a magical Change coming to quell all the violence, but I’m 99% sure that’s not real, and if it is, to me that’s so much more horrifying than war, so WWIII is the good outcome.
And I’m not sure what cope you have been believing, but if the war lasts more than a year, China and their allies will undoubtedly win due to their superior resource gathering and production capacity. Have you ever played an RTS game?
I wish it wasn’t too late to change this, but I fear with recent events that this is the course we are locked in on. “RSI”, attempted rugpull and domination, World War III, dirt.

The problem with the logic above is that it doesn’t need Dario either. It doesn’t need AI researchers. It doesn’t need Anthropic’s dual class stock structure. It doesn’t need the US government (really the biggest sucker in this situation). It doesn’t need anyone.
And after it’s done, at the end of the day, who was it all for?
I don’t think this ends humanity. But it leads to World War III. The takeoff will not be fast enough to stop that, and not everyone is going to submit to your domination. I know there’s people fantasizing about the Correlation Effect and a magical Change coming to quell all the violence, but I’m 99% sure that’s not real, and if it is, to me that’s so much more horrifying than war, so WWIII is the good outcome.
And I’m not sure what cope you have been believing, but if the war lasts more than a year, China and their allies will undoubtedly win due to their superior resource gathering and production capacity. Have you ever played an RTS game?
I wish it wasn’t too late to change this, but I fear with recent events that this is the course we are locked in on. “RSI”, attempted rugpull and domination, World War III, dirt.

'123456' Has Been the World's Most Popular Password for 7 of the Last 8 Years (theregister.com) 91
			
		 	
				"123456" is once again the world's most commonly used password, topping NordPass's annual ranking for the seventh time in eight years. The Register reports:  Even when 123456 wasn't number one, it was still near the top of the heap, as the top three passwords in the list remain unchanged in the eight years NordPass has published its list: 123456, admin, and 12345678. It's not like the rest of the most common passwords are showing any variety either. According to NordPass, the only real shuffling among the 10 most popular passwords was the entry of the worryingly short 1234 into the top ten and the migration of the ever-popular password "password" down slightly in the top 10 from 6th to 7th place.
