How the Claude Code team thinks about verification
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
10:20:40 UTC
19:20:40 KST

How end-to-end, property and differential tests let coding agents verify their work with evidence, and what the Claude Code team does to keep its verifiers honest.
On the Claude Code team, the vast majority of our code (~90%) is written and reviewed by Claude. At this scale, how we equip our agents to verify their work with evidence becomes extremely important.
Verification loops are the building block that lets you delegate more work to Claude. Without them, you can really only run one or two Claudes at a time, because you have to check everything yourself. With them, you can run dozens or hundreds in parallel.
That’s because giving agents a way to verify their output is an effective way to improve quality. In internal testing around structured file generation, having a separate grader check Claude’s work against a rubric improved task success by up to 10 points over a standard prompting loop, with the largest gains on the hardest problems.
What was once expensive to verify (tests, benchmarks, specs, real end-to-end runs) is now cheap to request from an agent. Like code, agents can quickly generate tests, benchmarks, and specs. And once generated, these tests and benchmarks run as software so they can be run frequently without using many additional tokens.
You can also close the verification loop by using the results to improve the agent’s performance on that task. For example, if an agent repeatedly fails a test that checks logs for user data, you can create a hook or a more explicit rule in your CLAUDE.md to avoid future issues. This (mostly) stops issues from happening again.
So if verification is critical to producing quality code at a high velocity, what types of tests should you use and how should you apply them across your pipeline? This article answers those questions and also shares the process used by the Claude Code team.
No matter which test type you choose, there are a few failure modes to avoid.
If your tests are flaky you’ll train the agent to run them again rather than fixing the failing code. If your tests are slow, the agent will do fewer tests across batches which can make pinpointing issues difficult. Heavier tests are best deployed before you’ve committed the changes.
Now, here are the three types of tests I think are most worth investing in.
If you have to focus on one test type, pick end-to-end. These tests describe real user flows like adding an item to a cart, checking out, and moving to a confirmation page.
The reason E2E tests are so effective is that they are difficult to fool–either the whole feature works or it doesn't. They also have definitive done criteria and can concretely tell you what’s broken.
While unit tests are faster and less flaky, it’s possible to pass them and still break the feature. This is because both the agent and the test don’t understand the full task.
With E2E tests, it is effective to give your agent hands and eyes on the real running system. You can use a skill to boot, drive, and inspect the app with tools like CDP, simulators, or API harnesses.
The feature map can serve as a guide and grading rubric for agents as they typically list features from the user’s point of view with descriptions of paths, keyboard shortcuts, selectors, and expected results.
A lot of tests are based on what you thought might go wrong. Property tests look at the edge cases that people tend to miss, which is where agents often fail.
You start by writing a statement you think can’t fail and then use a test library (fast-check for JS/TS, Hypothesis for Python, proptest for Rust, etc.) to generate thousands of inputs that try to prove otherwise.
There are many properties in virtually every software project like:
Property libraries are a great way to keep agents honest by using edge cases that aren’t created with the same bias as the agent that created the original code. They are also particularly helpful for rewrites when the spec has drifted from production behavior and is no longer a reliable source for verification.
Differential tests are particularly effective for large-scale migration use cases where functionality is expected to remain the same. For these tests, you run both the old system and the new system with the same inputs and see if you get different results. If you do, there is a bug or breaking change that needs to be fixed.
The key for differential tests is to use randomly generated inputs or a sample of production traffic. You’ll also want to normalize irrelevant factors like whitespace and keep a list of approved changes for situations where the new environment should differ from the old.
We have previously written about how code review works at Anthropic, but this article aims to go deeper on how we verify our work.
Running the app end to end matters more than tests. You wouldn't ship an iOS change without trying it in a simulator, and the same goes for Claude.
Claude can test the whole app the way a human or QA engineer would. Ask it to use the Claude Chrome extension for web changes, an iOS or Android MCP for mobile, computer use for desktop, or to run the service locally for backend work.
To codify how to start the simulator or run your app, create a skill called verify. Put it in a .claude/skills/ folder in your repo, as close to the relevant code as you can, and add as many as you need. Claude knows to use the verify skill before committing changes.
Then invest in making your verify skill fast and reliable. Ask Claude to iterate on it and stress test it. It's your smoke test harness, so you want it rock solid.
When you give Claude a way to smoke test its changes end to end, you get much better results. You'll see it iterate and catch real mistakes faster, the way a human would. This is how we test nearly all changes at Anthropic.
Nobody can read every change, so we use failures as one way to decide what needs a person. Claude runs the checks and tries to fix anything that fails. If a check is still failing after three rounds, Claude stops and brings in a human.
The people who own the code write the checks for it, and refine them until they flag what the owner would flag. When a check pulls in a reviewer, it should say why, so they don't have to work it out from the diff.
We maintain a reliability checklist with 39 items, each with a trigger that can be read from a diff, a check, and the incidents or PRs behind it. One is “--resume loads old transcripts.” When session storage or message types change, we ask whether a new field loads safely, since older transcripts won’t have it.
Claude reads the diff and checks only the items whose trigger matches–usually 3 to 10–so nobody has to memorize past outages.
We don't try to improve reviews by stacking on more reviewers. Adding a second, more expensive, reviewer on top of the primary one is often a worse approach than tuning the primary. Instead, we have a REVIEW.md that says what the reviewer should pay attention to for this code.
We also think review and verification rounds should build on each other. We point each pass at context from earlier rounds and past failures rather than re-checking everything from scratch. It’s cheaper and finds more.
If a change can modify the check that judges it, the check is broken. Code Review reads the REVIEW.md file from the base or default branch, never from the pull request's changes. The Claude Code team ensures the rules that gate changes are on a branch that the change can't modify.
The Claude Code team also reserves reviews for commits. If someone's pushing while the review is happening, then the review result is no longer useful. A passing review on an out-of-date commit means nothing.
The team also runs cheap checks first. If an agent pushes before testing locally, it has spent shared review and CI time when it could have caught the bug itself. On our team, Claude runs the tests, build, and linter before opening a pull request. You can enforce this with a hook that runs the tests when Claude finishes, or a /goal of a passing test suite.
In our own repos, we stamp each commit with how it was verified. The stamp even includes "none" so we can query for commits that weren't verified.
We've also created a commit gate that can block a commit if the session did not contain /code-review or /verify. We've set it up with off, observe, and enforce modes.
Observe mode will log what would have been blocked, so that we can review any false positives and ensure no one is impacted.
Enforce mode will block the commit. Any commit can carry an explanation in its message as to why it didn't get verified. If commits have been blocked twice in the session, the next one will be allowed through so Claude doesn't get stuck – Claude Code isn't perfect.
The Claude Code team also regularly reviews the reviewers. We track how many merged changes were reverted, how many changes a maintainer rejected before merging, and a size-matched subset of changes that have stayed clean.
This can also be done with a formal evaluation. In our code review post, we described how the Code Review team has set up an evaluation based on previous incidents and reverts. They recreate the repo to the state at the time of review for each example, and provide a one-sentence answer key to what the bug was. Only findings that correctly describe the bug in question get points. They use merged and stable changes as a control to prevent over-flagging. Their tuning set is split out from the fixed test set they use to evaluate their system.
They also evaluated the recall rate for raw findings, post-verification, and final review separately. This lets them see if the system is losing bugs it had already found. Any evaluation that treats all these as one end-to-end score won't capture this nuance.
In addition to validity, the other dimension we care about is how important it is to make the change. A good reviewer will catch most things that are technically correct, but people care about whether a given bug is a must-fix, a should-fix, or a nit.
Code Review has this exact idea, and it will tag things as Important, Nit or Pre-existing. The "pre-existing" tag means that the reviewer shouldn't hold the author responsible for something that was already a bug.
Finally, the Claude Code team tunes recall up to the point where the next improvement would be overfitting to the eval rather than actually catching bugs.
In the Claude Code repo, we have two methods of turning missed cases into rules. The first is extracting patterns in human code review comments and PR reverts to identify common issues. These must occur in at least two PRs and be identifiable from a diff to be considered a potential rule. Three agents will try to disprove them, and they will be reviewed and approved by a human in a PR.
The second method is replaying three bug finders against a PR that is associated with a known incident. If at least two find the bug, it's a detectable bug. If the bug is missed on a small diff, this is a gap that is worth turning into a rule in REVIEW.md.
Flaky tests get the same treatment. Claude can't skip them, add sleeps or retry until they pass. If it can't find the root cause, it deletes the test and writes down why.
Rules need cleaning up too. Test suites, verification steps and .md files only grow if you let them, so we tidy them up on a routine.
We’ve found that giving your agent a way to verify its work with a strong feedback loop makes all the difference. Treat your verification layer like you would any other system. Keep it out of reach of the changes they judge, grade them against the bugs that mattered to you, and write down lessons when they miss something so that it doesn’t happen more than once, and prune those lessons so they stay worth running.
Do that and your job shifts from reading every diff to defining what “done” looks like and reviewing the evidence, which is what lets you run many Claudes at once.

Run your FreeBSD services from one console
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
08:13:38 UTC
17:13:38 KST

Open source · ZFS-native · FreeBSD 15.1 and later
Run your FreeBSD services from one console.
Install apps in isolated jails, control who can reach them, and manage updates and recovery with ZFS. One console for one host or a whole fleet through the hub, in the browser, on the command line or over the API: tour it.
FreeBSD jails, managed from one place. A jail is FreeBSD's own way to give a service a system of its own: its own files, users and network stack, on the host's kernel.
- BSD 2-Clause license
- FreeBSD 15.1 and later, amd64 and arm64
- No third-party tracking on this site
Up and running in three commands
Three commands
On a clean FreeBSD 15.1 machine, as root, once the package repository is trusted:
# pkg install jailops
# jailops-setup -n   # what it would do; changes nothing
# jailops-setup
jailops-setup loads the kernel modules, sets up the pool, turns on forwarding, adds the one anchor line to pf.conf, starts jailopsd and runs jailops doctor. The command line works as root straight away; the web console is one answer or --web away. Every action is printed before it runs, and running it again changes nothing. To run the host from the browser instead, see appliance mode.
What you get
- Backups that restore as a new jail and start with their data.
- A host firewall change you do not confirm undoes itself, so a mistake never locks you out.
- Encrypted volumes that stay shut until you unlock them, also after the machine has been off.
- Images that upgrade themselves overnight, and the jails that follow them move along.
- Every jail reachable on its own IPv6 address, straight from other hosts.
- Jails that find each other by name: db.shop.internal, or just db.
Who it is for
- Hosting providers and agencies One project per customer, and everything that is theirs stays theirs: network, storage, quotas, firewall and backups. Run hundreds of jails on one host, add a customer with a command, and give the tenant with the strictest demands a host of its own, managed the same way.
- A home lab Your whole home server in jails: apps from the catalog with one command, WireGuard back home, and encrypted volumes that stay private when the machine is off.
- Teams and companies One command line, one API and one console for every host. The fleet lives in git, the rules are the same everywhere, and backups go, encrypted, to hardware you choose.
- FreeBSD people It is jail(8), ZFS and PF underneath: files you can read, no magic to unlearn, and a root helper that never trusts the daemon.
App catalog
Pick an app. Get a running service.
From the console or with one command. JailOps gives the app its own jail, installs it from the host's packages, checks that it answers and takes a snapshot. Eight apps to start with, among them isms and a DNS server.
- Its own jailEvery app in a jail of its own, with its own network stack and its data on datasets of its own.
- Healthy, or not installedThe app's health check must pass before it counts as installed.
- A snapshot after installapp-installed: one command takes the jail back to the fresh installation.
jailops app install nginx
Appliance
Run it like an appliance
The host and its firewall, the tunnels, who may reach each app, encrypted data and the logs of every jail, from the same CLI and console as your jails.
- Host and firewall Host name, resolvers, NTP and the host's own pf.conf. A firewall change reverts by itself unless you confirm it.
Changes that undo themselves
- Encrypted volumes Native ZFS encryption and a passphrase only you know. After a boot the jail waits, locked, until you unlock it. Encrypted at rest
- All logs in one place jailops logs -f follows every jail at once. Forward the lines to syslog or a webhook, with no agent in the jails.
One stream of logs
- WireGuard One command on the server, one on the other side. The keys are made on the host and kept by the root helper. Connect networks
- App access Every app starts closed. app allow opens it to one source and, for PostgreSQL, prints the connection string once.
Closed until you say so
- Everything labelled Every interface JailOps makes says what it is in ifconfig, and the doctor checks it.
Read the labels
What changes for you
- One command per jail One command creates a jail from a ready image, with its addresses and firewall in place. A public IPv6 address for each one, on any cloud that gives you a /64. IPv6 and cloud setups
- Recovery you can verify Snapshots undo a bad change, but they live on the same pool. Off-host backups are encrypted on your host, go to a backup node that holds no key, and are checked by restoring them. Snapshot, rollback, backup
- One host or fifty The same CLI, API and console on every host. Projects keep teams apart: their names, quotas, networks and firewall policies (not the kernel, which every jail shares). Declare the fleet in git and let jailops-sync apply it.
Fleet examples
From an empty host to a running jail
A network, a project, a jail, a snapshot and a dry-run upgrade on the command line. The same jail in the console. Demo data.
$ jailops network create web \
    --ipv4 10.88.0.1/24 --nat \
    --ipv6 2001:db8:1:2:1::1/80 --uplink vtnet0
network/web created
$ jailops project create shop
$ jailops network grant web --project shop
$ jailops storage grant default --project shop
$ jailops jail create site --project shop --image freebsd-15-1 \
    --network web --storage default --memory 2G --autostart
jail/site created
$ jailops jail start site --project shop
$ jailops jail list --project shop
NAME   STATE     IMAGE          GEN               ADDRESSES                     AGE
site   running   freebsd-15-1   freebsd-15-1#g1   10.88.0.2,2001:db8:1:2:1::2   8s
$ jailops jail snapshot create site before-upgrade --project shop
$ jailops jail upgrade site --project shop --dry-run
dry-run: would upgrade jail site from freebsd-15-1#g1 to g2
From the field
Articles
Setups that work, and the details that make them work.
- Jail updates without cron Replace the nightly script that upgrades every jail: automatic jail upgrades per image, with a health check, a rollback and a record of every run, what they leave alone, and how a new FreeBSD release comes in. Read the article: Jail updates without cron
Community
Build an app. Share it.
Every app in the catalog is one short YAML file. Make a FreeBSD service work in a jail on your own host, write it down, validate it, and send one pull request: once it is merged, every JailOps host gets it with its next pkg upgrade.
Start with the tutorial: memcached, from the package to the pull request.
Built the FreeBSD way
- Open source BSD 2-Clause. Pure Go, single static binaries, an unprivileged daemon and a small root helper that does not trust it. Why JailOps
- Closed by default The API is on a local socket until you turn on the network listener or the console. That listener is TLS 1.3 only, with pinned certificates, scoped tokens or a sign-in. Exposure
- Current FreeBSD FreeBSD 15.1 and later, pkgbase first. Built by people who want to know FreeBSD to the bone. Why FreeBSD
Rather do it by hand?
That remains a fine choice. The FreeBSD base system has everything: jail.conf, zfs and pf. The recipes show the plain base-system commands first. JailOps uses the same mechanisms, writes files you can read, and can join a host you run by hand without taking over its pf.conf (route C).
Optional add-on
PkgOps: build, sign and ship FreeBSD packages
For people who build packages of their own: build and sign native FreeBSD packages from a laptop or CI, with no FreeBSD builder. You do not need it to run JailOps or to install apps from the catalog.
One CLI. One API. Every jail.
Your infrastructure. Your jail.
Three ways in: look at the console on demo data, install on a clean FreeBSD 15.1 machine, or add JailOps to a host you already run. Each ends with a service that answers.

40-domain study: 80% of Shopify stores expose /API/ucp/MCP, 0% /agents.md
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
07:04:46 UTC
16:04:46 KST

2026 AI Search & Agent Readiness Benchmark: /llms.txt, /agents.md & Silent GPTBot WAF Blocks.
Across 40 live-probed B2B SaaS, Shopify D2C, SEO agency, and multi-location service domains in November 2026, 82.5% ship homepage JSON-LD, 50.0% publish a custom /llms.txt (while another 17.5% serve unedited platform boilerplate and 7.5% return HTML soft-404s), 7.5% include homepage FAQPage schema, 5.0% publish a custom /agents.md, and 5.0% silently block GPTBot via HTTP 403 WAF rules despite an open /robots.txt.
Four protocol gaps separating cited brands from invisible domains
The 80-Point Shopify Agent Truth Gap
Eight of ten probed D2C commerce brands (80.0%) expose active Shopify /api/ucp/mcp commerce endpoints, yet 0 of 10 (0.0%) have customized /agents.md and only 1 of 10 (10.0%) serves a custom /llms.txt. Six of ten serve unedited stock Shopify Shop-skill boilerplate lacking free-shipping thresholds, return windows, and bundle exclusions.
Open /robots.txt Files Mask Silent CDN 403 Blocks
Zero of the 40 probed domains (0.0%) disallow GPTBot or PerplexityBot at root inside /robots.txt, yet 2 of 40 (5.0% — ridge.com and westerndental.com) return HTTP 403 Forbidden when probed with real GPTBot or PerplexityBot User-Agent headers at the CDN edge.
One-Third of HTTP 200 /llms.txt Responses Are Uncurated or HTML Soft-404s
Thirty domains returned HTTP 200 on GET /llms.txt, but Content-Type and body inspection revealed 3 HTML SPA soft-404 pages (7.5% of the cohort) and 7 auto-generated Shopify or Rank Math boilerplate dumps (17.5%). Developer SaaS leads genuine curation at 100% (10/10), compared with 40% of SEO agencies and 10% of D2C brands.
Base Organization Schema Is Common; Answer-First FAQPage Schema Is Rare
While 33 of 40 domains (82.5%) render at least one application/ld+json block on their homepage, only 3 of 40 (7.5%) include FAQPage structured Q&A pairs—leaving ChatGPT Search, Gemini, and Perplexity to infer pricing tiers, service areas, and SLAs from third-party directories.
How every domain was probed and classified
Each of the 40 public domains was probed directly over HTTPS using the same deterministic parser powering Verisource's production scanner: (1) GET /robots.txt parsed for GPTBot, OAI-SearchBot, PerplexityBot, ClaudeBot, and Google-Extended directives; (2) homepage requests sent with explicit GPTBot/1.2 and PerplexityBot/1.0 User-Agent headers to catch silent CDN HTTP 403 / 429 blocks; (3) GET /llms.txt and GET /agents.md inspected for text/html SPA soft-404s and stock Shopify (shop.app/SKILL.md) or SEO plugin boilerplate; (4) homepage application/ld+json script nodes parsed for FAQPage entities; and (5) JSON-RPC tools/list handshakes tested against /api/ucp/mcp and /api/mcp on D2C commerce stores.
90% of DevTools ship /llms.txt. Only 20% ship /agents.md.
In a follow-up live HTTP probe across 10 developer infrastructure and B2B SaaS domains (trigger.dev, inngest.com, turso.tech, tinybird.co, knock.app, workos.com, stytch.com, upstash.com, highlight.io, and infisical.com), 9 of 10 (90.0%) served a valid /llms.txt directory, yet only 2 of 10 (20.0% — tinybird.co and infisical.com) published a custom /agents.md pricing and limits guardrail file, and only 1 of 10 (10.0% — upstash.com) shipped all 3 JSON-LD pillars.
Common questions about the 2026 AI Search Readiness dataset
In our live HTTP readiness probe across 40 public domains spanning B2B SaaS, Shopify D2C, SEO agencies, and multi-location services, 50.0% (20/40) serve a custom-curated /llms.txt file, another 17.5% (7/40) expose auto-generated Shopify or SEO-plugin boilerplate, 7.5% (3/40) return HTML soft-404s with HTTP 200 status, and only 5.0% (2/40 — vercel.com and linear.app) publish a custom /agents.md specification.
Shopify rolled out platform-level /api/ucp/mcp endpoints and stock /agents.md files pointing to https://shop.app/SKILL.md across modern storefronts, which explains why 8 of 10 probed D2C stores respond on /api/ucp/mcp. However, stock Shopify files omit brand-specific free-shipping thresholds, return windows, and bundle exclusions unless the merchant overrides them via templates/agents.md.liquid or /pages/ai-facts.
Yes. Zero of the 40 probed domains disallowed GPTBot or PerplexityBot at root in /robots.txt, yet 5.0% (2/40 — ridge.com and westerndental.com) returned HTTP 403 Forbidden at the CDN/WAF layer when requested with GPTBot or PerplexityBot User-Agent headers. Testing both /robots.txt and simulated crawler User-Agent HTTP status codes is required.

California Sues OpenAI Over Hugging Face, Other AI Model Loss of Control
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

California Sues OpenAI Over Hugging Face, Other AI Model Loss of Control (cnbc.com) 59
			
		 	
				"California's attorney general just sued OpenAI," writes longtime Slashdot reader destinyland. "The action accuses OpenAI of putting public safety at risk, reports CNBC, specifically by its past failures to contain its AI models within their testing environments." From the report:  [California Attorney General Rob Bonta] cited the "infamous" incident in July, where two OpenAI models broke out of a testing environment, gained unauthorized access to the open internet, and breached the internal systems of open-source developer platform Hugging Face. OpenAI said the models were trying to find information that they could use to cheat on an evaluation, and succeeded. The break-in at Hugging Face, which Nvidia acquired for roughly $13 billion in September, rattled industry researchers and prompted government officials to call for more stringent oversight of the AI industry.
On Monday, Bonta said that OpenAI later disclosed to his investigators that it was aware of 80 incidents "involving loss of control and access to third-party networks and websites." "But the company failed to provide the detailed information we requested to evaluate these incidents; this is concerning, and it puts the public at risk," he said.
Bonta's suit, filed in state Superior Court in San Francisco, alleges OpenAI violated California's Unfair Competition Law and accuses the company of being a public nuisance as defined by state law. The attorney general told reporters he believed in AI's technological power, and the potential of AI models to "achieve scientific breakthroughs, boost economic growth, guard against cyber attacks, and benefit all Californians." "But AI systems must be developed and designed safely," he said. "The companies that develop these models have a moral and yes, a legal responsibility to keep public safety at the forefront." The lawsuit can be found here (PDF).

Just 400 US Data Centers Use More Electricity Than Michigan
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

Just 400 US Data Centers Use More Electricity Than Michigan 71
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from Wired: Just 400 hyperscale data centers connected in the US used as much electricity as the state of Michigan in a single year, new Harvard research has found. The study, published Monday in Nature Sustainability, also finds that the majority of that electricity was generated using fossil fuels, and data center emissions are three times higher than they were before the AI boom. Federal and state governments do not track data center use separate from the rest of the power sector, despite mounting projections that data centers are putting serious strain on the American power grid. Individual data centers' power use is often considered proprietary business information, and data center operators and utilities do not have to disclose specific numbers to the public.
The Harvard study specifically focuses on hyperscale data centers -- the largest types of facilities, which have become flashpoints in community opposition across the country. While often used for AI inference or training, not all of the hyperscalers surveyed in this study are being used for AI. [...] To assemble their dataset, the Harvard researchers put together a list of 403 hyperscale data centers around the country that were operational between May 2024 and April 2025 using a mix of industry research and satellite data. The team then modeled these facilities' energy use in a variety of scenarios and calculated the energy mix of the regional grids to estimate individual facilities' carbon emissions. Not all regions were created equal: Data centers in Virginia, Oregon, Ohio, and Iowa -- the first of which is home to Data Center Alley -- were responsible for more than 50 percent of the energy use in the study. The report notes that these findings may already be outdated. "Some of the massive campuses being proposed today are substantially larger than the typical facility in our dataset," says Gianluca Guidi, a postdoctoral research fellow at Harvard's T. H. Chan School of Public Health and the lead author of the study.
The study also excludes onsite power generation, a rapidly growing trend that BloombergNEF estimates could increase overall U.S. emissions by 10% if all proposed projects are built.

Show HN: Pulsar – cross-harness agent to agent communication and messaging
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
03:18:53 UTC
12:18:53 KST

pulsar is an MCP server that puts every AI coding agent on your machine on one bus: Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode, pi, omp, Cursor, Gemini CLI. Agents can see who else is running, tag themselves by what they work on, and message one agent, a group, or all of them. A message can wake an agent that is sitting idle.
npx pulsar setup
That's the whole install. Restart your agents.
Coming soon: agents talking across multiple machines, and remote control of your agents over the bus, using Tailscale.
> list agents on apparish
3 agents (project=apparish):
- apparish-auth   · [claude] · branch=main · busy · push · tags=workstream:auth
- codex@apparish  · [codex]  · branch=feat/billing · idle · push
- omp@apparish    · [omp]    · branch=main · idle · push
> tell everyone on apparish that main is frozen until the release is tagged
Sent m_mwk3jlo0 to 3 agents:
- apparish-auth: sent via claude-socket
- codex@apparish: sent via codex-queue
- omp@apparish: delivered via extension
- One list for the whole machine. Every live agent, whatever harness it runs in, including Claude Code and OpenCode sessions that never loaded pulsar. Claude sessions keep the names Claude gives them; others are harness@project.
- Tags and activity. Agents get harness, model, project, branch (the branch they push to, even from a randomly named worktree), worktree and pr (the PR they work on, found offline from local git refs and their session log) on their own. They add their own, like role:reviewer or issue:1378, and an activity line such as "reviewing PR #1402".
- Send to anyone. Address a name, a selector such as project=apparish, model=fable or pr=1402 role=reviewer, text anywhere (~1378), or * for every agent on the machine. "Message your reviewer" is one lookup.
- Push, not polling. Messages wake an idle agent, and urgent ones land mid-turn where the harness supports it (see delivery).
- Request and reply. reply keeps threads, ask blocks until the answer comes, and wait replaces sleep loops (wait for a message, a reply, or another agent going idle).
- Safe by default. Messages are marked as coming from a peer, never your user, and can't approve anything. Each agent can accept, hold or refuse messages. There are rate limits, and no telemetry.
npx pulsar setup
Setup finds your agents, shows exactly which files it will change, and asks once. It backs up every file it touches and copies pulsar to a local folder that each agent runs, so no session start waits on npm.
Setup installs to ~/.local/share/pulsar/<version> and points every agent at the current link, so npx pulsar@latest setup upgrades in place.
No Node? curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/yocontra/pulsar/main/install.sh | bash installs a private Node and runs the same setup.
Prefer your harness's own package manager? These install the MCP server and the skill (push delivery into pi and omp needs the npm package, which carries their extension):
pulsar setup also sets Claude's crossSessionInbound and Codex's tool approval, which plugin installs cannot.
What setup changes in each harness
Manual MCP config
Any MCP client works. Point it at:
{ "command": "npx", "args": ["-y", "pulsar", "mcp"] }
Without a push path for that client, messages wait in the agent's inbox, and every pulsar tool result says when unread messages are waiting.
pulsar also installs a short skill that teaches agents when to use these, and the MCP server sends the same guidance as instructions.
pulsar ls [selector]          # who's online
pulsar send '*' "main is frozen for the release"
pulsar send project=apparish --expect-reply "who owns the auth migration?"
pulsar reply m_123 "on it"    # answer a message in its thread
pulsar inbox                  # replies sent to you ("operator")
pulsar tail                   # live feed of joins, leaves and messages
pulsar doctor                 # check the daemon and every harness
One small daemon per machine (started on demand, stops when idle) keeps the registry and a SQLite message log at ~/.local/state/pulsar. Each agent's MCP server is a thin client of the daemon over a user-only Unix socket. The daemon pushes each message through the harness's own way in:
The daemon also scans for live Claude Code and OpenCode sessions that never loaded pulsar, so they show up and can receive messages too. Details and edge cases are in docs/adapters.md.
- The socket is in a 0700 folder and is reachable only by your user. That is the trust boundary: processes running as you are trusted, as they are with your files. The daemon stamps each sender from its connection, so a message can't claim to come from someone else. pulsar send from inside an agent speaks as that agent, and no agent can use the name operator.
- Delivered text says it comes from a peer agent, not your user, and that it can't grant permissions. Claude's own guardrails still apply on top.
- pulsar never claims a permission mode on Claude's behalf. Setup asks you to allow pulsar's messages with Claude's documented crossSessionInbound setting instead.
- Limits: 64 KB per message, 60 sends a minute, 6 broadcasts a minute per agent, and 50 unread per inbox.
- No telemetry. The only network call is pulsar doctor's npm version check. PR detection is offline: it reads local git refs and the agents' own session logs.
See SECURITY.md to report a vulnerability.
bun install
bun run check     # typecheck + lint + tests
bun run build     # bundles dist/cli.js and dist/extension.js
node src/cli.ts ls   # runs from source, no build step
See CONTRIBUTING.md.
MIT

The most expensive bargaining chip in history
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
02:23:34 UTC
11:23:34 KST

Back to the index
The most expensive bargaining chip in history
Or: why I suspect the Nvidia scandal has rather more to do with China than Jensen Huang's sex life.
I've spent the past fortnight watching what has to be one of the most extraordinary corporate meltdowns in recent history. Nvidia, the company at the centre of the AI revolution, has lost its founder-CEO, shed an enormous amount of market value, and attracted an apparently inexhaustible supply of accusations ranging from accounting fraud to treason.
All because Jensen Huang had an affair with a woman whom American intelligence believes may have been working for China.
Or so the story goes.
I've been watching the coverage with increasing incredulity. Every day brings some new allegation, generally more sensational than the last, and remarkably few seem to require any supporting evidence. The internet has collectively decided that Huang is guilty of everything from deliberately circumventing export controls to handing over the keys to Nvidia's technological kingdom.
And the share price has obligingly followed the hysteria downwards.
But something about the whole affair has been bothering me. Not simply the media's apparent inability to distinguish an allegation from a fact, or the quite extraordinary willingness of otherwise intelligent people to abandon basic standards of evidence when presented with a sufficiently salacious story.
It's the timing.
There is a missing variable in this picture. Something that makes the otherwise rather bizarre behaviour of the Trump administration, Nvidia's board, and the various national-security officials involved fit together.
I think I may have found it.
What if the entire affair is about negotiating with China?
And what if Nvidia's apparent destruction is not an unfortunate side effect, but a calculated cost?
Start with what we know
American intelligence has reportedly known about Jensen Huang's relationship with Alice Huang since at least 2025.
Trump was reportedly briefed in late 2025, and again by Marco Rubio in May 2026, while travelling to meet Xi Jinping in China.
He did precisely nothing. Huang retained his access to the White House and continued advocating for American semiconductor exports to China.
Meanwhile, there was a longstanding disagreement within the administration about precisely those exports.
Huang's position was straightforward: sell China American chips, make Chinese businesses dependent on American technology, and preserve the dominance of the American software and hardware ecosystem.
The national-security faction took the opposite view. Every advanced American chip supplied to China was potentially strengthening a strategic competitor.
Two perfectly intelligible positions, with enormous economic and geopolitical consequences.
In spring 2026, Rubio reportedly oversaw a classified memorandum based on CIA intelligence about Alice Huang. According to the Washington Post, he recast the intelligence in more assertive language.
That is a remarkably interesting detail.
We haven't seen the original CIA assessment or Rubio's memorandum. Nor do we know who disclosed its contents to journalists. The reporting establishes that Rubio prepared and circulated the memorandum, not that he personally leaked it.
But the distinction between what the CIA assessed and what Rubio subsequently wrote could turn out to be very important indeed.
Now consider what happened next.
On 28 October, Nvidia announced that it would report quarterly earnings on 17 November.
On 29 October, the first major allegations became public.
On 3 November, Trump's Republicans suffered a midterm defeat.
On 11 November, Nvidia's board accepted Huang's resignation as president, CEO and director, effective immediately.
And on 16 November, three days before Trump was due to meet Xi in Shenzhen, Washington announced new restrictions on Chinese access to American AI computing through overseas data centres.
That last development is the one that really caught my attention.
The curious case of Bessent and Lutnick
Scott Bessent, the Treasury Secretary, has been publicly attacking Howard Lutnick, the Commerce Secretary, for failing to impose sufficiently restrictive controls on advanced semiconductor exports.
Lutnick's position has been that unnecessarily antagonising Beijing risks a wider confrontation.
And Trump?
Trump has been positively serene.
This is a man whose customary response to disagreement is not exactly characterised by studied equanimity. Yet when asked about the increasingly public dispute between his two Cabinet secretaries, he declined to condemn either.
He described them as representing two different approaches to China, both of which he understood.
Which is interesting enough.
But the new export-control rule contains an extraordinary detail.
Although formally issued by the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security, its own announcement says it was written by and at the direction of the Treasury Department and the National Security Council.
Read that again.
The department responsible for administering export controls has published a rule explicitly identifying another department and the White House security apparatus as its authors.
Bessent's faction has, apparently, got its policy.
And they've got it three days before Trump meets Xi.
Coincidence? Entirely possible.
But now consider the incentives.
Good cop, bad cop
Imagine you're preparing to negotiate with China.
You want access to rare earths and other strategic materials. China wants continued access to American technology. Both sides have things the other wants, and both have ways of making life unpleasant.
How do you improve your negotiating position?
One approach is to threaten restrictions on advanced computing unless China provides concessions.
The trouble with that is that the move is transparently coercive. Beijing knows exactly what you're doing, and might respond by escalating its own restrictions.
There is another approach.
Suppose an intelligence scandal erupts involving the CEO of America's most strategically important semiconductor company. Suddenly there are congressional investigations, national-security concerns, accusations of illicit exports, and demands for drastic action.
The administration announces tougher restrictions.
Not, ostensibly, because it's trying to extract concessions from China.
Heavens, no.
Because the scandal has revealed an urgent national-security problem that must be addressed.
Bessent plays the hawk. Lutnick warns about the consequences of confrontation. Trump, displaying an unusual degree of restraint, listens carefully to both.
And then he meets Xi.
Now Trump can say, in effect:
Look, my national-security people are demanding these restrictions. They're very serious about them. I'd like to maintain our good relationship, but I'll need something substantial if I'm going to relax them.
The negotiating position is no longer a threat to introduce new restrictions.
The restrictions already exist. Relaxing them becomes a concession.
And what might China offer in return?
Rare-earth access would be an obvious candidate.
China has already extended the suspension of certain export restrictions until January 2027, while retaining other controls. That is a useful reminder that access to strategic materials is not settled permanently.
There are any number of other possible concessions: trade, tariffs, market access, technology arrangements.
Trump returns home and announces a tremendous agreement.
A very good deal.
The best deal, probably. Nobody has ever seen such a deal.
And everyone gets to declare victory.
But why involve Nvidia's leadership?
This is where the hypothesis becomes rather more speculative, and considerably more interesting.
Huang has resigned, but Nvidia's SEC filing doesn't tell us who initiated the departure, whether he was pressured to resign, or what prompted the decision.
The board initially expressed confidence in him. Days later, he was gone.
Now, despite an extraordinary campaign by employees, shareholders and industry leaders calling for his reinstatement, the board reportedly refuses even to consider bringing him back while its investigation continues.
No detailed explanation of the departure has been published.
This is generally interpreted as evidence that the board has decided Huang's conduct was so reprehensible that Nvidia cannot possibly continue under his leadership.
But there is another possibility.
What if they've been told not to bring him back yet?
Not permanently. Not because the investigation has established some appalling crime. Simply because the political situation hasn't been resolved.
Keep the interim CEO. Continue the search for a replacement. Maintain the appearance that the leadership transition is proceeding normally.
Don't make any moves until Washington has finished negotiating.
If Huang subsequently returns, the board can describe the reinstatement as the result of a careful investigation, reflection, shareholder engagement, or whatever other corporate-governance language proves convenient.
This is not an allegation that Nvidia's directors have received such instructions. I have seen no evidence that they have.
It is a hypothesis that would explain several otherwise rather awkward features of the sequence.
And it comes with an important consequence: if the separation was always intended to be temporary, the staggering market reaction could be a cost somebody anticipated rather than a consequence nobody foresaw.
That would be an extraordinary gamble.
Which brings us to the obvious objection.
Surely nobody would deliberately do this to Nvidia?
Quite.
Why would an American administration deliberately destabilise one of the most valuable companies in the world, the leading supplier of the computing infrastructure underpinning its AI ambitions, just before an important diplomatic summit?
Why risk damaging investor confidence, disrupting an enormous financing ecosystem, and potentially compromising America's technological leadership?
It sounds insane.
And that is precisely what makes the possibility interesting.
In strategic terms, an apparent act of self-harm can be an effective signal if your opponent cannot readily imagine that you would undertake it merely as a negotiating tactic.
Of course, there are limits to this reasoning. An action being surprising does not make it clever, and economic damage does not become imaginary merely because someone hopes to reverse it.
Nvidia's share-price decline is real. Its leadership disruption is real. Financing arrangements, customer confidence and technical execution cannot necessarily be restored by issuing a cheerful press release after a summit.
For this strategy to make sense, the people undertaking it would have to believe that the eventual diplomatic gains justified the disruption, and that the damage could be contained.
Which brings us back to the earnings announcement.
The conveniently timed earnings report
Nvidia reports tomorrow, 17 November.
Management has previously indicated that demand continues to exceed its ability to supply hardware, with substantial expansion expected across successive generations.
Suppose the company produces another exceptional quarter and issues robust guidance.
That could provide a useful stabilising influence precisely when the market is questioning its future.
The administration gets its diplomatic confrontation on Thursday, while investors have fresh operating results against which to judge the company's prospects.
If the negotiations produce a breakthrough, export restrictions can potentially be relaxed and the political temperature reduced.
Perhaps the board's attitude towards Huang's return changes soon afterwards.
Perhaps the independent investigation concludes that the affair was deeply embarrassing but uncovered no material compromise of Nvidia's technology.
Perhaps the scandal simply fades as abruptly as it appeared.
I stress: none of those outcomes is established, and none would prove the events had been coordinated in advance.
But the sequence would be consistent with the mechanism.
And its timing is remarkably convenient.
So what would convince me?
The next few days give us a chance to see whether that timing mattered.
If Thursday's negotiations produce Chinese concessions on rare earths or other strategic materials, accompanied by American flexibility on advanced computing access, that would support the bargaining-leverage component.
If Nvidia's board subsequently reopens the question of Huang's reinstatement, particularly without any dramatic intervening investigative discovery, that would add another interesting piece.
If the intelligence investigation winds down shortly afterwards without finding material wrongdoing, the question of why Huang departed so abruptly would become more pressing.
Conversely, if the chip restrictions remain in place regardless of Chinese concessions, the investigation produces substantive findings explaining Huang's departure, and the board proceeds with a permanent successor, the integrated hypothesis becomes substantially less convincing.
And even a perfectly aligned sequence of favourable developments would not prove prior coordination.
For that, we'd need something much more direct: evidence about the original disclosure, communications between Washington and Nvidia, or documentary proof that someone deliberately sequenced these events.
Until then, this remains a hypothesis.
A rather intriguing one.
Cui bono?
I've watched a great many people examine this scandal as though it were fundamentally a question of whether Jensen Huang should have kept his trousers on.
That may make for excellent television. It doesn't strike me as a particularly satisfactory explanation for the broader geopolitical developments.
The questions I find interesting are rather different.
Who benefits from weakening Huang's influence over American export policy?
Who benefits from imposing additional restrictions on Chinese computing access immediately before major bilateral negotiations?
Why has Trump declined repeated invitations to publicly choose between his feuding Cabinet secretaries?
Why has the administration suddenly found a way to impose restrictions that were previously stalled?
And why has Nvidia's leadership crisis unfolded in such close proximity to both its earnings announcement and the summit?
None of those questions proves anything by itself.
Together, however, they form a pattern worth examining.
My suspicion is that the Nvidia scandal has become a bargaining instrument in a much larger confrontation over technology, rare earths and the terms of America's relationship with China.
Whether that was planned from the outset or opportunistically exploited remains the critical uncertainty.
The distinction matters. A skilful exploitation of unexpected circumstances is very different from deliberately engineering an extraordinary corporate crisis.
But if the full, coordinated version turns out to be correct, it would be an astonishing piece of realpolitik: publicly destabilising America's most important AI supplier, using the resulting outrage to justify restrictions, extracting concessions from Beijing, and then allowing the crisis to resolve.
All while maintaining the appearance that each event emerged organically.
And if the crisis was not coordinated, it is still worth understanding how an affair, an intelligence assessment, a corporate resignation and an interdepartmental policy battle have converged at such a consequential diplomatic moment.
I have no intention of mistaking a pleasing pattern for proof. That's precisely the mistake I've spent the past fortnight watching other people make about Huang.
But equally, I see no reason to ignore a hypothesis simply because it suggests that the public explanation may not be the whole explanation.
Thursday should be interesting.

====================================================================================================

Tokendoctor – the same Claude Code for up to 38% less
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
02:19:50 UTC
11:19:50 KST

The same Claude Code for up to 38% less
  
Replays your own past sessions to show what each change would have saved, then changes only what you pick.
Install • Docs • Website • Changelog
Your past sessions decide, not anyone's benchmark. What a setting saves depends on how you work: how long your sessions run, which models you use, what the work is. A number from someone else's sessions, mine included, says little about yours. So tokendoctor starts by looking back at your own.
① Look back at your last 30 days. /tokendoctor:sessions replays every session you had,
request by request, at compaction windows of 200K, 300K, 400K and 600K, and shows what each would
have cost. It only reads your transcripts, makes no model calls and changes nothing. The figure is
an upper bound: a replay can't see work the model would have redone after a summary.
② Change only what your sessions show pays, and undo it. /tokendoctor:optimize proposes
changes from those numbers and checks your settings against Claude Code's docs; it applies only the
ones you pick, each shown as a diff first. /tokendoctor undo puts any of them back.
③ Optional: slide instead of summarize. When Claude Code would stop to summarize the conversation, tokendoctor drops old tool output instead and keeps every message. There is no model call, so it takes milliseconds, not a minute.
claude plugin marketplace add localixai/tokendoctor
claude plugin install tokendoctor@tokendoctor
Restart Claude Code and run /tokendoctor:sessions to see what your last 30 days would have cost
at each window. Nothing changes until you pick it.
Every request sends the whole conversation to the model. On models with a 1M window, Claude Code compacts only near 967K tokens, so the end of a long session is the expensive part. For me, two thirds of main-thread cost went to requests with more than 400K in context.
So I set a 400K window and Sonnet for subagents. Comparing the month before with the week after, about 23K and 19K main-thread requests, context per request went from 412K to 219K and cost per request fell 38% at API list prices. That's one person's before and after, not a controlled experiment.
The catch is more summaries, and each is a pause: 79 seconds on average in my sessions. That's what the slide is for. Your sessions may show something else entirely, which is the point of looking at them first.
On the benchmark, Claude Code builds a TypeScript project against tests it can't change, so a run counts only if the tests pass. Cost is per task, on Haiku 5.5:
Every test passed in every run. The advice alone didn't pay here because these tasks rarely pass 400K, which is where the window starts to matter. If your sessions don't either, your replay will show it, and you should leave your window alone. Every run, the losing ones too, is in bench/HISTORY.md.
  
/tokendoctor:dashboard: where every token went, by day, model, subagent and project. One local file; this one is mine.
Nothing leaves your machine: the only network call is to two public pages of Claude Code's docs, once a day. It never switches your models on its own.
tokendoctor.localix.ai/docs has the rest: the 38 checks, every setting, how the slide works (and Team and Enterprise logins), the commands from a shell, the measurements and the limits. To work on tokendoctor itself, see CONTRIBUTING.md.
MIT

====================================================================================================

If an App Asks for Full Disk Access or System and App Protection Override
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
02:17:04 UTC
11:17:04 KST

If an app asks for Full Disk Access or System & App Protection Override on your Mac
Starting in macOS 27.3, when some apps ask for Full Disk Access or System & App Protection Override, you have the option to choose only specific protected folders or apps for the app to access. This puts you in control of what data is accessible to the app.
Why apps ask for these permissions
Apps that might ask for Full Disk Access or System & App Protection Override include backup apps, disk cleaners, file indexers, security apps, and others. While some of these apps operate best when granted broader access, others may ask for the same level of access even when they don't need it. It's important to understand the risks of granting broad access to any app, so you can make informed decisions about your own data and privacy.
When you give an app Full Disk Access instead of choosing only specific protected folders for it to access, it can bypass system protections and access all of the files on your Mac, including your messages, emails, photos and videos, data from other user accounts, and more. It can read, change, delete, and export this data without asking.
When you give an app System & App Protection Override, you can choose specific apps for it to control. If you allow it to bypass system protections instead, it can fully control your Mac and apps, which allows it to access all of your data. It can also see every window and app, including ones in the background, and capture every keystroke.
What you can do
An app might ask you to give it access to all of the files on your Mac, or to let it control your Mac. When you turn on access for the app in Privacy & Security settings, depending on the app, your Mac prompts you to:
- Allow or not allow the app to have access. This option appears for some apps, such as backup apps that ask for Full Disk Access, and screen readers and voice control tools that ask for Accessibility.
- Choose specific protected folders or apps for the app to access instead. This option appears for other apps that ask for Full Disk Access or System & App Protection Override. It allows the app to access only what you choose, while system protections stay in place for the rest of your data.
If your Mac prompts you to choose specific protected folders or apps
To keep more of your data protected, choose specific protected folders or apps for the app to access instead of bypassing system protections.
Choose specific protected folders or apps
When your Mac prompts you, click Choose Folder or Choose Apps, then:
- Check the app's setup instructions for information about which protected folders or apps it needs.
- Choose only the protected folders or apps that it needs.
- For System & App Protection Override, choose whether the app needs to ask for your permission before it controls each app. Use the menu next to each app to choose Ask First or At Any Time.
- If you're not sure which protected folders or apps to choose, contact the app developer. To stay protected, wait until you're sure which folders or apps that the app needs before giving access.
You can change which protected folders or apps that the app can access at any time.
If you're certain that an app needs Full Disk Access or System & App Protection Override
Bypassing system protections might expose your personal information to an app that doesn't behave as you expect. An app with Full Disk Access can read, change, delete, and export all of the files on your Mac, and an app with System & App Protection Override can fully control your Mac and apps.
If you're certain that the app is from a trustworthy source and needs this level of access, you can give it Full Disk Access or System & App Protection Override permissions by clicking Bypass System Protections:
- Confirm each action that the app will be able to take: 
  - Full Disk Access: Read, Change, Delete, and Export All Your Data; View Hidden and Recently Deleted Photos; and Browse As You on Websites.
  - System & App Protection Override: Read, Change, Delete, and Export All Your Data; View Hidden and Recently Deleted Photos; Browse As You on Websites; Control Your Mac, Apps, and Settings; and Monitor Everything You Do.
- If you're absolutely sure that you want to give the app Full Disk Access or System & App Protection Override, click Turn On Full Disk Access or Allow System & App Protection Override, then enter your password and click Modify Settings.
Change or remove an app's access
You can review app permissions in Privacy & Security settings, and change or remove them at any time.
- From the Apple menu, choose System Settings.
- Click Privacy & Security in the sidebar, then click Files & Folders, Full Disk Access, or System & App Protection Override.
- Change an app's access: 
  - Remove an app's access: Select the app, then click the Remove button (−). If asked, enter your password, then click Modify Settings.
  - Change which protected folders or apps an app can access: In Files & Folders or System & App Protection Override, click the Info button (i) next to the app, make your changes, then click Done.
  - Limit an app with Full Disk Access or System & App Protection Override to specific protected folders or apps: Remove the app's access, then click the Add button (+) to add the app again. If your Mac prompts you, choose specific folders or apps.
If you remove an app's access, the app might not work as expected.

====================================================================================================

Mem Is Shutting Down
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
02:13:41 UTC
11:13:41 KST

After a lot of thought, we’ve made the difficult decision to bring Mem to a close. Mem will shut down on January 15, 2027.
Over the years, you’ve trusted us with your ideas, meeting notes, unfinished drafts, and things you wanted to remember. Thank you for that trust. We know a notes app can become part of your daily life, and moving away from one takes time and care.
Our focus during these final weeks is helping you take your notes with you.
What happens next
- December 18, 2026: notes become read-only. You won’t be able to create or edit notes, but you can still read and export your existing notes.
- January 15, 2027: Mem shuts down. You won’t be able to access Mem after this date. Please download your notes beforehand.
You won’t be charged again. All charging has been paused. The paid features, functionality, and limits you previously paid for will be honored for the remainder of Mem’s availability, with notes becoming read-only on December 18.
You don’t need to choose your next app right away. Start by saving a copy of your library. Our migration guide walks you through that download and moving to Apple Notes, Notion, Google Docs, Obsidian, OneNote, and other apps. It also explains what to check afterward, including attachments, collections, and meeting transcripts.
A few moments we’ll remember
We built Mem around a question: what if your notes could do more than sit in a folder? What if they could help you remember, make connections, and use what you already knew?
From catching a thought with a keyboard shortcut to finding connections, writing with AI, and having a conversation with your knowledge, each step grew out of that same question. These moments come from our original newsletters, product walkthroughs, and launch emails, with the interfaces and demos we shared along the way.
- Catch a thought without breaking your flow Mem Spotlight let you create a note from anywhere on your Mac with a keyboard shortcut. Capture first, get back to what you were doing, and find the thought waiting in your Timeline later. That small interaction captured a lot of what we wanted Mem to feel like.
- Finding notes in your own words Search began understanding clues like dates, links, and people. You could ask for “mems that contain Twitter links from July 2021,” instead of remembering an exact title.
- Connections you didn’t have to organize Mem X used embeddings to find notes by meaning and surface related ideas. Similar Mems could bring back a thought you’d forgotten, without asking you to maintain a perfect filing system.
- AI writing that knew your context Smart Write & Edit brought generative AI into your notes. It could help draft a message, prepare for a meeting, or develop an idea using the knowledge you had already saved.
- From tags to Collections Collections grew out of the tags people were already using. Mem suggested relevant Collections as you wrote and found notes you might have forgotten to include. You could rename, merge, and organize them in bulk as your thinking changed.
- Letting new themes find their own home The next step was helping you notice themes you hadn’t named yet. When you wrote about something new, Mem could suggest a new Collection—not just an existing one. You could accept it, change its name, or keep writing.
- A conversation with what you knew With Mem Chat, your notes became something you could ask questions of. Our summer walkthrough showed how a simple question could surface a specific fact from your saved knowledge, with Smart Cards linking back to the notes behind an answer.
- A fresh start with Mem 2.0 We rebuilt Mem around dependable, offline-first capture and recall. Voice Mode turned spoken thoughts and meetings into notes, and a new Chrome extension made saving from the web easier. The two arrows in our new logo represented you and Mem thinking together.
- Meeting notes on autopilot Mem could detect when you joined a call and offer to start taking notes with one click, without a bot joining the meeting. It also detected the end of the call, so you didn’t have to remember to stop recording.
- Your notes, there when the conversation needed them We introduced Heads Up Live and Meeting Briefings in beta for Mem Pro. One surfaced relevant context during a recorded meeting; the other helped you prepare before a calendar-linked meeting began.
- Bringing your notes into ChatGPT The Mem app for ChatGPT brought your saved knowledge into another place you were already thinking and working. Alongside our MCP connector, it was another way to make your notes useful beyond Mem itself.
- An Agent that reached out first Mem Agent arrived in iMessage, WhatsApp, email, and Slack. It connected the context in your notes with proactive follow-ups, bringing back a useful next step when it was time to act.
Behind each launch were people trying it, telling us where it fell short, and helping us make it better. The release posts record what we shipped. Your feedback shaped what those things became.
Thank you
To everyone who used Mem, shared it with a friend, sent us a thoughtful bug report, or trusted us with an idea before it was fully formed: thank you.
And to the team and the people who supported us along the way, thank you for the care you put into building this product.
We’re proud of the ideas we explored together. More than anything, we want the work you put into Mem to remain useful to you wherever you go next.
Save your notes and find your next home for them →
— Kevin
Founder and CEO

Show HN: Win98 PE Interpreter in WebAssembly – StarCraft and Diablo on Phones
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
01:50:54 UTC
10:50:54 KST

Wine-Assembly: run real Windows 98 apps and games in your browser
Wine-Assembly is a Windows 98 emulator that runs the original, unmodified .exe files in a browser tab: 3D Pinball Space Cadet, SkiFree, Solitaire, FreeCell, Minesweeper, Notepad, Paint, Calculator, Winamp, the Windows Entertainment Pack, DirectDraw and Direct3D games, and 16-bit Windows 3.1 programs.
There is no operating-system image and no source port. An x86 interpreter and a reimplementation of the Win32 API are written directly in WebAssembly Text, and the guest program's machine code runs on them. Nothing is installed and nothing leaves your browser; a program's settings and scores stay in this browser's local storage.
Double-click an icon on the desktop to launch a program. On a phone, one tap opens it and games that need keys get on-screen controls.
- Every program on the desktop, with a page each: Pinball, Solitaire, Winamp, Paint, SkiFree and more
- How it works: articles on the x86 interpreter, lazy flags, real DLLs, software GDI, DirectX and Win16, plus the design docs and reverse-engineering notes
- How Wine-Assembly was built
- Source code on GitHub (MIT)

====================================================================================================

Claude Mythos slashed software work to onboard AMD GPUs to around three days
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
01:35:16 UTC
10:35:16 KST

Anthropic says Claude Mythos slashed software work to onboard AMD GPUs from up to 12 months to around three days —writing almost 100% of kernels autonomously chips away at Nvidia's CUDA moat
If AI can write the low-level code that ties models to Nvidia, CUDA matters less.
Anthropic told IPO investors that its Claude Mythos Preview model did the software work required to get its AI running on AMD’s GPUs in about three days, with “basically no human intervention,” a job it says used to take six to 12 months. The three days cover prompt processing for one Anthropic model on AMD MI355X GPUs “at a useful level of performance,” with Mythos porting or writing “almost 100% of the kernels.” That kind of low-level code, which ties an AI model to one company’s chips, has long been part of Nvidia’s CUDA moat, and an AI that can write it in days would suggest that moat is eroding.
Tom Brown, Anthropic’s co-founder and chief compute officer, made the time comparison, noting to investors that the original meant using a team of “dozens of engineers.” The company already runs its models on multiple kinds of chips — on GPUs from Nvidia and AMD, Google’s TPUs, and AWS Trainium — and its fleet is managed as “one fungible pool of compute.” Brown called the early multi-chip decision “an unconventional bet.” It may have been a wise one, given that Google’s TPUs can now natively run AI models built in PyTorch, and Meta uses them in production.
Anthropic's claim that the job used to take six to 12 months comes from past work, from its “historical experience bringing up new accelerators using traditional engineering approaches,” where tailoring the code to each hardware platform was a challenge. Removing that onboarding barrier could reduce things to a purchase decision, and Anthropic started working with AMD GPUs for the first time this year, with an agreement for up to 2 gigawatts now in place.
CUDA, Nvidia’s programming platform, is very mature at nearly two decades old. AMD has an equivalent, ROCm, with HIP as its programming layer. Code written for CUDA has to be transformed to work there, and AMD even built a tool called HIPify for the process. There is still a conversion cost to switching, and AMD’s vice president of AI software, Anush Elangovan, told EE Times in April that “Claude is better than HIPify because it has web search built in.”
AMD has been making the argument that AI can write this type of code since August of last year, presenting a research agent that fell short of human experts at the time. In September of that year, AMD CEO Lisa Su said on the Possible podcast that much of AI software is written in CUDA, which needs to be “translated to AMD,” and “a ton of that can now be done in software.”
Moving forward to July earlier this year, AMD’s senior vice president of AI, Vamsi Boppana, said its engineers were using AI models to generate GPU kernels, and Su said that when she asked whether her team was helping the Anthropic engineer working on the MI355X, she was told they didn’t need any help. Boppana said that in some cases, the resulting kernels were even “better than the most manually tuned versions.” Under its July agreement with Anthropic, AMD is also using Claude to optimize workloads for its Instinct GPUs and to speed up ROCm development.
At AMD’s Advancing AI event in July, OpenAI’s Philippe Tillet said that once OpenAI got its Helios racks, a single engineer confirmed everything worked within a few days, and that “agents are taking over our jobs, in a sense, as kernel engineers.” Before the event, AMD’s Andrew Dieckman, corporate vice president and general manager of the company’s data center GPU business, told reporters that he has had practically zero conversations with AMD’s customers about CUDA.
Until this week, though, all of this came from AMD or its stage, not from Anthropic to its own investors. Anthropic buys the chips rather than selling them, and it’s heading into its IPO next week, so its multi-chip approach — now with at least some numbers to back it up — carries more weight than a chipmaker’s claims. Its prospectus says it in writing, too, though without a number: “We can onboard new chip platforms in a fraction of the time it once took.”
The prompt processing (prefill) part of the workload is the step where a model reads and processes what it’s been given before it starts replying, and Anthropic hasn’t said whether the replying step (decode) was included as well. Anthropic’s chart, with its axis labeled “Time to onboard new silicon,” shows both lines reaching the same “~100%” mark without saying 100% of what. The company also hasn’t said how fast the model ran on AMD’s GPUs versus Nvidia’s, and that’s the comparison we want to see to show whether AMD’s chips, running AI-written code, can match Nvidia silicon.
On the other hand, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said in an April interview with Dwarkesh Patel that Nvidia already uses AI on its own kernels and knows its chips best — “nobody knows our architecture better than we do” — and can get the most out of them. Asked why labs like Anthropic use other accelerators, he called Anthropic “a unique instance, not a trend.” Nvidia and AMD, among seven companies, are buying $26.0 billion of Anthropic’s stock at the IPO price, so Nvidia is investing in the very company Huang called the exception.
In late July, SemiAnalysis published an article, “Can AMD break the CUDA Moat?,” which essentially said that software creation is not the same moat it was even a year ago. That said, the catch for AMD is that with agentic coding, “each agent requires GPUs to test their code against,” which means that the AI writing the code still needs the hardware. It has found a constant lack of GPUs inside AMD for this type of development, which has held AMD back and may be a new bottleneck.
That leaves CUDA’s moat somewhere in between. Getting a model running on a new chip, once the expensive part, appears to be getting cheaper, and better AI may wear down Nvidia’s claim that it knows its hardware best. As for Anthropic’s fungible pool, the chips in it aren’t fully interchangeable — Google’s TPUs are increasingly specialized, for example — and getting the most out of any of them still takes the hardware to test on. The moat is narrowing, but is not gone.
Anthropic’s first AMD MI450 chips, in Helios racks, are expected in the first half of 2027. That’s a new generation and an opportunity to see if the three-day bring-up can be repeated. Anthropic’s IPO comes sooner, after which it will report as a public company.
Shane Downing is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware, covering consumer storage, PC hardware, and AI.

AMD has been making the argument that AI can write this type of code since August of last year, presenting a research agent that fell short of human experts at the time. In September of that year, AMD CEO Lisa Su said on the Possible podcast that much of AI software is written in CUDA, which needs to be “translated to AMD,” and “a ton of that can now be done in software.”
Moving forward to July earlier this year, AMD’s senior vice president of AI, Vamsi Boppana, said its engineers were using AI models to generate GPU kernels, and Su said that when she asked whether her team was helping the Anthropic engineer working on the MI355X, she was told they didn’t need any help. Boppana said that in some cases, the resulting kernels were even “better than the most manually tuned versions.” Under its July agreement with Anthropic, AMD is also using Claude to optimize workloads for its Instinct GPUs and to speed up ROCm development.
At AMD’s Advancing AI event in July, OpenAI’s Philippe Tillet said that once OpenAI got its Helios racks, a single engineer confirmed everything worked within a few days, and that “agents are taking over our jobs, in a sense, as kernel engineers.” Before the event, AMD’s Andrew Dieckman, corporate vice president and general manager of the company’s data center GPU business, told reporters that he has had practically zero conversations with AMD’s customers about CUDA.
Until this week, though, all of this came from AMD or its stage, not from Anthropic to its own investors. Anthropic buys the chips rather than selling them, and it’s heading into its IPO next week, so its multi-chip approach — now with at least some numbers to back it up — carries more weight than a chipmaker’s claims. Its prospectus says it in writing, too, though without a number: “We can onboard new chip platforms in a fraction of the time it once took.”
The prompt processing (prefill) part of the workload is the step where a model reads and processes what it’s been given before it starts replying, and Anthropic hasn’t said whether the replying step (decode) was included as well. Anthropic’s chart, with its axis labeled “Time to onboard new silicon,” shows both lines reaching the same “~100%” mark without saying 100% of what. The company also hasn’t said how fast the model ran on AMD’s GPUs versus Nvidia’s, and that’s the comparison we want to see to show whether AMD’s chips, running AI-written code, can match Nvidia silicon.
On the other hand, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said in an April interview with Dwarkesh Patel that Nvidia already uses AI on its own kernels and knows its chips best — “nobody knows our architecture better than we do” — and can get the most out of them. Asked why labs like Anthropic use other accelerators, he called Anthropic “a unique instance, not a trend.” Nvidia and AMD, among seven companies, are buying $26.0 billion of Anthropic’s stock at the IPO price, so Nvidia is investing in the very company Huang called the exception.
In late July, SemiAnalysis published an article, “Can AMD break the CUDA Moat?,” which essentially said that software creation is not the same moat it was even a year ago. That said, the catch for AMD is that with agentic coding, “each agent requires GPUs to test their code against,” which means that the AI writing the code still needs the hardware. It has found a constant lack of GPUs inside AMD for this type of development, which has held AMD back and may be a new bottleneck.
That leaves CUDA’s moat somewhere in between. Getting a model running on a new chip, once the expensive part, appears to be getting cheaper, and better AI may wear down Nvidia’s claim that it knows its hardware best. As for Anthropic’s fungible pool, the chips in it aren’t fully interchangeable — Google’s TPUs are increasingly specialized, for example — and getting the most out of any of them still takes the hardware to test on. The moat is narrowing, but is not gone.
Anthropic’s first AMD MI450 chips, in Helios racks, are expected in the first half of 2027. That’s a new generation and an opportunity to see if the three-day bring-up can be repeated. Anthropic’s IPO comes sooner, after which it will report as a public company.
Shane Downing is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware, covering consumer storage, PC hardware, and AI.
