A Colony Of Cuties
Posted on November 17, 2026 Posted by Athena Scalzi 32 Comments
Okay I may have buried the lede a bit, but Big Grey is actually from a colony of cats. Down the street from me is a colony of about fifteen cats, maybe more, including three kittens and a pregnant long haired pastel calico. I didn’t know this colony existed until last week, and since then I have not stopped thinking about how I am going to save this colony. Because I will.
Big Grey was only the first of many cats I plan on rescuing, and he was first because as you have seen, he was in the worst shape I’ve ever seen a cat be in. Even though kittens would usually take priority in these kinds of situations, the kittens were extremely hard to get to (hiding under a trailer in someone’s backyard), and Grey was just so much sadder looking.
So, with Grey in recovery and safe in my basement sulking, I headed back out to catch more cats. It’s only thanks to a woman in town (who does TNR missions regularly) lending me traps and support that we managed to catch three cats yesterday. I caught the two most friendly and sociable in the whole colony, the sweetest of the sweet ones, and she caught a very skittish calico.
Later that night, she came back by herself and caught the three kittens and the pregnant cat in one go. I applaud her, honestly. I’ve never seen someone make such quick work of catching kittens. It always takes me a bit longer, anyways.
With my two catches in tow, I took them both into the urgent care vet that I took Grey to, and thankfully they are in much better shape! Though that was a low bar to clear. Fleas and ear infections, a bit of upper respiratory infection, but otherwise okay. And FeLV, FIV, and heartworm negative. They’re a little less than a year old, and very bonded.
The lady got an appointment for the pregnant one to get taken care of and fixed, and has all of them in her possession right now, thank goodness.
So, I have three, she caught two plus the three kittens, and there’s still like a dozen left.
While TNR is obviously the most important step in dealing with any rampantly breeding colony, I want to do so much more than that for them. I want them healthy, happy, and to find homes for as many as I can after getting them vetted, vaccinated, and fixed. I want to adopt out every single one I possibly can, and I honestly think it’s doable!
I also want to be transparent about this entire process and the money, time, and commitment it takes to doing it. I’m not a pro or that experienced, but how do experienced people get experienced? They try. And with y’all as my witness, I’m going to try.
Now, let’s get into the real reason you’re here, cat photos of the colony cats. This is not all of them, just a few I managed to snap pics of.
First up is the pregnant one, who I lovingly call Fluffy Sugar:

She looks like she belongs curled up in a comfy bed next to a fireplace, not wandering out of the bushes with goopy eyes in forty degree weather. I actually want her very badly. Thankfully this is the one in custody who is getting fixed up.

The Weather at Home
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
01:24:31 UTC
10:24:31 KST

The weather at home
A little closer to the outdoors.
16 November 2026 · 5 min read · 1,198 words
A Tempest weather station outside our house has reported to Home Assistant for months. Last week it got a website from WeeWX’s stock template. Every reading sat in gauges, tables, and monthly summaries, and none of it answered what we actually ask. What’s it like out there right now?
So this weekend we built a replacement and called it Field Guide. It’s only reachable from our home network or Tailscale, so I can’t link to it, but these screenshots come close.
Why bother
Forecasts describe a region. The station describes our yard, and the two don’t always agree.
The first screen gives you the temperature, a sentence about how it feels, and the day’s high and low, which is enough to decide on a coat. This morning the low was -0.6 °C at 7:50, and the dewpoint sat right on top of the temperature for most of the night. That means frost on the car, and it’s nicer to know before you go outside.
In summer, the line we’ll read most is the lightning note. East of the Cascades, a dry thunderstorm can start a wildfire, so “Quiet skies.” is the sentence we want to see.
Celsius and millimeters are the default, and Fahrenheit and inches are one tap away. The page remembers which you picked, so nobody in the house has to convert anything in their head.
The first screen
I wanted the temperature big enough to read from across the room, with something nice to look at beside it. That’s the Stuart Range on the right. Its ridgeline comes from USGS elevation data, so the shape of the mountains is real; the snow, trees, and sky are illustrated. Day and night moods switch at sunset, which the page works out from the almanac rather than a clock. There’s a button for switching by hand.
Headlines are set in Fraunces, a soft serif that suits a notebook. Body text is Manrope, and small labels and figures use IBM Plex Mono.
Under the first screen are four plates, numbered I to IV like the color plates in a field guide. Each one has a small label (“The moving air,” “Every little drop,” “Above the treetops,” and “The weight of the sky”), a headline, and one big number. More detail sits behind a disclosure, written as full sentences like “The moon is in its first quarter phase and 44% illuminated.”
Live wind
Only the wind plate moves. Every three seconds the hub reports the wind, and a live needle follows it around the compass rose. Underneath is a trace of the last ten minutes with the peak marked.
If the readings stop, the needle comes to rest and the number falls back to the five-minute average. With reduced motion turned on, nothing animates. Other headline numbers update every five minutes, and the page checks for them every minute so an open tab stays current all day.
It doesn’t make things up
Field Guide never fills a gap. Zero means a measured zero, and a missing reading shows as a dash. Chart lines stop where data is missing and start again after it. Sunrise and sunset in the sky chapter carry the label “calculated crossings, not a forecast,” and the lightning note won’t call the sky quiet unless the sensor is reporting.
Pressure needed the most care. We noticed the station’s pressure readings weren’t adding up, and we’re working through that with Tempest. On Sunday afternoon we also corrected the station’s altitude, which the software uses to turn measured pressure into a sea-level reading. That made the line on the chart jump by more than an inch of mercury in one step. Our first version called that “Rising.” Now the chart marks the jump as a calibration, pressure records start again after it, and the plate says “Record stepped: calibration, not weather.”
When we measured it on Sunday, November 15, about one in five of the station’s minute reports never reached the house. A warning about that every morning would teach us to ignore warnings, so the page stays quiet at normal levels. It speaks up after fifteen minutes without a reading, when a day’s coverage falls below 60%, or when a sensor reports a fault.
The notebook and the sky
The station notebook keeps the extremes and the date each one happened: the warmest moment, the coldest, the strongest gust, and the rain so far. Under the rain total it says, “This is the observed total, without filling gaps.”
A single line in the sky chapter draws the day from sunrise through solar noon to sunset, with golden hour shaded at each end, beside a short note about the moon.
Plain sentences
Early versions read like a status screen, full of “Disabled,” “No cloud contact yet,” and counters without context. We rewrote every reading as a sentence with the number inside it.
At the bottom, the Instrument chapter covers the station itself, in sentences like “The battery sits at 2.80 volts.” Signal strength is described in words, and reset codes become reasons, so the hub “restarted 4 days ago after losing power.”
Field Guide never talks about itself. It doesn’t mention versions, when recording began, or why a chart is short, and if something has nothing useful to say, it doesn’t appear at all. Its build history lives in the documentation.
What it’s built on
Field Guide is a skin for WeeWX, the open source weather software that receives the station’s broadcasts, keeps the archive, and draws every chart. The setup also uses:
- weatherflow-udp, the WeeWX driver that decodes the Tempest’s broadcasts
- Cheetah, the template engine WeeWX uses
- PyEphem, for the sun and the moon
- Home Assistant, which gets a copy of every reading
- Fraunces, Manrope, and IBM Plex Mono, all under the SIL Open Font License
- The USGS 3D Elevation Program, whose public-domain data gives the Stuart Range its shape
The hardware is a Tempest weather system (the station and its hub) and a UGREEN iDX6011 Pro server that runs everything. I’ve also written about how we self-host it.
What’s next
The Tempest is getting company. An Ecowitt GW3000 gateway will collect readings from a second outdoor sensor array, a second lightning detector, soil sensors, a pool thermometer, and a laser that measures snow depth.
The Tempest measures rain well, but it can’t tell you what’s piling up. This winter, the number we’ll want first thing in the morning is how much snow is on the ground, and the laser will measure it.

Running coding agents without handing them my credentials
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
01:18:56 UTC
10:18:56 KST

Running coding agents without handing them my credentials
Table of Contents
I built lend to let remote agents use tools on my laptop with my approval. Here's how it fits into my workflow.
Background #
I use agents heavily these days as part of my toolchain. I usually have a few going at once: Claude Code working in one repo, Codex reviewing a branch in another, and something long-running in the background. I often have them review each other's work too. None of them run on my laptop. They run on VMs I can throw away, and my laptop keeps the stuff I care about: my SSH keys, my 1Password vault, and the gh and cloud CLIs that are logged in as me.
I run those agents on picd, our hosting service at pico.sh. It gives me my own VM with a Docker engine and a shell over SSH. I run herdr and the agents there, and my home directory persists across restarts so my dotfiles and tools stay in place.
This works great until an agent needs something on my laptop. In this post I'll go over how I run agents in parallel with herdr and Ovis, and the tool I wrote to deal with credentials, lend.
herdr #
herdr is a terminal multiplexer built for coding agents. You get the usual workspaces, tabs, and panes, and it also knows which agent is running in each pane and what it's doing. The sidebar shows whether each agent is working, waiting on me, or done, so I can tell at a glance which one needs attention.
The CLI talks to the running session, so agents can drive each other. An agent can start Codex in another pane, give it a prompt, wait for it to finish, and read what it said:
1herdr agent start reviewer --kind codex --pane "$PANE"
2herdr agent prompt reviewer "Review the diff on am/fix-retries" --wait --until done
3herdr agent read reviewer --source recent
Remote hosts work the same way. herdr machine add agent-vm sets up herdr on the VM and adds its sessions to my local client. Those sessions keep running after I disconnect.
Ovis #
Ovis is a browser and phone client for herdr. It shows each agent's transcript as a chat, with the live terminal a tap away. When an agent asks for approval or has a question, Ovis turns it into a card I can answer and sends me a push notification. It picks up the machines saved in herdr, so my VMs show up without any extra setup. I reach it over Tailscale.
If an agent gets stuck on a question while I'm away from my desk, I answer it from my phone and the other agents keep going.
The credential problem #
Agents on a VM still need to act as me. I use them to monitor CI with gh, call APIs with tokens from 1Password, and SSH into other hosts. There are two usual ways to make that work, and I don't love either of them.
The first is copying credentials onto the VM. A token in ~/.config/gh/hosts.yml can be read by every process on that machine, including the agent and anything it runs, for as long as the token is valid.
The second is forwarding my SSH agent with ssh -A. The private keys stay on my laptop, but the VM can use every key in the agent without asking and without any record of what it signed. It also only covers SSH keys.
What I wanted was for the credentials to stay on the laptop and for the commands that need them to run there too. The agent asks to run something, I approve it with Touch ID, and the agent gets the output back.
lend #
That's where lend comes in. lend is a proxy that sits in front of your existing SSH agent, and you forward its socket in place of the real one. SSH and Git keep working like normal, and lend adds a way to run commands through an SSH agent extension.
When an agent on the VM asks to run gh, the request goes over the socket and gh runs on my laptop with my login. The token never leaves the laptop, so the agent never has it. Every request, whether it's a signature or a command, is checked against a policy on the laptop and needs my approval before anything happens.
1Remote: lend exec -- command args
2                  |
3          forwarded SSH agent socket
4                  |
5Laptop: lend agent -> policy + local approval -> local command
6                                           -> original SSH agent (signing)
The policy lives on the laptop and lists what the VM is allowed to ask for. Everything else is denied. Mine is pretty short:
 1{
 2  "default": "deny",
 3  "commands": [
 4    {"executable": "/opt/homebrew/bin/gh", "approval": "biometric:inline"}
 5  ],
 6  "ssh": {
 7    "default": "deny",
 8    "keys": [
 9      {"fingerprint": "SHA256:...", "approval": "biometric:inline"}
10    ]
11  }
12}
A rule can also take an args_prefix like ["pr", "view"] if you only want to allow some subcommands. biometric:inline puts Touch ID right in the request dialog, so I read the request and touch once to approve it. Plain biometric asks for Touch ID in a second prompt after I click Allow.
To connect, I run lend ssh from the laptop. It starts a proxy for that one connection and stops it when SSH exits:
1lend ssh --config ./policy.json -- agent-vm
I run herdr on the VM. Claude Code talks to lend through lend mcp, an MCP server with a single tool called run_local_command. The tool takes the command to run and a reason, and the reason is required:
1{
2  "mcpServers": {
3    "laptop": {"command": "lend", "args": ["mcp"]}
4  }
5}
Here I asked Claude Code on the VM for the lend repo's description. It called the tool with gh repo view, my Mac showed the request, and one touch approved it:
The dialog shows the full path to the executable, every argument, the directory it'll run in on the laptop, the reason, and what the remote process said about itself. Once I approve, gh runs on the laptop and stdout, stderr, and the exit code stream back to the agent. The reason and caller details come from the VM, so they're marked as unverified. They're there to help me decide and can't change what the policy allows.
If you'd rather skip MCP, the same request works as a plain CLI call on the VM:
1lend exec --reason "Read the repo description" -- gh repo view antoniomika/lend
Keeping the socket stable across reconnects #
herdr panes survive an SSH disconnect, but the forwarded socket belongs to that connection. When I reconnect, SSH gives me a new socket path. Agents still running in the panes keep the old SSH_AUTH_SOCK.
I use a symlink at a fixed path and update it from ~/.ssh/rc on the VM. SSH runs this before starting the shell, so it sees the socket forwarded by the new connection:
 1#!/bin/sh
 2
 3if [ -n "${SSH_AUTH_SOCK:-}" ] &&
 4    [ "$SSH_AUTH_SOCK" != "$HOME/.ssh/lend/agent.sock" ] &&
 5    "$HOME/.local/bin/lend" doctor --remote --socket "$SSH_AUTH_SOCK" --timeout 1s >/dev/null 2>&1; then
 6  install -d -m 700 "$HOME/.ssh/lend" &&
 7    ln -sfn -- "$SSH_AUTH_SOCK" "$HOME/.ssh/lend/agent.sock"
 8fi
 9
10if [ -n "${DISPLAY:-}" ] && read -r _ssh_proto _ssh_cookie; then
11  case $DISPLAY in
12    localhost:*) _ssh_display="unix:${DISPLAY#localhost:}" ;;
13    *) _ssh_display=$DISPLAY ;;
14  esac
15  printf 'add %s %s %s\n' "$_ssh_display" "$_ssh_proto" "$_ssh_cookie" | /usr/bin/xauth -q -
16fi
The check confirms the forwarded socket speaks lend before updating the link. The directory is private to my user and keeps this link separate from other agent sockets. I use lend's absolute path because this runs before my shell adds ~/.local/bin to PATH. The X11 block handles cookie setup, which SSH delegates to this file when it exists.
My ~/.bashrc checks the stable path before exporting it:
1if [ -S "$HOME/.ssh/lend/agent.sock" ] &&
2    "$HOME/.local/bin/lend" doctor --remote --socket "$HOME/.ssh/lend/agent.sock" --timeout 1s >/dev/null 2>&1; then
3  export SSH_AUTH_SOCK="$HOME/.ssh/lend/agent.sock"
4fi
New Bash shells in herdr panes pick up the stable path when it works. If the check fails, Bash leaves SSH_AUTH_SOCK alone. MCP, approval hooks, and Git inherit that path when I start them. After reconnecting, the SSH hook updates the symlink and those processes use the new socket for subsequent requests. Agents already running with an old socket path need to be restarted once with the stable path in their environment.
The SSH connection from the laptop still has to stay open. Requests fail while it's disconnected, and reconnecting starts a new lend session with fresh approvals and leases. The link points at one connection at a time, so the last update wins.
Secrets the agent never sees #
gh works because it's already logged in on my laptop. Some tools need a token passed in. For those, a command rule can read secrets with something like op read and hand them to the approved command as environment variables:
 1{
 2  "secret_reader": "/opt/homebrew/bin/op",
 3  "secret_reader_args": ["read", "--no-newline"],
 4  "commands": [
 5    {
 6      "executable": "/opt/homebrew/bin/gh",
 7      "args_prefix": ["pr", "view"],
 8      "approval": "biometric",
 9      "secrets": {"GH_TOKEN": "op://Development/github/token"}
10    }
11  ]
12}
When the agent asks to run gh pr view and I approve it, lend reads the token from 1Password, runs gh with GH_TOKEN set, and sends back the output. The token stays on the laptop. The VM can't pick which variables get set or which references get read. The prompt shows the variable names and references, and the values never show up in prompts or history.
Here's the VM running gh api user with a rule that injects GH_TOKEN. After I approve in lend, 1Password asks for its own Touch ID before op read returns the token. The VM gets my login back, and its own environment has no GH_TOKEN:
There's one catch: this only works if the approved command doesn't print its environment. If you approve env or sh, you've handed the values right back. The same goes for allowing op itself as a command, since op read prints the secret and that output goes straight to the agent.
SSH keys #
Keys get their own policy too. Keys that aren't listed are hidden from the VM, and listed keys can be limited by what they're allowed to sign. max_signatures caps how many signatures a key can make in a session.
Leases #
A rule can offer a lease when I approve, like five minutes or three uses. Leases only exist inside a lend ssh session and only match the exact command and arguments I approved. They go away when the session ends or my screen locks.
Seeing what happened #
lend status --all --watch shows what's connected, what's waiting, what's running, and any active leases. lend pause --all cancels everything and refuses new requests until I resume. lend history keeps a record of what ran and how it was approved. It leaves out arguments, output, and secrets, and you can opt into logging arguments.
Answering agent prompts from the laptop #
Agents also ask permission for things that have nothing to do with my credentials, like running a shell command on the VM, editing a file, or fetching a URL. lend approve sends those questions to the same dialog on my Mac. It doesn't run anything on the laptop. It asks me, and the agent decides what to do with the answer.
For Claude Code, it's a PermissionRequest hook on the VM:
1{
2  "hooks": {
3    "PermissionRequest": [
4      {"matcher": "*", "hooks": [{"type": "command", "command": "lend approve --hook claude-code"}]}
5    ]
6  }
7}
Now when Claude Code would normally stop and ask in the terminal, my Mac shows the command or tool input and I answer with Touch ID. The laptop policy lists which topics are allowed to ask, each with its own approval:
1"approvals": {
2  "topics": [{"name": "claude-code", "approval": "biometric:inline"}]
3}
There are adapters for Codex, Antigravity, and pi, plus a plain JSON format for anything else. You can use it in scripts too: lend approve --topic deploy --title "terraform apply" && terraform apply only applies after I approve on the laptop. If the laptop can't be reached, the hook stays out of the way and the agent asks in its own UI like it normally would.
What lend doesn't protect against #
An approved command runs as my user and can touch my files. Approval decides what runs, and lend doesn't sandbox it. If I approve a shell, an interpreter, or anything that reads instructions from stdin, I've basically approved running anything on my laptop, since stdin streams after approval and isn't shown in the prompt.
The caller details in the prompt come from the VM and can be faked. Anyone who can reach the forwarded socket on the VM, root included, can make requests while the session is open. The policy still limits what they can ask for, and every request still needs my approval.
lend is still early and runs on macOS and Linux. It doesn't allocate a PTY, so interactive programs won't work through it. On Linux, approval is a Zenity dialog or a terminal prompt, and biometric approval is macOS only.
Prior art #
ssh-rev-exec sends local execution requests through an SSH agent proxy, and op-forward forwards 1Password CLI commands to a machine with desktop authentication. lend is a separate implementation that uses the same agent extension trick and adds per-request policy, local approval, and key filtering.
Trying it #
Install lend on both your laptop and the remote host:
1brew install antoniomika/tap/lend
2# or
3curl -fsSL https://github.com/antoniomika/lend/releases/latest/download/install.sh | sh
Run lend doctor on the laptop to check your policy, executables, upstream agent, and approval setup. lend doctor --remote on the VM checks that the forwarded socket is actually lend. The getting started guide walks through a first session, and the docs cover policies, approval, and MCP. If you give it a try, I'd love to hear how it goes on GitHub.

Running coding agents without handing them my credentials
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
01:18:56 UTC
10:18:56 KST

Running coding agents without handing them my credentials
Table of Contents
I built lend to let remote agents use tools on my laptop with my approval. Here's how it fits into my workflow.
Background #
I use agents heavily these days as part of my toolchain. I usually have a few going at once: Claude Code working in one repo, Codex reviewing a branch in another, and something long-running in the background. I often have them review each other's work too. None of them run on my laptop. They run on VMs I can throw away, and my laptop keeps the stuff I care about: my SSH keys, my 1Password vault, and the gh and cloud CLIs that are logged in as me.
I run those agents on picd, our hosting service at pico.sh. It gives me my own VM with a Docker engine and a shell over SSH. I run herdr and the agents there, and my home directory persists across restarts so my dotfiles and tools stay in place.
This works great until an agent needs something on my laptop. In this post I'll go over how I run agents in parallel with herdr and Ovis, and the tool I wrote to deal with credentials, lend.
herdr #
herdr is a terminal multiplexer built for coding agents. You get the usual workspaces, tabs, and panes, and it also knows which agent is running in each pane and what it's doing. The sidebar shows whether each agent is working, waiting on me, or done, so I can tell at a glance which one needs attention.
The CLI talks to the running session, so agents can drive each other. An agent can start Codex in another pane, give it a prompt, wait for it to finish, and read what it said:
1herdr agent start reviewer --kind codex --pane "$PANE"
2herdr agent prompt reviewer "Review the diff on am/fix-retries" --wait --until done
3herdr agent read reviewer --source recent
Remote hosts work the same way. herdr machine add agent-vm sets up herdr on the VM and adds its sessions to my local client. Those sessions keep running after I disconnect.
Ovis #
Ovis is a browser and phone client for herdr. It shows each agent's transcript as a chat, with the live terminal a tap away. When an agent asks for approval or has a question, Ovis turns it into a card I can answer and sends me a push notification. It picks up the machines saved in herdr, so my VMs show up without any extra setup. I reach it over Tailscale.
If an agent gets stuck on a question while I'm away from my desk, I answer it from my phone and the other agents keep going.
The credential problem #
Agents on a VM still need to act as me. I use them to monitor CI with gh, call APIs with tokens from 1Password, and SSH into other hosts. There are two usual ways to make that work, and I don't love either of them.
The first is copying credentials onto the VM. A token in ~/.config/gh/hosts.yml can be read by every process on that machine, including the agent and anything it runs, for as long as the token is valid.
The second is forwarding my SSH agent with ssh -A. The private keys stay on my laptop, but the VM can use every key in the agent without asking and without any record of what it signed. It also only covers SSH keys.
What I wanted was for the credentials to stay on the laptop and for the commands that need them to run there too. The agent asks to run something, I approve it with Touch ID, and the agent gets the output back.
lend #
That's where lend comes in. lend is a proxy that sits in front of your existing SSH agent, and you forward its socket in place of the real one. SSH and Git keep working like normal, and lend adds a way to run commands through an SSH agent extension.
When an agent on the VM asks to run gh, the request goes over the socket and gh runs on my laptop with my login. The token never leaves the laptop, so the agent never has it. Every request, whether it's a signature or a command, is checked against a policy on the laptop and needs my approval before anything happens.
1Remote: lend exec -- command args
2                  |
3          forwarded SSH agent socket
4                  |
5Laptop: lend agent -> policy + local approval -> local command
6                                           -> original SSH agent (signing)
The policy lives on the laptop and lists what the VM is allowed to ask for. Everything else is denied. Mine is pretty short:
 1{
 2  "default": "deny",
 3  "commands": [
 4    {"executable": "/opt/homebrew/bin/gh", "approval": "biometric:inline"}
 5  ],
 6  "ssh": {
 7    "default": "deny",
 8    "keys": [
 9      {"fingerprint": "SHA256:...", "approval": "biometric:inline"}
10    ]
11  }
12}
A rule can also take an args_prefix like ["pr", "view"] if you only want to allow some subcommands. biometric:inline puts Touch ID right in the request dialog, so I read the request and touch once to approve it. Plain biometric asks for Touch ID in a second prompt after I click Allow.
To connect, I run lend ssh from the laptop. It starts a proxy for that one connection and stops it when SSH exits:
1lend ssh --config ./policy.json -- agent-vm
I run herdr on the VM. Claude Code talks to lend through lend mcp, an MCP server with a single tool called run_local_command. The tool takes the command to run and a reason, and the reason is required:
1{
2  "mcpServers": {
3    "laptop": {"command": "lend", "args": ["mcp"]}
4  }
5}
Here I asked Claude Code on the VM for the lend repo's description. It called the tool with gh repo view, my Mac showed the request, and one touch approved it:
The dialog shows the full path to the executable, every argument, the directory it'll run in on the laptop, the reason, and what the remote process said about itself. Once I approve, gh runs on the laptop and stdout, stderr, and the exit code stream back to the agent. The reason and caller details come from the VM, so they're marked as unverified. They're there to help me decide and can't change what the policy allows.
If you'd rather skip MCP, the same request works as a plain CLI call on the VM:
1lend exec --reason "Read the repo description" -- gh repo view antoniomika/lend
Keeping the socket stable across reconnects #
herdr panes survive an SSH disconnect, but the forwarded socket belongs to that connection. When I reconnect, SSH gives me a new socket path. Agents still running in the panes keep the old SSH_AUTH_SOCK.
I use a symlink at a fixed path and update it from ~/.ssh/rc on the VM. SSH runs this before starting the shell, so it sees the socket forwarded by the new connection:
 1#!/bin/sh
 2
 3if [ -n "${SSH_AUTH_SOCK:-}" ] &&
 4    [ "$SSH_AUTH_SOCK" != "$HOME/.ssh/lend/agent.sock" ] &&
 5    "$HOME/.local/bin/lend" doctor --remote --socket "$SSH_AUTH_SOCK" --timeout 1s >/dev/null 2>&1; then
 6  install -d -m 700 "$HOME/.ssh/lend" &&
 7    ln -sfn -- "$SSH_AUTH_SOCK" "$HOME/.ssh/lend/agent.sock"
 8fi
 9
10if [ -n "${DISPLAY:-}" ] && read -r _ssh_proto _ssh_cookie; then
11  case $DISPLAY in
12    localhost:*) _ssh_display="unix:${DISPLAY#localhost:}" ;;
13    *) _ssh_display=$DISPLAY ;;
14  esac
15  printf 'add %s %s %s\n' "$_ssh_display" "$_ssh_proto" "$_ssh_cookie" | /usr/bin/xauth -q -
16fi
The check confirms the forwarded socket speaks lend before updating the link. The directory is private to my user and keeps this link separate from other agent sockets. I use lend's absolute path because this runs before my shell adds ~/.local/bin to PATH. The X11 block handles cookie setup, which SSH delegates to this file when it exists.
My ~/.bashrc checks the stable path before exporting it:
1if [ -S "$HOME/.ssh/lend/agent.sock" ] &&
2    "$HOME/.local/bin/lend" doctor --remote --socket "$HOME/.ssh/lend/agent.sock" --timeout 1s >/dev/null 2>&1; then
3  export SSH_AUTH_SOCK="$HOME/.ssh/lend/agent.sock"
4fi
New Bash shells in herdr panes pick up the stable path when it works. If the check fails, Bash leaves SSH_AUTH_SOCK alone. MCP, approval hooks, and Git inherit that path when I start them. After reconnecting, the SSH hook updates the symlink and those processes use the new socket for subsequent requests. Agents already running with an old socket path need to be restarted once with the stable path in their environment.
The SSH connection from the laptop still has to stay open. Requests fail while it's disconnected, and reconnecting starts a new lend session with fresh approvals and leases. The link points at one connection at a time, so the last update wins.
Secrets the agent never sees #
gh works because it's already logged in on my laptop. Some tools need a token passed in. For those, a command rule can read secrets with something like op read and hand them to the approved command as environment variables:
 1{
 2  "secret_reader": "/opt/homebrew/bin/op",
 3  "secret_reader_args": ["read", "--no-newline"],
 4  "commands": [
 5    {
 6      "executable": "/opt/homebrew/bin/gh",
 7      "args_prefix": ["pr", "view"],
 8      "approval": "biometric",
 9      "secrets": {"GH_TOKEN": "op://Development/github/token"}
10    }
11  ]
12}
When the agent asks to run gh pr view and I approve it, lend reads the token from 1Password, runs gh with GH_TOKEN set, and sends back the output. The token stays on the laptop. The VM can't pick which variables get set or which references get read. The prompt shows the variable names and references, and the values never show up in prompts or history.
Here's the VM running gh api user with a rule that injects GH_TOKEN. After I approve in lend, 1Password asks for its own Touch ID before op read returns the token. The VM gets my login back, and its own environment has no GH_TOKEN:
There's one catch: this only works if the approved command doesn't print its environment. If you approve env or sh, you've handed the values right back. The same goes for allowing op itself as a command, since op read prints the secret and that output goes straight to the agent.
SSH keys #
Keys get their own policy too. Keys that aren't listed are hidden from the VM, and listed keys can be limited by what they're allowed to sign. max_signatures caps how many signatures a key can make in a session.
Leases #
A rule can offer a lease when I approve, like five minutes or three uses. Leases only exist inside a lend ssh session and only match the exact command and arguments I approved. They go away when the session ends or my screen locks.
Seeing what happened #
lend status --all --watch shows what's connected, what's waiting, what's running, and any active leases. lend pause --all cancels everything and refuses new requests until I resume. lend history keeps a record of what ran and how it was approved. It leaves out arguments, output, and secrets, and you can opt into logging arguments.
Answering agent prompts from the laptop #
Agents also ask permission for things that have nothing to do with my credentials, like running a shell command on the VM, editing a file, or fetching a URL. lend approve sends those questions to the same dialog on my Mac. It doesn't run anything on the laptop. It asks me, and the agent decides what to do with the answer.
For Claude Code, it's a PermissionRequest hook on the VM:
1{
2  "hooks": {
3    "PermissionRequest": [
4      {"matcher": "*", "hooks": [{"type": "command", "command": "lend approve --hook claude-code"}]}
5    ]
6  }
7}
Now when Claude Code would normally stop and ask in the terminal, my Mac shows the command or tool input and I answer with Touch ID. The laptop policy lists which topics are allowed to ask, each with its own approval:
1"approvals": {
2  "topics": [{"name": "claude-code", "approval": "biometric:inline"}]
3}
There are adapters for Codex, Antigravity, and pi, plus a plain JSON format for anything else. You can use it in scripts too: lend approve --topic deploy --title "terraform apply" && terraform apply only applies after I approve on the laptop. If the laptop can't be reached, the hook stays out of the way and the agent asks in its own UI like it normally would.
What lend doesn't protect against #
An approved command runs as my user and can touch my files. Approval decides what runs, and lend doesn't sandbox it. If I approve a shell, an interpreter, or anything that reads instructions from stdin, I've basically approved running anything on my laptop, since stdin streams after approval and isn't shown in the prompt.
The caller details in the prompt come from the VM and can be faked. Anyone who can reach the forwarded socket on the VM, root included, can make requests while the session is open. The policy still limits what they can ask for, and every request still needs my approval.
lend is still early and runs on macOS and Linux. It doesn't allocate a PTY, so interactive programs won't work through it. On Linux, approval is a Zenity dialog or a terminal prompt, and biometric approval is macOS only.
Prior art #
ssh-rev-exec sends local execution requests through an SSH agent proxy, and op-forward forwards 1Password CLI commands to a machine with desktop authentication. lend is a separate implementation that uses the same agent extension trick and adds per-request policy, local approval, and key filtering.
Trying it #
Install lend on both your laptop and the remote host:
1brew install antoniomika/tap/lend
2# or
3curl -fsSL https://github.com/antoniomika/lend/releases/latest/download/install.sh | sh
Run lend doctor on the laptop to check your policy, executables, upstream agent, and approval setup. lend doctor --remote on the VM checks that the forwarded socket is actually lend. The Big Idea: Will Ludwigsen
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
01:16:22 UTC
10:16:22 KST

The Big Idea: Will Ludwigsen
Posted on November 16, 2026 Posted by John Scalzi 2 Comments
Elder gods and geopolitical quagmires: Are these two great tastes that go great together? Will Ludwigsen mashes up both in A Scout is Loyal — and as this Big Idea shows, the result is intriguing.
WILL LUDWIGSEN:
If you’re wondering (as we all do) how the Vietnam War could possibly have been worse with H.P. Lovecraft’s Deep Ones lurking in the rivers and sea, I’ve written just the book for you.
A Scout is Loyal takes place five years after the events of A Scout is Brave in which Bud Castillo and his Deep One friend Aubrey Marsh thwart a conspiracy to drill into Devil’s Reef in 1963. I wrapped up that Lovecraftian Catcher in the Rye with Bud wistfully gazing out to sea for his lost friend, both of us believing the story was over.
But people had questions, chief among them how discovering a colony of monstrous beings interbreeding with humanity would influence the life of pleasant conformity we’re all expected to embrace. How do you go off to college or get a job without squinting at every bug-eyed pedestrian you pass? Does knowing about a vast cosmic conspiracy make you a Nixon voter or a Humphrey man? Why save for retirement if trans-dimensional beings beyond time can put your brain in a jar?
Lovecraft’s protagonists rarely contend with those long-term implications. They tend to simply faint or gibber when they discover the horrible truth of existence, which appears to be—checking my notes—that humanity’s struggles make no difference to the monstrous beings who truly command the universe.
Which hardly seems a revelation, much less worth going insane over.
At the end of A Scout is Brave, Bud is thirteen years old and now fully aware that the Scout Handbook contains no practical advice for a struggle against overwhelming evil. The best he figures is that we don’t win a single dramatic battle but tiny skirmishes every day, opening literal and figurative doors for others and picking up after ourselves and nudging circumstances toward the good.
My question for the sequel became, “What the hell does a Boy Scout like that do in Vietnam?”
A lunatic—say, me—could make a case that the Vietnam War may well have been our most Boy Scout-y conflict, driven by total arrogant confidence that we were rescuing “helpless” people to impose our values on them. We rationalized our baser motives with the spangled bunting of altruism because we were the Good Guys walking little old Vietnamese ladies across the street to freedom.
It also had lots of hiking and camping.
What Bud discovers when he gets to Vietnam in A Scout is Loyal isn’t a lack of idealism but a glut of it. He’s surrounded by people who are proud of the extremes they’ll take to prove their vision for reality is the right one. How can you talk someone off a ledge when they think they’re virtuous for being there?
That’s where Lovecraft, scouting, and Vietnam converge: the myopia of utter conviction. Lovecraft’s (human) bad guys were true believers with the advantage of certainty, and his good guys were skeptics with the “disadvantage” of an open mind. Those strike me as the real-world bad guys and good guys in Vietnam, too, confident zealots versus the few still asking questions of themselves.
In A Scout is Loyal, Bud’s worst errors of perception and action occur when he feels the most certain, the most electrified with righteous purpose. His struggle is to let new information change his mind, and in that way, he has a lot in common with Nixon and McNamara and Kissinger, and honestly the rest of us.
Sometimes the smoothest road to being wrong is being sure.
I didn’t write A Scout is Loyal as a “message” book—every great error of my life came from thinking I knew an absolute truth—but as an experiential one. I wondered how someone like Bud (or like me) would react to that impossible situation where being right didn’t seem to matter as much as believing you were. I ran the scenario and reported what happened.
The closest my book gets to a “Big Idea” is that Big Ideas are liars. They’re the smoothed edges and blunted corners of reality, the models and rules of thumb that make us miss the details that hurt other people.
Lovecraft’s big idea was that we don’t matter in a terrifying cosmos. Mine, if these books have one, is that we only have to matter to each other.
A Scout is Loyal: Amazon|Barnes & Noble|Bookshop|Lethe Press
Author socials: Website|Bluesky
Read an excerpt.

Why 3dfx 1 cards had such a washed out color output?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-17
00:16:24 UTC
09:16:24 KST

Both NeXT and 3DFX tried to “do the right thing” with color spaces, and it wound up generally making them look worse than competing options. I didn’t really understand it properly until years later.
It is natural to think that a pixel with a value of 1.0 is emitting twice the photons as a pixel with a value of 0.5, so you can do the obvious linear math to calculate partial pixel coverage, blending a foreground over a background, or interpolating between colors in a texture.
If displays are “linear”, that is correct. NeXT and 3DFX were much closer to linear than other contemporary displays. NeXT had crossover from the deep graphics understanding at Pixar, and 3DFX had the SGI heritage. They did this intentionally.
If you have 10+ bits per channel like the professional rendering systems, linear displays are The Right Thing.
Unfortunately, NeXTStation Colors were 4 bits per channel and 3DFX was 5 or 6 bits per channel, and there… it was not the right thing.
Human eyes aren’t linear intensity sensors. We mostly register relative changes in intensity, so we can tell the difference between 0.01 and 0.02 as a huge doubling, while 0.99 to 1.00 is almost imperceptible.
This distribution of intensities is often referred to as the “gamma” of the display system, but the actual curves like sRGB are a bit more complicated.
If you only have a small number of discrete pixel values, you definitely don’t want to spread them evenly from 0 to 1! If you do, you will have good precision in the brights, but very stark “intensity bands” in the dark colors.
If everything was authored specifically for 3DFX, you would still have the fundamental problem with the dark bands, but more accurate blending and filtering. The problem was that artists made all of their textures in paint programs on conventional non-linear displays, so when they were drawn by 3DFX cards, they got the characteristic “washed out color” look.
You can mathematically correct textures for differences in display gamma, but I didn’t really understand the issue back in the glQuake days, and we were so enthralled by the absolute performance of 3DFX that the color fidelity didn’t get as much attention as it should have.
Even today, tons of games don’t properly handle gamma across their textures and render targets. High resolutions and 24 bit color masks most of it, but you still see it in some slight shimmerings in high contrast textures and edges.
Does anybody know why 3DfX 1 cards had such a washed out color output? In my research, circa '97 I found many people complaining about the colors. Is it gamma? 16-bit color? What can it be?

JPMorgan Chase Glitch Disrupts Online Banking Across Major US Banks 40
			
		 	
				A software bug at JPMorgan Chase has disrupted online banking services Monday, triggering transaction delays for thousands of customers at Chase and other major U.S. banks. "JPMorgan Chase has experienced a temporary issue related to a software bug in an on-premise hardware system -- it is not cyber-related," said Joe Evangelisti, spokesman for the bank. "This is not a systemic issue -- other banks may have been impacted by slowness in transactions only to the extent that they were a party to a transaction with us," he added. The Associated Press reports:  Monday's technical issues seem to be centered at Chase customers and customers at other banks trying to transact with Chase. Those banks, including Bank of America and Citigroup, were not having issues with their banking services. A spokesman for U.S. Bank said its systems are not directly impacted but that "clients attempting to send or receive Zelle payments with another financial institution may experience transaction delays." Hacker behind 2022 GTA VI leaks pleads guilty
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-16
23:35:58 UTC
08:35:58 KST

Arion Kurtaj, a member of the Lapsus$ cybercrime group who leaked 90 videos of Grand Theft Auto VI in 2022 and hacked other big companies, pleaded guilty on Monday, according to the UK’s Crown Prosecution Service (CPS).
Hacker behind 2022 GTA VI leaks pleads guilty
Arion Kurtaj leaked 90 videos of GTA VI years before its release.
Kurtaj, who is now 21, hacked BT/EE sometime between August and November 2021, and starting in early November of that year, the company “noted a marked increase in unauthorised SIM swaps of their customers mobile phones.” He was charged for those offenses and moved to a hotel for his “own protection” in September 2022, but “breached his bail conditions to carry out further hacks on major companies using a mobile phone, the hotel TV’s Wi-Fi and a firestick,” including hacks on Rockstar Games, Uber, and Revolut, the CPS says.
He was sentenced to life in a secure hospital in December 2023 because of his autism, but in January, Kurtaj was deemed fit for trial and moved to prison, the BBC reports.
Kurtaj is set to be sentenced on November 23rd. GTA VI will be released on November 19th.

Cybersecurity AI startup Corma reaches $1B valuation in about 3 months
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-16
23:32:32 UTC
08:32:32 KST

Three months after introducing its product, AI cybersecurity startup Corma has raised a $125 million Series A round at a $1 billion valuation led by NEA, the company said. Corma offers AI models and agents trained for cybersecurity defense. It has now raised a total of $185 million from backers, including Sequoia Capital, Khosla Ventures, and Coatue.
The startup came out of stealth in August. Interest in its product skyrocketed after a series of security incidents sent chief information security officers scrambling on October 21 when a critical flaw was found in PHP, a popular web programming language. Another flaw was found in Node.js, the open source JavaScript runtime environment. Popular open source tool LiteLLM, a proxy for routing requests to multiple LLM providers, also disclosed a security incident. Other incidents were disclosed that day, too. The day showcased the kind of onslaught on security that AI has enabled.
Corma isn’t the only company using AI to play defense. Other examples include Kevin Mandia’s new startup Armadin, as well as Anthropic, OpenAI, Microsoft, and other established players, and too many other startups to count. Still, it’s a young market and a necessary one. Corma says it stands out from the crowd by being a frontier AI research lab focused on protection.

====================================================================================================

Show HN: Sutra-OS – A self-hosted ontology platform
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-16
23:25:41 UTC
08:25:41 KST

Scarce Units, Typed, Related, Actionable.
  An open-source, self-hosted ontology platform.
Your data becomes one model you own: typed, linked, changed only through
  rules the server enforces, and every number traces back to its source.
Quick start · How it works · Proposals · Python SDK · Contributing
Beta. Only small parts are still alpha: the model language and APIs may still change between versions, and parts of the user interface and docs are unfinished.
Maintainers wanted. Sutra-OS is a one-person project so far. See Contributing.
Sutra-OS began in late 2025 as a thought experiment: a free version of the system described in Ontology-Oriented Software Development, whose creators I deeply admire.
Much talent today goes into software that sells attention, while the software everything else runs on is mostly free: by a rough estimate, 70–90% of US economic output depends on free software at some critical point. That openness is not guaranteed. In November 2026, Nvidia's board parted with the founder who had its AI models released openly. Sutra-OS adds to the commons under the AGPL, so that it stays readable by anyone.
The past ten years have shown what it costs when nobody can see what depends on what. In 2017, NotPetya spread through a single Ukrainian accounting program and stopped Maersk's ports and Merck's factories. In 2023, a corrupted file in one FAA system halted every domestic departure in the US. In 2024, one faulty CrowdStrike update crashed millions of Windows machines, and airlines, hospitals and banks stood still. In November 2026, a single vendor took Outlook down for a day and some cities went back to fax. That same week, data flooded the FAA's telecommunications network, aging routers buckled, and the US saw only the third nationwide ground stop in its history. No one, it seems, had those routers on the map of what keeps planes in the air, so no one could ask beforehand what would stop if they failed.
That question is what Sutra-OS is built for. Every system, device, dataset and decision becomes an object with links to what it feeds and what feeds it, and every number can be traced back to its source. Whoever holds such a model can ask, before the outage, what stops when one piece fails, and get an answer, at every scale from a single person to a government (hopefully never a world government). Many small ontologies, each held by those it describes, keep power spread out; one model of everything would not.
Scarce Units: energy, water, land, housing and time will shape the coming decades. Whoever counts what is scarce decides how it is shared, and today that bookkeeping sits with a few platforms; for most people, the only trace their own data leaves in their life is the tendinitis from the hundredth weary click on "Accept all" in a cookie banner. Sutra-OS keeps the bookkeeping with those it concerns, under rules everyone can read: households, farms, workshops, clubs, newsrooms and public administrations get their records back, and their wrist with them. That is what a democracy needs from its software: a council that can trace the number behind a decision to its source, a change that stays a proposal until someone answers for it, and a public administration accountable to its voters rather than to a vendor under another country's law.
Almost everyone. Whoever you are, someone else keeps a better-connected record of your affairs than you do. Sutra is for anyone who wants their own.
Requirements: Linux x86_64, JDK 21, the Clojure CLI, Node.js, Python 3, git.
git clone <repository-url> sutra && cd sutra
(cd clients/frontend && npm install)
bin/sutra-solo
This starts an embedded PostgreSQL and the server on 127.0.0.1 and prints the
address. Data lives in $SUTRA_HOME (default ~/.local/share/sutra). The admin
password is in zugang.edn ("access") there. Run the bin/sutra bind … line it
prints once.
Three ideas carry the whole platform: object types, link types and
action types. The examples below use the farm model in
resources/presets.
You start by saying what exists: a lot of hay, from one field and one cut; a sale, to one buyer. Each is a typed thing with a kind and a key.
:objects
{:lot
 {:name "Hay lot" :plural "Hay lots" :symbol "🌾" :title :name
  :primary-key :name :match-keys [:name]
  :attributes
  [[:name    :string  {:name "Lot"}]                    ; "North Meadow, 1st cut 2026"
   [:baled   :integer {:name "Bales made" :constraints {:min 0}}]
   [:sold    :integer {:derived {:expression [:connected-sum :sales :bales]}}]
   [:in-barn :integer
    {:name "In the barn" :visibility :prominent
     :derived {:language :clojure
               :expression [:- [:get :baled] [:get :sold]]
               :inputs [:baled :sold]}}]]}
 :sale
 {:name "Sale" :symbol "🤝" :primary-key :sale-nr :match-keys [:sale-nr]
  :attributes
  [[:sale-nr        :string]
   [:lot            :string]
   [:buyer          :string]
   [:bales          :integer {:constraints {:min 1}}]
   [:price-per-bale :decimal]
   [:sold-on        :date]
   [:sold-by        :string]]}}
Nobody types or counts :in-barn: it is the bales made minus every bale sold,
computed by the server. :bales is an integer, so "about forty" or -3 is
refused before anything is written.
An object type is a typed table with a name, a face in the UI and a class in the generated SDK. Properties are typed, constrained and can be derived, so a value nobody typed in still shows up like any other column.
A sale on its own is just a number. A sale linked to the lot it came out of is a fact everyone can see.
:links
[[:sale-lot :sale :lot :many-to-one
  {:fk :lot :forward-api-name :lot :reverse-api-name :sales}]]
The sale names its lot and the link follows it: from the lot you see its
sales, from a sale its lot, and :connected-sum above sums across that link.
One line, and the barn knows what is in it.
Links are first-class: named in both directions, navigable in the UI, traversable
in the SDK (lot.sales), and usable in derived values, filters and object
sets.
In most systems anybody may change anything, and nobody can say who did. Here there is no "update row". There are only actions: named decisions with typed inputs and rules the server enforces. Selling hay is one of them, and it has a condition.
:sell-hay
{:on     :lot
 :params {:lot            {:type :lot :required true}
          :sale-nr        {:type :string :required true}
          :buyer          {:type :string :required true}
          :bales          {:type :integer :required true :constraints {:min 1}}
          :price-per-bale {:type :decimal}
          :sold-on        {:type :date :prefill :now}
          :sold-by        {:type :string :exposure :hidden :prefill :current-user}}
 :submission-criteria
 [{:message "Not that many bales in the barn."
   :test    {:want [:get :bales] :have [:get :lot :in-barn]}
   :pred    [:<= :want :have]}]
 :rules  [{:op :create :object-type :sale
           :values {:sale-nr [:get :sale-nr] :lot [:get :lot :name]
                    :buyer [:get :buyer] :bales [:get :bales]
                    :price-per-bale [:get :price-per-bale]
                    :sold-on [:get :sold-on] :sold-by [:get :sold-by]}}]
 :edits  [{:object-type :sale}]}
Ask to sell more bales than are in the barn and the server refuses and says why, with the same sentence for the phone, the CLI, the Python script and the AI agent. A refused action writes nothing. Every sale records who made it, and nobody can put someone else's name there.
Actions are the only write path: typed parameters, validation, submission criteria and state transitions, defined once and enforced everywhere.
Every rule you would otherwise keep in your head has this shape: a fact the server computes, and an action that checks it. Declare it once, and stop remembering it.
The same three ideas run the arable side of the farm. A field's margin is derived from its paid harvests and the hay sold off it, two links away. A harvest moves from planted to harvested, delivered and paid only through its actions, and Sell harvest refuses to run without a signed delivery note and a quality test of your own.
In most software, deciding and doing are one step. In Sutra they are two. When you sell hay, the server first writes down what would change: one new sale, forty bales, your name on it. Nothing has happened yet. Only when that note is approved does the barn change.
Everything else follows from that:
- You can always ask first. "Would this work?" is the same question, without the last step.
- An agent can only make suggestions. What it hands back is the note, never the change. You decide.
- You can try things out. Run the same decisions on a copy, compare, and keep the real one untouched.
- You can look back. Every approved change is kept, so last Tuesday's state is still there to read.
- The answer is the same everywhere. The rule is checked in one place, so the phone, the script and the agent hear the same no.
And it is not a promise. A test fails if the part that decides ever does anything but decide.
- Your data comes in as it is. Mail over IMAP, CSV exports, REST APIs, PostgreSQL, MySQL, SQL Server and SSH, shaped by transforms in Python, SQL or Clojure.
- Every number has a past. Lineage runs from each object back through the
transforms and datasets to the mail, statement or row it came from
(sutra lineage <node>). When someone else's figures disagree with yours,
you open the source.
- Agents get only the tools you name. An action, a function, a query on
one type. By default (:writes :propose) their changes are staged as
proposals; a rejected one doesn't come back, and the model's prompt and
answer are kept with the run.
- The model is versioned like code. Model and data live on branches. You
stage a change, commit it, open a proposal, see who consumes what you
touched, and merge into main only when it's reviewed. Webhooks and
notifications stay silent on a branch unless you say otherwise.
- Share exactly what you choose. Markings on types and properties are mandatory access control; grants on projects decide who may read, act and invoke. A group (a co-op, a tenants' association, a family) can pool what its members agree to share, and nothing else, and the server enforces it on every read: lists, filters, counts and totals. One person's data can't show what everyone else was offered. A group's can.
- Builders and users are different people. One person writes the model; everyone else gets apps with the build tools locked away, and a server that says no when no is the right answer.
- Secrets stay out of reach. A separate egress agent holds every external credential, so the code that reads your mail never sees the password.
You described the farm once. Generate a Python SDK from it, and your code speaks the farm's language: lots, harvests and buyers, and the things you are allowed to do with them, not tables and joins.
pip install ./clients/python-platform-sdk
sutra-platform-sdk generate --base-url http://127.0.0.1:<port> \
                            --username admin --password "…" --out farm_sdk.py
farm_sdk.py is plain, typed Python: a class per object type, an attribute
per property and link, a method per action with the action's parameters as
its signature. Sutra's language server (clients/ent-lsp)
runs pyright for Python and labels each completion as what it is in the model
(property, link-type, action, state); any editor that lets you pick the
language server can run it (Neovim config).
An action is a method. Its signature comes from the model; a missing argument is flagged before anything runs.
lot = Lot.get("North Meadow, 1st cut 2025")
lot.sell_hay(bales=20, buyer="Neighbour")
# error: Argument missing for parameter "sale_nr"
lot.sell_hay(bales=20, buyer="Neighbour", sale_nr="S-2026-010")   # ok
States are types. A harvest's status is
Literal['planted', 'harvested', 'delivered', 'paid'], so the editor offers
exactly those four.
for h in Field.get("hilltop").harvests:
    if h.status == "planted":       # completes: planted, harvested, delivered, paid
        h.record_harvest(harvested_on=date(2027, 7, 28), tonnes=61.5)
Filters are checked too. Comparing bales with a string, or reading a link that may be empty, fails in the editor, not on the farm.
Lot.where(Lot.in_barn < "forty")   # error: Operator "<" not supported for types
                                   #   "Property[int]" and "Literal['forty']"
Lot.where(Lot.in_barn < 50)        # ok
lot.field.margin                   # error: "margin" is not a known attribute of "None"
if lot.field:
    lot.field.margin               # ok
The walkthrough in code: objects with their links, acting, asking first, proposing, lifecycles, server-side aggregates, sets, branches and revisions
from sutra_platform_sdk import SutraClient, sum_, avg, count, by, bucket
from farm_sdk import Lot, Sale, Field, Buyer, register_all
client = register_all(SutraClient("http://127.0.0.1:<port>", username="admin", password="…"))
An object, not a row. A lot brings its derived values and its links along.
Nobody stored in_barn; the server computes it.
lot = Lot.get("North Meadow, 1st cut 2025")   # a Lot, or NotFound
lot.baled, lot.sold, lot.in_barn            # (120, 95, 25)
lot.field                                   # <Field 'North Meadow'>: the field it was cut from
[(s.buyer, s.bales) for s in lot.sales]     # its three sales, each a Sale
Act on it. Selling hay is something you do to a lot. The server checks the rule, writes the sale, stamps your name on it and tells you what changed.
lot.sell_hay(sale_nr="S-2026-010", buyer="Neighbour", bales=20, price_per_bale=4.50)
Lot.get(lot.name).in_barn                   # 5
Ask first, or only propose. The same call can be a question (nothing is written) or a proposal (staged until someone approves it). That is how an agent gets to use it.
from sutra_platform_sdk.actions import ActionConfig, ActionMode
ask = ActionConfig(mode=ActionMode.VALIDATE_ONLY)
lot.sell_hay(sale_nr="S-2026-011", buyer="Neighbour", bales=40,
             action_config=ask).validation.submission_criteria
# ('Not that many bales in the barn.',)
lot.sell_hay(sale_nr="S-2026-011", buyer="Neighbour", bales=5,
             publish=False).staged          # True: a proposal, the barn is unchanged
Walk a lifecycle. A harvest goes from planted to harvested,
delivered and paid, one action per step, and the rules travel with them.
The states are declared in the model, so status is typed
Literal['planted', 'harvested', 'delivered', 'paid'] and your editor offers
exactly those four.
from datetime import date
wheat = next(h for h in Field.get("hilltop").harvests if h.status == "planted")
mill  = Buyer.get("stone-mill")
wheat.record_harvest(harvested_on=date(2027, 7, 28), tonnes=61.5)
wheat.sell_harvest(buyer=mill, price_per_tonne=212)
# ServerError: HTTP 422: No signed delivery note. Without one, the buyer decides later what you delivered.
wheat.sell_harvest(buyer=mill, price_per_tonne=212, delivery_note=True, quality_tested=True)
Buyer.get("stone-mill").unpaid              # 13038: what the mill owes you, until …
wheat.record_payment()
Field.get("hilltop").margin                 # … the wheat lands in the field's margin
Ask the whole farm, in one line. Who bought how much hay, month by month, at what price. Grouped, summed and sorted on the server; nothing is downloaded to be counted.
Sale.aggregate(
    sum_(Sale.bales), avg(Sale.price_per_bale), count(),
    group_by=[bucket(Sale.sold_on, "month"), by(Sale.buyer)],
    order_by=sum_(Sale.bales), desc=True,
).flat()
# [{'sold_on_month': '2025-12', 'buyer': 'Riding stables', 'bales_sum': 80,
#   'price_per_bale_avg': Decimal('4.5000000000000000'), 'count': 2}, …]
Money stays Decimal all the way from the database; nothing is rounded
through a float.
Sets, not lists. On the class, a property is a filter and a link is a hop.
Combine sets with |, & and -; the server plans it, and you fetch only
what you iterate. Each set knows what it holds: running_low is an
ObjectSet[Lot], and one hop along Lot.sales makes it an ObjectSet[Sale].
running_low = Lot.where(Lot.in_barn < 50)
neighbours  = Sale.where(Sale.buyer == "Neighbour").around(Sale.lot)
(running_low - neighbours).count()          # 3: running low, and the neighbour never bought from them
running_low.around(Lot.sales).aggregate(sum_(Sale.bales), group_by=[by(Sale.buyer)])   # who emptied them
Try tomorrow's rule on today's farm, or look at last Tuesday. Everything above runs unchanged against a branch, and every action tells you the revision it landed in, a state you can read again later.
with client.branch("new-rule"):             # made with: sutra branch new-rule
    lot.sell_hay(sale_nr="S-2026-012", buyer="Neighbour", bales=5)   # main never notices
sale = lot.sell_hay(sale_nr="S-2026-013", buyer="Neighbour", bales=1)
client.at(sale.revision).objects("sale")    # the sales as they stood right then
And what it refuses. Wrong names, missing arguments and wrong types fail
in your editor, before anything runs. Broken rules fail on the server, with
the same sentence for every client. If the model changes underneath you,
register_all refuses to run until you regenerate.
lot.sell_hay(bales=20, buyer="Neighbour")   # error: Argument missing for parameter "sale_nr"
lot.sell_hay(sale_nr="S-1", buyer="Neighbour", bales="20")
                                            # error: "Literal['20']" is not assignable to "int"
Lot.where(Lot.in_barn < "about forty")      # error: Operator "<" not supported for types
                                            #   "Property[int]" and "Literal['about forty']"
lot.field.margin                            # error: "margin" is not a known attribute of "None"
These are pyright's messages against the generated farm SDK. The SDK's tests run the same lines through pyright, against an SDK generated from a schema captured from a running server, not from a hand-written imitation of one.
None of this is a reporting layer bolted on afterwards. The UI, the CLI, the
REST API and the agents ask the same questions and take the same actions
through the same door, which is why an agent can be handed Lot and
sell_hay and nothing else.
./bin/start.sh   # backend + shadow-cljs watch; PostgreSQL from SUTRA_PG_URL
./bin/stop.sh
(cd tests/frontend && npm install)   # once: the browser tests' driver (playwright-core)
bin/test         # full suite; integration tiers start PostgreSQL in podman
The semantics are pinned by an executable scenario, an aircraft maintenance
shop in tests/scenario: "no release while a
defect is open" is the barn rule above in another world.
Security: set SUTRA_HTTP_BIND when running the plain server (it defaults
to 0.0.0.0). ./bin/start.sh allows anonymous access, so never expose it.
Transforms run user code on the server, so grant that right accordingly.
Maintainers wanted. Sutra-OS needs people who will take responsibility for part of it over time: the server, the web client, the Python SDK, connectors, packaging and installation, docs, or the example models. You don't need to know all of it. Start with a few contributions in one area; if you'd like to stay, say so in an issue.
The most useful contributions are not always code:
- A model for an everyday problem. A tenancy, a pension, a medical bill,
a small association's membership. If a rule protects someone, it belongs in
resources/presets.
- Plain-language guides for the person who sets Sutra up for others.
- Translations. Parts of the code base and the examples are in German; the user interface should speak more languages than two.
- Code. Send a failing test or a fix.
Open an issue for questions, ideas and bug reports. Be kind: the person on the other side may be the one this was built for.
The code is offered as is, in the spirit of Rich Hickey's Open Source is Not About You: nobody is owed a feature, a fix or an answer, and that includes the maintainers. What keeps it going is people choosing to help.
The ontology model of objects, links and actions follows the one popularised
by Palantir Foundry; Sutra-OS was built from its public documentation.
The web client is built on Palantir's Blueprint
UI toolkit (Apache-2.0), vendored as a ClojureScript port in
clients/frontend/vendor/blueprint-cljs.
The implementation owes everything to Rich Hickey and Clojure, and to his
almost spiritual talk
Are We There Yet?,
which will perhaps continue to transform the software craft into eternity.
Claude Code wrote all of the code.
AGPL-3.0-or-later, with a section 7 exception for linking with EPL libraries such as Clojure. See LICENSE. The AGPL means anyone who runs a modified Sutra as a service for others must offer them the source, so the tool stays in the hands of the people it serves.
No patent search has been carried out. The AGPL licenses contributors' patents only; checking third-party patents where they apply is up to you. Palantir and Foundry are trademarks of Palantir Technologies Inc.; Sutra-OS is independent and not affiliated with or endorsed by Palantir.
