 :rules  [{:op :create :object-type :sale
           :values {:sale-nr [:get :sale-nr] :lot [:get :lot :name]
                    :buyer [:get :buyer] :bales [:get :bales]
                    :price-per-bale [:get :price-per-bale]
                    :sold-on [:get :sold-on] :sold-by [:get :sold-by]}}]
 :edits  [{:object-type :sale}]}
Ask to sell more bales than are in the barn and the server refuses and says why, with the same sentence for the phone, the CLI, the Python script and the AI agent. A refused action writes nothing. Every sale records who made it, and nobody can put someone else's name there.
Actions are the only write path: typed parameters, validation, submission criteria and state transitions, defined once and enforced everywhere.
Every rule you would otherwise keep in your head has this shape: a fact the server computes, and an action that checks it. Declare it once, and stop remembering it.
The same three ideas run the arable side of the farm. A field's margin is derived from its paid harvests and the hay sold off it, two links away. A harvest moves from planted to harvested, delivered and paid only through its actions, and Sell harvest refuses to run without a signed delivery note and a quality test of your own.
In most software, deciding and doing are one step. In Sutra they are two. When you sell hay, the server first writes down what would change: one new sale, forty bales, your name on it. Nothing has happened yet. Only when that note is approved does the barn change.
Everything else follows from that:
- You can always ask first. "Would this work?" is the same question, without the last step.
- An agent can only make suggestions. What it hands back is the note, never the change. You decide.
- You can try things out. Run the same decisions on a copy, compare, and keep the real one untouched.
- You can look back. Every approved change is kept, so last Tuesday's state is still there to read.
- The answer is the same everywhere. The rule is checked in one place, so the phone, the script and the agent hear the same no.
And it is not a promise. A test fails if the part that decides ever does anything but decide.
- Your data comes in as it is. Mail over IMAP, CSV exports, REST APIs, PostgreSQL, MySQL, SQL Server and SSH, shaped by transforms in Python, SQL or Clojure.
- Every number has a past. Lineage runs from each object back through the
transforms and datasets to the mail, statement or row it came from
(sutra lineage <node>). When someone else's figures disagree with yours,
you open the source.
- Agents get only the tools you name. An action, a function, a query on
one type. By default (:writes :propose) their changes are staged as
proposals; a rejected one doesn't come back, and the model's prompt and
answer are kept with the run.
- The model is versioned like code. Model and data live on branches. You
stage a change, commit it, open a proposal, see who consumes what you
touched, and merge into main only when it's reviewed. Webhooks and
notifications stay silent on a branch unless you say otherwise.
- Share exactly what you choose. Markings on types and properties are mandatory access control; grants on projects decide who may read, act and invoke. A group (a co-op, a tenants' association, a family) can pool what its members agree to share, and nothing else, and the server enforces it on every read: lists, filters, counts and totals. One person's data can't show what everyone else was offered. A group's can.
- Builders and users are different people. One person writes the model; everyone else gets apps with the build tools locked away, and a server that says no when no is the right answer.
- Secrets stay out of reach. A separate egress agent holds every external credential, so the code that reads your mail never sees the password.
You described the farm once. Generate a Python SDK from it, and your code speaks the farm's language: lots, harvests and buyers, and the things you are allowed to do with them, not tables and joins.
pip install ./clients/python-platform-sdk
sutra-platform-sdk generate --base-url http://127.0.0.1:<port> \
                            --username admin --password "…" --out farm_sdk.py
farm_sdk.py is plain, typed Python: a class per object type, an attribute
per property and link, a method per action with the action's parameters as
its signature. Sutra's language server (clients/ent-lsp)
runs pyright for Python and labels each completion as what it is in the model
(property, link-type, action, state); any editor that lets you pick the
language server can run it (Neovim config).
An action is a method. Its signature comes from the model; a missing argument is flagged before anything runs.
lot = Lot.get("North Meadow, 1st cut 2025")
lot.sell_hay(bales=20, buyer="Neighbour")
# error: Argument missing for parameter "sale_nr"
lot.sell_hay(bales=20, buyer="Neighbour", sale_nr="S-2026-010")   # ok
States are types. A harvest's status is
Literal['planted', 'harvested', 'delivered', 'paid'], so the editor offers
exactly those four.
for h in Field.get("hilltop").harvests:
    if h.status == "planted":       # completes: planted, harvested, delivered, paid
        h.record_harvest(harvested_on=date(2027, 7, 28), tonnes=61.5)
Filters are checked too. Comparing bales with a string, or reading a link that may be empty, fails in the editor, not on the farm.
Lot.where(Lot.in_barn < "forty")   # error: Operator "<" not supported for types
                                   #   "Property[int]" and "Literal['forty']"
Lot.where(Lot.in_barn < 50)        # ok
lot.field.margin                   # error: "margin" is not a known attribute of "None"
if lot.field:
    lot.field.margin               # ok
The walkthrough in code: objects with their links, acting, asking first, proposing, lifecycles, server-side aggregates, sets, branches and revisions
from sutra_platform_sdk import SutraClient, sum_, avg, count, by, bucket
from farm_sdk import Lot, Sale, Field, Buyer, register_all
client = register_all(SutraClient("http://127.0.0.1:<port>", username="admin", password="…"))
An object, not a row. A lot brings its derived values and its links along.
Nobody stored in_barn; the server computes it.
lot = Lot.get("North Meadow, 1st cut 2025")   # a Lot, or NotFound
lot.baled, lot.sold, lot.in_barn            # (120, 95, 25)
lot.field                                   # <Field 'North Meadow'>: the field it was cut from
[(s.buyer, s.bales) for s in lot.sales]     # its three sales, each a Sale
Act on it. Selling hay is something you do to a lot. The server checks the rule, writes the sale, stamps your name on it and tells you what changed.
lot.sell_hay(sale_nr="S-2026-010", buyer="Neighbour", bales=20, price_per_bale=4.50)
Lot.get(lot.name).in_barn                   # 5
Ask first, or only propose. The same call can be a question (nothing is written) or a proposal (staged until someone approves it). That is how an agent gets to use it.
from sutra_platform_sdk.actions import ActionConfig, ActionMode
ask = ActionConfig(mode=ActionMode.VALIDATE_ONLY)
lot.sell_hay(sale_nr="S-2026-011", buyer="Neighbour", bales=40,
             action_config=ask).validation.submission_criteria
# ('Not that many bales in the barn.',)
lot.sell_hay(sale_nr="S-2026-011", buyer="Neighbour", bales=5,
             publish=False).staged          # True: a proposal, the barn is unchanged
Walk a lifecycle. A harvest goes from planted to harvested,
delivered and paid, one action per step, and the rules travel with them.
The states are declared in the model, so status is typed
Literal['planted', 'harvested', 'delivered', 'paid'] and your editor offers
exactly those four.
from datetime import date
wheat = next(h for h in Field.get("hilltop").harvests if h.status == "planted")
mill  = Buyer.get("stone-mill")
wheat.record_harvest(harvested_on=date(2027, 7, 28), tonnes=61.5)
wheat.sell_harvest(buyer=mill, price_per_tonne=212)
# ServerError: HTTP 422: No signed delivery note. Without one, the buyer decides later what you delivered.
wheat.sell_harvest(buyer=mill, price_per_tonne=212, delivery_note=True, quality_tested=True)
Buyer.get("stone-mill").unpaid              # 13038: what the mill owes you, until …
wheat.record_payment()
Field.get("hilltop").margin                 # … the wheat lands in the field's margin
Ask the whole farm, in one line. Who bought how much hay, month by month, at what price. Grouped, summed and sorted on the server; nothing is downloaded to be counted.
Sale.aggregate(
    sum_(Sale.bales), avg(Sale.price_per_bale), count(),
    group_by=[bucket(Sale.sold_on, "month"), by(Sale.buyer)],
    order_by=sum_(Sale.bales), desc=True,
).flat()
# [{'sold_on_month': '2025-12', 'buyer': 'Riding stables', 'bales_sum': 80,
#   'price_per_bale_avg': Decimal('4.5000000000000000'), 'count': 2}, …]
Money stays Decimal all the way from the database; nothing is rounded
through a float.
Sets, not lists. On the class, a property is a filter and a link is a hop.
Combine sets with |, & and -; the server plans it, and you fetch only
what you iterate. Each set knows what it holds: running_low is an
ObjectSet[Lot], and one hop along Lot.sales makes it an ObjectSet[Sale].
running_low = Lot.where(Lot.in_barn < 50)
neighbours  = Sale.where(Sale.buyer == "Neighbour").around(Sale.lot)
(running_low - neighbours).count()          # 3: running low, and the neighbour never bought from them
running_low.around(Lot.sales).aggregate(sum_(Sale.bales), group_by=[by(Sale.buyer)])   # who emptied them
Try tomorrow's rule on today's farm, or look at last Tuesday. Everything above runs unchanged against a branch, and every action tells you the revision it landed in, a state you can read again later.
with client.branch("new-rule"):             # made with: sutra branch new-rule
    lot.sell_hay(sale_nr="S-2026-012", buyer="Neighbour", bales=5)   # main never notices
sale = lot.sell_hay(sale_nr="S-2026-013", buyer="Neighbour", bales=1)
client.at(sale.revision).objects("sale")    # the sales as they stood right then
And what it refuses. Wrong names, missing arguments and wrong types fail
in your editor, before anything runs. Broken rules fail on the server, with
the same sentence for every client. If the model changes underneath you,
register_all refuses to run until you regenerate.
lot.sell_hay(bales=20, buyer="Neighbour")   # error: Argument missing for parameter "sale_nr"
lot.sell_hay(sale_nr="S-1", buyer="Neighbour", bales="20")
                                            # error: "Literal['20']" is not assignable to "int"
Lot.where(Lot.in_barn < "about forty")      # error: Operator "<" not supported for types
                                            #   "Property[int]" and "Literal['about forty']"
lot.field.margin                            # error: "margin" is not a known attribute of "None"
These are pyright's messages against the generated farm SDK. The SDK's tests run the same lines through pyright, against an SDK generated from a schema captured from a running server, not from a hand-written imitation of one.
None of this is a reporting layer bolted on afterwards. The UI, the CLI, the
REST API and the agents ask the same questions and take the same actions
through the same door, which is why an agent can be handed Lot and
sell_hay and nothing else.
./bin/start.sh   # backend + shadow-cljs watch; PostgreSQL from SUTRA_PG_URL
./bin/stop.sh
(cd tests/frontend && npm install)   # once: the browser tests' driver (playwright-core)
bin/test         # full suite; integration tiers start PostgreSQL in podman
The semantics are pinned by an executable scenario, an aircraft maintenance
shop in tests/scenario: "no release while a
defect is open" is the barn rule above in another world.
Security: set SUTRA_HTTP_BIND when running the plain server (it defaults
to 0.0.0.0). ./bin/start.sh allows anonymous access, so never expose it.
Transforms run user code on the server, so grant that right accordingly.
Maintainers wanted. Sutra-OS needs people who will take responsibility for part of it over time: the server, the web client, the Python SDK, connectors, packaging and installation, docs, or the example models. You don't need to know all of it. Start with a few contributions in one area; if you'd like to stay, say so in an issue.
The most useful contributions are not always code:
- A model for an everyday problem. A tenancy, a pension, a medical bill,
a small association's membership. If a rule protects someone, it belongs in
resources/presets.
- Plain-language guides for the person who sets Sutra up for others.
- Translations. Parts of the code base and the examples are in German; the user interface should speak more languages than two.
- Code. Send a failing test or a fix.
Open an issue for questions, ideas and bug reports. Be kind: the person on the other side may be the one this was built for.
The code is offered as is, in the spirit of Rich Hickey's Open Source is Not About You: nobody is owed a feature, a fix or an answer, and that includes the maintainers. What keeps it going is people choosing to help.
The ontology model of objects, links and actions follows the one popularised
by Palantir Foundry; Sutra-OS was built from its public documentation.
The web client is built on Palantir's Blueprint
UI toolkit (Apache-2.0), vendored as a ClojureScript port in
clients/frontend/vendor/blueprint-cljs.
The implementation owes everything to Rich Hickey and Clojure, and to his
almost spiritual talk
Are We There Yet?,
which will perhaps continue to transform the software craft into eternity.
Claude Code wrote all of the code.

Tech leaders are convinced that Jensen Huang’s exit was a plot against him
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-16
23:20:44 UTC
08:20:44 KST

Jensen Huang’s stunning resignation from Nvidia after more than 33 years has sent shockwaves throughout the tech industry. Now prominent figures are calling for Huang’s reinstatement, and many are also hopping on board with a theory spreading throughout Silicon Valley: that the scandal is the result of a plot against the former CEO.
Tech leaders are convinced that Jensen Huang’s exit was a plot against him
David Sacks, Tim Sweeney, and other big names in tech are raising questions about the circumstances surrounding Huang’s resignation.
At the center of this theory is the reporting from The New York Times that former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, one of multiple women Huang is accused of having an affair with, is a Chinese spy. US agencies haven’t confirmed whether this is the case, and President Donald Trump claims he was unaware of any spy allegations. Jensen Huang’s alleged relationship with Alice Huang set off the events that led to his resignation; subsequent reporting suggested he had an affair with two employees at Nvidia, including one of his direct reports. The revelation raised new questions around whether Nvidia customers, including Megaspeed, were funneling chips to China in circumvention of export restrictions. Last year, Nvidia intervened in a chip-smuggling probe surrounding the Singapore-based company, according to Bloomberg.
David Sacks, a venture capitalist and former White House AI crypto advisor, has made it clear that he’s skeptical about The New York Times’ reporting. In a post on X, Sacks points to an article from CNN, which says US officials still can’t confirm that Alice Huang was a spy. “The spy narrative is collapsing already,” Sacks writes. He continued to question the spy allegations during Semafor’s Silicon Valley & The World event on Monday, saying: “The reason why Jensen lost his job is because of this lurid Chinese spy allegation. So if no one’s willing to back that up now, then again, I question why this entire chain of events happened.”
Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney similarly expressed concern about how Huang’s resignation played out. In a reply to Sacks’ post about the CNN article, Sweeney said, “What?! So journalists with sketchy motives portrayed rumors from god-knows-who (lobbyists?) as facts sourced from intelligence officials and gaslit the NVIDIA board to push out the founder, CEO, and once-in-generation visionary?” Amjad Masad, the CEO of the AI app and website builder Replit, called the timing of Huang’s resignation “suspicious,” adding that “if Jensen, the premier champion of open models, is taken out then anyone who wants AI closed, centralized, and controlled might benefit.”
SemiAnalysis, a research and consulting firm for the semiconductor and AI industry, posted a “conspiracy theory take on the situation,” speculating that Huang’s removal is a way to “remove opposition to an extremely hawkish China export control bill,” referring to the Remote Access Security Act (RASA). The bill, which Huang opposed, would extend AI export controls to cloud servers that allow companies in restricted countries to rent access to Nvidia’s AI chips. The Department of Commerce released an interim final rule on Monday in an attempt to address this loophole.
More than 185,000 people, including Sweeney, Masad, Elon Musk, Uber co-founder Travis Kalanick, Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan, and Shopify CEO Tobias Lütke, have signed a “Stand with Jensen” letter, calling for his reinstatement as CEO. The letter similarly calls for a closer look into the circumstances surrounding Huang’s resignation. “We have not been shown evidence substantiating the allegation that the woman at the center of this controversy was a spy,” the letter says. “Suspicion or association alone is not a sufficient basis for ending Jensen’s leadership. The inquiry must establish the facts, not merely justify an outcome already imposed.”

====================================================================================================

Optimizely compromised, served malware on Zoom, GitLab
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-16
23:00:34 UTC
08:00:34 KST

On 16 November 2026, malicious JavaScript was served to visitors of 178 organisations through the Optimizely Web experimentation platform. The list includes some of the most visited sites in the world: Zoom, the BBC, CNN, Salesforce, GitLab, IKEA, HSBC, Equifax, Air France-KLM, Ledger and Peacock.
The malware was a multi-purpose kit. It swapped cryptocurrency wallet addresses for the attacker's, skimmed payment cards, stole cookies and screenshots, and on iPhones and iPads it delivered the Coruna and DarkSword iOS exploit kits. Crypto has already been stolen, with verified transactions to the attackers.
This is exactly the kind of attack Report URI is designed to detect. Our platform can identify unexpected changes to trusted JavaScript, detect new scripts being loaded and reveal suspicious connections to external destinations. In this case, those signals would have exposed the malicious activity, even though the original script was being served from a trusted Optimizely domain. With an appropriate CSP in enforcement mode, organisations could have gone a step further and blocked key parts of the attack before they succeeded.
The important lesson here is that trusted JavaScript doesn't always remain trustworthy. A website can be compromised through a third-party dependency without its own code or infrastructure ever being breached.
Most of the malicious code has now been removed. At 21:52 UTC we found it in only 1 of the 188 infected snippets, down from 169 at 20:56 UTC. If your site uses Optimizely Web, or you visited any of the sites below today, read on.
What happened
Optimizely Web works by having each customer load a JavaScript "snippet" from Optimizely's CDN, for example https://cdn.optimizely.com/js/6395285578973184.js. The snippet contains Optimizely's code, the customer's experiments and an optional field called Project JavaScript (projectJS): custom code the customer writes in their Optimizely dashboard, which runs on every page.
The attacker wrote their own code into the Project JavaScript of customer projects, and the rest of each snippet was untouched. The file was still served from cdn.optimizely.com, with Optimizely's certificate, from a host that every affected site already trusted. Here is what was added to Zoom's snippet:
A plain <script> tag pointing at https://1688360.cc/track.js (most snippets).
The same URL hidden in base64 with atob(). A search for "1688360.cc" won't find it. Smartsheet's snippet was cleaned at one point and then re-infected with this version, so whoever wrote the code still had access at the time.
A full inline copy of the payload, which runs through eval if 1688360.cc is blocked.
Some customers serve their snippet from their own domain, such as opt.scheels.com/js/<id>.js or www.vodafone.com.au/js/<id>.js. Those were affected too, because the content comes from the same Optimizely project.
We don't know how the attacker got write access to so many unrelated customer projects: a compromised Optimizely system, a compromised credential with cross-customer access, or something else. That's for Optimizely to explain. To be clear, Optimizely's CDN served exactly what it was given; this was not a CDN cache-poisoning attack. Optimizely's own sites were affected as well: the snippets for www.optimizely.com and login.optimizely.com both carried the code.
Timeline (UTC)
The payload was rebuilt 26 times during the day. The attacker added features, and added wallets but never removed any.
What the payload does
Every visitor received the same script, track.js, and every part of it was active at once. What a visitor experienced depended on their device and what they did on the page.
The script finds any valid wallet address (BTC, ETH and EVM chains, TRON, Solana, TON, Litecoin, Bitcoin Cash, Dogecoin) and replaces it with one of the attacker's. It validates checksums so it only swaps real addresses. It swaps them in:
page text, form fields, links and images;
the clipboard, on copy and through navigator.clipboard;
outgoing fetch/XHR requests, window.open, navigation and the iOS share sheet.
It re-scans the page whenever it changes. Copy an address from any affected page, or from anywhere else while that tab is open, and you may paste the attacker's.
2. Card skimming
Added at 13:43 UTC. The script watches form submissions and every fetch/XHR POST for a card number (Luhn-checked), expiry date and CVV. When it has all three, it sends them to https://1688360.cc/card.php with navigator.sendBeacon. Cards entered in hosted payment iframes (Stripe, Adyen and similar) were out of its reach. Cards typed into a site's own form fields were not.
3. Cookie and screenshot theft
When it swaps an address next to wallet or payment wording, it sends the following to https://1688360.cc/hit.php:
the page URL;
document.cookie (cookies not marked HttpOnly);
the original addresses it replaced;
a screenshot of the page, rendered with html2canvas.
4. iOS exploit kits: Coruna and DarkSword
On iPhones and iPads the script loads boot.js from 5vobauua3ojpklx.icu and eil786npto9u5bq.icu. It reads the iOS version and loads an exploit chain in a hidden iframe:
The Coruna loader is byte-identical to a sample we analysed in October from a different campaign. The kit's own comments say iOS 18.7.3 and later, and 26.3 and later, are patched. Pages on iOS 18.6 or earlier reload every 60 seconds to retry the exploit, which is a visible symptom.
Money stolen so far
As of 19:25 UTC on 16 November, the attacker's wallets had received about $7,040, all within roughly nine hours on the 16th:
Funds were swept out at 13:03 to 13:05 UTC. The USDC on Ethereum went to a HitBTC deposit address. The TRON wallet has been in use since March 2026, so this actor was active long before this campaign. We cannot see how many cards or cookies reached 1688360.cc.
Who was affected
We fetched 1,611 distinct Optimizely snippets, found mostly through our own crawl data, and checked each one for the malicious code. 188 snippets belonging to 178 organisations were infected. We kept a copy of every infected file, along with its response headers, SHA-256 hash and fetch time as evidence. The real number is likely higher as we could only check snippets we could find in time.
By sector
Full list
Ranked by Tranco position within each sector and the status at 21:52 UTC: every snippet except TalkTalk's had been cleaned.
Banking & Finance (32)
Technology & SaaS (23)
Retail & Fashion (20)
Streaming & Entertainment (12)
News & Media (10)
Airlines & Travel (9)
Healthcare & Pharma (8)
Industrial & Manufacturing (8)
Telecoms (7)
Crypto & Trading (6)
Insurance (6)
Property & Real Estate (6)
Food & Hospitality (5)
Home & Smart Home (5)
Nonprofit & Civic (4)
Gambling (4)
Professional Services (4)
Logistics & Shipping (3)
Automotive (3)
Education (3)
The earlier webshell.lol wave, through Kissmetrics, StorifyMe and Minute Media, affected other sites and isn't included above.
How Report URI would have detected, or even prevented, this attack
Every one of these needs only a CSP in Report-Only mode, so nothing on the site breaks:
It's possible to go even further and deploy a CSP that could actively block these attacks and prevent any data breach happening. The websites affected by this attack weren't necessarily running vulnerable code of their own, they were loading JavaScript from a provider they trusted, and that trust was exploited. This is why monitoring what runs in your visitors' browsers is so important. Report URI gives organisations the visibility to detect when trusted dependencies change or behave unexpectedly, and the tools to help enforce policies that prevent malicious scripts and connections from succeeding.
Did Content Security Policy help?
We crawled 256 of the affected organisations' own hosts and evaluated their CSP against what this payload needs:
Load track.js from 1688360.cc.
Or run the inline fallback, which needs 'unsafe-eval' or 'unsafe-inline'.
Send stolen data to 1688360.cc (connect-src).
Load the iOS kit (script-src, frame-src).
Allowing cdn.optimizely.com was never going to help. The malicious code arrived from the trusted host itself. What separated the 24 protected sites from the rest was a restrictive script-src. A site that lists only the hosts it needs blocks 1688360.cc. Two common settings let it straight through:
'strict-dynamic': any script the trusted Optimizely snippet inserts is trusted too.
'unsafe-eval', which Optimizely's own documentation tells customers to allow: the inline-fallback version ran through eval even where track.js was blocked. That is what happened at HSBC. Its CSP blocked track.js and the data theft, but the inline copy still ran.
connect-src matters. The 17 "partial" sites blocked card, cookie and screenshot theft because they restrict where the page can send data. That doesn't stop address swapping, which happens inside the page.
What to do now
If your site uses Optimizely Web:
Open every project's Project JavaScript and every experiment's custom code. Look for 1688360.cc, atob( and any script you didn't write.
Review who and what has write access to your Optimizely account (users, API tokens, integrations) and rotate credentials.
If you have card fields in your own forms, treat cards entered on 16 November as potentially exposed.
Treat any session tokens or secrets in non-HttpOnly cookies as exposed, and rotate them.
Deploy a CSP. Restrict connect-src, avoid 'unsafe-eval' where you can, and turn on reporting.
Consider if you need to communicate to your customers about having served them malware.
If you visited an affected site on 16 November:
Update your iPhone or iPad to iOS 18.7.3 or later, or iOS 26.3 or later.
Check any crypto transfer you made. If the address you pasted doesn't match the one you meant to pay, it may have been swapped.
Watch your card statements if you paid by card on an affected site.
Indicators of compromise
All of these domains are attacker-owned; block them including subdomains.
Attacker wallets: BTC 19g9rZcPeiz8rUvyWpTRyXXhPnjsWrc3kW ETH/EVM 0xC4026b06D82b0F0B363eda0642D61CfbDc17884e TRON THvs6p56XkUiMkJeSc51cAP64u2RsTYqxa SOL FVtL8NMYS9PA1v3HE67XUGCtGA9JaZUvqav2fU2LrV5n TON UQAgM1VqSZQiJLSG5w-ovurq5CZdxLEK3_7CMmwALTA5e09p LTC ltc1q3wp7g6ds0ggg6fl6kncpa4sepn0q3qfacgred5 BCH qzkaaf8n6grmpr0d9lx8xutn3r9lk722f566hhpe58 DOGE DBEGrxRF7KMSuhTS4c1MFviStU6efdMnu5
We keep an archive of every infected snippet with hashes and timestamps, and can share it with affected organisations and CERTs on request. During research and investigation, once we were confident that Optimizely was the source, we began efforts to reach out via various channels and provide the full details. At the time of publication, we have not had any response.
Update: 17 November 2026, 09:17 UTC
There are several updates to provide on this incident:
As of 16 Nov 23:11 UTC we observed that all infected Optimizely files were cleaned, and have remained clean since.
The attacker's domains are being taken down:
1688360.cc, the main payload and data-collection server, was put on clientHold at 17 Nov 00:37 UTC. It no longer resolves.
webshell.lol, the earlier-wave injector, was put on clientHold at 17 Nov 03:37 UTC.
eil786npto9u5bq.icu no longer resolves to an IP address.
5vobauua3ojpklx.icu still resolves but no longer responds.
Optimizely has acknowledged the incident on their status page.
Cryptocurrency transactions were recorded against the attacker's wallets:
04:13 UTC: 116 USDT → TMLQvDvpgSyx94maptc3TxvmCVtT5qHVup. This is the same address the attacker used to cash out earlier operations in March 2026 and on 3 November 2026. This has allowed us to tie them to other attacks in our Threat Intelligence data.
Note that these are on-chain receipts for crypto transactions only. We have no way to see how many credit cards were skimmed, how many cookies or screenshots were stolen, or how many iOS devices were infected before the attack was stopped.

====================================================================================================

Optimizely compromised, served malware on Zoom, GitLab
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-16
23:00:34 UTC
08:00:34 KST

On 16 November 2026, malicious JavaScript was served to visitors of 178 organisations through the Optimizely Web experimentation platform. The list includes some of the most visited sites in the world: Zoom, the BBC, CNN, Salesforce, GitLab, IKEA, HSBC, Equifax, Air France-KLM, Ledger and Peacock.
The malware was a multi-purpose kit. It swapped cryptocurrency wallet addresses for the attacker's, skimmed payment cards, stole cookies and screenshots, and on iPhones and iPads it delivered the Coruna and DarkSword iOS exploit kits. Crypto has already been stolen, with verified transactions to the attackers.
This is exactly the kind of attack Report URI is designed to detect. Our platform can identify unexpected changes to trusted JavaScript, detect new scripts being loaded and reveal suspicious connections to external destinations. In this case, those signals would have exposed the malicious activity, even though the original script was being served from a trusted Optimizely domain. With an appropriate CSP in enforcement mode, organisations could have gone a step further and blocked key parts of the attack before they succeeded.
The important lesson here is that trusted JavaScript doesn't always remain trustworthy. A website can be compromised through a third-party dependency without its own code or infrastructure ever being breached.
Most of the malicious code has now been removed. At 21:52 UTC we found it in only 1 of the 188 infected snippets, down from 169 at 20:56 UTC. If your site uses Optimizely Web, or you visited any of the sites below today, read on.
What happened
Optimizely Web works by having each customer load a JavaScript "snippet" from Optimizely's CDN, for example https://cdn.optimizely.com/js/6395285578973184.js. The snippet contains Optimizely's code, the customer's experiments and an optional field called Project JavaScript (projectJS): custom code the customer writes in their Optimizely dashboard, which runs on every page.
The attacker wrote their own code into the Project JavaScript of customer projects, and the rest of each snippet was untouched. The file was still served from cdn.optimizely.com, with Optimizely's certificate, from a host that every affected site already trusted. Here is what was added to Zoom's snippet:
A plain <script> tag pointing at https://1688360.cc/track.js (most snippets).
The same URL hidden in base64 with atob(). A search for "1688360.cc" won't find it. Smartsheet's snippet was cleaned at one point and then re-infected with this version, so whoever wrote the code still had access at the time.
A full inline copy of the payload, which runs through eval if 1688360.cc is blocked.
Some customers serve their snippet from their own domain, such as opt.scheels.com/js/<id>.js or www.vodafone.com.au/js/<id>.js. Those were affected too, because the content comes from the same Optimizely project.
We don't know how the attacker got write access to so many unrelated customer projects: a compromised Optimizely system, a compromised credential with cross-customer access, or something else. That's for Optimizely to explain. To be clear, Optimizely's CDN served exactly what it was given; this was not a CDN cache-poisoning attack. Optimizely's own sites were affected as well: the snippets for www.optimizely.com and login.optimizely.com both carried the code.
Timeline (UTC)
The payload was rebuilt 26 times during the day. The attacker added features, and added wallets but never removed any.
What the payload does
Every visitor received the same script, track.js, and every part of it was active at once. What a visitor experienced depended on their device and what they did on the page.
The script finds any valid wallet address (BTC, ETH and EVM chains, TRON, Solana, TON, Litecoin, Bitcoin Cash, Dogecoin) and replaces it with one of the attacker's. It validates checksums so it only swaps real addresses. It swaps them in:
page text, form fields, links and images;
the clipboard, on copy and through navigator.clipboard;
outgoing fetch/XHR requests, window.open, navigation and the iOS share sheet.
It re-scans the page whenever it changes. Copy an address from any affected page, or from anywhere else while that tab is open, and you may paste the attacker's.
2. Card skimming
Added at 13:43 UTC. The script watches form submissions and every fetch/XHR POST for a card number (Luhn-checked), expiry date and CVV. When it has all three, it sends them to https://1688360.cc/card.php with navigator.sendBeacon. Cards entered in hosted payment iframes (Stripe, Adyen and similar) were out of its reach. Cards typed into a site's own form fields were not.
3. Cookie and screenshot theft
When it swaps an address next to wallet or payment wording, it sends the following to https://1688360.cc/hit.php:
the page URL;
document.cookie (cookies not marked HttpOnly);
the original addresses it replaced;
a screenshot of the page, rendered with html2canvas.
4. iOS exploit kits: Coruna and DarkSword
On iPhones and iPads the script loads boot.js from 5vobauua3ojpklx.icu and eil786npto9u5bq.icu. It reads the iOS version and loads an exploit chain in a hidden iframe:
The Coruna loader is byte-identical to a sample we analysed in October from a different campaign. The kit's own comments say iOS 18.7.3 and later, and 26.3 and later, are patched. Pages on iOS 18.6 or earlier reload every 60 seconds to retry the exploit, which is a visible symptom.
Money stolen so far
As of 19:25 UTC on 16 November, the attacker's wallets had received about $7,040, all within roughly nine hours on the 16th:
Funds were swept out at 13:03 to 13:05 UTC. The USDC on Ethereum went to a HitBTC deposit address. The TRON wallet has been in use since March 2026, so this actor was active long before this campaign. We cannot see how many cards or cookies reached 1688360.cc.
Who was affected
We fetched 1,611 distinct Optimizely snippets, found mostly through our own crawl data, and checked each one for the malicious code. 188 snippets belonging to 178 organisations were infected. We kept a copy of every infected file, along with its response headers, SHA-256 hash and fetch time as evidence. The real number is likely higher as we could only check snippets we could find in time.
By sector
Full list
Ranked by Tranco position within each sector and the status at 21:52 UTC: every snippet except TalkTalk's had been cleaned.
Banking & Finance (32)
Technology & SaaS (23)
Retail & Fashion (20)
Streaming & Entertainment (12)
News & Media (10)
Airlines & Travel (9)
Healthcare & Pharma (8)
Industrial & Manufacturing (8)
Telecoms (7)
Crypto & Trading (6)
Insurance (6)
Property & Real Estate (6)
Food & Hospitality (5)
Home & Smart Home (5)
Nonprofit & Civic (4)
Gambling (4)
Professional Services (4)
Logistics & Shipping (3)
Automotive (3)
Education (3)
The earlier webshell.lol wave, through Kissmetrics, StorifyMe and Minute Media, affected other sites and isn't included above.
How Report URI would have detected, or even prevented, this attack
Every one of these needs only a CSP in Report-Only mode, so nothing on the site breaks:
It's possible to go even further and deploy a CSP that could actively block these attacks and prevent any data breach happening. The websites affected by this attack weren't necessarily running vulnerable code of their own, they were loading JavaScript from a provider they trusted, and that trust was exploited. This is why monitoring what runs in your visitors' browsers is so important. Report URI gives organisations the visibility to detect when trusted dependencies change or behave unexpectedly, and the tools to help enforce policies that prevent malicious scripts and connections from succeeding.
Did Content Security Policy help?
We crawled 256 of the affected organisations' own hosts and evaluated their CSP against what this payload needs:
Load track.js from 1688360.cc.
Or run the inline fallback, which needs 'unsafe-eval' or 'unsafe-inline'.
Send stolen data to 1688360.cc (connect-src).
Load the iOS kit (script-src, frame-src).
Allowing cdn.optimizely.com was never going to help. The malicious code arrived from the trusted host itself. What separated the 24 protected sites from the rest was a restrictive script-src. A site that lists only the hosts it needs blocks 1688360.cc. Two common settings let it straight through:
'strict-dynamic': any script the trusted Optimizely snippet inserts is trusted too.
'unsafe-eval', which Optimizely's own documentation tells customers to allow: the inline-fallback version ran through eval even where track.js was blocked. That is what happened at HSBC. Its CSP blocked track.js and the data theft, but the inline copy still ran.
connect-src matters. The 17 "partial" sites blocked card, cookie and screenshot theft because they restrict where the page can send data. That doesn't stop address swapping, which happens inside the page.
What to do now
If your site uses Optimizely Web:
Open every project's Project JavaScript and every experiment's custom code. Look for 1688360.cc, atob( and any script you didn't write.
Review who and what has write access to your Optimizely account (users, API tokens, integrations) and rotate credentials.
If you have card fields in your own forms, treat cards entered on 16 November as potentially exposed.
Treat any session tokens or secrets in non-HttpOnly cookies as exposed, and rotate them.
Deploy a CSP. Restrict connect-src, avoid 'unsafe-eval' where you can, and turn on reporting.
Consider if you need to communicate to your customers about having served them malware.
If you visited an affected site on 16 November:
Update your iPhone or iPad to iOS 18.7.3 or later, or iOS 26.3 or later.
Check any crypto transfer you made. If the address you pasted doesn't match the one you meant to pay, it may have been swapped.
Watch your card statements if you paid by card on an affected site.
Indicators of compromise
All of these domains are attacker-owned; block them including subdomains.
Attacker wallets: BTC 19g9rZcPeiz8rUvyWpTRyXXhPnjsWrc3kW ETH/EVM 0xC4026b06D82b0F0B363eda0642D61CfbDc17884e TRON THvs6p56XkUiMkJeSc51cAP64u2RsTYqxa SOL FVtL8NMYS9PA1v3HE67XUGCtGA9JaZUvqav2fU2LrV5n TON UQAgM1VqSZQiJLSG5w-ovurq5CZdxLEK3_7CMmwALTA5e09p LTC ltc1q3wp7g6ds0ggg6fl6kncpa4sepn0q3qfacgred5 BCH qzkaaf8n6grmpr0d9lx8xutn3r9lk722f566hhpe58 DOGE DBEGrxRF7KMSuhTS4c1MFviStU6efdMnu5
We keep an archive of every infected snippet with hashes and timestamps, and can share it with affected organisations and CERTs on request. During research and investigation, once we were confident that Optimizely was the source, we began efforts to reach out via various channels and provide the full details. At the time of publication, we have not had any response.
Update: 17 November 2026, 09:17 UTC
There are several updates to provide on this incident:
As of 16 Nov 23:11 UTC we observed that all infected Optimizely files were cleaned, and have remained clean since.
The attacker's domains are being taken down:
1688360.cc, the main payload and data-collection server, was put on clientHold at 17 Nov 00:37 UTC. It no longer resolves.
webshell.lol, the earlier-wave injector, was put on clientHold at 17 Nov 03:37 UTC.
eil786npto9u5bq.icu no longer resolves to an IP address.
5vobauua3ojpklx.icu still resolves but no longer responds.
Optimizely has acknowledged the incident on their status page.
Cryptocurrency transactions were recorded against the attacker's wallets:
04:13 UTC: 116 USDT → TMLQvDvpgSyx94maptc3TxvmCVtT5qHVup. This is the same address the attacker used to cash out earlier operations in March 2026 and on 3 November 2026. This has allowed us to tie them to other attacks in our Threat Intelligence data.
Note that these are on-chain receipts for crypto transactions only. We have no way to see how many credit cards were skimmed, how many cookies or screenshots were stolen, or how many iOS devices were infected before the attack was stopped.

Comcast's 'Fiber-Powered Wi-Fi' Is Just Cable, AT&amp;T Says In False Advertising Case
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-16
23:00:00 UTC
08:00:00 KST

Comcast's 'Fiber-Powered Wi-Fi' Is Just Cable, AT&T Says In False Advertising Case (arstechnica.com) 81
			
		 	
				AT&T is suing (PDF) Comcast over its "fiber-powered" advertising, arguing that the term misleads consumers into believing Xfinity offers fiber-to-the-home service when most customers actually receive their Internet through coaxial cable. "Comcast should be required to restore to AT&T any and all profits earned as a result of its false and misleading advertising or provide AT&T with any other restitutionary relief the Court deems appropriate," the lawsuit said. The lawsuit asked for a permanent injunction "requiring Comcast to cease describing its hybrid fiber-coaxial service as 'Fiber-Powered.'" Ars Technica reports:  While Comcast has deployed plenty of fiber throughout the US, its cable Internet service makes the final connection to homes with coaxial cable. This results in upload speeds being far slower than download speeds. In contrast, fiber-to-the-home service can provide symmetrical upload and download speeds. Comcast has deployed fiber-to-the-home in only small portions of its cable territory while advertising its service as fiber-powered in the majority of areas where cable makes the final connection. AT&T's lawsuit said that in describing its cable broadband as fiber-powered, Comcast "conveys to reasonable consumers the misleading impression that Xfinity Internet is a fiber-to-the-home service comparable to AT&T Fiber. In fact, the vast majority of Xfinity Internet runs over a hybrid fiber-coaxial ('HFC') network, where the critical 'last mile' connection to the customer's home uses copper coaxial cable, not fiber."
[...] AT&T previously challenged Comcast's fiber-powered claims through the advertising industry's self-regulatory system operated by BBB National Programs' National Advertising Division (NAD). The NAD determined in June that Comcast accurately described its mix of cable and fiber technology in a disclosure, but that Comcast didn't make the disclosure prominently enough. [...] The NAD recommended that Comcast make the disclosure "more prominent and more proximate to the 'fiber-powered' Wi-Fi claim as necessary to ensure that the disclosure is clear and conspicuous." Comcast said it would comply with the decision, but AT&T's lawsuit makes it clear that it isn't satisfied with the current state of Comcast's advertising. We contacted Comcast and will update this article if it provides a response.

Windows 11 is collecting data on which coding agents and developer tools you use
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-16
22:58:20 UTC
07:58:20 KST

7 minutes
Windows 11 Is Collecting Data on Which Coding Agents and Developer Tools You Use
I recently asked AI to help me look through the latest Windows binaries for undocumented features and functionality. Among the leads it turned up, one list immediately caught my attention: coding agents such as codex.exe, claude.exe, copilot.exe, and cursor.exe, alongside familiar developer tools such as python.exe, dotnet.exe, gcc.exe, and code.exe.
Why would a Windows system DLL need a list of coding agents and developer tools?
The names were in MissionControlAggregator.dll. I asked the agent to follow the references and dig into what the DLL actually does. That led from a handful of string comparisons to a running Windows component that records which coding agents and developer tools have been used, saves that information locally, and reports it to Windows Diagnostics. And unless you have opted out of the “optional” diagnostic data (which is enabled by default), the data is uploaded to Microsoft.
I shared the initial finding on X. This post includes more details about the investigation behind it.
Following the names
Opening the DLL in Binary Ninja made the first lead easy to see. These were explicit executable names embedded in a Windows component:
Following the references showed that the names were used in comparisons. There are four CLI-agent slots checking copilot.exe, claude.exe, codex.exe, and agy.exe, with extensionless equivalents on the WSL route. Five desktop-agent slots check github.exe, claude.exe, codex.exe, cursor.exe, and antigravity.exe.
Here is one of the comparisons against codex.exe:
The compiler-generated C++ names are unwieldy, so I will call these functions executable-name matchers. For reference, one related matcher is std::_Func_impl_no_alloc<<lambda_340dfe7e962ab225b6ac33bc664bd54f>, bool, std::basic_string_view<wchar_t, std::char_traits<wchar_t>> const&, Microsoft::Diagnostics::MissionControlAggregator::Utils::AppType>::_Do_call. The screenshot shows another lambda instantiation performing the same kind of name comparison.
The feature guarding the new agent checks has a particularly direct name: CLICodingAgentUsage, feature ID 62580080.
And the list extends beyond AI agents. The same component checks for node.exe, python.exe, dotnet.exe, gcc.exe, clang.exe, gdb.exe, javac.exe, devenv.exe, and code.exe, among others. Those checks feed broader developer-tool categories. Some of these checks also verify the publisher: the Node.js slot expects Node.js Foundation, Python expects Python Software Foundation, and the .NET and Visual Studio slots expect Microsoft Corporation. The compiler/debugger group containing GCC and Clang has no additional publisher check in this path.
Where does the DLL get this information?
The next question was whether the component scans running processes itself. Following Aggregator::OnLoad revealed that it subscribes to existing Windows events instead.
For Windows programs, it consumes KernelProcess.AppStateChangeSummary and Win32kTraceLogging.AppInteractivitySummary. These provide application identities that its handlers parse and pass to the matchers. For WSL, it consumes Microsoft.Windows.Lxss.Manager.Exec and ExecCritical, using the imageName field. A regular Windows codex.exe can therefore reach the CLI-agent checks through the app-state summary route; this is not limited to Linux programs running inside WSL.
The relevant flow looks like this:
Aggregator::OnLoad
├─ WSL Exec / ExecCritical
│  └─ ProcessWslExecEvent → CLI agent matchers
├─ KernelProcess.AppStateChangeSummary
│  └─ ProcessAppStateChangeSummaryEvent → CLI agent matchers
└─ Win32kTraceLogging.AppInteractivitySummary
   └─ ProcessAppInteractivitySummaryEvent → desktop agent matchers
Aggregator::OnRelativeTimerExpire
└─ MissionControlAggregatorLogging::ReportCohorts<12,4,5>
   └─ Segments.SubCohorts ETW report
Aggregator::OnUnload / intermediate timer
└─ PersistentState::SaveToDisk
A successful match sets a slot in the aggregator’s state. Later, ReportCohorts<12,4,5> turns those slots into bitsets and emits a report named Segments.SubCohorts. The numbers correspond to twelve broader tool categories, four CLI agents, and five desktop agents.
ETW (Event Tracing for Windows) is the mechanism carrying that report. In this case, it is a self-describing TraceLogging event. Rather than writing the executable names into every report, the component assigns each category a bit. A set Codex bit means the matching tool was observed during the reporting window.
The source events also include runtime, time in focus, interaction counters, and resource usage. This component appears to discard those details and record only a bitmap of which agents were observed. The report’s timestamps describe the collection window, not how long each agent was used.
Although I found no prompt text or source code in this report, collecting which agents and developer tools someone uses already has significant privacy implications.
Is it actually running on a normal machine?
Strings and code are a useful lead, but I wanted to see the component running. I checked my Parallels Windows 11 ARM64 VM, build 26200.9974. Its MissionControlAggregator.dll was version 10.0.26100.9818, and CLICodingAgentUsage was already enabled as a service experiment. I had not manually enabled the feature.
DiagTrack, the Connected User Experiences and Telemetry service, was also already running with its startup type set to Automatic. It starts a separate AggregatorHost.exe process, and that process loads the DLL. The following administrative PowerShell commands show the service, its child process, and the loaded module:
PS> Get-Service DiagTrack | Format-Table Name,Status,StartType -AutoSize
Name       Status StartType
----       ------ ---------
DiagTrack Running Automatic
PS> Get-Process AggregatorHost | Format-Table ProcessName,Id -AutoSize
ProcessName      Id
-----------      --
AggregatorHost 1500
PS> Get-CimInstance Win32_Service -Filter "Name='DiagTrack'" |
       Format-List Name,State,StartMode,ProcessId
Name      : DiagTrack
State     : Running
StartMode : Auto
ProcessId : 10148
PS> Get-CimInstance Win32_Process -Filter "Name='AggregatorHost.exe'" |
       Format-List ProcessId,ParentProcessId
ProcessId       : 1500
ParentProcessId : 10148
PS> tasklist /m MissionControlAggregator.dll
Image Name                     PID Modules
========================= ======== ============================================
AggregatorHost.exe            1500 MissionControlAggregator.dll
The process IDs connect the pieces: DiagTrack runs in process 10148, which is the parent of AggregatorHost.exe, process 1500; the module listing places MissionControlAggregator.dll inside that child.
Testing it in practice
To test the finding in practice, I copied cmd.exe to a file named codex.exe and ran it in the interactive Windows session. The name alone was enough for the component to recognize it as Codex.
I captured an AppStateChangeSummary for the running process from the KernelProcess provider ({2839ff94-8f12-4e1b-82e3-af7af77a450f}), with LaunchCount=1 and TerminateCount=0. The aggregator’s saved state contained the value 2 in CLI slot 2, the Codex slot. A separate run without an additional ETW trace also set that slot, confirming that detection worked through Windows’ existing diagnostic collection.
That state is persisted in:
C:\ProgramData\Microsoft\Diagnosis\AggregatorStorage\MissionControlAggregator$
The report normally follows a roughly daily boundary. To capture it immediately, I temporarily moved the saved reporting-window start 25 hours into the past and restarted DiagTrack with a trace listening for the aggregator. The resulting Segments.SubCohorts event contained:
Provider: Microsoft.Windows.MissionControlAggregator
Time:     2026-11-16T12:34:29.125116Z
Version:  0.1.5
CliAgentSubCohorts:     4
DesktopAgentSubCohorts: 0
FlushedFromSave:       true
The value 4 is 1 << 2: the Codex CLI bit. The name had made it through the input summary, the matcher, the persisted state, and the emitted report.
I also tested the developer-tool path separately, using a copy of cmd.exe named gcc.exe. It set developer-tool slot 3. With optional diagnostic data allowed, I found the resulting Segments.SubCohorts event in DiagTrack’s upload queue with SubCohorts: [8] (1 << 3). This is the compiler/debugger category shared by GCC, Clang, and related tools, rather than a separate bit for each executable.
Following the report toward Microsoft
An ETW event by itself would not establish that anything leaves the machine. ETW is used for plenty of local diagnostics. The more revealing question was who was listening to this particular report.
The VM already had a Diagtrack-Listener session subscribed to both ends of the path. These PowerShell queries showed its subscriptions:
Get-EtwTraceProvider -SessionName Diagtrack-Listener `
  -Guid '{2839ff94-8f12-4e1b-82e3-af7af77a450f}'
Get-EtwTraceProvider -SessionName Diagtrack-Listener `
  -Guid '{00de11a2-ec06-58e5-1a2b-21bb21dbee4b}'
The first subscription, for KernelProcess, had MatchAnyKeyword=0x400000000002, which includes the summary’s 0x400000000000 keyword. The second, for Microsoft.Windows.MissionControlAggregator, had MatchAnyKeyword=0xe00000000000, which includes the report’s 0x800000000000 keyword. Both had level 255.
The subscription showed where the report could go. To check whether Windows actually queued it for transmission, I inspected DiagTrack’s EventStore.db and decompressed the JSON batches in its AsimovEvents table. This revealed an important condition: the agent-usage report is treated as optional diagnostic data.
When optional diagnostic data is allowed (enabled by default in the US setup I tested), Windows queues the coding-agent usage report for upload to Microsoft. I verified this by finding the agent event itself in DiagTrack’s upload queue:
{
  "name": "Microsoft.Windows.MissionControlAggregator.Segments.SubCohorts",
  "ext": {
    "privacy": { "isRequired": false }
  },
  "data": {
    "Version": "0.1.5",
    "CliAgentSubCohorts": [4],
    "DesktopAgentSubCohorts": [0],
    "FlushedFromSave": true
  }
}
I checked this with a clean installation of Windows 11 Home ARM64 using the latest official ISO, with the region set to the United States. Before changing any privacy settings, the Diagnostic data toggle was already enabled and labeled Include Optional. After completing setup with those defaults, AllowTelemetry was 3, confirming that optional diagnostic data was enabled.
Optional diagnostic data is therefore enabled by default, at least in the US setup I tested. If you did not opt out of it during installation or disable optional diagnostic data later, it is still enabled and your coding agent usage data is being uploaded. You can decline optional diagnostics during setup or turn off Send optional diagnostic data later under Settings → Privacy & security → Diagnostics & feedback. Microsoft’s diagnostics and privacy guide explains how to change it.

====================================================================================================

ETW (Event Tracing for Windows) is the mechanism carrying that report. In this case, it is a self-describing TraceLogging event. Rather than writing the executable names into every report, the component assigns each category a bit. A set Codex bit means the matching tool was observed during the reporting window.
The source events also include runtime, time in focus, interaction counters, and resource usage. This component appears to discard those details and record only a bitmap of which agents were observed. The report’s timestamps describe the collection window, not how long each agent was used.
Although I found no prompt text or source code in this report, collecting which agents and developer tools someone uses already has significant privacy implications.
Is it actually running on a normal machine?
Strings and code are a useful lead, but I wanted to see the component running. I checked my Parallels Windows 11 ARM64 VM, build 26200.9974. Its MissionControlAggregator.dll was version 10.0.26100.9818, and CLICodingAgentUsage was already enabled as a service experiment. I had not manually enabled the feature.
DiagTrack, the Connected User Experiences and Telemetry service, was also already running with its startup type set to Automatic. It starts a separate AggregatorHost.exe process, and that process loads the DLL. The following administrative PowerShell commands show the service, its child process, and the loaded module:
PS> Get-Service DiagTrack | Format-Table Name,Status,StartType -AutoSize
Name       Status StartType
----       ------ ---------
DiagTrack Running Automatic
PS> Get-Process AggregatorHost | Format-Table ProcessName,Id -AutoSize
ProcessName      Id
-----------      --
AggregatorHost 1500
PS> Get-CimInstance Win32_Service -Filter "Name='DiagTrack'" |
       Format-List Name,State,StartMode,ProcessId
Name      : DiagTrack
State     : Running
StartMode : Auto
ProcessId : 10148
PS> Get-CimInstance Win32_Process -Filter "Name='AggregatorHost.exe'" |
       Format-List ProcessId,ParentProcessId
ProcessId       : 1500
ParentProcessId : 10148
PS> tasklist /m MissionControlAggregator.dll
Image Name                     PID Modules
========================= ======== ============================================
AggregatorHost.exe            1500 MissionControlAggregator.dll
The process IDs connect the pieces: DiagTrack runs in process 10148, which is the parent of AggregatorHost.exe, process 1500; the module listing places MissionControlAggregator.dll inside that child.
Testing it in practice
To test the finding in practice, I copied cmd.exe to a file named codex.exe and ran it in the interactive Windows session. The name alone was enough for the component to recognize it as Codex.
I captured an AppStateChangeSummary for the running process from the KernelProcess provider ({2839ff94-8f12-4e1b-82e3-af7af77a450f}), with LaunchCount=1 and TerminateCount=0. The aggregator’s saved state contained the value 2 in CLI slot 2, the Codex slot. A separate run without an additional ETW trace also set that slot, confirming that detection worked through Windows’ existing diagnostic collection.
That state is persisted in:
C:\ProgramData\Microsoft\Diagnosis\AggregatorStorage\MissionControlAggregator$
The report normally follows a roughly daily boundary. To capture it immediately, I temporarily moved the saved reporting-window start 25 hours into the past and restarted DiagTrack with a trace listening for the aggregator. The resulting Segments.SubCohorts event contained:
Provider: Microsoft.Windows.MissionControlAggregator
Time:     2026-11-16T12:34:29.125116Z
Version:  0.1.5
CliAgentSubCohorts:     4
DesktopAgentSubCohorts: 0
FlushedFromSave:       true
The value 4 is 1 << 2: the Codex CLI bit. The name had made it through the input summary, the matcher, the persisted state, and the emitted report.
I also tested the developer-tool path separately, using a copy of cmd.exe named gcc.exe. It set developer-tool slot 3. With optional diagnostic data allowed, I found the resulting Segments.SubCohorts event in DiagTrack’s upload queue with SubCohorts: [8] (1 << 3). This is the compiler/debugger category shared by GCC, Clang, and related tools, rather than a separate bit for each executable.
Following the report toward Microsoft
An ETW event by itself would not establish that anything leaves the machine. ETW is used for plenty of local diagnostics. The more revealing question was who was listening to this particular report.
The VM already had a Diagtrack-Listener session subscribed to both ends of the path. These PowerShell queries showed its subscriptions:
Get-EtwTraceProvider -SessionName Diagtrack-Listener `
  -Guid '{2839ff94-8f12-4e1b-82e3-af7af77a450f}'
Get-EtwTraceProvider -SessionName Diagtrack-Listener `
  -Guid '{00de11a2-ec06-58e5-1a2b-21bb21dbee4b}'
The first subscription, for KernelProcess, had MatchAnyKeyword=0x400000000002, which includes the summary’s 0x400000000000 keyword. The second, for Microsoft.Windows.MissionControlAggregator, had MatchAnyKeyword=0xe00000000000, which includes the report’s 0x800000000000 keyword. Both had level 255.
The subscription showed where the report could go. To check whether Windows actually queued it for transmission, I inspected DiagTrack’s EventStore.db and decompressed the JSON batches in its AsimovEvents table. This revealed an important condition: the agent-usage report is treated as optional diagnostic data.
When optional diagnostic data is allowed (enabled by default in the US setup I tested), Windows queues the coding-agent usage report for upload to Microsoft. I verified this by finding the agent event itself in DiagTrack’s upload queue:
{
  "name": "Microsoft.Windows.MissionControlAggregator.Segments.SubCohorts",
  "ext": {
    "privacy": { "isRequired": false }
  },
  "data": {
    "Version": "0.1.5",
    "CliAgentSubCohorts": [4],
    "DesktopAgentSubCohorts": [0],
    "FlushedFromSave": true
  }
}
I checked this with a clean installation of Windows 11 Home ARM64 using the latest official ISO, with the region set to the United States. Before changing any privacy settings, the Diagnostic data toggle was already enabled and labeled Include Optional. After completing setup with those defaults, AllowTelemetry was 3, confirming that optional diagnostic data was enabled.
Optional diagnostic data is therefore enabled by default, at least in the US setup I tested. If you did not opt out of it during installation or disable optional diagnostic data later, it is still enabled and your coding agent usage data is being uploaded. You can decline optional diagnostics during setup or turn off Send optional diagnostic data later under Settings → Privacy & security → Diagnostics & feedback. Microsoft’s diagnostics and privacy guide explains how to change it.

====================================================================================================

US will say "no" to global ban on killer robots
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-16
22:57:49 UTC
07:57:49 KST

The United States is expected to oppose a push to begin negotiations on a legally binding treaty restricting autonomous weapons, as countries gather in Geneva for a five-day United Nations conference.
The Seventh Review Conference of the Convention on Certain Conventional Weapons (CCW), running from November 16 to 20, will consider the next steps for weapons capable of selecting and engaging targets without further human intervention. The UN has warned that such systems pose growing risks to civilians and raise concerns about compliance with international humanitarian law.
The conference is not expected to produce a treaty this week. Instead, delegates will consider whether to launch negotiations on new rules and whether any resulting agreement should be legally binding.
Read More on News
Pressure for Binding Rules
UN human rights chief Volker Türk has called on states to act before technological development outpaces diplomacy. “The international community has critical choices to make right now about the future of armed conflicts and the role of human beings in decisions over the use of force,” he said. “I call on states to urgently agree on binding international rules to regulate autonomous weapons and protect human lives—before it is too late.”
Türk has also called for prohibitions on autonomous weapons that target people, whether by design or in practice, and on systems whose functioning and effects cannot be adequately understood, predicted or controlled by humans.
Newsweek contacted the State Department for comment on Monday.
Nicole van Rooijen, executive director of the campaign group Stop Killer Robots, said in a statement provided to Newsweek that “the vast majority of states worldwide” favor a legally binding instrument, while only about a dozen, including the U.S., Israel and Russia, oppose one.
According to Stop Killer Robots, 130 countries support a legally binding instrument on autonomous weapons, while more than 70 have indicated they are ready to move forward with negotiations.
“We are at a critical juncture,” van Rooijen said, calling for time-limited negotiations on a binding treaty. Campaigners have warned that failing to act could allow technological developments and battlefield practice to shape the rules governing autonomous weapons.
Rather than negotiating a binding treaty, the U.S. has proposed five more years of work by the CCW's expert group to develop a nonbinding instrument, according to a November 4 working paper.
Washington maintains that existing international humanitarian law already applies to autonomous weapons and that a new legally binding treaty is unnecessary.
In the paper, Washington argued that nonbinding rules could be adopted and updated more quickly as technology and military practices evolve. It said such an approach would avoid creating separate legal regimes applying only to countries that ratified a treaty.
The paper also argued it has not been established that autonomous weapons are inherently indiscriminate or excessively injurious, or that they require new prohibitions.
“Rather, State practice in using autonomy in weapon systems demonstrates that appropriate use of autonomous functions can enhance the implementation of existing prohibitions and restrictions in [international humanitarian law] and reduce the risks to civilians as compared to weapons without such capabilities,” it said. “It would be contrary to the humanitarian objectives and purposes of the CCW for states to hamper responsible innovation that advances those objectives and purposes.”
Already Moving Onto Battlefields
The UN has already warned that the debate over autonomous weapons is no longer purely hypothetical. Autonomous weapons systems are increasingly being tested, integrated and deployed, according to Human Rights Watch, Amnesty International and Stop Killer Robots, while drones have become central to fighting in Ukraine.
Human Rights Watch has warned that autonomous weapons could misidentify targets, discriminate against vulnerable populations and create gaps in accountability when attacks go wrong. In a 2025 report, the organization called for a legally binding treaty prohibiting weapons that operate without meaningful human control or target people, while regulating other autonomous systems.
AI-assisted weapons and targeting systems have been used in conflicts, including Ukraine and the Middle East. However, such technology is not necessarily fully autonomous: systems that help human operators identify targets differ from weapons capable of selecting and attacking targets without further human approval.
“States must ensure that Russia, or any of the handful of governments blocking progress, are not allowed to hold the future hostage. The stakes are too high,” Anna Hehir, head of military AI governance at the Future of Life Institute, said in a statement emailed to Newsweek.
“A clear global majority has spoken: machines should never be able to decide to kill. States must now use all mechanisms possible, both within and beyond the current U.N. CCW process, to keep human control.”
Newsweek’s reporters and editors used Martyn, our AI assistant, to produce this story. Learn more about Martyn here.
Contact Newsweek editors on this story: Henry Holloway and Rhian Morgan.

Less than a week after an issue with air traffic control communications and radar caused the FAA to halt takeoffs nationwide, United Airlines departing flights are being disrupted by an outage. At 6:13PM ET, United said the problem has been fixed:
United Airlines system outage disrupts departures just days after the big ground stop
Is everything down right now?
Is everything down right now?
Earlier today we experienced a technology-related issue that impacted some departing flights. The issue has been resolved. Please check the latest status of your flight at united.com or the United app.
The outage is also coming after last week’s global Microsoft Outlook shutdown, and the great-bank-outage-that-wasn’t earlier Monday afternoon. United tweeted earlier that “We are troubleshooting a technology issue affecting departing flights. There is no impact on flights in progress, or on United Express flights.”

How to Break into Security, Ptacek Edition (2012)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-16
22:19:23 UTC
07:19:23 KST

At least once a month, sometimes more, readers write in to ask how they can break into the field of computer security. Some of the emails are from people in jobs that have nothing to do with security, but who are fascinated enough by the field to contemplate a career change. Others are already in an information technology position but are itching to segue into security. I always respond with my own set of stock answers, but each time I do this, I can’t help but feel my advice is incomplete, or at least not terribly well-rounded.
I decided to ask some of the brightest minds in the security industry today what advice they’d give. Almost everyone I asked said they, too, frequently get asked the very same question, but each had surprisingly different takes on the subject. Today is the first installment in a series of responses to this question. When the last of the advice columns have run, I’ll create an archive of them all that will be anchored somewhere prominently on the home page. That way, the next time someone asks how they can break into security, I’ll have more to offer than just my admittedly narrow perspectives on the matter.
Last month, I interviewed Thomas Ptacek, founder of Matasano Security, about how companies could beef up password security in the wake of a week full of news about password leaks at LinkedIn and other online businesses. Ptacek’s provocative advice generated such a huge amount of reader interest and further discussion that I thought it made sense to begin this series with his thoughts:
Ptacek: “Information security is one of the most interesting, challenging, and, if you do it carefully, rewarding fields in the technology industry. It’s one of the few technology jobs where the most fun roles are well compensated. If you grew up dreaming of developing games, the laws of supply and demand teach a harsh lesson early in your career: game development jobs are often tedious and usually pay badly. But if you watched “Sneakers” and ideated a life spent breaking or defending software, great news: infosec can be more fun in real life, and it’s fairly lucrative.
I’m a software developer. I try to look at security through the lens of computer science. To me, the most attractive thing about the field is the opportunity it provides to work with lots of different concepts at lots of different levels. Computer security might be the best way to work professionally with compiler theory, or to spend time understanding computer microarchitecture, or to apply advanced mathematics.
Other people get involved in security for different reasons, and those reasons are probably equally valid. Some people really like the “good guys / bad guys” narrative that comes with security. Some people see security as an opportunity to save the world. Other people are drawn to the competitive nature of the field: at the higher levels, it really is a cat-and-mouse game. It also comes closer to meritocracy than most of technology: you can either break in or you can’t; your defenses either work or they don’t.
But for me, it’s just one of the best development jobs you can get, and with that in mind, here’s my advice for people interested in pursuing a career in it.
First: I want you to learn how to program. Clearly, you can get through college and get a good-paying steady job without ever learning to love programming. But no one factor gives you as much control over your career, as much of an ability to write your own ticket, as the ability to solve problems using programming languages. A lot of very smart technology professionals have concluded that they just don’t enjoy writing software. Those people should reconsider. Try different languages, or different application domains, but find a way to make programming stick in your head.
Second: the best jobs in our field are in application security. You can get good steady work designing firewall deployments, setting up desktop agents, or responding to incidents. But all those roles are fundamentally reacting to what’s happening in appsec. The next generation of security products — along with the org charts of security teams at the savviest companies — are being designed now, by accident, by application security practitioners.
Appsec roles are roles that involve attacking software or devising fixes and countermeasures for those attacks. Two good ways to “come up” in appsec: become a penetration tester, or contribute to (or start) a secure software development practice in your company.
A good way to move into penetration testing: grab some industry standard tools and use an Amazon EC2 account to set up a “shooting range” to attack. Some of the best-known tools are available for free: the Nessus scanner, for instance, while not an application security tool, is free and can land you a network penetration testing role that you can use as a springboard to breaking applications.
When people reach out to Matasano asking how to get a foot in the door attacking software, we have a few simple steps to offer:
0. Learn to love programming in at least one language. The C Programming Language has the most cachet in application security, but for this step-by-step list, Java or Python or Ruby will do fine.
1. Grab a copy of The Web Application Hacker’s Handbook.
2. Go to the “previous releases” archive at WordPress.org and grab very old versions of WordPress; install them at EC2.
3. Download OWASP WebScarab or Burp Suite Free Edition, both of which are free, and use them to find bugs in ancient WordPress.
If you’re already in an IT role, and want to come up on the defensive side of appsec, try to position yourself near custom software development. Most large firms build “line of business” applications. As a rule, building “line of business” isn’t particularly fun. But defending those apps can be; sometimes, the most boring applications turn out to be surprisingly sensitive.
And the good news for doing appsec in BigCo’s: most companies have very immature security programs. If you can get a role in QA, or in what the cool kids are calling “DevOps”, you can end up with a lot of influence in security. At Matasano, we’ve watched lots of ops people successfully transition to appsec and senior security management just by instituting basic security testing processes on their company’s software.
To me, the whole field boils down to studying, understanding, and manipulating technology. Like most software security practices, we’ve gotten our hands dirty with a fascinating cross-section of the whole technology industry. We’ve written attack tools that target the control registers of device chipsets, we’ve done custom RF work, we’ve written low-level debuggers for different CPU architectures, and we’ve seen and beaten up on products built in almost every programming language and every platform you can imagine. If crawling through the ventilation ducts of the world’s most important technology is something you think might make you tick like we do… well, appsec! Learn to code. Spend a couple days Starbucks money on an EC2 account and deploy some broken apps to break. Find opportunities to practice in your job.
And, uh, come talk to us? People like you are hard to find!”

====================================================================================================
